Our first goal is to show that
\Gamma
can be used as a covariance function.

Lemma 3.1
.

\mathbf{P}
-a.s., the function
\Gamma:[0,1]^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
is symmetric and nonnegative definite i.e:

\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}\Gamma(t_{i},t_{j})\geqslant 0,

(3.3)

for every integer
n\geqslant 1
, every
(t_{1},...,t_{n})\in[0,1]^{n}
and
(\lambda_{1},...,\lambda_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Proof.

The function
\Gamma
is clearly symmetric
\mathbf{P}
-a.s. Let us show that it is also nonnegative definite. This is a straightforward verification. One can establish directly from the definition that
\mathbf{P}
-a.s.:

\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}\Gamma(t_{i},t_{j})\geqslant\big(\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}\big)^{2}\tilde{d}(0,t_{1}\curlywedge...\curlywedge t_{n}),

(3.4)

simultaneously for every integer
n\geqslant 1
,
(t_{1},...,t_{n})\in[0,1]^{n}
and
(\lambda_{1},...,\lambda_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. This can be proved by induction on
n
, by cutting at time
t_{1}\curlywedge...\curlywedge t_{n}
.
Since this verification is a little bit tedious, we are going to deduce (
3.4
) from the following result due to Archer
[
9
, Lemma 4.5]
. Under
\mathbf{P}
, for every
n\geqslant 1
and
(t_{1},...,t_{n})\in[0,1]^{n}
, there exists a graph
G
with conductances such that:

—

The set vertices of
G
is
{V}(G):=\{t_{i}:\>1\leqslant i\leqslant n\}\cup\{t_{i}\curlywedge t_{j}:\>1\leqslant i\leqslant j\leqslant n\}
;

—

For every
s,t\in{V}(G)
:

\widetilde{d}(s,t)=R_{G}(s\leftrightarrow t),

where
R_{G}(s\leftrightarrow t)
stands for the effective resistance in
G
between
s
and
t
.

In particular, notice that the point
r:=t_{1}\curlywedge...\curlywedge t_{n}
is a vertex of
G
. Moreover, by a classical result on networks
[
103
, Exercise 2.59 and the comments about it]
the function:

{V}({G})\ni(s,t)\mapsto\frac{1}{2}R_{G}(r\leftrightarrow s)+\frac{1}{2}R_{G}(r\leftrightarrow t)-\frac{1}{2}R_{G}(s\leftrightarrow t),

is nonnegative definite. Since for every
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
we have
\tilde{d}(0,t_{i})=\tilde{d}(0,r)+\tilde{d}(r,t_{i})
,
we infer that for every
\lambda_{1},...,\lambda_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
:

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}\Gamma(t_{i},t_{j})

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}\tilde{d}(0,r)+\frac{1}{2}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}\big(\tilde{d}(r,t_{i})+\tilde{d}(r,t_{j})-\tilde{d}(t_{i},t_{j})\big)

\displaystyle=\big(\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}\big)^{2}\tilde{d}(0,r)+\frac{1}{2}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}\big(R_{G}(r\leftrightarrow t_{i})+R_{G}(r\leftrightarrow t_{j})-R_{G}(t_{i}\leftrightarrow t_{j})\big)

\displaystyle\geqslant\big(\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}\big)^{2}\tilde{d}(0,r),

and we obtain (
3.4
). Consequently,
\mathbf{P}
-a.s., the function
\Gamma:[0,1]^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
is symmetric and nonnegative definite.
∎

As a consequence of Lemma
3.1
we can consider, conditionally on
X
, a centered Gaussian process
(Z^{*}_{t}:t\in[0,1])
with covariance function
\Gamma
.
In particular, since
\tilde{d}(0,0)=\tilde{d}(0,1)=0
, we have
Z^{*}_{0}=Z^{*}_{1}=0
. To avoid confusion, we denote the conditional distribution (resp. expectation) of the above process by
\mathbf{P}_{X}
(resp.
\mathbf{E}_{X}
). In particular, we have
\mathbf{E}_{X}[Z^{*}_{s}Z^{*}_{t}]=\Gamma(s,t)
. Although the looptree
\mathcal{L}
is itself a random fractal object, the machinery of Gaussian processes is powerful enough to prove that there is a modification of
Z^{*}
continuous with respect to
\tilde{d}
with a strong control on the modulus of continuity:

Proposition 3.2
.

There exists a modification of
Z^{*}
which is continuous for the pseudo-distance
\tilde{d}
(hence for the Euclidean norm on
[0,1]
) for which the following hold:

(i)

There exist constants
c,C,\beta>0
, such that, for every
x\geqslant 0
,

\mathbf{P}(\sup Z^{*}>x)\leqslant C\cdot\exp(-cx^{\beta}).

(ii)

There exist constants
\mathrm{c},\mathrm{C}>0
,
x_{0}>1
such that, for every
x\geqslant x_{0}
and every integer
n\geqslant 1
,

\mathbf{P}\left(\exists s,t\>\text{with}\>\tilde{d}(s,t)<2^{-n}\>\text{such that}\>|Z_{s}^{*}-Z_{t}^{*}|\geqslant x\sqrt{n}2^{-n/2}\right)\leqslant\mathrm{C}\cdot\exp(-\mathrm{c}x^{2}n).

Proof.

The proof relies on classical results on Gaussian processes such as Dudley’s theorem. In this direction, recall our upper bound
N_{r}
on the
r
-covering number of
(\mathcal{L},\tilde{d})
defined just before Lemma
2.4
. We introduce the random variable

M:=\sup\limits_{r\in(0,1]}\frac{N_{r}}{\big(r^{-1}\log(1+r^{-1})\big)^{\alpha}},

which by Lemma
2.4
is almost surely finite,
and we write
\mathrm{diam}=\sup\{\tilde{d}(s,t):\penalty\ s,t\in[0,1]\}
for the diameter of
\mathcal{L}
with respect to
\tilde{d}
. Next, we observe that:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\sqrt{\log\big(N_{r^{2}}\big)}\penalty\ {\rm d}r

\displaystyle\leqslant\int_{0}^{1}\sqrt{\log\big(N_{r^{2}}\big)}\penalty\ {\rm d}r+\mathrm{diam}\cdot\sqrt{\log\big(N_{1}\big)}.

Since
\mathrm{diam}\leqslant 2N_{1}
, a direct computation using Jensen’s inequality combined with the fact that
M\geqslant N_{1}/\log(2)^{\alpha}>1
yields the existence of a constant
C_{1}\in(0,\infty)
such that:

\int_{0}^{\infty}\sqrt{\log\big(N_{r^{2}}\big)}\penalty\ {\rm d}r\leqslant C_{1}\cdot M\sqrt{\log(M)}.

(3.5)

Since
M
is almost surely finite we can apply Dudley’s theorem (see
[
107
, Theorem 6.1.2]
for a reference, where the distance
d_{X}
used therein corresponds to
\sqrt{\tilde{d}}
in our setting), which implies that the process
Z^{*}
admits a continuous modification for
\tilde{d}
satisfying

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{X}[\sup Z^{*}]

\displaystyle\leqslant C_{2}\cdot M\sqrt{\log(M)},

(3.6)

for some constant
C_{2}\in(0,\infty)
. In the remainder of the proof, we consider this continuous modification. Dudley’s theorem (see the same reference) also ensures the existence of another constant
C_{3}\in(0,\infty)
such that

\mathbf{E}_{X}\Big[\sup\limits_{\tilde{d}(s,t)<2^{-n}}|Z^{*}_{s}-Z^{*}_{t}|\Big]\leqslant C_{3}\cdot\int_{0}^{2^{-\frac{n}{2}}}\sqrt{\log\big(N_{r^{2}}\big)}\penalty\ \mathrm{d}r,

for every
n\geqslant 0
. Therefore, another application of Jensen inequality, combined with
M\geqslant 1
and the identify
\int_{0}^{t}\log(u)\mathrm{d}u=t\log(t)-t
, ensures that there exists
a constant
C_{4}\in(0,\infty)
such that:

\mathbf{E}_{X}\Big[\sup\limits_{\tilde{d}(s,t)<2^{-n}}|Z^{*}_{s}-Z^{*}_{t}|\Big]\leqslant C_{4}\cdot 2^{-\frac{n}{2}}\left(\sqrt{\log(M)}+\sqrt{n}\right),

(3.7)

for every
n\geqslant 0
. We stress that the constants
C_{1},C_{2},C_{3}
and
C_{4}
are universal and do not depend on
X
. To simplify the following expressions, we set
C:=3\vee C_{1}\vee C_{2}\vee C_{3}\vee C_{4}
. To strengthen these expectation estimates into tail estimates as stated in points (i) and (ii), we shall use a version of the Borell–TIS inequality, which states that the supremum of a Gaussian process exhibits Gaussian tails when centered around its mean.

Let us start proving (i). In this direction, an application of
[
107
, Theorem 5.4.3]
entails that, for every
x>0
, we have

\mathbf{P}_{X}\Big(\sup Z^{*}-\mathbf{E}_{X}[\sup Z^{*}]>x\Big)\leqslant 2\exp(-\frac{x^{2}}{2\text{v}_{*}}),\quad\mbox{
where }\text{v}_{*}:=\sup\limits_{s\in[0,1]}\mathbf{E}_{X}[(Z^{*}_{s})^{2}].

Let
x>C
, and, to simplify notation, consider the unique
a>1
such that
x=C\cdot a\log(a)
. Then, by (
3.6
), we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}(\sup Z^{*}>2x)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{E}\big[\mathbf{P}_{X}(\sup Z^{*}>2x)\big]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{E}\Big[\mathbf{P}_{X}\big(\sup Z^{*}-\mathbf{E}_{X}[\sup Z^{*}]>2x-\mathbf{E}_{X}[\sup Z^{*}]\big)\Big]

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathbf{P}\big(M\geqslant a\big)+\mathbf{E}\Big[\mathbbm{1}_{M<a}\mathbf{P}_{X}(\sup Z^{*}-\mathbf{E}_{X}[\sup Z^{*}]>{x})\Big]

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathbf{P}\big(M\geqslant a\big)+2\mathbf{E}[\mathbbm{1}_{M<a}\exp(-\frac{x^{2}}{2\text{v}_{*}})].

Point (i) now follows by using Lemma
2.4
and noticing that, under the event
\{M<a\}
, we have:

\displaystyle\text{v}_{*}

\displaystyle=\sup\limits_{s\in[0,1]}\mathbf{E}_{X}[(Z^{*}_{s})^{2}]\leqslant\mathrm{diam}\leqslant 2N_{1}\leqslant 2M\leqslant 2x.

We proceed similarly to establish (ii). Recall the notation
0<s_{1}<...<s_{N_{2^{-n}}}\leqslant 1
introduced prior to Lemma
2.4
, so that
[0,1]=\bigcup\limits_{i=1}^{N_{2^{-n}}}B_{\tilde{d}}(s_{i},2^{-n})
. In particular, for every
s,t\in[0,1]
with
\tilde{d}(s,t)<2^{-n}
, there exists
1\leqslant i\leqslant N_{2^{-n}}
such that
s,t\in B_{\tilde{d}}(s_{i},2^{-n+1})
. It follows that:

\sup\limits_{\tilde{d}(s,t)<2^{-n}}|Z_{s}^{*}-Z_{t}^{*}|\leqslant 2\cdot\sup\limits_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N_{2^{-n}}}\sup\limits_{s\in B_{\tilde{d}}(s_{i},2^{-n+1})}|Z_{s}^{*}-Z_{s_{i}}^{*}|.

To control the right-hand side we note that under
\mathbf{P}_{X}
, for every
1\leqslant i\leqslant N_{2^{-n}}
, the process

\Big(Z_{s}^{*}-Z_{s_{i}}^{*}:s\in B_{\tilde{d}}(s_{i},2^{-n+1})\Big)

is a centered Gaussian process with

\sup\limits_{s\in B_{\tilde{d}}(s_{i},2^{-n+1})}\limits\mathbf{E}_{X}\big[(Z_{s}^{*}-Z_{s_{i}}^{*})^{2}\big]\leqslant 2^{-n+1}.

We can then apply
[
107
, Theorem 5.4.3]
again combined with the bound (
3.7
), to obtain as above that, under the event
\{\log(M)\leqslant x^{2}n\}
, for every
x\geqslant 1
and
1\leqslant i\leqslant N_{2^{-n}}
:

\mathbf{P}_{X}\Big(\sup\limits_{s\in B_{\tilde{d}}(s_{i},2^{-n+1})}|Z_{s}^{*}-Z_{s_{i}}^{*}|>5Cx\cdot\sqrt{n}2^{-n/2}\Big)\leqslant 2\exp(-C^{2}x^{2}n).

Next, since
\alpha\in(1,2)
, a direct computation shows that under
\{\log(M)\leqslant x^{2}n\}
we also have
N_{2^{-n}}\leqslant\exp((4+x^{2})n)
. Therefore, recalling that
C\geqslant 3
, a union bound gives

\mathbf{P}\Big(\sup\limits_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N_{2^{-n}}}\sup\limits_{s\in B_{d}(s_{i},2^{-n+1})}|Z_{s}^{*}-Z_{s_{i}}^{*}|>5Cx\cdot\sqrt{n}2^{-n/2}\Big)\leqslant\mathbf{P}\big(\log(M)\geqslant x^{2}n\big)+2\exp\big(-4x^{2}n\big).

The desired result now follows by Lemma
2.4
.
∎

From now on we consider the
\tilde{d}
-continuous modification of
Z^{*}
, that we still denote by
Z^{*}
. As a direct consequence of Proposition
3.2
, we have
\mathbf{P}\text{-a.s.}
, for all
s,t\in[0,1]

d(s,t)=0\implies Z_{s}^{*}=Z_{t}^{*}.

(3.8)

This allows to interpret the process
Z^{*}
as a label process on
\mathcal{L}
, and, with a slight abuse of notation, we continue to denote it by
Z^{*}
. Namely, for every
u\in\mathcal{L}
, set
Z_{u}^{*}:=Z_{t}^{*}
where
t
is any preimage of
u
by
\Pi_{d}
. Furthermore, point
\mathrm{(ii)}
and the Borel-Cantelli lemma show that
u\in\mathcal{L}\mapsto Z^{*}_{u}
is
(\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon)
- Hölder continuous for every
\varepsilon>0
. By (
2.11
), the mapping
(s,t)\mapsto d(s,t)
is itself
\frac{1}{\alpha}-\varepsilon
Hölder continuous for every
\varepsilon>0
. We deduce that for every
\varepsilon>0
, there exists a random variable
W\equiv W_{\varepsilon}\in(0,\infty)
such that

\displaystyle|Z_{u}^{*}-Z_{v}^{*}|\leqslant W\cdot d(u,v)^{\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon},\>\>u,v\in\mathcal{L},\quad\mbox{ and }\quad|Z_{s}^{*}-Z_{t}^{*}|\leqslant W\cdot|s-t|^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}-\varepsilon},\>\>s,t\in[0,1].

(3.9)

In particular,
t\mapsto Z^{*}_{t}
is
\frac{1}{2\alpha}-\varepsilon
Hölder continuous, for every
\varepsilon>0
.

Figure 11:
Simulation of the label process
Z^{*}
over
[0,1]
(on the left) and seen on the looptree of Figure
5
on the right.

3.2
Equivalence of the constructions

The goal of this section is to establish that
(X,Z)
and
(X,Z^{*})
have the same distribution under
\mathbf{P}
. To this end, it will be useful to consider the trace of
Z^{*}
on the loops of
\mathcal{L}
. Recall that
(\mathrm{t}_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is a measurable indexation of the jumping times of
X
, and recall the notation

\mathrm{f}_{\mathrm{t}_{i}}(s)=\inf\{r\geqslant\mathrm{t}_{i}:\penalty\ X_{r}=X_{\mathrm{t}_{i}}-s\Delta_{\mathrm{t}_{i}}\}
for
s\in[0,1]
, which stands for the parametrization of the loop associated with
\mathrm{t}_{i}
. For every index
i\in\mathbb{N}
, we consider the function:

\mathrm{b}_{i}^{*}(s):=\Delta_{\mathrm{t}_{i}}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\cdot\big(Z_{\mathrm{f}_{\mathrm{t}_{i}}(s)}^{*}-Z_{\mathrm{t}_{i}}^{*}\big),\quad s\in[0,1],

(3.10)

which is a continuous process. We have the following result:

Proposition 3.3

(Equivalence of the constructions)
.

With the above notation, under
\mathbf{P}
and conditionally on
X
, the family
(\mathrm{b}_{i}^{*})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is formed of i.i.d. Brownian bridges with lifetime
1
starting and ending at
0
. Moreover, we have
(X,Z^{*})=(X,Z)
in distribution under
\mathbf{P}
.

Proof.

We begin by showing that
(\mathrm{b}_{i}^{*})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is a sequence of i.i.d. Brownian bridges with lifetime
1
, starting and ending at
0
, independent of
X
. In this direction, we work under
\mathbf{P}_{X}
. Note that by construction, conditionally on
X
, the process
(\mathrm{b}_{i}^{*}(s))_{(i,s)\in\mathbb{N}\times[0,1]}
is a Gaussian process. Furthermore, for every
i\in\mathbb{N}
and
s,t\in[0,1]
, we have:

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{X}\big[\mathrm{b}_{i}^{*}(t)\mathrm{b}_{i}^{*}(s)]

\displaystyle=\Delta_{\mathrm{t}_{i}}^{-1}\cdot\Big(\mathbf{E}_{X}[(Z_{\mathrm{f}_{\mathrm{t}_{i}}(s)}^{*}-Z_{\mathrm{t}_{i}}^{*})(Z_{\mathrm{f}_{\mathrm{t}_{i}}(t)}^{*}-Z_{\mathrm{t}_{i}}^{*})]\Big)

\displaystyle=\Delta_{\mathrm{t}_{i}}^{-1}\cdot\Big(\Gamma(\mathrm{f}_{\mathrm{t}_{i}}(s),\mathrm{f}_{\mathrm{t}_{i}}(t))+\Gamma(\mathrm{t}_{i},\mathrm{t}_{i})-\Gamma(\mathrm{f}_{\mathrm{t}_{i}}(s),\mathrm{t}_{i})-\Gamma(\mathrm{f}_{\mathrm{t}_{i}}(t),\mathrm{t}_{i})\Big).

A direct computation using (
3.2
) gives that the previous display is equal to
\frac{1}{2}\tilde{\delta}(0,t)+\frac{1}{2}\tilde{\delta}(0,s)-\frac{1}{2}\tilde{\delta}(t,s)
,
which is the covariance function of a Brownian bridge with lifetime
1
starting and ending at
0
. In particular, it does not depend on the realization of
X
. Moreover, for
i\neq j
in
\mathbb{N}
and
s,t
in
[0,1]
, a similar computation shows that

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{X}\big[\mathrm{b}^{*}_{i}(t)\mathrm{b}^{*}_{j}(s)]=\Delta_{\mathrm{t}_{i}}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Delta_{t_{j}}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\mathbf{E}_{X}\big[(Z_{\mathrm{f}_{\mathrm{t}_{i}}(s)}^{*}-Z_{\mathrm{t}_{i}}^{*})(Z_{\mathrm{f}_{\mathrm{t}_{i}}(t)}^{*}-Z_{t_{j}}^{*})\big]=0.

Therefore,
(\mathrm{b}_{i}^{*})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
has the same finite-dimensional marginals as a family of i.i.d. Brownian bridges with lifetime
1
, starting and ending at
0
, independent of
X
, and since the processes
b^{*}_{i}
,
i\in\mathbb{N}
, have continuous sample paths, we deduce that they indeed form a family of i.i.d. Brownian bridges. It is then clear that we can couple the construction of
Z
and
Z^{*}
using the same Brownian bridges
\mathrm{b}_{i}=\mathrm{b}_{i}^{*}
. Since both processes are continuous, to conclude it suffices to establish that, with this coupling and for every fixed
t\in[0,1]
, we have
Z_{t}=Z_{t}^{*}
,
\mathbf{P}
-a.s. To this end, note that this reduces to showing that:

\mathbf{E}_{X}\Big[\Big(Z_{t}^{*}-\sum\limits_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\Delta_{\mathrm{t}_{i}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\cdot\mathrm{b}_{i}^{*}\big(\frac{x_{\mathrm{t}_{i},t}}{\Delta_{\mathrm{t}_{i}}}\big)\Big)^{2}\Big]=0,\quad t\geqslant 0.

For the remaining of the proof we fix
t\in[0,1]
. To simplify notation, for
i\in\mathbb{N}
, set
u_{i}=\frac{x_{\mathrm{t}_{i},t}}{\Delta_{\mathrm{t}_{i}}}
, and observe that the expectation in the previous display can be decomposed in the form:

\mathbf{E}_{X}\big[(Z_{t}^{*})^{2}\big]+\mathbf{E}_{X}\Big[\Big(\sum\limits_{\overset{i\in\mathbb{N}}{\mathrm{t}_{i}\preccurlyeq t}}\Delta_{\mathrm{t}_{i}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\cdot\mathrm{b}_{i}^{*}\big(u_{i}\big)\Big)^{2}\Big]-2\sum\limits_{\mathrm{t}_{i}\preccurlyeq t}\Delta_{\mathrm{t}_{i}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\cdot\mathbf{E}_{X}\big[Z_{t}^{*}\>\mathrm{b}_{i}^{*}\big(u_{i}\big)\big].

(3.11)

We are going to conclude by computing each term separately. First, it follows from (
3.2
) that
\mathbf{E}_{X}\big[(Z_{t}^{*})^{2}\big]=\tilde{d}(0,t)
. Moreover, using the independence of the
(b_{i}^{*})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
, we get:

\mathbf{E}_{X}\Big[\Big(\sum\limits_{\overset{i\in\mathbb{N}}{\mathrm{t}_{i}\preccurlyeq t}}\Delta_{\mathrm{t}_{i}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\cdot\mathrm{b}_{i}^{*}\big(u_{i}\big)\Big)^{2}\Big]=\sum\limits_{\overset{i\in\mathbb{N}}{\mathrm{t}_{i}\preccurlyeq t}}\Delta_{t_{i}}\cdot\widetilde{\delta}(0,u_{i})=\tilde{d}(0,t).

Therefore to conclude, we need to show that the remaining term in (
3.11
) equals
-2\tilde{d}(0,t)
. To this end, note that it follows straightforwardly from definitions (
3.2
) and (
3.10
), that under
\mathbf{P}_{X}
, the random variables
Z_{\mathrm{t}_{i}}^{*}
and
Z_{t}^{*}-Z_{\mathrm{f}_{\mathrm{t}_{i}}(u_{i})}^{*}
are independent of
\mathrm{b}_{i}^{*}(u_{i})
. We infer that:

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{X}\big[Z_{t}^{*}\>\mathrm{b}_{i}^{*}\big(u_{i}\big)\big]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{E}_{X}\big[\big(Z_{t}^{*}-Z_{\mathrm{f}_{\mathrm{t}_{i}}(u_{i})}^{*}\big)\>\mathrm{b}_{i}^{*}(u_{i})\big]+\mathbf{E}_{X}\big[\big(Z_{\mathrm{f}_{\mathrm{t}_{i}}(u_{i})}^{*}-Z_{\mathrm{t}_{i}}^{*}\big)\>\mathrm{b}_{i}^{*}(u_{i})\big]+\mathbf{E}_{X}\big[Z_{\mathrm{t}_{i}}^{*}\>\mathrm{b}_{i}^{*}\big(u_{i})\big]

\displaystyle=\Delta_{\mathrm{t}_{i}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\cdot\mathbf{E}_{X}\big[\mathrm{b}_{i}^{*}(u_{i})^{2}\big]=\Delta_{\mathrm{t}_{i}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\cdot\widetilde{\delta}(0,u_{i}),

As a consequence, we get:

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\mathrm{t}_{i}\preccurlyeq t}\Delta_{\mathrm{t}_{i}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\cdot\mathbf{E}_{X}\big[Z_{t}^{*}\>\mathrm{b}_{i}^{*}\big(u_{i}\big)\big]=\sum\limits_{\overset{i\in\mathbb{N}}{\mathrm{t}_{i}\preccurlyeq t}}x_{\mathrm{t}_{i},t}(\Delta_{\mathrm{t}_{i}}-x_{\mathrm{t}_{i},t})=\widetilde{d}(0,t),

as wanted.
∎

We conclude this subsection with the re-rooting invariance property. For every
s,t\in[0,1]
, set
s\oplus t=s+t
if
s+t\leqslant 1
and
s\oplus t=(s+t)-1
otherwise. We then claim that:

Lemma 3.4

(Invariance by re-rooting and time reversal)
.

For every
s\in[0,1]
, under
\mathbf{P}
we have:

\big(d(s\oplus t,s\oplus t^{\prime}),\tilde{d}(s\oplus t,s\oplus t^{\prime}),Z_{s\oplus u}-Z_{s}\big)_{t,t^{\prime},u\in[0,1]}\overset{(d)}{=}\big(d(t,t^{\prime}),\tilde{d}(t,t^{\prime}),Z_{u}\big)_{t,t^{\prime},u\in[0,1]}

(3.12)

and

\big(d(1-t,1-t^{\prime}),\tilde{d}(1-t,1-t^{\prime}),Z_{1-u}\big)_{t,t^{\prime},u\in[0,1]}\overset{(d)}{=}\big(d(t,t^{\prime}),\tilde{d}(t,t^{\prime}),Z_{u}\big)_{t,t^{\prime},u\in[0,1]}.

(3.13)

Proof.

First, remark that for every
s\in[0,1]
, conditionally on
X
, the process
(Z_{s\oplus u})_{u\in[0,1]}
is a Gaussian process with covariance function characterized by
\mathbf{E}_{X}[(Z_{s\oplus u}-Z_{s\oplus u^{\prime}})^{2}]=\tilde{d}(s\oplus u,s\oplus u^{\prime})
, for every
u,u^{\prime}\in[0,1]
. Similarly, the process
(Z_{1-u})_{u\in[0,1]}
is, conditionally on
X
, a Gaussian process with covariance function characterized by
\mathbf{E}_{X}[(Z_{1-u}-Z_{1-u^{\prime}})^{2}]=\tilde{d}(1-u,1-u^{\prime})
, for every
u,u^{\prime}\in[0,1]
. Consequently, it suffices to prove that:

\big(d(s\oplus t,s\oplus t^{\prime}),\tilde{d}(s\oplus t,s\oplus t^{\prime})\big)_{t,t^{\prime}\in[0,1]}\overset{(d)}{=}\big(d(t,t^{\prime}),\tilde{d}(t,t^{\prime})\big)_{t,t^{\prime}\in[0,1]}

and

\big(d(1-t,1-t^{\prime}),\tilde{d}(1-t,1-t^{\prime})\big)_{t,t^{\prime}\in[0,1]}\overset{(d)}{=}\big(d(t,t^{\prime}),\tilde{d}(t,t^{\prime})\big)_{t,t^{\prime}\in[0,1]}.

In principle, this should follows from invariance properties of the excursion process
X
. However, we take a different route
by reasoning from the discrete setting. Indeed, the looptree
\mathcal{L}
can be obtained as limit (in Gromov-Hausdorff sense) of discrete dissections equipped with the graph distance (we refer to
[
47
, Theorem 1.3]
for more details). These discrete dissections are also symmetric with respect to the root and are invariant by rotation (a more formal statement is given in Section
4.2
and Remark 4.6 therein). Taking the limit, this entails the desired invariance properties for
d
. It is also possible to define a discrete analog of
\tilde{d}
in the setting of discrete dissections, then a minor adaptation of the proof of
[
9
, Proposition 4.6]
gives the desired properties for
(d,\tilde{d})
.
∎

3.3
Markov property

This section is devoted to the Markov properties of the process
(X,Z)
, inherited from the standard Markov properties of stable Lévy processes. Recall from the beginning of Part
I
that the canonical process
X
is distributed under
\mathbf{Q}
as the
\alpha
-stable Lévy process without negative jumps and under
\mathbf{N}^{(v)}
as the excursion above its running infimum with lifetime
v>0
. Recall also that
\mathbf{N}
stands for the excursion measure and we write
\sigma:=\sup\{t\geqslant 0:\penalty\ X_{t}\neq 0\}
for the excursion lifetime. As in the case of the measure
\mathbf{P}
, we can enrich
\mathbf{N}^{(v)}
,
\mathbf{N}
, and
\mathbf{Q}
so that they support an i.i.d. sequence of Brownian bridges. More precisely, recall that at the beginning of Section
3
we considered an auxiliary probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{G},P)
that supports a countable collection
(\mathrm{b}_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
of independent Brownian bridges starting and ending at
0
with lifetime
1
. Then, as we did for
\mathbf{P}
, we work on the product space
\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{R})\times\Omega
and consider the measures
\mathbf{N}^{(v)}\otimes P
,
\mathbf{N}\otimes P
, and
\mathbf{Q}\otimes P
. For simplicity we will continue to use the notation
\mathbf{N}^{(v)}
,
\mathbf{N}
and
\mathbf{Q}
to refer to these measures. In particular, the disintegration relation (
1.12
) still holds.

Next note that, for
v>0
, the notation and results of the previous sections extend directly replacing
\mathbf{P}
by
\mathbf{N}^{(v)}
and the interval
[0,1]
by
[0,v]
. In particular, we can consider the associated pseudo-distance
d
, canonical projection
\Pi_{d}
, height process
H
and label process
Z
. Moreover, by the scaling property of the underlying Lévy process, we have:

\Big(\big(X,Z,H\big):\penalty\ \text{ under }\mathbf{N}^{(v)}\Big)\overset{(d)}{=}\Big(\big(v^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}X_{t/v},v^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}}Z_{t/v},v^{\frac{\alpha-1}{\alpha}}H_{t/v}\big)_{t\geqslant 0}:\penalty\ \penalty\ \text{ under }\mathbf{P}\Big)

(3.14)

Using the disintegration relation (
1.12
), it follows that we can extend also to
\mathbf{N}
the notation used under
\mathbf{P}
and in particular the process
Z
becomes a continuous process with lifetime
\sigma
.

We can argue similarly under
\mathbf{Q}
, although some clarifications are needed. Specifically, we extend the definitions of
\preccurlyeq
,
\prec
and
x_{s,t}
for
s,t\geqslant 0
directly by replacing
[0,1]
by
\mathbb{R}_{+}
while adding the convention that
0
is an ancestor for all
s\geqslant 0
.
Then under
\mathbf{Q}
, we continue to use the same definition
d_{0}
, given in (
2.1
), but we set:

d(s,t):=d_{0}(s\curlywedge t,s)+d_{0}(s\curlywedge t,t)+\Delta_{s\curlywedge t}\cdot\delta\big(\frac{x_{s\curlywedge t,s}}{\Delta_{s\curlywedge t}},\frac{x_{s\curlywedge t,t}}{\Delta_{s\curlywedge t}}\big)+|I_{t}-I_{s}|,

for
s,t\geqslant 0
. In words, we treat time
0
as corresponding to an “infinite loop” consisting of the points
\{s\geqslant 0:X_{s}=I_{s}\}
. These definitions are consistent with the previous ones since, under
\mathbf{N}
and
\mathbf{P}
, the process
X
is non negative and thus
I_{t}=0
for every
t\geqslant 0
. The map
d:\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}\mapsto\mathbb{R}_{+}
, under
\mathbf{Q}
, is a pseudo-distance and we can then consider the associated equivalence relation
\sim_{d}
and canonical projection
\Pi_{d}
. Lastly, we must extend the label process. Under
\mathbf{Q}
the limit of the series (
3.1
) also exists for the
L^{2}
-norm (see
[
97
]
and note that the sum in (
3.1
) does not take into account time
0
) and has a continuous modification
\widetilde{Z}
as in the previous section. We then specify the labels on the “infinite loop” by further enlarging the underlying probability space and introducing an extra standard Brownian motion
(\mathrm{b}_{0}(t))_{t\geqslant 0}
independent of
X
and
(\mathrm{b}_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
. Under
\mathbf{Q}
, we then set:

\displaystyle Z_{t}:=\mathrm{b}_{0}(-I_{t})+\widetilde{Z}_{t},\quad t\geqslant 0,

(3.15)

Equivalently, the process
Z
is obtained by concatenating the processes
Z
constructed in each excursion of
X
above its running infimum
t\mapsto I_{t}
, with each one shifted by the associated value
\mathrm{b}_{0}(-I_{t})
, for
t\geq 0
. We emphasize again that all these definitions are compatible with the previous ones, so there should be no ambiguity.

Under
\mathbf{Q}
, we extend the notation used under
\mathbf{P}
. To keep the framework consistent, we set
\mathrm{b}_{0}:=0
under both
\mathbf{N}
and
\mathbf{P}
by convention. Moreover, it follows directly from Lemma
3.3
and excursion theory that, under
\mathbf{N}
and
\mathbf{Q}
, and conditioned on
X
, the process remains a Gaussian process with the same covariance function given by (
3.2
), except that the interval
[0,1]
is replaced by
[0,\sigma]
and
\mathbb{R}_{+}
, respectively.

Markov property under
\mathbf{Q}
.

For
t\geqslant 0
, introduce
\mathcal{F}_{t}
the
sigma-field generated by
(X_{s}:\penalty\ 0\leqslant s\leqslant t)
, by
(\mathrm{b}_{0}(s):\penalty\ 0\leqslant s\leqslant-I_{t})
, and by the point measure

\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N},\mathrm{t}_{i}\leqslant t}\delta_{\mathrm{t}_{i},\mathrm{b}_{i}}\penalty\ ,

and completed by the collection of all
\mathbf{Q}
-negligible sets. Let
T
be an
(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}
–stopping time such that
T<\infty
almost surely under
\mathbf{Q}
. Since
X
is strong Markov,
(X_{t\wedge T})_{t\geqslant 0}
is
\mathcal{F}_{T}
–measurable. Also,
it follows from (
3.1
) that
(X_{t\wedge T},Z_{t\wedge T})_{t\geqslant 0}
is
\mathcal{F}_{T}
–measurable. It is then straightforward to infer from the classical strong Markov property of
X
that conditionally on
\mathcal{F}_{T}
, the shifted process
(X_{T+s}-X_{T}:s\geqslant 0)
whose jumps are decorated with the Brownian bridges has the same law as the initial decorated process. To be more precise, note that we could have constructed the process
(X,Z)
under
\mathbf{Q}
as a measurable function of the Brownian motion
\mathrm{b}_{0}
and of an indendent Poisson point measure

\mathcal{M}=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\delta_{(\mathrm{t}_{i},\Delta_{i},\mathrm{b}_{i})}

with intensity
\mathrm{d}t\,\Gamma(-\alpha)^{-1}x^{-\alpha-1}\mathrm{d}x\mathbbm{1}_{x>0}\,\mathbb{P}^{(1)}_{0\to 0}(\mathrm{d}b)
, where the atoms
\Delta_{i}
are the jumps of the process
X
. Indeed, by the Lévy-Itô representation,
X
can be reconstructed from this jump process, and this reprensentation is a convenient way to express the fact that every jump of
X
is marked by an independent Brownian bridge. It is now straightforward that if
T
is a finite
(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}
–stopping time, then conditionally on
\mathcal{F}_{T}
, the measure
\mathcal{M}^{(T)}=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}:t_{i}>T}\delta_{(\mathrm{t}_{i}-T,\Delta_{i},\mathrm{b}_{i})}
has same distribution as
\mathcal{M}
: this is proved, as is usual, by first assuming that
T
takes countably many values, and then by approximating a general stopping time
T
by
2^{-n}\lceil 2^{n}T\rceil
, first considering expectations of functionals of
\mathcal{M}^{(T)}
that do not depend on the values of the atoms
\Delta<\varepsilon
for some
\varepsilon>0
.

Let us recast this “enriched” version of the strong Markov property in terms of excursion theory for
(X,Z)
in a more practical way. Let
t>0
, under
\mathbf{Q}
, consider
(u_{i},v_{i})_{i\geqslant 1}
the connected components of the open set
\{r>t:\>X_{r}>I_{t,r}\}
and
introduce the excursions processes

X_{s}^{i}:=X_{(u_{i}+s)\wedge v_{i}}-X_{u_{i}}\>\>\text{and}\>\>Z_{s}^{i}:=Z_{(u_{i}+s)\wedge v_{i}}-Z_{u_{i}},\qquad s\geqslant 0,

and the point measure

\mathcal{N}^{[t]}:=\sum\limits_{i\geqslant 1}\delta_{X_{u_{i}},X^{i},Z^{i}}.

(3.16)

Lemma 3.5

(Markov property via excursion theory)
.

Let
T
be an
(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}
–stopping time such that
\mathbf{Q}(T<\infty)=1
. Under
\mathbf{Q}(\cdot\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{T})
, the point measure
\mathcal{N}^{[T]}
is a Poisson measure with intensity

\mathbbm{1}_{(-\infty,X_{T}]}(x)\mathrm{d}x\>\mathbf{N}(\mathrm{d}X\>\mathrm{d}Z).

Proof.

By the preceding discussion, it suffices to prove this statement for
T=0
. The excursions of the Lévy process
X
above its past minimum
I
form a Poisson random measure by a classical result of excursion theory (see
[
17
, Chap. IV]
). Here, we use the fact that the local time associated with the excursion measure
\mathbf{N}
is
t\mapsto-I_{t}
. Now, given the fact that the Brownian bridges decorating the jumps of
X
are i.i.d. conditionally on
X
, we deduce that the same property is true within each of the excursions
X^{i}
of
X
.
We conclude since, by (
3.1
), the process
Z^{i}
is a measurable functional of the jump process of
X^{i}
decorated by the Brownian bridges.
∎

Figure 12:
Illustration of the Markov property under
\mathbf{Q}
. Conditionally on
\mathcal{F}_{T}
, the excursions of
X
above the running infimum starting at
T
(in red above), together with the shifted
Z
processes, form a Poisson process of intensity given in Lemma
3.5
.

Note that for a given
t>0
, the definitions of
X_{u_{i}},X^{i},Z^{i}
and
\mathcal{N}^{[t]}
can be directly extended under
\mathbf{N}
, where we let
\mathcal{N}^{[t]}=0
on the event
\{\sigma\leqslant t\}
. By standard arguments, see for instance
[
125
, Theorem XII.4.1]
, the previous discussion applies under
\mathbf{N}
, for a positive stopping time, replacing the Markov property of
X
under
\mathbf{P}
by its analog under
\mathbf{N}
. The only difference is that the filtration
(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}
should now be completed by the negligible sets under
\mathbf{N}
, but we keep the same notation for simplicity.

Corollary 3.6
.

Let
T
be an
(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}
–stopping time such that
\mathbf{N}(T\in\{0,\infty\})=0
. Then, under

\mathbf{N}(\cdot\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{T})
, the point measure
\mathcal{N}^{[T]}
is a Poisson point measure with intensity

\mathbbm{1}_{[0,X_{T}]}(x)\mathrm{d}x\>\mathbf{N}(\mathrm{d}X\mathrm{d}Z).

Figure 13:
Illustration of the Markov property in terms of the looptree. We explore (in blue on the figure) in clockwise order a portion of the looptree –together with its labeling
Z
– until a stopping time
T
. The remaining pieces (in gray) attached to the “trunk” made of the (closure of the) loops linking
\Pi_{d}(0)
to
\Pi_{d}(T)
are distributed according to
\mathbf{N}

provided we shift their labels by the labels of their roots
. Indeed, the
Z
-labels on the blue part in the above figure are
\mathcal{F}_{T}
-measurable and have to be added to recover the actual
Z
-labels in the gray parts. In the proofs of Proposition
4.2
and Theorem
4.4
, we shall use
a priori
estimates given by Proposition
3.2
on the red part.

4
Local minima and records of
Z

In this section, we begin our study of the fine properties of the process
Z
, focusing in particular on its two-sided and one-sided minimal (local) records. Namely, we say that a time
t
is a
local minimal record
of
Z
and that
Z_{t}
is a
local minimal value
on the right, on the left, or on both sides respectively if there exists
\varepsilon>0
such that we have:

(\mbox{left})\quad Z_{t}=\min_{[(t-\varepsilon)\vee 0,t)}Z,\qquad\quad(\mbox{right})\quad Z_{t}=\min_{[t,t+\varepsilon]}Z,\qquad\quad(\mbox{two-sided})\quad Z_{t}=\min_{[(t-\varepsilon)\vee 0,t+\varepsilon]}Z.

To simplify notation, we write

\displaystyle\mathrm{LeftRec}\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\mathrm{RightRec},

(4.1)

for the set of all times
t\geqslant 0
at which
Z
attains a local left or right minimum, respectively. Thus,
\mathrm{LeftRec}\cup\mathrm{RightRec}
stands for the set of all one-sided records, while
\mathrm{LeftRec}\cap\mathrm{RightRec}
is the set of all two-sided local records. Their images by
Z
are the corresponding (local) minimal values.
Recall also from Section
2.2
that local minimal records of
Z
on one side (resp. both sides) correspond to points of degree at least
2
(resp.
3
) in the tree
\mathcal{T}_{\mathfrak{z}}
. In Section
4.1
, we prove that, almost surely under
\mathbf{P}
, the following properties hold:

(i)

one-sided records do not happen on the skeleton of
\mathcal{L}
(Proposition
4.2
). That is:

\Pi_{d}\big(\mathrm{LeftRec}\cup\mathrm{RightRec}\big)\cap\mathrm{Skel}=\varnothing.

(ii)

two-sided local minima of
Z

over

\mathbf{[0,1]}
are distinct (Proposition
4.3
). In other words:

\mbox{$Z$ is injective on $\mathrm{LeftRec}\cap\mathrm{RightRec}$}.

In particular, as a consequence of (ii), the process
Z
attains its global minimum at a unique time
t_{*}\in[0,1]
. As explained in Section
2.2
, the image of this time
t_{*}
under
\Pi_{\mathfrak{z}}
is the root of the tree
\mathcal{T}_{\mathfrak{z}}
. Section
4.2
uses this
to derive tail bounds for the volume of balls near the root in
\mathcal{T}_{\mathfrak{z}}
(Theorem
4.4
). Finally, in Section
4.3
, we study the behavior of
Z
over branches of
\mathcal{L}
and prove that, under
\mathbf{P}
, it holds that:

(iii)

one-sided local minima of
Z

over a branch
of
\mathcal{L}
are surrounded by “blocking” loops (Proposition
4.5
).

The above results on
Z
are key ingredients in identifying the quotient set
\mathcal{S}
(see Theorem
8.1
) and in obtaining technical estimates for the volume of balls of
\mathcal{S}
(Theorem
4.4
).

4.1
Local minima of
Z
over
[0,1]

Recall the definition of pinch points in
\mathcal{L}
, which, by Proposition
2.1
, correspond to the equivalence classes of
\sim_{d}
with two elements. The goal of this section is to establish the first two items above. Their proofs rely on the Markov property and the study of the minima of
Z
. We begin with a direct consequence of the Markov property of Corollary
3.6
and the scaling property:

Corollary 4.1
.

We have
\mathbf{N}(\inf Z\leqslant-1)\in(0,\infty)
. Moreover, for every
r>0
:

\mathbf{N}(\inf Z\leqslant-r)=\frac{\mathbf{N}(\inf Z\leqslant-1)}{r^{2}}\penalty\ .

In Proposition
5.4
we will complete this picture by giving the exact value of
\mathbf{N}(\inf Z\leqslant-1)
, which will require a detailed analysis.

Proof.

Recall the notation
\sigma
for the lifetime of
X
under
\mathbf{N}
. Under
\mathbf{N}(\cdot\>|\>\sigma>1)
and conditionally on
X_{1}
, Lemma
3.5
entails that the number of atoms of
\mathcal{N}^{[1]}:=\sum_{i\geqslant 1}\delta_{X_{u_{i}},X^{i},Z^{i}}
for which
\inf Z^{i}\leqslant-1

is a Poisson random variable with parameter
X_{1}\cdot\mathbf{N}(\inf Z\leqslant-1)
. This random variable is non-trivial and the continuity of
Z
entails that it is finite
\mathbf{N}(\cdot\>|\>\sigma>1)
– almost surely. Therefore, we have
\mathbf{N}(\inf Z\leqslant-1)\in(0,\infty)
. The second point follows by disintegration with respect to
\sigma
and the scaling property. Specifically, we have:

\displaystyle\mathbf{N}\big(\inf Z\leqslant-r\big)

\displaystyle\underset{\eqref{eq:excursionmeasuredecomp}}{=}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\Gamma(-\frac{1}{\alpha})|}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{{\rm d}v}{v^{\frac{1}{\alpha}+1}}\mathbf{N}^{(v)}\big(\inf Z\leqslant-r\big)

\displaystyle\underset{\mathrm{scaling}}{=}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\Gamma(-\frac{1}{\alpha})|}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{{\rm d}v}{v^{\frac{1}{\alpha}+1}}\mathbf{N}^{(v/r^{2\alpha})}(\inf Z\leqslant-1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\Gamma(-\frac{1}{\alpha})|}r^{-2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{{\rm d}u}{u^{\frac{1}{\alpha}+1}}\mathbf{N}^{(u)}\big(\inf Z\leqslant-1\big)=\ \frac{\mathbf{N}\big(\inf Z\leqslant-1\big)}{r^{2}}\penalty\ .

∎

First we show (i), i.e. that pinch points of
\mathcal{L}
cannot be minimal records (from one side) of the process
Z
, see
[
89
, Proposition 4.2]
for the similar statement in the case of Brownian motion on the Brownian tree.

Proposition 4.2
.

\mathbf{P}
-a.s., we have
\Pi_{d}(\mathrm{LeftRec}\cup\mathrm{RightRec})\cap\mathrm{Skel}=\varnothing
.

Proof.

We start by remarking that, by the re-rooting property (
3.12
) and the invariance by time-reversal (
3.13
), it is enough to show that

\mathbf{P}
-a.s., we have:

Z_{s}>\inf\limits_{[0,s]}Z,\quad\mbox{ for every $0<s<t<1$ such that $d(s,t)=0$.}

(4.2)

Recall that by Proposition
2.1
, the condition
d(s,t)=0
, also written
s\sim_{d}t
, is equivalent to

X_{t}=X_{s-}\mbox{ and \ \ }X_{r}>X_{s-}\mbox{ \ for every }r\in(s,t).

The strategy of the proof involves discretizing the times at which
Z
reaches a new minimal record. We then apply the Markov property at these times to establish that it is very unlikely to have times nearby corresponding to pinch points of
\mathcal{L}
supporting a large dangling looptree. First remark that by a scaling argument and (
3.15
), it suffices to prove the lemma for the excursion above the running infimum straddling time
1
under
\mathbf{Q}
. More precisely, under
\mathbf{Q}
, set:

\tau_{*}:=\sup\big\{t\in[0,1]:X_{t}=\inf\limits_{[0,1]}X\big\}

the starting time of this excursion and
\tau_{q}:=\inf\{t\geqslant 1:Z_{t}=\inf_{[\tau_{*},1]}Z-q\},
for
q>0
. It will also be useful to introduce, for every
\delta,q\in(0,\infty)
, the event
\mathcal{A}_{\delta,q}
defined by:

\bullet
There exist
1\leqslant s<s+\delta<t
, with
s<\tau_{q}
and
s\sim_{d}t
, such that
Z_{s}=\inf_{[\tau_{*},s]}Z
.

By the density of
\mathbb{Q}_{+}
in
\mathbb{R}_{+}
and since
\mathbf{Q}(\tau_{*}\in[1-\varepsilon,1])>0
for every
\varepsilon>0
, to obtain (
4.2
) it is enough to show that
\mathbf{Q}(\mathcal{A}_{\delta,q})=0
, for every
\delta,q\in(0,\infty)\cap\mathbb{Q}
. In this direction, we fix two such
\delta,q
and
n\geqslant 1
. We now discretize time and introduce the stopping times
(S^{n}_{i},T^{n}_{i})_{i\geqslant 1}
defined by induction as follows. First take

S^{n}_{1}:=\inf\big\{t\geqslant 1:\>\>Z_{t}=\inf\limits_{[\tau_{*},1]}Z\big\}\quad\text{and}\quad T^{n}_{1}:=\inf\big\{t\geqslant S^{n}_{1}:\>\>X_{t}=X_{S_{1}^{n}}-2^{-n}\big\},

and then, for every
i\geqslant 1
, take

S^{n}_{i+1}:=\inf\big\{t\geqslant T^{n}_{i}:\>\>Z_{t}=\inf\limits_{[\tau_{*},T^{n}_{i}]}Z\big\}\quad\text{and}\quad T^{n}_{i+1}:=\inf\big\{t\geqslant S^{n}_{i+1}:\>\>X_{t}=X_{S^{n}_{i+1}}-2^{-n}\big\}.

Remark that for every
i\geqslant 1
, the random variables
S^{n}_{i}
and
T^{n}_{i}
are
(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}
–stopping times which are finite
\mathbf{Q}
– a.s. For every
i\geqslant 1
, consider
(s^{n}_{i,k},t^{n}_{i,k})_{k\geqslant 1}
the connected components of the open set
\{s\in[S^{n}_{i},T^{n}_{i}]:\>X_{s}>\inf\limits_{[S^{n}_{i},s]}X\}
and introduce the random variables:

R^{n}_{i}:=\inf\limits_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\inf\limits_{r\in[s^{n}_{i,k},t^{n}_{i,k}]}\big(Z_{r}-Z_{s^{n}_{i,k}}\big)\quad;\quad\widetilde{R}^{n}_{i}:=\inf\limits_{r\in[S^{n}_{i},T^{n}_{i}]}\big(Z_{r}-Z_{S^{n}_{i}}\big)\quad\mbox{ and }\quad V^{n}_{i}:=\sup\limits_{k\geqslant 1}\big(t^{n}_{i,k}-s^{n}_{i,k}\big).

In words, the random variable
V^{n}_{i}
is the size of the largest excursion coding for a looptree grafted on the first
2^{-n}
unit of length on the trunk after time
S_{i}^{n}
, the random variable
\widetilde{R}^{n}_{i}
is the smallest displacement of
Z
on these excursions, whereas
R^{n}_{i}
is the smallest displacement of the process
Z
shifted by the label of the root in each of these excursions – see Figure
14
.

Figure 14:
Illustration of the Markov property applied at time
S_{i}^{n}
: among the gray looptrees grafted on the
2^{-n}
unit of length on the right of the trunk at time
S_{i}^{n}
(recall the caption of Figure
13
), the random variable
V_{i}^{n}
is the maximal size of such a gray looptree, whereas
R_{i}^{n}
is the minimal
Z
displacement within those looptrees. Note that on this picture, both happen to belong to the same looptrees, which need not be the case in general. The minimal displacement
\widetilde{R}_{i}^{n}
is obtained by further shifting the labels of the gray looptrees by the values of
Z
on the light red part.

Note now that if there exist
s\sim_{d}t
as in the definition of
\mathcal{A}_{\delta,q}
, it follows that when
S_{i}^{n}\leqslant s
and
V^{n}_{i}\leqslant\delta
, we must have
T_{i}^{n}\leqslant s
, since
t-s\geqslant\delta
. Furthermore, when
T_{i}^{n}\leqslant s
we also have
S^{n}_{i+1}\leqslant s
, because
Z_{s}=\inf_{[\tau_{*},s]}Z
. We infer that
\mathcal{A}_{\delta,q}
is contained in the event:

\big\{\exists i\geqslant 1:\penalty\ S_{i}^{n}<\tau_{q}\>\>\text{and}\>\>V_{i}^{n}\geqslant\delta\big\}.

(4.3)

Moreover, an application of the Markov property, combined with Lemma
3.5
and Corollary
4.1
, gives that the sequence
(R^{n}_{i},V^{n}_{i})_{i\geqslant 1}
is i.i.d., and we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{Q}(R^{n}_{i}<-r)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1-\exp\big(-\mathbf{N}(\inf Z<-1)2^{-n}r^{-2}\big)

(4.4)

\displaystyle\mathbf{Q}(V^{n}_{i}>r)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1-\exp\big(-\mathbf{N}(\sigma>1)2^{-n}r^{-\frac{1}{\alpha}}\big)\penalty\ ,

(4.5)

for every
r>0
.
Let us now explain the intuition of the rest of the proof. If we had the analog of (
4.4
) for
\widetilde{R}
instead of
R
, then each
\widetilde{R}_{i}^{n}
would be typically of order
at least

2^{-n/2}
so that we would need fewer than
\approx q\cdot 2^{n/2}
steps of the above process to reach
\tau_{q}
. In the meantime, at each step of the discrete exploration process, the probability that
V_{i}^{n}>\delta
, i.e. that
S_{i}^{n}
is an approximate pinch time “with a mass at least
\delta
above it”, is of order
2^{-n}
. Since
2^{-n}\cdot 2^{n/2}\ll 1
it is very unlikely that we encounter such times. The problem is that the variation
\widetilde{R}
is governed by
R
and the labels of the process
Z_{s_{i,k}^{n}}
; the labels on the light blue part of Figure
14
. To circumvent this difficulty, we will use Lemma
3.2
(or more prosaically (
3.9
)) as an a priori control on the variations of
Z
to transfer our estimates on
R
to
\widetilde{R}
.
To this end, fix

0<\eta<\frac{1}{2}<\eta^{\prime}<\beta<1

with
1-2\eta+\eta^{\prime}<\beta
and set

M_{n}:=\#\{i\leqslant 2^{\beta n}:\>V^{n}_{i}>\delta\}\>\>;\>\>N_{n}:=\#\{i\leqslant 2^{\beta n}:\>R^{n}_{i}<-2^{-\eta n}\}.

By (
4.4
), we have
\mathbf{Q}(V^{n}_{i}>\delta)\leqslant C\cdot 2^{-n}
and
\mathbf{Q}(R^{n}_{i}<-2^{-\eta n})\geqslant c\cdot 2^{-n(1-2\eta)}
, for some constants
c,C>0
. Since
\beta<1
and
1-2\eta+\eta^{\prime}<\beta
it follows by crude bounds and the Borel–Cantelli lemma that,
\mathbf{Q}
– a.s. , we have

M_{n}=0\quad\mbox{ and }\quad N_{n}\geqslant 2^{\eta^{\prime}n},

for
n\geqslant 1
large enough. Fix
\tilde{\eta}\in(\eta,\frac{1}{2})
and for every
n>1
, let
\mathcal{B}_{n}
be the event defined as follows:

\bullet
For every
m\geqslant n
,
M_{m}=0
and
N_{m}\geqslant 2^{\eta^{\prime}m}
;

\bullet
For every
s,t\in[0,\tau_{q}]
, we have
|Z_{s}-Z_{t}|\leqslant n\cdot d(s,t)^{\tilde{\eta}}.

Since
\tau_{q}<\infty
almost surely, the above considerations and excursion theory, combined with Equation (
3.9
) and the fact that the Brownian motion
\mathrm{b}_{0}
on the infinite loop is
\tilde{\eta}
-Hölder continuous, imply that

\mathbf{Q}(\mathcal{B}_{n})\to 1
as
n\to\infty
. Let us now conclude that
\mathbf{Q}(\mathcal{A}_{\delta,q})=0
. In this direction, remark that we can write

\displaystyle\mathbf{Q}(\mathcal{A}_{\delta,q})

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbf{Q}(\mathcal{A}_{\delta,q}\cap\mathcal{B}_{n})\underset{\eqref{**:recod}}{\leqslant}\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbf{Q}(\{T_{2^{n\beta}}^{n}<\tau_{q}\}\cap\mathcal{B}_{n}),

where in the last inequality we use that, under
\mathcal{B}_{n}
, we have
M_{m}=0
for every
m\geqslant n
. Consider
n_{0}
, the smallest integer such that for every
n\geqslant n_{0}
:

2^{(\eta^{\prime}-\eta)n}\big(-1+n2^{(\tilde{\eta}-\eta)n}\big)<-q

We are going to show that for every
n\geqslant n_{0}
, we have
\mathbf{Q}\big(\{T^{n}_{2^{n\beta}}<\tau_{q}\}\cap\mathcal{B}_{n}\big)=0
, which will complete the proof of the proposition. We argue by contradiction. First remark that, by definition, we have:

\sup\limits_{k\in\mathbb{N}}d(S_{i}^{n},s^{n}_{i,k})\leqslant 2^{-n},\quad\text{ for }n\geqslant 1.

So if
T^{n}_{2^{n\beta}}<\tau_{q}
under
\mathcal{B}_{n}
, we will have:

\displaystyle Z_{T^{n}_{2^{\beta n}}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum\limits_{1\leqslant i\leqslant 2^{\beta n}}\widetilde{R}^{n}_{i}\leqslant\sum\limits_{1\leqslant i\leqslant 2^{\beta n}}\min\left\{(R^{n}_{i}+n2^{-\tilde{\eta}n});0\right\}\leqslant N_{n}\cdot\big(-2^{-\eta n}+n2^{-\tilde{\eta}n}\big)

which, for
n\geqslant n_{0}
, is smaller that
2^{\eta^{\prime}n}\big(-2^{-\eta n}+n2^{-\tilde{\eta}n}\big)<-q
and we obtain a contradiction.
∎

We conclude this section deducing item (ii) from Proposition
4.2
.

Proposition 4.3
.

\mathbf{P}
-a.s. the (two-sided) local minima of
Z
are distinct.

Proof.

It follows straightforwardly from the invariance under re-rooting and continuity of
Z
that for every fixed time
t
, with
\mathbf{P}
-probability one,
t
is not a local minimal record, so we exclude times
0
and
1
in the following. Suppose that
0<s<t<1
are two times such that
Z
reaches the same local minima at
s
and at
t
. First, by Proposition
4.2
, we notice that
s
and
t
cannot be pinch point times.
Moreover, if
\Pi_{d}(s)
and
\Pi_{d}(t)
are leaves belonging to a common loop, then
Z
cannot realize the same minima at
s
and
t
since the labels along this loop evolve as a Brownian bridge – and the minima of a Brownian bridge are distinct. Consequently, by Property
(A_{4})
, the points
\Pi_{d}(s)
and
\Pi_{d}(t)
are separated in
\mathcal{L}
by a countable collection of loops. Let us now show that this is also impossible. To study this case we work conditionally on
X
, that is under
\mathbf{P}_{X}
. By definition, one can find rationals
p_{1}<s<p_{2}<q_{1}<t<q_{2}
, with
Z_{s}=\inf_{[p_{1},p_{2}]}Z
and
Z_{t}=\inf_{[q_{1},q_{2}]}Z
, such that
\Pi_{d}([p_{1},p_{2}])
is separated from
\Pi_{d}([q_{1},q_{2}])
in the looptree
\mathcal{L}
by a non-trivial loop associated with a time
r\in\mathrm{Branch}(s,t)\setminus\{s,t\}
. Recall the notation
\mathrm{f}_{r}
for the parametrization of the loop associated with
r
, and let
u\in\mathrm{f}_{r}([0,1])
(resp.
v\in\mathrm{f}_{r}([0,1])
) be the closest point of the loop from
\Pi_{d}\big([p_{1},p_{2}]\big)
(resp.
\Pi_{d}\big([q_{1},q_{2}]\big)
). In particular,
u
and
v
are pinch points of
\mathcal{L}
, and we write
s_{1}<s_{2}
and
t_{1}<t_{2}
for the elements of
\Pi_{d}^{-1}(u)
and
\Pi_{d}^{-1}(v)
respectively. We refer to Figure
15
for an illustration.

Figure 15:
The two times
s
and
t
are separated by the loop
\Pi_{d}\circ\mathrm{f}_{r}([0,1])
. In blue are represented the set
\Pi_{d}([p_{1},p_{2}])
and
\Pi_{d}([q_{1},q_{2}])
.

Next, using (
3.1
) we notice that we have
\inf_{[p_{1},p_{2}]}Z=\inf_{[q_{1},q_{2}]}Z
if and only if

Z_{s_{1}}-Z_{t_{1}}=\inf_{[q_{1},q_{2}]}\big(Z-Z_{s_{1}})-\inf_{[p_{1},p_{2}]}(Z-Z_{t_{1}}).

(4.6)

Furthermore, by Propositon
3.3
, the processes

\big(Z_{\ell}-Z_{s}\big)_{\ell\in[s_{1},s_{2}]}\penalty\ ;\penalty\ \big(Z_{\mathrm{f}_{r}(\ell)}\big)_{\ell\in[0,1]}
and
\big(Z_{\ell}-Z_{s}\big)_{\ell\in[t_{1},t_{2}]}

are independent under
\mathbf{P}_{X}
. We infer that the left and right sides of (
4.6
) are independent. Finally since
Z_{s_{1}}-Z_{t_{1}}
is a non-trivial Gaussian random variable, we deduce that,
\mathbf{P}_{X}
-a.s., identity (
4.6
) cannot hold. The desired result follows
since
p_{1}<p_{2}<q_{1}<q_{2}
are arbitrary rational numbers.
∎

4.2
The mass of balls centered at the root of
\mathcal{T}_{\mathfrak{z}}

The continuity of
Z
, together with Proposition
4.3
, implies the existence,
\mathbf{P}
-almost surely, of a unique
t_{*}\in[0,1]
realizing the minimum of
Z
, i.e.
Z_{t_{*}}=\inf_{[0,1]}Z
. We shall consider the tree
\mathcal{T}_{\mathfrak{z}}
coded by
Z
as in (
1.10
). We recall that
\Pi_{\mathfrak{z}}(t_{*})
is the root of
\mathcal{T}_{\mathfrak{z}}
and that
\mathrm{Vol}_{\mathfrak{z}}
is the pushforward of the Lebesgue measure by
\Pi_{\mathfrak{z}}
. The goal of this section is to control the mass – for
\mathrm{Vol}_{\mathfrak{z}}
– of closed balls in
\mathcal{T}_{\mathfrak{z}}
centered at
\Pi_{\mathfrak{z}}(t_{*})
. Namely, for every
r\geqslant 0
, we set

B_{\mathfrak{z}}(\Pi_{\mathfrak{z}}(t_{*}),r):=\{u\in\mathcal{T}_{\mathfrak{z}}:\penalty\ \mathfrak{z}(u,\Pi_{\mathfrak{z}}(t_{*}))\leqslant r\}.

The goal of this section is to prove that:

Theorem 4.4
.

Fix
\delta>0
. There exist
c,C\in(0,\infty)
such that

\mathbf{P}\Big(\mathrm{Vol}_{\mathfrak{z}}\big(B_{\mathfrak{z}}(\Pi_{\mathfrak{z}}(t_{*}),r)\big)\geqslant r^{2\alpha-\delta}\Big)\leqslant C\cdot\exp(-r^{-c}),

(4.7)

for every
r>0
.

In Part
II
of this paper, this estimate will be used to bound the mass of balls centered at the root in the subsequential scaling limits
(\mathcal{S},D)
of
{\bf q}
-Boltzmann maps that were discussed in the Introduction. In turn, this will allow us to deduce an
a priori
bound of the form
D^{*}\leqslant D^{1-\delta}
(locally), that is required in the final steps of the proof of our main result stating that
D=D^{*}
, see Proposition
8.8
in Section
8.5
.

Proof.

In order to prove Theorem
4.4
, we are going to write
\mathrm{Vol}_{\mathfrak{z}}\big(B_{\mathfrak{z}}(\Pi_{\mathfrak{z}}(t_{*}),r)\big)
in terms of the time that
Z
spends near its infimum. Since
t_{*}
is the argmin of
Z
, and
\mathrm{Vol}_{\mathfrak{z}}
is the pushforward of the Lebesgue measure on
[0,1]
under the projection
\Pi_{\mathfrak{z}}
, we have

\mathrm{Vol}_{\mathfrak{z}}\big(B_{\mathfrak{z}}(\Pi_{\mathfrak{z}}(t_{*}),r)\big)\underset{\eqref{eq:defpseudodistancearbre}}{=}\int_{0}^{1}{\rm d}s\penalty\ \mathbbm{1}_{Z_{s}\leqslant Z_{t_{*}}+r}\penalty\ .

Consequently, Theorem
4.4
can be translated in terms of
Z
in the following form:
Fix
\delta>0
, then there exist
c,C\in(0,\infty)
such that

\mathbf{P}\Big(\int_{0}^{1}{\rm d}s\penalty\ \mathbbm{1}_{Z_{s}\leqslant Z_{t_{*}}+r}\geqslant r^{2\alpha-\delta}\Big)\leqslant C\cdot\exp(-r^{-c}),

for every
r>0
.
We stress that the constants
C,c
might depend on
\delta
. Let us start by presenting the idea of the proof. We will first show using a re-rooting argument due to Chaumont and Uribe Bravo that

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}\Big(\int_{0}^{1}{\rm d}s\penalty\ \mathbbm{1}_{Z_{s}\leqslant Z_{t_{*}}+r}\geqslant r^{2\alpha-\delta}\Big)\quad\leqslant\quad\mathbf{P}\Big(\int_{0}^{1}{\rm d}s\mathbbm{1}_{Z_{s}\leqslant r}\geqslant r^{2\alpha-\delta}\>\>\Big|\inf Z\geqslant-r\Big),

(4.8)

for every
r,\delta>0
.
Then we will argue that the process
Z
conditioned on the event
\{\inf Z\geqslant-r\}
cannot spend too much time near
0
, since informally each “time” the process
Z
returns close to
0
, it has a positive chance to drop below
-r
in the next
r^{{2\alpha}}
units of time.

To prove (
4.8
), let
r,\delta>0
and introduce
U
a uniform random variable on
[0,1]
– independent of
(X,Z)
. Recall the notation
s\oplus t=s+t
if
s+t\leqslant 1
and
s\oplus t=s+t-1
otherwise. We also introduce
(A^{(r)}_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
the occupation time process associated with
Z
, i.e.

A_{t}^{(r)}:=\int_{0}^{t}{\rm d}s\penalty\ \mathbbm{1}_{Z_{s}\leqslant\inf Z+r}\penalty\ ,\quad t\in[0,1],

and we consider the quantity
t^{(r)}:=\inf\big\{t\in[0,1]:\>A_{t}^{(r)}=UA_{1}^{(r)}\big\}
.
Now remark that by construction we have:

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}\Big(\int_{0}^{1}{\rm d}s\penalty\ \mathbbm{1}_{Z_{s}\leqslant Z_{t_{*}}+r}\geqslant r^{2\alpha-\delta}\Big)

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathbf{P}\Big(\int_{0}^{1}{\rm d}s\penalty\ \mathbbm{1}_{Z_{s}\leqslant Z_{t^{(r)}}+r}\geqslant r^{2\alpha-\delta}\Big)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{P}\Big(\int_{0}^{1}{\rm d}s\penalty\ \mathbbm{1}_{Z_{t^{(r)}\oplus s}-Z_{t^{(r)}}\leqslant r}\geqslant r^{2\alpha-\delta}\Big).

Since by (
3.12
) the process
Z
has cyclically exchangeable increments, we can apply
[
43
, Theorem 2.2]
to deduce that the process
\big(Z_{t^{(r)}\oplus s}-Z_{t^{(r)}}\big)_{s\in[0,1]}
, under
\mathbf{P}
, is distributed as the process
Z
under the probability measure
\mathbf{P}(\cdot\>|\>\inf Z>-r)
. In particular, we get (
4.8
).

Hence, to obtain the desired result, it suffices to show that, for every
\delta\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{*}
, there exist
c_{1},C_{1}\in(0,\infty)
such that for every
r>0
:

\mathbf{P}\Big(\int_{0}^{1}{\rm d}s\mathbbm{1}_{Z_{s}\leqslant r}\geqslant r^{2\alpha-\delta}\>\>\Big|\>\>\inf Z\geqslant-r\Big)\leqslant C_{1}\cdot\exp(-r^{-c_{1}}).

(4.9)

We start by showing that for every
\delta>0
, there exists
C_{1}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{*}
such that:

\mathbf{P}(\inf Z\geqslant-r)\geqslant C_{1}^{\prime}\cdot\big(1\wedge r^{2\alpha+\delta}\big),

for every
r>0
.
We mention that the previous inequality is not sharp, as we expect
\mathbf{P}(\inf Z\geqslant-r)
to be of order
r^{-2\alpha}
. Remark that by (
3.9
), the random variable

\displaystyle W:=\sup\limits_{s\neq t}|Z_{t}-Z_{s}|\cdot|t-s|^{-\frac{1}{2\alpha+\delta}}

(4.10)

is finite
\mathbf{P}
-a.s so that
|Z_{t}-Z_{s}|\leqslant r
if
|t-s|\leqslant(r/W)^{2\alpha+\delta}
. We thus have:

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}\big(\inf Z\geqslant-r\big)

\displaystyle\underset{\mathrm{re-rooting\ }\eqref{re-rooting}}{=}

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}{\rm d}s\penalty\ \mathbf{E}\big[\mathbbm{1}_{\inf Z_{s\oplus\cdot}-Z_{s}\geqslant-r}\big]

\displaystyle\underset{\mathrm{Fubini}}{=}

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\big[\int_{0}^{1}{\rm d}s\mathbbm{1}_{\inf Z_{s\oplus\cdot}-Z_{s}\geqslant-r}\big]

\displaystyle\underset{}{=}

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\big[\int_{0}^{1}{\rm d}s\mathbbm{1}_{Z_{s}\leqslant\inf Z+r}\big]\penalty\ ,

\displaystyle\underset{\eqref{eq:variation2}}{\geqslant}

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}[(r/W)^{2\alpha+\delta}],

which gives the desired inequality. Hence, to derive (
4.9
) it suffices to show that for every
\delta\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{*}
there exist
c_{2},C_{2}\in(0,\infty)
such that:

\mathbf{P}\Big(\{\inf Z>-r\}\cap\big\{\int_{0}^{1}{\rm d}s\penalty\ \mathbbm{1}_{Z_{s}\leqslant r}\geqslant r^{2\alpha-\delta}\big\}\Big)\leqslant C_{2}\cdot\exp(-r^{-c_{2}}),\quad r>0.

The idea now is to argue similarly as in the proof of Proposition
4.2
. We are going to translate the problem under the probability measure
\mathbf{Q}
and discretize time in order to apply the Markov property. First, under
\mathbf{Q}
, set:

\tau_{*}:=\sup\big\{t\in[0,1]:\penalty\ X_{t}=\inf\limits_{[0,1]}X\big\}\>\>\text{and}\>\>\tau^{*}:=\inf\big\{t\geqslant 1:\penalty\ X_{t}=\inf\limits_{[0,1]}X\big\}.

Under
\mathbf{Q}
, the excursion
(X_{t}-\inf_{[0,1]}X)_{t\in[\tau_{*},\tau^{*}]}
is the excursion above the running infimum straddling time
1
.
To be able to relate it to the label process under
\mathbf{P}
, we shall, as in (
3.15
), consider the process
\widetilde{Z}_{t}:=Z_{t}-\mathrm{b}_{0}(-I_{t})
. We then introduce
\widetilde{\tau}_{r}:=\inf\{t\geqslant 1:\penalty\ \widetilde{Z}_{t}=-r\}
.
Since
\mathbf{Q}(1-r<\tau_{*}<2-r<\tau^{*}<2)
decreases polynomially to
0
as
r\to 0
, an application of the scaling property shows that to obtain (
4.9
) it is enough to prove that for every
\delta>0
we can find some constants
c_{3},C_{3}>0
such that:

\mathbf{Q}\Big(\int_{1}^{2\wedge\widetilde{\tau}_{r}}{\rm d}s\penalty\ \mathbbm{1}_{\widetilde{Z}_{s}\leqslant r}\geqslant r^{2\alpha-\delta}\Big)\leqslant C_{3}\cdot\exp(-r^{-c_{3}}),\quad r>0.

(4.11)

From now on we fix
\delta>0
and we consider
r\in(0,1)
. Let us now discretize time to give an upper bound of the left-hand side of the display above. We introduce the stopping times
(S_{i},T_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
defined by induction as follows. First take
S_{1}:=1\>\>\text{;}\>\>T_{1}:=S_{1}+r^{2\alpha}
,
and for every integer
i\geqslant 0
set

S_{i+1}:=\inf\big\{s\geqslant T_{i}\penalty\ :\penalty\ \widetilde{Z}_{s}\in[-r,r]\big\}\quad\text{;}\quad T_{i+1}:=S_{i+1}+r^{2\alpha}.

We have the following trivial inequality:

\displaystyle\mathbf{Q}\Big(\int_{1}^{2\wedge\widetilde{\tau}_{r}}{\rm d}s\penalty\ \mathbbm{1}_{\widetilde{Z}_{s}\leqslant r}\geqslant r^{2\alpha-\delta}\Big)\leqslant\mathbf{Q}\Big(S_{\lfloor r^{-\delta}\rfloor}\leqslant 2\wedge\widetilde{\tau}_{r}\Big).

(4.12)

Here is the rough idea to obtain an upper bound of the right-side hand of (
4.12
). First, by scaling, the variations of the process
\widetilde{Z}
over the time interval
[S_{i},T_{i}]
of length
r^{2\alpha}
should be of order
r
. Then, since
\widetilde{Z}_{S_{i}}\leqslant r
, there should be a probability bounded away from
0
that
\widetilde{Z}
touches
-r
within
[S_{i},T_{i}]
. As a consequence, the probability of
\{S_{n}\leqslant\widetilde{\tau}_{r}\}
should decrease exponentially fast in
n
. As in the proof of Proposition
4.2
, the problem may come from the fact that in the Markov property, the
\widetilde{Z}
-labels after time
S_{i}
are shifted by the labels on the loops connecting the root
\Pi_{d}(\tau_{*})
to
\Pi_{d}(S_{i})
– see Figure
16
for an illustration. We shall again bound them using
a priori
estimates resulting from Proposition
3.2

\rm(ii)
. Of course, this discussion is informal, and we now make this picture precise.

Figure 16:
Illustration of the proof. At time
S_{i}
the
\widetilde{Z}
-value is less than
r
. By scaling, in the forthcoming
r^{{2\alpha}}
units of time, the variation of the infimum process
I
of
X
is of order
r^{2}
, while one expects the variation of process
\widetilde{Z}
to be of order
r
. One way to ensure this is to ask for an excursion (in green above) to have a
\widetilde{Z}
-variation less than
-2r
, and to happen “early” on the trunk, i.e. within
r^{2+\eta}
units of length after
S_{i}
(in bold red above). Here we write
[\cdot]
to denote a variation.

Similarly to the proof of Proposition
4.2
, for every
i\geqslant 1
, let
(s_{i,k},t_{i,k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
be the connected components of the open set

\{s\geqslant S_{i}:\>X_{s}>\inf\limits_{[S_{i},s]}X\}

and consider the associated excursions
X^{i,k}_{s}:=X_{(s_{i,k}+s)\wedge t_{i,k}}-X_{s_{i,k}}
and
\widetilde{Z}^{i,k}_{s}:=\widetilde{Z}_{(s_{i,k}+s)\wedge t_{i,k}}-\widetilde{Z}_{s_{i,k}}
, for
s\geqslant 0
. We also write
\sigma_{i,k}:=t_{i,k}-s_{i,k}
and
\ell_{i,k}:=X_{S_{i}}-\inf_{[S_{i},s_{i,k}]}X
. Next, we fix
\eta\in(0,1/2)
and, for every
i\in\mathbb{N}
, we introduce the event:

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{i}:=\Big\{\exists k\in\mathbb{N}:\penalty\ \ell_{i,k}\leqslant r^{2+\eta}\mbox{ and }\min\widetilde{Z}^{i,k}\leqslant-4r\mbox{ and }\sum_{\ell_{i,j}<\ell_{i,k}}\sigma_{i,j}<r^{2\alpha}\Big\}.

(4.13)

In words, under
\mathcal{B}_{i}
, at step
i
a looptree with a (shifted) minimal label below
-4r
is branched within less than
r^{2+\eta}
unit of length along the “trunk”, see Figure
16
. By construction the
\mathcal{B}_{i}
’s are independent, and we are going to show using excursion theory that we have
\mathbf{Q}(\mathcal{B}_{i})\geqslant a\cdot r^{\eta}
, for some constant
a>0
(not depending on
r\in(0,1)
). The theorem will then follow easily from this estimate. Fix
i\in\mathbb{N}
, and remark that by excursion theory the measure

\sum\limits_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\delta_{\ell_{i,k},\sigma_{i,k},X^{i,k},\widetilde{Z}^{i,k}}

is a Poisson point measure with intensity
\mathbbm{1}_{[0,\infty)}(\ell)\mathrm{d}\ell\big(\mathrm{d}v/(|\Gamma(-\frac{1}{\alpha})|v^{\frac{1}{\alpha}+1})\big)\mathbf{N}^{(v)}({\rm d}X,{\rm d}Z)
. In particular, the process
R^{i}_{t}:=\sum_{\ell_{i,k}\leqslant t}\sigma_{i,k},

t\geqslant 0,

is an
1/\alpha
-stable subordinator. By definition we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{Q}(\mathcal{B}_{i})\geqslant\mathbf{Q}\Big(\exists k\in\mathbb{N}:\penalty\ \ell_{i,k}\leqslant r^{2+\eta}\penalty\ ,\penalty\ R^{i}_{\ell_{i,k}-}\in[0,r^{2\alpha}/{2})\penalty\ ,\penalty\ R^{i}_{\ell_{i,k}}\in[2r^{2\alpha}/3,r^{2\alpha})\penalty\ ,\penalty\ \min\widetilde{Z}^{i,k}\leqslant-4r\Big).

Since
R^{i}
is non-decreasing, we infer from the discussion above that the right-hand side of the previous display equals

\displaystyle\mathbf{Q}\Big(\sum\limits_{k\in\mathbb{N}}

\displaystyle\mathbbm{1}_{\ell_{i,k}\leqslant r^{2+\eta},R^{i}_{\ell_{i,k}-}\in[0,r^{2\alpha}/2),R^{i}_{\ell_{i,k}}\in[2r^{2\alpha}/3,r^{2\alpha})}\cdot\mathbf{N}^{(\Delta R^{i}_{\ell_{i,k}})}(\min Z<-4r)\Big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|\Gamma(-\frac{1}{\alpha})|}\int_{0}^{r^{2+\eta}}{\rm d}\ell\penalty\ \mathbf{Q}\Big(\mathbbm{1}_{R^{i}_{\ell-}\in[0,r^{2\alpha}/2)}\int_{2r^{2\alpha}/3-R_{\ell-}^{i}}^{r^{2\alpha}-R_{\ell-}^{i}}{\rm d}v\penalty\ v^{-\frac{1}{\alpha}-1}\cdot\mathbf{N}^{(v)}(\min Z<-4r)\Big),

where
\Delta R^{i}_{\ell_{i,k}}
stands for the jump of
R^{i}
at
\ell_{i,k}
, and where the second line follows by an application of the compensation formula. Using again scaling invariance, we derive that
\mathbf{N}^{(v)}(\min Z<-4r)\geqslant\mathbf{P}(\min Z<-24)
, for every
v\geqslant r^{2\alpha}/6
. Therefore, a straightforward computation, using again that
R^{i}
is non-decreasing, entails that the previous display is bounded below by:

\frac{\mathbf{P}(\min Z<-24)}{2|\Gamma(-\frac{1}{\alpha})|r^{2}}\int_{0}^{r^{2+\eta}}{\rm d}\ell\penalty\ \mathbf{Q}\Big(\mathbbm{1}_{R^{i}_{\ell-}\in[0,r^{2\alpha}/2)}\Big)\geqslant\frac{\mathbf{P}(\min Z<-24)\mathbf{Q}(R^{i}_{r^{2+\eta}}<r^{2\alpha})}{2|\Gamma(-\frac{1}{\alpha})|}\cdot r^{\eta}.

Since
R^{i}
is an
1/\alpha
-stable subordinator, we have
\mathbf{Q}(R^{i}_{r^{2+\eta}}<r^{2\alpha})=\mathbf{Q}(R^{i}_{1}<r^{-\alpha\eta})>\mathbf{Q}(R^{i}_{1}<1)
which is a positive quantity. Furthermore, it follows from the Gaussian character of
Z
that
\mathbf{P}(\min Z<-24)>0
. We derive that there exists a constant
a>0
(not depending on
r\in(0,1)
) such that
\mathbf{Q}(\mathcal{B}_{i})\geqslant a\cdot r^{\eta}
. Finally, to conclude the proof of the theorem, we consider the event

\mathcal{A}:=\left\{|\widetilde{Z}_{t}-\widetilde{Z}_{s}|\leqslant d(s,t)^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\eta}{10}}:\penalty\ \penalty\ \mbox{ for every }s,t\in[0,2]\mbox{ with }|s-t|\leqslant r\right\}.

Proposition
3.2

\rm(ii)
and excursion theory imply that
\mathbf{Q}(\mathcal{A}^{\text{c}})\leqslant C_{4}\cdot\exp(-r^{-c_{4}})
for some
c_{4},C_{4}>0
(which only depend on
\eta
). Notice now that by construction, for every
i,k\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\ell_{i,k}\leqslant r^{2+\eta}
, we must have
d(S_{i},s_{i,k})\leqslant r^{2+\eta}
. Thus, on the event
\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{B}_{i}
, the minimal
\widetilde{Z}
label over the time interval
[S_{i},T_{i}]
is smaller than
\widetilde{Z}_{S_{i}}-4r+\left(r^{2+\eta}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\eta}{10}}\leqslant-2r
, which implies that
\widetilde{\tau}_{r}\leqslant T_{i}
. We infer that:

\displaystyle\mathbf{Q}\big(S_{\lfloor r^{-\delta}\rfloor}\leqslant 2\wedge\widetilde{\tau}_{-r}\big)

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathbf{Q}\big(\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{c}})+\mathbf{Q}\big(\cap_{i\leqslant\lfloor r^{-\delta}\rfloor}\mathcal{B}_{i}^{\mathrm{c}}\big)\leqslant\exp(-r^{-c_{4}})+(1-a\cdot r^{\eta})^{\lfloor r^{-\delta}\rfloor}

where to obtain the second inequality we used the independence of the
\mathcal{B}_{i}
’s and (
4.13
). Putting everything together, assuming
\eta\in(0,\delta)
and using (
4.12
), we deduce that we can find two constants
c,C>0
(only depending on
\eta
), such that we have

\mathbf{Q}\Big(\int_{0}^{2\wedge\widetilde{\tau}_{r}}{\rm d}s\penalty\ \mathbbm{1}_{\widetilde{Z}_{s}\leqslant r}\geqslant r^{(2\alpha-\delta)}\Big)\leqslant C_{4}\cdot\exp(-r^{-c_{4}})+(1-a\cdot r^{\eta})^{\lfloor r^{-\delta}\rfloor}\leqslant C\cdot\exp(-r^{-c}).

Since the previous display holds for every
r\in(0,1)
, this concludes the proof of the theorem.
∎

4.3
Local minima of
Z
along branches of
\mathcal{L}

In this section, we refine Proposition
4.2
by studying the local minima of
Z
along “branches” of the looptree
\mathcal{L}
. This analysis culminates in Proposition
4.5
below (see Figure
17
for an illustration). In this direction, recall the definition of
\mathrm{Branch}(s,t)
, for
s,t\in[0,1]
, given in (
2.7
) as well as its simpler form (
2.8
) when either
s
or
t
equals
0
.

Proposition 4.5

(Local minimum of labels along branches)
.

The following property holds
\mathbf{P}
-almost surely:
For any
0<t_{1}<t_{2}<1
with
d(t_{1},t_{2})=0
such that

Z_{t_{1}}=Z_{t_{2}}<Z_{r}\>\>\text{for every}\>\>r\in\mathrm{Branch}(0,t_{1})\setminus\{t_{1}\},

(4.14)

and every
\varepsilon>0
, there exist
t^{\prime}_{1}\in\big((t_{1}-\varepsilon)\vee 0,t_{1}\big)
and
t^{\prime}_{2}\in\big(t_{2},(t_{2}+\varepsilon)\wedge 1\big)
pinch point times such that:

Z_{t^{\prime}_{1}}<Z_{t_{1}}=Z_{t_{2}}<Z_{r}\>\>\text{for every}\>\>r\in\mathrm{Branch}(t_{1},t^{\prime}_{1})\setminus\{t_{1},t^{\prime}_{1}\}

and

Z_{t^{\prime}_{2}}<Z_{t_{1}}=Z_{t_{2}}<Z_{r}\>\>\text{for every}\>\>r\in\mathrm{Branch}(t_{2},t^{\prime}_{2})\setminus\{t_{2},t^{\prime}_{2}\}.

Figure 17:
Illustration of the result of Proposition
4.5
: Consider a branch in
\mathcal{L}
and stop it at a pinch point
\Pi_{d}(t_{1})
when reaching a new minimum for the label along that branch. Then we can find two blue loops on both sides “blocking” the level
Z_{t_{1}}
.

Let us describe first informally the main observations of this section before setting some notation which is necessary for the proofs. For this informal discussion, we argue under
\mathbf{P}
. If
s,t\in[0,1]
, by analogy with the stable tree, we refer to
\Pi_{d}(\mathrm{Branch}(s,t))
as the branch between
x=\Pi_{d}(s)
and
y=\Pi_{d}(t)
in
\mathcal{L}
. Let us stress that this notion is consistent with the definition of
\mathcal{L}
since as explained in Section
2.1
, the set
\Pi_{d}(\mathrm{Branch}(s,t))
corresponds to the points in
\mathcal{L}
separating
x
and
y
, and thus does not depend on the choice of representatives
s
and
t
. It was already observed in
[
97
, Lemma 16 (ii)]
that the process
Z
over a typical branch of
\mathcal{L}
evolves as a symmetric Lévy process with index
2\alpha-2
, see (
4.16
) below. In particular, the range
5
5

5

In this work we use the standard convention that the range of a function is the closure of its image.
of
Z
along a typical branch has Hausdorff dimension
1\wedge(2\alpha-2)
. Notice that when
\alpha\in(1,3/2)
, this dimension is strictly less than
1
and so the intersection of
\lceil\frac{1}{1-(2\alpha-2)}\rceil+1
“independent” such closed subsets is reduced to
\varnothing
. This heuristic suggests that:

the process
Z
cannot take more than
\lceil\frac{1}{1-(2\alpha-2)}\rceil+1
times the same value on the skeleton of
\mathcal{L}
.

Although we do not prove it, this fact together with Proposition
4.2
actually yield the above Proposition
4.5
. In general, when
\alpha\in(1,2)
we rather look more precisely at the set of record values along branches. Indeed, when going along a branch in a given direction, since the process of labels evolves as a symmetric
2\alpha-2
stable process, its running infimum evolves as an
\alpha-1
stable subordinator, so that its range is of Hausdorff dimension
\alpha-1
. The same argument as above shows that the intersection of more than
\lceil\frac{1}{2-\alpha}\rceil+1
such sets is reduced to
\varnothing
. This suggests as above that:

the running infimum process of
Z
along branches cannot take more than
\lceil\frac{1}{2-\alpha}\rceil+1
times

the same value on the skeleton of
\mathcal{L}
.

In order to make this statement precise, which is the key ingredient in the proof of Proposition
4.5
, let us introduce some notation and intermediate results. In this direction, recall the construction of the height process
H
given in Section
2.2
and in particular the notation
\xi_{t}(r)
which gives a pre-image of the ancestor of
\Pi_{\mathfrak{h}}(t)
at height
r\leqslant H_{t}
in the stable tree
\mathcal{T}_{\mathfrak{h}}
. Now remark that thanks to the continuity of
Z
and Lemma
2.5
, we get:

Corollary 4.6
.

The following holds
\mathbf{P}
-a.s., for every
t\in[0,1]
and
0\leqslant r_{1}\leqslant r_{2}\leqslant H_{t}
, we have:

\big\{Z_{s}:\penalty\ \xi_{t}(r_{1})\preccurlyeq s\preccurlyeq\xi_{t}(r_{2})\big\}=\big\{Z_{\xi_{t}(r)}:\penalty\ r\in[r_{1},r_{2}]\big\}\cup\big\{Z_{\xi_{t}(r-)}:\penalty\ r\in[r_{1},r_{2}]\big\}.

(4.15)

In particular, even though
\xi_{t}
does not parametrize
\mathrm{Branch}(0,t)
, the (closed) range of the process
r\mapsto Z_{\xi_{t}(r)}
covers all the labels along
\mathrm{Branch}(0,t)
. To simplify some arguments, recall that
H
and
\xi
can also be defined under the measure
\mathbf{N}
by scaling. Then
[
97
, Lemma 16 (ii)]
states that for every bounded continuous function,
F:\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{R})\mapsto\mathbb{R}
, we have

\mathbf{N}\Big(\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\rm d}tF\big((Z_{\xi_{t}(r)})_{r\geqslant 0}\big)\Big)=\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}h\>\mathbf{E}\big[F((Y_{r\wedge h})_{r\geqslant 0})\big],

(4.16)

where
Y
is a symmetric stable processes with Lévy-Khintchine function
6
6

6

Lemma 16 (ii) in
[
97
]
does not provide the explicit normalization constant
\alpha 2^{1-\alpha}\frac{\Gamma(\alpha)^{2}}{\Gamma(2\alpha)}
. The exact value is not essential for our study. However, we include it since it can be obtained by a straightforward integral computation.

\lambda\mapsto\alpha 2^{1-\alpha}\frac{\Gamma(\alpha)^{2}}{\Gamma(2\alpha)}|\lambda|^{2(\alpha-1)}
. Equation (
4.16
) has the following direct consequence for labels along branches:

Lemma 4.7
.

Under
\mathbf{P}
, let
U_{1}\in[0,1]
be uniform and independent of
(X,Z)
. Almost surely, for any
[a,b]\subset[0,H_{U_{1}}]
, the infimum of
s\mapsto Z_{\xi_{U_{1}}(s)}
over
[a,b]
is attained. Furthermore, its two-sided minima are distinct, so that the process attains its overall minimum only once.

Proof.

By a standard result for symmetric stable Lévy processes, it is well known that, for any interval
[a,b]\subset\mathbb{R}_{+}
, the process
Y|_{[a,b]}
attains its infimum. Moreover, all the local minima of
Y
are distinct. The statement of the lemma is then a direct consequence of (
4.16
) and the scaling property.
∎

Let us also mention that the above statement also holds true simultaneously for all pinch point times
t
instead of the uniform time
U_{1}
, simply because for every pinch points time
t
, there is a positive probability that
t\prec U_{1}
, and this implies that
(\xi_{t}(r))_{r\geqslant 0}=(\xi_{U_{1}}(r\wedge H_{t}))_{r\geqslant 0}
.

We can now introduce, under
\mathbf{P}
, the necessary notation to study the intersections of the range of the running infimum of
Z
along branches. We refer to Figure
18
for an illustration. Let
m\geqslant 1
and
0<t_{1}<t_{2}<\cdots<t_{m}<1
. For
1\leqslant i\leqslant m
, we set

r_{i}:=\sup\limits_{j\neq i}(t_{i}\curlywedge t_{j}),

the largest ancestor of
t_{i}
with another
t_{j}
. Remark that for every
1\leqslant i\leqslant m
, the set
\mathrm{Branch}\big(r_{i},t_{i}\big)\setminus\{r_{i}\}
is the set of
t\in\mathrm{Branch}(0,t_{i})
such that, for every
j\neq i
,
t
is not an ancestor of
t_{j}
. In particular, these sets (and their projections by
\Pi_{d}
) are disjoint. For every
1\leqslant i\leqslant m
, introduce the rcll processes
R_{t_{1},...,t_{m}}^{(i)}
defined by:

R_{t_{1},...,t_{m}}^{(i)}(r):=Z_{\xi_{t_{i}}(H_{r_{i}}+r)}\penalty\ \penalty\ ;\quad\text{for }r\geqslant 0.

In words, by (
4.15
), the range of the process
R_{t_{1},...,t_{m}}^{(i)}
is encoding the labels
Z_{r}
for
r\in\mathrm{Branch}\big(r_{i},t_{i}\big)\setminus\{r_{i}\}
, when going from
r_{i}
to
t_{i}
. We denote the minimal record values of
R_{t_{1},...,t_{m}}^{(i)}
by

\mathcal{R}_{t_{1},...,t_{m}}^{(i)}:=\Big\{\inf\limits_{[0,r]}R_{t_{1},...,t_{m}}^{(i)}(r):\penalty\ r\geqslant 0\Big\},

where we recall from Lemma
4.7
that these infima are attained, and consequently
\mathcal{R}_{t_{1},...,t_{m}}^{(i)}
is a closed set.

Figure 18:
Illustration of
\big(R_{t_{1},...,t_{m}}^{(i)}\big)_{1\leqslant i\leqslant m}
in the case
m=4
.

We are going to deduce Proposition
4.5
from the following result, which formalizes the above discussion:

Proposition 4.8
.

Fix
m\geqslant\lceil\frac{1}{2-\alpha}\rceil+1
. Under
\mathbf{P}
, let
U_{1},...,U_{m}
be independent uniform random variables on
[0,1]
, also independent of
(X,Z)
. Then we have:

\bigcap\limits_{i=1}^{m}\mathcal{R}_{U_{1},...,U_{m}}^{(i)}=\varnothing,\quad\mathbf{P}-\mbox{a.s.}.

Notice that the proposition does not hold simultaneously for all times
t_{1},...,t_{m}
because
Z
does attain some common values at infinitely many (leaf) times. However, by the same argument as the one appearing after Lemma
4.7
, it holds that
\mathbf{P}
almost surely, for all pinch points times
t_{1},...,t_{m}\in\mathcal{L}
such that
t_{i}\npreceq t_{j}
for every
i\neq j
, we have
\bigcap\limits_{1\leqslant i\leqslant m}\mathcal{R}_{t_{1},...,t_{m}}^{(i)}=\varnothing
. Indeed, as already mentioned above, for every pinch point times
t_{1},...,t_{m}
, there is a positive probability that
t_{i}\prec U_{i}
, for every
1\leqslant i\leqslant m
.

Proof.

We work again under
\mathbf{N}
, since by a scaling argument it is equivalent to prove the proposition under
\mathbf{P}
or
\mathbf{N}
, and we fix
m_{0}:=\lceil\frac{1}{2-\alpha}\rceil+1
. For technical reasons it will be useful to consider a homogeneous Poisson point process on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
with intensity
1
defined under the measure
\mathbf{N}
, independently of
X
and
Z
. We write
0<\tau_{1}<\tau_{2}<\cdots
for the jumping times of this Poisson point process and set
M:=\#\{i:\penalty\ \tau_{i}\leqslant\sigma\}
. In particular, remark that:

\mathbf{N}\big(M\geqslant 1\big)=\mathbf{N}\big(1-\exp(-\sigma)\big)\underset{\eqref{eq:excursionmeasuredecomp}}{=}1,

and we get that the measure
\overline{\mathbf{N}}:=\mathbf{N}(\cdot\>|\>M\geqslant 1)
is a probability measure. By comparing
\mathbf{N}
and
\overline{\mathbf{N}}
, to prove the proposition it suffices to show that we have

M<m_{0}\>\>\>\>\text{ or }\>\>\>\>\bigcap\limits_{i=1}^{M}\mathcal{R}_{\tau_{1},...,\tau_{M}}^{(i)}=\varnothing\penalty\ \penalty\ ,\quad\overline{\mathbf{N}}\text{-a.s.}

(4.17)

Let us prove (
4.17
), reasoning on the event
M\geqslant m_{0}
, so in particular
m\geqslant 2
. As we noticed after Proposition
2.1
, for every
i\leqslant M
, the ancestor time

r_{i}:=\sup\limits_{j\neq i}(\tau_{i}\curlywedge\tau_{j})

is a.s. a jumping time for
X
, corresponding to a loop in
\mathcal{L}
. Let us introduce the looptree dangling from this loop and containing
\Pi_{d}(\tau_{i})
. In this direction, we set
s_{i}:=\inf\{r\geqslant r_{i}:\penalty\ X_{r}\leqslant I_{r,\tau_{i}}\}\>\>\text{and}\>\>s_{i}^{\prime}:=\inf\{r>s_{i}:\penalty\ X_{r}\leqslant X_{s_{i}}\}.
Remark now that by
(A_{2})
we have
s_{i}<\tau_{i}<s_{i}^{\prime}
and
\Delta_{s_{i}}=0
and, to simplify notation, we write
X^{(i)}
for the excursion

X^{(i)}_{r}:=X_{s_{i}+r}-X_{s_{i}}\penalty\ \penalty\ ;\quad r\in[0,s^{\prime}_{i}-s_{i}].

We can use the excursion
X^{(i)}
to define a looptree exactly in the same way as in Section
2.1
, which is in fact the same as the set
\{u\in\mathcal{L}:\penalty\ \Pi_{d}(s_{i})\preccurlyeq u\}
equipped with the restriction of the distance
d
, and rooted at
\Pi_{d}(s_{i})=\Pi_{d}(s_{i}^{\prime})
. It is then easy to see that the process
R_{\tau_{1},...,\tau_{M}}^{(i)}-R_{\tau_{1},...,\tau_{M}}^{(i)}(0)
only depends on
X^{(i)}
and on the Brownian bridges attached to its jumps.
The idea now is to prove that under the event
M\geqslant m_{0}
, the sequence
(X^{(i)},\tau_{i}-s_{i})_{1\leqslant i\leqslant m_{0}}
is absolutely continuous with respect to a sequence of
m_{0}
independent copies of
(X,\tau_{1})
under
\mathbf{N}(\cdot\>|\>M=1)
. Let us assume this temporarily, and explain why it implies the statement of the proposition. By independence of the Brownian bridges conditionally on the looptree, we can then apply
equation (
4.16
) to see that under the event
M\geqslant m_{0}
, the law of the sequence
(\mathcal{R}_{\tau_{1},...\tau_{M}}^{(i)}-R_{\tau_{1},...\tau_{M}}^{(i)}(0))_{1\leqslant i\leqslant m_{0}}
is absolutely continuous with respect to that of a sequence of
m_{0}
independent copies of the range of an
(\alpha-1)
-stable subordinator (each of these copies being stopped at a random time).
Moreover, conditionally on
X
, on
M\geqslant m_{0}
and on the times
\tau_{1},...,\tau_{M}
, the random variables
(R_{\tau_{1},...\tau_{M}}^{(i)}-R_{\tau_{1},...\tau_{M}}^{(i)}(0))_{1\leqslant i\leqslant m_{0}}
and
(R_{\tau_{1},...\tau_{M}}^{(i)}(0))_{1\leqslant i\leqslant m_{0}}
are independent. In order to see this, just remark that under this conditioning, all the random variables
(R_{\tau_{1},...\tau_{M}}^{(i)}-R_{\tau_{1},...\tau_{M}}^{(i)}(0))_{1\leqslant i\leqslant m_{0}}
and
(R_{\tau_{1},...\tau_{M}}^{(i)}(0))_{1\leqslant i\leqslant m_{0}}
are Gaussian, and a direct computation of the covariance function gives the desired independence.
Now remark that by the Gaussian character of
Z
conditionally on
X
, the quantities
R_{\tau_{1},...\tau_{M}}^{(i)}(0)
, for
1\leqslant i\leqslant m_{0}
are a.s. all different. It follows that, when we condition on
M\geqslant m_{0}
, the law of the intersection in (
4.17
) is absolutely continuous with respect to the law of the intersection of pieces of
m_{0}
independent ranges of
(\alpha-1)
-stable subordinators, started from independent random points with different locations. It is then a classical result of regenerative sets theory, see
[
72
, Example 1]
, that since
m_{0}=\lceil\frac{1}{2-\alpha}\rceil+1
this intersection is almost surely empty.

Hence, to conclude, it remains to show that, on the event
M\geqslant m_{0}
, the sequence
(X^{(i)},\tau_{i}-s_{i})_{1\leqslant i\leqslant m_{0}}
has a law that is absolutely continuous with respect to that of a sequence of
m_{0}
independent copies of
(X,\tau_{1})
under
\mathbf{N}(\cdot\>|\>M=1)
. To this end, under
\mathbf{N}
and independently of
M
, we introduce
(\widetilde{X}^{(i)},\widetilde{T}^{(i)})_{i\geqslant 1}
an i.i.d. sequence distributed as
(X,\tau_{1})
under
\mathbf{N}(\cdot\>|\>M=1)
, and we set:

(\overline{X}^{(i)},\overline{T}^{(i)}):=\begin{cases}(X^{(i)},\tau_{i}-s_{i})\penalty\ &\text{if}\penalty\ \penalty\ 1\leqslant i\leqslant M\\
(\widetilde{X}^{(i)},\widetilde{T}^{(i)})\penalty\ &\text{if}\penalty\ \penalty\ i>M.\end{cases}

Remark now that it suffices to show that:

(\overline{X}^{(i)},\overline{T}^{(i)})_{i\geqslant 1}\overset{(d)}{=}(\widetilde{X}^{(i)},\widetilde{T}^{(i)})_{i\geqslant 1}\penalty\ .

(4.18)

On the event
M=1
, the identity (
4.18
) holds directly from the definition. On the event
M\geqslant 2
, define
\ell=\tau_{1}\curlywedge\tau_{2}\curlywedge\dots\curlywedge\tau_{M}
, and denote by
(u_{j},v_{j})_{j\geqslant 1}
the connected components of the open set
\{s\geqslant\ell:\penalty\ I_{\ell,s}<X_{s}\}
. Next, introduce the excursions:

X^{j}_{r}:=X_{u_{j}+r}-X_{u_{j}}\penalty\ ;\quad r\in[0,v_{j}-u_{j}],

for every
j\geqslant 1
. In words,
(X^{j})_{j\geqslant 1}
are the excursions of
X
above the minimum after time
\ell
. We also introduce
\zeta:=\#\{j\in\mathbb{N}:\>\exists i\in[\![1,M]\!],\>\tau_{i}\in(u_{j},v_{j})\}
the number of such excursions having at least one Poissonnian mark
\tau_{1},...,\tau_{M}
. Write
j_{1},...,j_{\zeta}
for the elements of
\{j\in\mathbb{N}:\>\exists i\in[\![1,M]\!],\>\tau_{i}\in(u_{j},v_{j})\}
in increasing order. Then classical properties of excursion theory and Poisson measures give that, conditionally on
\zeta
, the random variables
(X^{j_{k}})_{1\leqslant k\leqslant\zeta}
with their Poissonnian marks (translated by
(-u_{j_{k}})_{1\leqslant k\leqslant\zeta}
) are i.i.d. copies of the process
X
marked at
\tau_{1},...,\tau_{M}
under
\overline{\mathbf{N}}
. Remark that
\ell
is not a stopping time, so to obtain this property an approximation procedure is needed. Since this a standard verification, for example cutting at dyadic times and using that
X
is rcll, we leave the details to the reader. Finally since
M<\infty
,
\overline{\mathbf{N}}
-a.s ., the identity (
4.18
) follows by iterating the previous argument.
∎

We can now use Propositions
4.2
and
4.8
to derive our Proposition
4.5
:

Proof of Proposition
4.5
.

We argue under
\mathbf{P}
. Fix
