0<t_{1}<t_{2}<1
such that
t_{1}\sim_{d}t_{2}
and verifying (
4.14
). To simplify notation, we set
z=Z_{t_{1}}=Z_{t_{2}}
. Note that by time-reversal (
3.13
), it suffices to show
that for every
\varepsilon\in(0,t_{1})
, we can find
t_{1}^{\prime}\in[t_{1}-\varepsilon,t_{1}]
such that

Z_{t^{\prime}_{1}}<z<Z_{r}\>\>\text{for every}\>\>r\in\mathrm{Branch}(t_{1},t^{\prime}_{1})\setminus\{t_{1},t^{\prime}_{1}\}.

In this direction, fix
\varepsilon\in(0,t_{1})
, and let

\mathrm{Trunk}:=\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}s\preccurlyeq t_{1}\\
\Delta_{s}>0\end{subarray}}\left\{\mathrm{f}_{s}(u):0\leqslant u\leqslant 1,\mbox{ such that }\mathrm{f}_{s}(u)\leqslant t_{1}\right\},

which informally corresponds (after taking a closure and projecting it to
\mathcal{L}
) to the left side of the trunk linking
\Pi_{d}(0)
to
\Pi_{d}(t_{1})
. For
\ell\in[0,t_{1}]
, we set
\mathrm{r}_{\ell}:=\sup\{s\in\mathrm{Trunk}:\penalty\ s\preccurlyeq\ell\}
. By our assumption (
4.14
), we know that
Z_{s}>z
as soon as
s\prec t_{1}
. If there exists
s\in\mathrm{Trunk}\cap[t_{1}-\varepsilon,t_{1}]
such that
Z_{s}<z
then, since for every jump time
t
of
X
the pinch point times of
\mathrm{f}_{t}([0,1])
are dense in
\mathrm{f}_{t}([0,1])
, without loss of generality we can assume that
s
is a pinch point time and we infer from the definition of
\mathrm{Trunk}
that we can directly take
t_{1}^{\prime}=s
. It remains to treat the case
Z\geqslant z
on
\mathrm{Trunk}\cap[t_{1}-\varepsilon,t_{1}]
, and we make this assumption for the rest of the proof.

Figure 19:
Illustration of the proof of Proposition
4.5
. Left: finding a time
t_{1}^{\prime}
corresponds to finding a loop (in orange on the figure) blocking level
z=Z_{t_{1}}
i.e. such that the label process along this loop takes values that are greater than
z
, and values that are less than
z
. Right: if such blocking loops cannot be found on the trunk from
t_{1}
to
0
, then by Proposition
4.2
we can find
m_{0}
distinct sub-looptrees (in blue in the figure) going from a level strictly above
z
to strictly below
z
. By Proposition
4.8
, one of them must possess such a blocking loop.

Now, recall from Proposition
4.2
that the time
t_{1}
cannot be a left minimal record of the process
Z
. This combined with the fact that pinch point times are dense, implies that for
m_{0}=\lceil\frac{1}{2-\alpha}\rceil+1
, we can find
t_{1}-\varepsilon<\ell_{1}<\cdots<\ell_{m_{0}}<t_{1}
such that:

—

\ell_{1},...,\ell_{m_{0}}
are pinch point times,

—

Z_{\ell_{i}}<z
,

—

the values
\mathrm{r}_{\ell_{1}},\mathrm{r}_{\ell_{2}},\dots,\mathrm{r}_{\ell_{m_{0}}}
are all distinct and are elements of
\mathrm{Trunk}\cap[t_{1}-\varepsilon,t_{1}]
.

One can then consider the processes
R_{\ell_{1},...,\ell_{m_{0}}}^{(i)}
,
1\leqslant i\leqslant m_{0}
, as above and the associated minimal records
\mathcal{R}^{{(i)}}_{\ell_{1},...,\ell_{m_{0}}}
,
1\leqslant i\leqslant m_{0}
, must all cross the value
z
by construction. If such a crossing is made by a jump, we have found a
t_{1}^{\prime}
as desired. Otherwise, the value
z
is common to the
m_{0}
closed sets
\mathcal{R}^{{(i)}}_{\ell_{1},...,\ell_{m_{0}}}
,
1\leqslant i\leqslant m_{0}
, which is impossible by Proposition
4.8
and the remark just after it.∎

5
The two-point function and local minima of
Z
on loops

We continue our investigation of local minima of
Z
and prove in this section that, under
\mathbf{P}
, we have:

-

the sets
\Pi_{d}(\mathrm{LeftRec})\cap\mathrm{Loops}
and
\Pi_{d}(\mathrm{RightRec})\cap\mathrm{Loops}
are dense in
\mathrm{Loops}
,

-

if
\alpha\in[\frac{3}{2},2)
the set of local right minimal values of
Z
that happen on a loop and the set of local left minimal values of
Z
that happen on a loop are disjoint, whereas if
\alpha\in(1,\frac{3}{2})
they almost surely intersect.

See Proposition
5.8
for a precise statement. These properties will play a crucial role when identifying the topology of
\mathcal{S}
as being that of the Sierpinski carpet in the dilute phase and when studying the behavior of simple geodesics (namely the fact that they bounce on the faces of
\mathcal{S}
). The proof is based on a covering argument due to Shepp
[
61
]
which has already been used in the study of increase points of Lévy processes by Bertoin
[
16
]
and later in the study of random maps
[
22
, Lemma 29]
or
[
52
, Lemma 14]
. It crucially relies on the computation of the constant
\mathbf{N}(\inf Z\leqslant-1)
which appeared in Corollary
4.1
, specifically we prove that
{\bf N}(\sup Z>1)=\frac{\alpha(\alpha-1)}{2}
in Proposition
5.4
. This computation is based on an integral equation satisfied by the function defined for every
(\lambda,x)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
by

w_{\lambda}(x)=\mathbf{N}\big(1-\exp(-\lambda\sigma)\mathbbm{1}_{\sup Z<x}\big).

(5.1)

This analysis is of independent interest and in particular, (
5.1
) characterizes the joint law of
(\sup Z,\sigma)
under
\mathbf{N}
, which can be related to the distance in
\mathcal{S}
between two typical points (see Proposition
7.1
and equation (
7.18
) below). For this reason we refer to (
5.1
) as the
two-point function
.

5.1
An equation for the two-point function

We begin by studying the function (
5.1
). We stress that when
x\leqslant 0
, we simply have
w_{\lambda}(x)=\infty
. Also note that, when
\lambda=0
, we have
w_{0}(x)=\mathbf{N}(\sup Z>x)
and, by dominated convergence and (
1.12
), we obtain

\lim\limits_{x\to\infty}w_{\lambda}(x)=\mathbf{N}\big(1-\exp(-\lambda\sigma)\big)=\lambda^{1/\alpha}.

The goal of this section is to prove that
w_{\lambda}(x)
satisfies an integral equation that allows us to compute
w_{0}(x)
. Since we will be dealing with several variants of Brownian motions and Bessel processes, in the next two sections, it will be convenient to denote the canonical process on
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{R})
by
\big(B_{t}:\penalty\ t\geqslant 0\big)
, and we will endow this space with various probability measures. Namely, for every
x\in\mathbb{R}
(resp.
x\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{*}
), the probability measure
\mathbb{P}_{x}
(resp.
\mathbb{P}^{\langle\nu\rangle}_{x}
) is the law of a Brownian motion (resp. Bessel process with index
7
7

7

There are several equivalent ways to parametrize Bessel processes, according to their parameter
a
, their index
\nu
, or their dimension
d
. These are related via the formulas
d=2a+1
and
\nu=a-\frac{1}{2}
and they are (locally) solutions to the stochastic differential equation

\mathrm{d}X_{t}=\frac{a}{X_{t}}\mathrm{d}t+\mathrm{d}W_{t},

where
W
is a standard Brownian motion. See
[
125
, Chapter XI]
.

\nu
) started from
x
. We also write
\mathbb{P}_{x}^{(s)}
for the law of a Brownian motion with lifetime
s>0
starting from
x
, as well as
\mathbb{P}_{x\to y}^{(s)}
for the law of a Brownian bridge with lifetime
s>0
starting from
x
and ending at
y
; where by convention under
\mathbb{P}_{x\to y}^{(s)}
we take
B_{t}=y
for every
t\geqslant s
. Specifically, this section is devoted to the proof of the following result:

Theorem 5.1

(The two-point function)
.

For every
(\lambda,x)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{*}
we have

\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{s^{\alpha+1}}\mathbb{E}_{0\rightarrow 0}^{(s)}\Big[1-sw_{\lambda}(x)-\exp\Big(-\int_{0}^{s}{\rm d}u\>w_{\lambda}(x+B_{u})\Big)\Big]=-\Gamma(-\alpha)\lambda,

(5.2)

where the integral is well-defined.

This result is later used in the case
\lambda=0
to prove Proposition
5.4
. The previous theorem is reminiscent of the connection between the Brownian motion indexed by the Brownian tree – or Brownian snake – and differential equations, see
[
87
]
. In this case, the analog of
w_{\lambda}(x)
was computed exactly in
[
55
, Lemma 6 & 7]
in the Brownian case (which morally corresponds to the limit
\alpha\to 2
) by showing that
x\mapsto\mathbf{N}\big(1-\exp(-\lambda\sigma)\mathbf{1}_{\sup Z<x}\big)
solves the differential equation:

\frac{1}{2}w^{\prime\prime}(x)=2w^{2}(x)-\lambda\quad\mbox{ in }(0,\infty)\quad\mbox{ with }w(0)=\infty\mbox{ and }w(\infty)=\sqrt{\lambda/2}.

A similar study of Brownian motion indexed by the stable tree (as opposed to the stable looptree here) has recently been done
[
10
]
. The equation (
5.2
) can be understood informally by exploring the underlying looptree infinitesimally: under
\mathbf{N}
“the first loop” encountered has a length
s
“distributed” according to the Lévy measure (
1.11
) given by
\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{\Gamma(-\alpha)s^{\alpha+1}}
. Then the Markov property (Corollary
3.6
) states that the looptrees (with labels shifted by the values at their root) attached to this loop form a Poisson process with intensity
\mathbbm{1}_{x\in[0,s]}{\rm d}x\mathbf{N}
. Conditionally on
s
, and neglecting the rest of the looptree, since the label of the loop is given by a Brownian bridge of length
s
, an application of the exponential formula for Poisson process shows that conditionally on
s
we have:

\exp(-\lambda\sigma)\mathbbm{1}_{\sup Z<x}=\mathbb{E}_{0\rightarrow 0}^{(s)}\Big[\exp\Big(-\int_{0}^{s}{\rm d}u\>w_{\lambda}(x+B_{u})\Big)\Big].

This explains the appearance of the corresponding term in (
5.2
). The proof is however more subtle since delicate compensations are involved.
Let us first gather some useful properties of
w_{\lambda}
.

Lemma 5.2
.

Fix
\lambda\geqslant 0
. The following properties hold.

\mathrm{(i)}

The function
x\mapsto w_{\lambda}(x)
is in
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}((0,\infty),\mathbb{R})
.

\mathrm{(ii)}

The function

x\longmapsto\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{s^{\alpha+1}}\mathbb{E}_{0\rightarrow 0}^{(s)}\Big[1-sw_{\lambda}(x)-\exp\big(-\int_{0}^{s}{\rm d}u\>w_{\lambda}(x+B_{u})\big)\Big]

is well-defined on
(0,\infty)
, in the sense that the integral is
absolutely convergent, and continuous.

Proof.

(i) The case
\lambda=0
follows directly from scaling, which gives
w_{0}(x)=x^{-2}\mathbf{N}(\sup Z>1)
, and the fact that
\mathbf{N}(\sup Z>1)\in(0,\infty)
by Corollary
4.1
. Next, for a fixed positive value of
x
, note that
\lambda\mapsto w_{\lambda}(x)
is of class
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
on
(0,\infty)
, since by dominated convergence, we have:

\frac{\partial^{n}w_{\lambda}(x)}{\partial\lambda^{n}}=(-1)^{n+1}\mathbf{N}\big(\sigma^{n}\exp(-\lambda\sigma)\mathbbm{1}_{\sup Z<x}\big).

Scaling properties under the measure
\mathbf{N}
yield
w_{\lambda}(x)=x^{-2}w_{\lambda x^{2\alpha}}(1)
. As a consequence, we conclude that
w_{\lambda}(x)
is also of class
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
in the variable
x\in(0,\infty)
.

(ii) Fix
\lambda\geqslant 0
and
x>0
, and observe that the integral expression

\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{s^{1+\alpha}}\big(1-sw_{\lambda}(x)-\exp(-sw_{\lambda}(x))\big)

is absolutely convergent, and a direct computation gives that it equals
-\Gamma(-\alpha)w_{\lambda}(x)^{\alpha}
, so that it is
continuous in
x
. Therefore, to obtain (ii), it is enough to show that the integral

\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{s^{1+\alpha}}\Big(\exp\big(-sw_{\lambda}(x)\big)-\mathbb{E}_{0\rightarrow 0}^{(s)}\big[\exp\big(-\int_{0}^{s}{\rm d}u\>w_{\lambda}(x+B_{u})\big)\big]\Big)

is also absolutely convergent and continuous in the variable
x
. By dominated convergence, this will be entailed by proving
that, for every compact set
K\subset(0,\infty)
, we have:

\exp\big(-sw_{\lambda}(x)\big)-\mathbb{E}_{0\rightarrow 0}^{(s)}\Big[\exp\big(-\int_{0}^{s}{\rm d}u\>w_{\lambda}(x+B_{u})\big)\Big]=O_{K}(1\wedge s^{2})\,,

where this notation means that the left-hand side divided by
1\wedge s^{2}
is uniformly bounded in absolute value for
s>0
and
x\in K
.
Since the left-hand side is clearly bounded by
1
in absolute value,
it suffices to show that it is
O_{K}(s^{2})
.
To obtain this
bound, note that by scaling and standard properties of Brownian
bridges, it follows that:

\mathbb{P}^{(s)}_{0\to 0}\Big(\sup|B|>\frac{x}{2}\Big)=\mathbb{P}^{(1)}_{0\to 0}\Big(\sup|B|>\frac{x}{2\sqrt{s}}\Big)\leqslant c_{1}\exp\Big(-c_{2}\frac{x^{2}}{s}\Big)=O_{K}(s^{2}),

for some universal constants
c_{1},c_{2}>0
.
Therefore, we can write:

\displaystyle\exp\big(-sw_{\lambda}(x)\big)-\mathbb{E}_{0\rightarrow 0}^{(s)}\Big[\exp\big(-\int_{0}^{s}{\rm d}u\>w_{\lambda}(x+B_{u})\big)\Big]

\displaystyle=\exp\big(-sw_{\lambda}(x)\big)\>\>\mathbb{E}_{0\rightarrow 0}^{(s)}\Big[1-\exp\Big(-\int_{0}^{s}{\rm d}u\>\big(w_{\lambda}(x+B_{u})-w_{\lambda}(x)\big)\Big)\Big]

\displaystyle=\exp\big(-sw_{\lambda}(x)\big)\>\>\mathbb{E}_{0\rightarrow 0}^{(s)}\Big[\Big(1-\exp\Big(-\int_{0}^{s}{\rm d}u\>\big(w_{\lambda}(x+B_{u})-w_{\lambda}(x)\big)\Big)\Big)\mathbbm{1}_{\sup|B|<\frac{x}{2}}\Big]+O_{K}(s^{2})\,.

By (i), we may Taylor expand
w_{\lambda}(x+B_{u})-w_{\lambda}(x)=w_{\lambda}^{\prime}(x)B_{u}+S_{\lambda}(x,B_{u})B_{u}^{2}
, where the remainder term

S_{\lambda}(x,y)
is a bounded function on
\{(x,y):x\in K,|y|\leqslant x/2\}
. Using the fact that
1-\mathrm{e}^{-y}=y+O(y^{2})
for
y
in a
compact neighborhood of
0
, we then write, on the event
\{\sup|B|<x/2\}
,

1-\exp\Big(-\int_{0}^{s}{\rm d}u\>\big(w_{\lambda}(x+B_{u})-w_{\lambda}(x)\big)\Big)\Big)=\int_{0}^{s}w_{\lambda}^{\prime}(x)B_{u}{\rm d}u+\int_{0}^{s}S_{\lambda}(x,B_{u})B_{u}^{2}{\rm d}u+O_{K}(s^{2})\,.

Taking expectations, and using the fact that
\mathbb{E}_{0\rightarrow 0}^{(s)}[B_{u}^{2}]\leqslant u
, we obtain:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{0\rightarrow 0}^{(s)}\Big[\Big(1-\exp\Big(-\int_{0}^{s}{\rm d}u\>\big(w_{\lambda}(x+B_{u})-

\displaystyle w_{\lambda}(x)\big)\Big)\Big)\mathbbm{1}_{\sup|B|<\frac{x}{2}}\Big]

\displaystyle=w_{\lambda}^{\prime}(x)\mathbb{E}_{0\rightarrow 0}^{(s)}\Big[\int_{0}^{s}B_{u}{\rm d}u\mathbbm{1}_{\sup|B|<\frac{x}{2}}\Big]+O_{K}(s^{2}).

By symmetry, the last expectation vanishes, and this completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Let us continue our way towards (
5.2
), and introduce some more notation. Fix
\ell>0
, and, working under the measure
\mathbf{N}
, write
s^{(\ell)}_{1},\dots,s^{(\ell)}_{M_{\ell}}
for the elements of

\Big\{t\geqslant 0:\>\Delta_{t}\geqslant\ell\text{ such that }\Delta_{s}<\ell\text{ for every }s\prec t\Big\}

in increasing order. Alternatively, these can be defined as the finite elements in the sequence of stopping times
(s_{i}^{(\ell)},i\geqslant 1)
defined inductively by

s^{(\ell)}_{1}=\inf\{t\geqslant 0:\Delta_{t}\geqslant\ell\}
, and then, for
i\geqslant 1
, by setting

t^{(\ell)}_{i}:=\inf\{t\geqslant s^{(\ell)}_{i}:\>X_{t}=X_{s^{(\ell)}_{i}-}\}\,,\qquad s^{(\ell)}_{i+1}:=\inf\{t\geqslant t_{i}^{(\ell)}:\Delta_{t}\geqslant\ell\}\,.

We also introduce the set

\mathcal{A}^{(\ell)}:=\bigcup_{1\leqslant i\leqslant M_{\ell}}[s^{(\ell)}_{i},t^{(\ell)}_{i}].

In words, the image by
\Pi_{d}
of the complement of
\mathcal{A}^{(\ell)}
corresponds to the looptree obtained after removing from
\mathcal{L}
all the loops with size larger than
\ell
as well as all their descendants. In order to encode the latter and the associated labels we reparametrize it. In this direction, we let
\Gamma^{(\ell)}
be the right-inverse of
t\mapsto\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s\mathbbm{1}_{s\notin\mathcal{A}^{(\ell)}}
, and set

(X_{t}^{(\ell)},Z^{(\ell)}_{t}):=(X_{\Gamma^{(\ell)}_{t}},Z_{\Gamma^{(\ell)}_{t}}),\quad\text{ for }t\geqslant 0.

Notice that
Z^{(\ell)}
can also be defined as a Gaussian process, by adapting the construction of
Z
from
X
, but using the process
X^{(\ell)}
instead. Furthermore, we claim that under
\mathbf{N}
, the process
X^{(\ell)}
is the excursion of a Lévy process with Laplace exponent

\displaystyle\phi_{\ell}(\lambda)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\Gamma(-\alpha)}\int_{0}^{\infty}(\exp(-\lambda r)-1+\lambda r)\frac{\mathrm{d}r}{r^{1+\alpha}}-\frac{1}{\Gamma(-\alpha)}\int_{\ell}^{\infty}(\exp(-\lambda r)-1)\frac{\mathrm{d}r}{r^{1+\alpha}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\alpha}{\Gamma(2-\alpha)}\ell^{-(\alpha-1)}\lambda+\frac{1}{\Gamma(-\alpha)}\int_{0}^{\ell}(\exp(-\lambda r)-1+\lambda r)\frac{\mathrm{d}r}{r^{1+\alpha}}.

Indeed, working first under the probability distribution
\mathbf{Q}

and applying the strong Markov property at the stopping times

s^{(\ell)}_{i},t^{(\ell)}_{i}
, we see that
X^{(\ell)}
is a version of
the process
X
, where the jumps of size
\geqslant\ell
have been
trimmed, and the computation of the Laplace exponent
\phi_{\ell}

follows from the Lévy-Itô representation of an
\alpha
-stable Lévy
process from a Poisson point measure, by restricting the latter to
atoms of size
<\ell
. Details are left to the reader, a similar discussion in the context of Lévy trees can be found in
[
2
, Proposition 1.1]
.
In particular, if we write
\sigma_{\ell}
for the lifetime of

X^{(\ell)}
under
\mathbf{N}
, we have
\mathbf{N}(1-\exp(-\lambda\sigma_{\ell}))=\phi^{-1}_{\ell}(\lambda)
, by
[
17
, Theorem 1, Chapter VII]
. As a consequence, note that:

\mathbf{N}(\sigma_{\ell})=(\phi_{\ell}^{-1})^{\prime}(0)=(\alpha-1)\Gamma(-\alpha)\ell^{\alpha-1}\,,\qquad\mathbf{N}(\sigma_{\ell}^{2})=(\phi_{\ell}^{-1})^{\prime\prime}(0)=\frac{(\alpha-1)^{3}\Gamma(-\alpha)^{2}}{2-\alpha}\ell^{2\alpha-1}\,.

(5.3)

Moreover, conditionally on
(X^{(\ell)},Z^{(\ell)})
,
M_{\ell}
is a Poisson random variable with intensity

\sigma_{\ell}\int_{\ell}^{\infty}\frac{{\rm d}r}{\Gamma(-\alpha)r^{1+\alpha}}=\sigma_{\ell}\frac{\ell^{-\alpha}}{\alpha\Gamma(-\alpha)},

and therefore we have

\mathbf{N}(M_{\ell})=\frac{\alpha-1}{\alpha\,\ell}\,,\qquad\mathbf{N}(M_{\ell}^{2})=\frac{\alpha-1}{\alpha^{2}(2-\alpha)\ell}.

(5.4)

The next lemma establishes that
Z^{(\ell)}
is in a sense small as
\ell\to 0
.

Lemma 5.3
.

For every
\delta>0
, we have:

\mathbf{N}(\sup|Z^{(\ell)}|>\ell^{\frac{1}{2}-\delta})=o(\ell).

(5.5)

We stress that (
5.5
) is not sharp, and we could use the same proof to obtain that
\mathbf{N}(\sup|Z^{(\ell)}|>\ell^{\frac{1}{2}-\delta})=o(\ell^{c})
for any exponent
c>0
.

Proof.

We first notice that it suffices to prove the one-sided estimate
\mathbf{N}(\sup Z^{(\ell)}>\ell^{\frac{1}{2}-\delta})=o(\ell)
, since
Z^{(\ell)}
and
-Z^{(\ell)}
have the same law.
By scaling, we have
\mathbf{N}(\sup Z^{(\ell)}>\ell^{\frac{1}{2}-\delta})=\ell^{-1}\mathbf{N}(\sup Z^{(1)}>\ell^{-\delta})
and it suffices to establish that:

\mathbf{N}(\sup Z^{(1)}>\ell^{-\delta})=o(\ell^{2}).

The idea is to control different events involving
\sup Z^{(1)}
in terms of the excursion lengths
\sigma_{\ell}
and
\sigma
, disintegrate with respect to the latter, and use the stretched-exponential controls on
\sup Z
under
\mathbf{P}
derived in Proposition

3.2
. Fix
\eta\in(0,2\alpha\delta)
. We first write

\mathbf{N}(\sup Z^{(1)}>\ell^{-\delta})=\mathbf{N}(\sigma_{1}>\ell^{-\eta})+\mathbf{N}(\sup Z^{(1)}>\ell^{-\delta},\sigma_{1}\leqslant\ell^{-\eta}),

(5.6)

and study each term separately. First, since
\mathbf{N}(1-\exp(-\lambda\sigma_{1}))=\phi_{1}^{-1}(\lambda)
, and since
\phi_{1}
is of class
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
on
\mathbb{R}
, it follows that
\mathbf{N}\big(\sigma_{1}^{n}\big)<\infty
for every
n\geqslant 1
. Thus, by the Markov inequality, we deduce that

\displaystyle\mathbf{N}(\sigma_{1}>\ell^{-\eta})=\mathbf{N}(\sigma_{1}^{n}\geqslant\ell^{-n\eta})\leqslant\ell^{n\eta}\mathbf{N}(\sigma_{1}^{n}).

By choosing
n
large enough, we obtain that
\mathbf{N}(\sigma_{1}\geqslant\ell^{-\eta})=o(\ell^{2})
. Let us now consider the remaining term in (
5.6
).
Since, conditionally on
(X^{(1)},Z^{(1)})
, the random variable
M_{1}
is Poisson with mean
\frac{\sigma_{1}}{\alpha\Gamma(-\alpha)}
we have:

\displaystyle\mathbf{N}(\sup Z^{(1)}>\ell^{-\delta},\sigma_{1}\leqslant\ell^{-\eta})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{N}\big(\mathbbm{1}_{\sup Z^{(1)}>\ell^{-\delta}}\mathbbm{1}_{\sigma_{1}\leqslant\ell^{-\eta}}\exp\big(\frac{\sigma_{1}}{\alpha\Gamma(-\alpha)}\big)\mathbbm{1}_{M_{1}=0}\big)

\displaystyle\leqslant\exp\big(\frac{\ell^{-\eta}}{\alpha\Gamma(-\alpha)}\big)\mathbf{N}\big(\mathbbm{1}_{\sup Z^{(1)}>\ell^{-\delta}}\mathbbm{1}_{\sigma_{1}\leqslant\ell^{-\eta}}\mathbbm{1}_{M_{1}=0}\big).

Now, since
(Z^{(1)},\sigma_{1})=(Z,\sigma)
on the event
\{M_{1}=0\}
, we infer that the previous display is bounded above by:

\exp\big(\frac{\ell^{-\eta}}{\alpha\Gamma(-\alpha)}\big)\mathbf{N}\big(\sup Z>\ell^{-\delta},\sigma\leqslant\ell^{-\eta}\big).

Moreover, by scaling and disintegration with respect to
\sigma
we have:

\displaystyle\mathbf{N}(\sup Z>\ell^{-\delta}\>;\>\sigma<\ell^{-\eta})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|\Gamma\big(-\frac{1}{\alpha}\big)|}\int_{0}^{\ell^{-\eta}}\frac{\mathrm{d}v}{v^{1+\frac{1}{\alpha}}}\mathbf{N}^{(v)}\big(\sup Z>\ell^{-\delta}\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|\Gamma\big(-\frac{1}{\alpha}\big)|}\int_{0}^{\ell^{-\eta}}\frac{\mathrm{d}v}{v^{1+\frac{1}{\alpha}}}\mathbf{P}\big(\sup Z>v^{-\frac{1}{2\alpha}}\ell^{-\delta}\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\Gamma\big(1-\frac{1}{\alpha}\big)}\ell^{2\delta}\int_{\ell^{\frac{\eta}{2\alpha}-\delta}}^{\infty}\,{\rm d}u\,u\cdot\mathbf{P}(\sup Z>u).

Finally, by (i) in Proposition
3.2
, we can find positive, finite constants
\beta,c^{\prime},C^{\prime}
such that, for every
\ell>0
, the previous display is bounded above by
C^{\prime}\exp(-c^{\prime}\ell^{-\beta(\delta-\eta/2\alpha)})
. Since
\eta<2\alpha\delta
, this implies

\mathbf{N}(\sup Z^{(1)}>\ell^{-\delta},\sigma_{1}\leqslant\ell^{-\eta})=o(\ell^{2})\,,

completing the proof of the lemma.
∎

We finally derive the integral equation (
5.2
).

Proof of Theorem
5.1
.

We fix
\lambda\geqslant 0
. For every
\ell,y>0
, consider the quantity

J_{\lambda}^{(\ell)}(y):=\alpha\ell^{\alpha}\int_{\ell}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{s^{\alpha+1}}\mathbb{E}_{0\rightarrow 0}^{(s)}\Big[1-\exp\big(-\int_{0}^{s}{\rm d}u\>w_{\lambda}(y+B_{u})\big)\Big]\quad\in\quad[0,1]\,.

Notice that we can rewrite
J_{\lambda}^{(\ell)}(y)
as follows:

\displaystyle J_{\lambda}^{(\ell)}(y)=\frac{\alpha}{\alpha-1}\ell w_{\lambda}(y)+\alpha\ell^{\alpha}\int_{\ell}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{s^{\alpha+1}}\mathbb{E}_{0\rightarrow 0}^{(s)}\Big[1-sw_{\lambda}(y)-\exp\big(-\int_{0}^{s}{\rm d}u\>w_{\lambda}(y+B_{u})\big)\Big]\,.

Therefore, by Lemma
5.2
(ii), if
y
is
restricted to a compact subset
K
of
(0,\infty)
, it holds that:

\displaystyle J_{\lambda}^{(\ell)}(y)

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha}{\alpha-1}\ell w_{\lambda}(y)+\alpha\ell^{\alpha}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{s^{\alpha+1}}\mathbb{E}_{0\rightarrow 0}^{(s)}\Big[1-sw_{\lambda}(y)-\exp\big(-\int_{0}^{s}\mathrm{d}u\>w_{\lambda}(y+B_{u})\big)\Big]+o_{K}(\ell^{\alpha})

\displaystyle=O_{K}(\ell)\,.

(5.7)

As before, the subscript
K
in the
o,O
notation above means that it is uniform in
y\in K
.

We now fix
x>0
in the rest of this proof, and observe that the desired integral equation (
5.2
) is equivalent to

\lim\limits_{\ell\to 0}\ell^{-\alpha+1}\big(w_{\lambda}(x)-\frac{\alpha-1}{\alpha}\ell^{-1}J_{\lambda}^{(\ell)}(x)\big)=\frac{\Gamma(2-\alpha)}{\alpha-1}\lambda.

(5.8)

Recall now the notation
s^{(\ell)}_{1},\ldots,s^{(\ell)}_{M_{\ell}}
, and for every
i\leqslant M_{\ell}
, introduce the process

B_{i}(t):=Z_{\mathrm{f}_{s^{(\ell)}_{i}}(t/\Delta_{s^{(\ell)}_{i}})}-Z_{s^{(\ell)}_{i}},\qquad 0\leqslant t\leqslant\Delta_{s^{(\ell)}_{i}}\,,

describing the labels
Z
as
one circles around the loop encoded by the jump time
s^{(\ell)}_{i}
,
shifted by their value at
s^{(\ell)}_{i}
. Note that conditionally
on
\Delta_{s^{(\ell)}_{i}}
, this process is a Brownian bridge with
duration
\Delta_{s^{(\ell)}_{i}}
.
We also let
(u_{i,j},v_{i,j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
be the connected components of the open set
\{t\in[s^{(\ell)}_{i},t^{(\ell)}_{i}]:\>X_{t}>I_{s^{(\ell)}_{i},t}\}
: these intervals describe the looptrees grafted on the latter loop. Precisely, we encode the positions, encodings and labelings of these looptrees by introducing the quantities

x^{i,j}:=X_{s^{(\ell)}_{i}}-X_{u_{i,j}}
and

X^{i,j}_{t}:=X_{(u_{i,j}+t)\wedge v_{i,j}}-X_{u_{i,j}}\quad\text{ and }\quad Z^{i,j}_{t}:=Z_{(u_{i,j}+t)\wedge v_{i,j}}-Z_{u_{i,j}}\>\>,\quad\text{for}\>\>t\geqslant 0,.

We also let
\sigma^{i,j}=v_{i,j}-u_{i,j}
.
Now notice that
w_{\lambda}(x)
equals

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbf{N}\big(1-\exp(-\lambda\sigma)\mathbbm{1}_{\sup Z<x}\big)

(5.9)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbf{N}\Big(1-\exp\Big(-\lambda\sigma_{\ell}-\sum\limits_{i=1}^{M_{\ell}}\sum\limits_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\big(\lambda\sigma^{i,j}-\log(\mathbbm{1}_{\sup_{[u^{i,j},v^{i,j}]}Z<x})\big)\Big)\Big)

\displaystyle\underset{Z_{u^{i,j}}=Z_{s^{(\ell)}_{i}}+B_{i}(x^{i,j})}{=}

\displaystyle\mathbf{N}\Big(1-\exp\Big(-\lambda\sigma_{\ell}-\sum\limits_{i=1}^{M_{\ell}}\sum\limits_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\big(\lambda\sigma^{i,j}-\log\big(\mathbbm{1}_{\sup Z^{i,j}<x-B_{i}(x^{i,j})-Z_{s^{(\ell)}_{i}}}\big)\big)\Big)\Big).

It is now a consequence of the strong Markov property at times

s^{(\ell)}_{i}
and
t^{(\ell)}_{i}
that conditionally on
\sigma_{\ell}
,
M_{\ell}
, and

(Z_{s^{(\ell)}_{i}},\Delta_{s^{(\ell)}_{i}},B_{i})
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant M_{\ell}
, the point measures

\mathcal{N}^{i}:=\sum\limits_{j\in J}\delta_{x^{i,j},X^{i,j},Z^{i,j}},\quad 1\leqslant i\leqslant M_{\ell},

are independent Poisson point measures with intensities

\mathbbm{1}_{x\in[0,\Delta_{s^{(\ell)}_{i}}]}\mathrm{d}x\mathbf{N}(\mathrm{d}(X,Z))\,,\qquad 1\leqslant i\leqslant M_{\ell}\,.

An application of the Laplace functional formula for Poisson measures shows that (
5.9
) can be written in the form:

\mathbf{N}\Big(1-\exp\Big(-\lambda\sigma_{\ell}-\sum\limits_{i=1}^{M_{\ell}}\int_{0}^{\Delta_{s^{(\ell)}_{i}}}{\rm d}u\,w_{\lambda}(x-B_{i}(u)-Z_{s^{(\ell)}_{i}})\Big)\Big)\,.

Now note that conditionally given
\sigma_{\ell},M_{\ell},Z_{s^{(\ell)}_{i}}
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant M_{\ell}
, the random variables
(\Delta_{s^{(\ell)}_{i}},B_{i})

are i.i.d. with common distribution
\alpha\ell^{\alpha}s^{-\alpha-1}{\rm d}s\mathbbm{1}_{s\geqslant\ell}\mathbb{P}^{(s)}_{0\rightarrow 0}({\rm d}b)
. Hence, we
may integrate with respect to these random variables and rewrite the
preceding expression as:

\displaystyle\mathbf{N}\Big(1-\exp(-\lambda\sigma_{\ell})\prod_{i=1}^{M_{\ell}}\Big(\alpha\ell^{\alpha}\int_{\ell}^{\infty}\frac{{\rm d}s}{s^{\alpha+1}}\mathbb{E}^{(s)}_{0\rightarrow 0}\Big[\exp\big(-\int_{0}^{s}{\rm d}u\,w_{\lambda}(x-B_{u}-Z_{s_{i}^{(\ell)}})\big)\Big]\Big)\Big)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{N}\Big(1-\exp(-\lambda\sigma_{\ell})\prod_{i=1}^{M_{\ell}}\big(1-J_{\lambda}^{(\ell)}(x-Z_{s^{(\ell)}_{i}})\big)\Big)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{N}\Big(\mathbbm{1}_{\sup|Z^{(\ell)}|<\ell^{\frac{1}{2}-\delta}}\big(1-\exp(-\lambda\sigma_{\ell})\prod_{i=1}^{M_{\ell}}\big(1-J_{\lambda}^{(\ell)}(x-Z_{s^{(\ell)}_{i}})\big)\big)\Big)+o(\ell)

(5.10)

where we used Lemma
5.3
in the second equality, for some
\delta
that we choose in
(0,1-\alpha/2)
, and
where the
o(\ell)
term is uniform in
x\in(0,\infty)
. Using

0\leqslant\mathrm{e}^{-y}-(1-y)\leqslant y^{2}
for
y\geqslant 0
, we obtain that this is equal to

\mathbf{N}\Big(\mathbbm{1}_{\sup|Z^{(\ell)}|<\ell^{\frac{1}{2}-\delta}}\big(1-\exp(-\lambda\sigma_{\ell}-\sum_{i=1}^{M_{\ell}}J_{\lambda}^{(\ell)}(x-Z_{s^{(\ell)}_{i}})\big)\big)\Big)+R(\ell,x)+o(\ell)

(5.11)

where the remainder satisfies, for fixed
x>0
,

0\leqslant R(\ell,x)\leqslant\mathbf{N}\Big(\mathbbm{1}_{\sup|Z^{(\ell)}|<\ell^{\frac{1}{2}-\delta}}\sum_{i=1}^{M_{\ell}}J^{(\ell)}_{\lambda}(x-Z_{s^{(\ell)}_{i}})^{2}\Big)=O(\ell^{2})\mathbf{N}(M_{\ell})=O(\ell)\,,

due to
(
5.7
) and (
5.4
).

Now note that, by (ii)
in Lemma
5.2
and (
5.7
), we have

J_{\lambda}^{(\ell)}(x+\eta)-J_{\lambda}^{(\ell)}(x)=\frac{\alpha}{\alpha-1}\ell w^{\prime}_{\lambda}(x)\eta+o(\ell^{\alpha})\,,

as
\ell\to 0
, where the remainder term is uniform over the
choice of
\eta\in(-\ell^{1/2-\delta},\ell^{1/2-\delta})
. Hence, by developing the exponential in
(
5.11
), we obtain:

\displaystyle w_{\lambda}(x)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{N}\Big(\mathbbm{1}_{\sup|Z^{(\ell)}|<\ell^{\frac{1}{2}-\delta}}\Big(1-\exp\big(-\lambda\sigma_{\ell}-\sum\limits_{i=1}^{M_{\ell}}J_{\lambda}^{(\ell)}\big(x-Z_{s^{(\ell)}_{i}}\big)\big)\Big)\Big)+o(\ell)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{N}\Big(1-\exp\big(-\lambda\sigma_{\ell}-\sum\limits_{i=1}^{M_{\ell}}J_{\lambda}^{(\ell)}(x)\big)\Big)+\frac{\alpha\,\ell}{\alpha-1}w_{\lambda}^{\prime}(x)\mathbf{N}\Big(\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda\sigma_{\ell}-\sum_{i=1}^{M_{\ell}}J^{(\ell)}_{\lambda}(x)}\sum_{i=1}^{M_{\ell}}Z_{s^{(\ell)}_{i}}\Big)

\displaystyle+o\Big(\mathbf{N}(M_{\ell})\ell^{\alpha}\Big)+O(\mathbf{N}(M_{\ell}^{2})\ell^{3-2\delta})\,.

Using the fact that

\mathbf{N}\big(\sum\limits_{i=1}^{M_{\ell}}Z_{s^{(\ell)}_{i}}\,|\,\sigma_{\ell},M_{\ell}\big)=0
by
symmetry of the law of
Z_{s^{(\ell)}_{i}}
,
and that the remainder terms are
o(\ell^{\alpha-1})
by (
5.4
), we finally obtain:

\displaystyle w_{\lambda}(x)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{N}\Big(1-\exp\Big(-(\lambda+\frac{\alpha-1}{\Gamma(2-\alpha)}\ell^{-\alpha}J_{\lambda}^{(\ell)}(x))\sigma_{\ell}\Big)\Big)+o(\ell^{\alpha-1})

\displaystyle=\phi_{\ell}^{-1}\Big(\lambda+\frac{\alpha-1}{\Gamma(2-\alpha)}\ell^{-\alpha}J_{\lambda}^{(\ell)}(x)\Big)+o(\ell^{\alpha-1})

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma(2-\alpha)}{\alpha}\ell^{\alpha-1}\Big(\lambda+\frac{\alpha-1}{\Gamma(2-\alpha)}\ell^{-\alpha}J_{\lambda}^{(\ell)}(x)\Big)+o(\ell^{\alpha-1})

and (
5.8
) follows.
∎

5.2
Computation of
\mathbf{N(\sup Z>1)}

We now use Theorem
5.1
in the case
\lambda=0
to deduce that:

Proposition 5.4
.

For
x>0
, we have

w_{0}(x)={\bf N}(\sup Z\geqslant x)=\frac{{\bf N}(\sup Z\geqslant 1)}{x^{2}}=\frac{\alpha(\alpha-1)}{2x^{2}}.

(5.12)

The proof of the above proposition heavily relies on exact computations involving Bessel and hypergeometric functions. We start with a lemma which can be understood as a variation on the well-known absolute continuity relations between Brownian motion and Bessel processes (see
[
125
, Exercise
XI.1.22]
).

Lemma 5.5
.

Let
a<b<x
and
\ell>0
. Then, for every
c>0
we have:

\mathbb{E}_{a\to b}^{(\ell)}\left[\exp\left(-\int_{0}^{\ell}\frac{c\penalty\ {\rm d}u}{(B_{u}+x)^{2}}\right)\right]=\sqrt{\frac{2\pi}{\ell}}\sqrt{(x+a)(x+b)}\exp\Big(-\frac{(x+a)(x+b)}{\ell}\Big)I_{\nu}\Big(\frac{(x+a)(x+b)}{\ell}\Big),

where
\nu=\frac{\sqrt{8c+1}}{2}
and
I_{\nu}
stands for the modified Bessel function of the first kind with index
\nu
:

I_{\nu}(z):=\left(\frac{z}{2}\right)^{\nu}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{(z^{2}/4)^{k}}{k!\,\Gamma(\nu+k+1)},\quad z\in\mathbb{R}.

Proof.

By translation invariance of Brownian motion, it is enough to establish the lemma in the case
0=a<b<x
. Now, note that by monotone convergence, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{0\to b}^{(\ell)}\Big[\exp\big(-c\int_{0}^{\ell}\frac{{\rm d}u}{(B_{u}+x)^{2}}\big)\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathbb{E}_{0\to b}^{(\ell)}\Big[\exp\Big(-c\int_{0}^{\ell-\varepsilon}\frac{{\rm d}u}{(B_{u}+x)^{2}}\Big)\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathbb{E}_{0}^{(\ell)}\Big[\exp\Big(-c\int_{0}^{\ell-\varepsilon}\frac{\,{\rm d}u}{(B_{u}+x)^{2}}\Big)\frac{p_{\varepsilon}(b-B_{\ell-\varepsilon})}{p_{\ell}(b)}\Big]

where
p_{t}(x)=(2\pi t)^{-1/2}\exp(-x^{2}/2t)
stands for the
one-dimensional Gaussian density function. Let us perform the computation of the previous display.
Using again the invariance by translation of Brownian motion and the absolute
continuity relations between Brownian motion and the Bessel processes (see
[
125
, Exercise
XI.1.22]
), we infer that:

\mathbb{E}_{0}^{\ell}\Big[\exp\Big(-c\int_{0}^{\ell-\varepsilon}\frac{\,{\rm d}u}{(B_{u}+x)^{2}}\Big)\frac{p_{\varepsilon}(b-B_{\ell-\varepsilon})}{p_{\ell}(b)}\mathbbm{1}_{T_{-x}>\ell-\varepsilon}\Big]=\mathbb{E}_{x}^{\langle\nu\rangle}\Big[\Big(\frac{x}{B_{t-\varepsilon}}\Big)^{\nu+\frac{1}{2}}\frac{p_{\varepsilon}(b+x-B_{\ell-\varepsilon})}{p_{\ell}(b)}\mathbbm{1}_{T_{0}>\ell-\varepsilon}\Big]

where
\nu=\frac{\sqrt{8c+1}}{2}
is the index of the Bessel process on the right-hand side (equivalently, dimension
d=2\nu+2
) starting from
x>0
, and as usual
T_{z}=\inf\{t\geqslant 0:B_{t}=z\}
. Notice that since
c>0
we have
\nu>1/2
so under
\mathbb{P}^{\langle\nu\rangle}_{x}
the process
B
never touches
0
and the indicator function in the right-hand side of the previous display is superfluous (it is also superfluous in the left-hand side since the integral a.s. diverges on the event when
B
touches
-x
). If we write

p^{\langle\nu\rangle}_{t}(x,y)=\frac{\mathbb{P}_{x}^{\langle\nu\rangle}(B_{t}\in{\rm d}y)}{{\rm d}y},

for the density function of the one-dimensional marginals of the
canonical process under
\mathbb{P}_{x}^{\langle\nu\rangle}
, then we obtain

\mathbb{E}_{0\to 0}^{(\ell)}\Big[\exp\Big(-\int_{0}^{\ell-\varepsilon}\frac{c\,{\rm d}u}{(x+B_{u})^{2}}\Big)\Big]=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}y\,p_{\ell-\varepsilon}^{\langle\nu\rangle}(x,y)\left(\frac{x}{y}\right)^{\nu+\frac{1}{2}}\frac{p_{\varepsilon}(b+x-y)}{p_{\ell}(b)}\,,

and by an easy dominated convergence argument, the latter converges as

\varepsilon\to 0
to

\frac{p_{\ell}^{\langle\nu\rangle}(x,x+b)}{p_{\ell}(b)}\,.

The desired result now follows using the explicit expression of Bessel densities that can be found
in
[
125
, Chapter XI.1 p.446]
.
∎

We can now proceed with the proof of (
5.4
).

Proof of Proposition
5.4
.

For simplicity, we let
c:=c(\alpha)={\bf N}(\sup Z>1)
. We first use Theorem
5.1
with
\lambda=0
together with Proposition
4.1
to deduce that, for every

x>0
, the function
w_{0}(x)=\frac{c}{x^{2}}
satisfies:

\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{{\rm d}t}{t^{1+\alpha}}\mathbb{E}^{(t)}_{0\to 0}\bigg[1-\frac{ct}{x^{2}}-\exp\Big(-\int_{0}^{t}\frac{c\,{\rm d}u}{(x+B_{u})^{2}}\Big)\bigg]=0\,.

(5.13)

We fix
x=1
henceforth. By Lemma
5.5
, we have

\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{{\rm d}t}{t^{1+\alpha}}\Big(1-ct-\sqrt{\frac{2\pi}{t}}\mathrm{e}^{-1/t}I_{\nu}\Big(\frac{1}{t}\Big)\Big)=0,

where
\nu=\frac{\sqrt{8c+1}}{2}
and
I_{\nu}
is the modified Bessel function with index
\nu
. We now perform
the change of variables
s=1/t
to get that:

\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}s\,\mathrm{e}^{-s}s^{\alpha-1/2}\bigg(I_{\nu}(s)-\frac{\mathrm{e}^{s}}{\sqrt{2\pi s}}\Big(1-\frac{c}{s}\Big)\bigg)=0.

(5.14)

It is not obvious at first that the integral on the left hand-side is
well-defined. However, since
\alpha\in(1,2)
and
\nu\geqslant 1/2
, the function
I_{\nu}
is
continuous with
I_{\nu}(0)=0
and the integral is well-defined in the
vicinity of
0
. On the other hand, the asymptotic properties of Bessel
functions are that, when
s\to\infty
,

I_{\nu}(s)=\frac{\mathrm{e}^{s}}{\sqrt{2\pi s}}\Big(1-\frac{4\nu^{2}-1}{8s}+O(\frac{1}{s^{2}})\Big)\,,

and
(4\nu^{2}-1)/8
is precisely
c
. Therefore,
as
s\to\infty
,

\mathrm{e}^{-s}s^{\alpha-1/2}\bigg(I_{\nu}(s)-\frac{\mathrm{e}^{s}}{\sqrt{2\pi s}}\Big(1-\frac{c}{s}\Big)\bigg)=O\Big(\frac{1}{s^{3-\alpha}}\Big)\,,

which is integrable near
\infty
. To evaluate the integral in
(
5.14
), we observe that it is equal to

\lim_{u\downarrow 1}\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}s\,\mathrm{e}^{-us}s^{\alpha-1/2}\bigg(I_{\nu}(s)-\frac{\mathrm{e}^{s}}{\sqrt{2\pi s}}\Big(1-\frac{c}{s}\Big)\bigg)\,,

which comes from a
dominated convergence argument, that is easily justified using the above asymptotic properties of
I_{\nu}
. Now, for every fixed

u>1
, we can split the above integral into

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}s\,\mathrm{e}^{-us}s^{\alpha-1/2}I_{\nu}(s)-\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}s\frac{s^{\alpha-1}\mathrm{e}^{-(u-1)s}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}+c\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}s\frac{s^{\alpha-2}\mathrm{e}^{-(u-1)s}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}

(5.15)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}s\,\mathrm{e}^{-us}s^{\alpha-1/2}I_{\nu}(s)-\frac{\Gamma(\alpha)}{(u-1)^{\alpha}\sqrt{2\pi}}+\frac{c\,\Gamma(\alpha-1)}{(u-1)^{\alpha-1}\sqrt{2\pi}}\,.

On the other hand, the value of the above Laplace-type transform involving

I_{\nu}
is known and equals

\frac{\Gamma(\nu+\alpha+1/2)}{2^{\nu}u^{\nu+\alpha+1/2}\Gamma(\nu+1)}\,\cdot\,{}_{2}F_{1}\bigg(\begin{array}[]{cc}\frac{\nu+\alpha+1/2}{2},\frac{\nu+\alpha+3/2}{2}\\
\nu+1\end{array};\frac{1}{u^{2}}\bigg)

(5.16)

where
{}_{2}F_{1}(a,b;c;z)
is a hypergeometric
function (we drop the indices in the sequel and simply write

F={}_{2}F_{1}
), as defined in
[
7
]
. The problem in letting

u\downarrow 1
in the latter expression is that (for real
parameters
a,b,c
) the analytic function
F(a,b;c;z)
diverges at its
radius of convergence
z=1
whenever
c-a-b\leqslant 0
, as it is the case
here. By
[
7
, Corollary 2.3.3]
we can rewrite (we let

z=1/u^{2}
):

\displaystyle F\bigg(\begin{array}[]{cc}\frac{\nu+\alpha+1/2}{2},\frac{\nu+\alpha+3/2}{2}\\
\nu+1\end{array};z\bigg)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\Gamma(\nu+1)\Gamma(-\alpha)}{\Gamma\Big(\frac{\nu-\alpha+1/2}{2}\Big)\Gamma\Big(\frac{\nu-\alpha+3/2}{2}\Big)}F\bigg(\begin{array}[]{cc}\frac{\nu+\alpha+1/2}{2},\frac{\nu+\alpha+3/2}{2}\\
\alpha+1\end{array};1-z\bigg)

\displaystyle+\frac{\Gamma(\nu+1)\Gamma(\alpha)}{\Gamma\Big(\frac{\nu+\alpha+1/2}{2}\Big)\Gamma\Big(\frac{\nu+\alpha+3/2}{2}\Big)}(1-z)^{-\alpha}F\bigg(\begin{array}[]{cc}\frac{\nu-\alpha+1/2}{2},\frac{\nu-\alpha+3/2}{2}\\
1-\alpha\end{array};1-z\bigg)\,.

Since the function
z\mapsto F(a,b;c;z)
is analytic in
\{|z|<1\}
, and
F(a,b;c;0)=1
,
this form allows to deduce that, as
z\uparrow 1
,

\displaystyle F\bigg(\begin{array}[]{cc}\frac{\nu+\alpha+1/2}{2},\frac{\nu+\alpha+3/2}{2}\\
\nu+1\end{array};z\bigg)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{A}{(1-z)^{\alpha}}+\frac{B}{(1-z)^{\alpha-1}}+\frac{\Gamma(\nu+1)\Gamma(-\alpha)}{\Gamma\Big(\frac{\nu-\alpha+1/2}{2}\Big)\Gamma\Big(\frac{\nu-\alpha+3/2}{2}\Big)}+O((1-z)^{2-\alpha})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{A}{(1-z)^{\alpha}}+\frac{B}{(1-z)^{\alpha-1}}+\frac{2^{\nu-\alpha-1/2}\Gamma(\nu+1)\Gamma(-\alpha)}{\sqrt{\pi}\,\Gamma(\nu-\alpha+1/2)}+O((1-z)^{2-\alpha})\,,

for some real constants
A,B
that can be made explicit. In the second
line, we rewrote the constant term using the Gauss duplication formula
for the Gamma function. Otherwise
said, using (
5.15
) and the following displayed expression, the
integral in (
5.14
) is equal to the limit as
z\uparrow 1
of an
expression of the form

\frac{A^{\prime}}{(1-z)^{\alpha}}+\frac{B^{\prime}}{(1-z)^{\alpha-1}}+\frac{\Gamma(\nu+\alpha+1/2)\Gamma(-\alpha)}{2^{\alpha+1/2}\sqrt{\pi}\,\Gamma(\nu-\alpha+1/2)}+O((1-z)^{2-\alpha})\,,

for some constants
A^{\prime},B^{\prime}
. A (tedious) computation shows that
A^{\prime}=B^{\prime}=0
, but we
can also argue that if this were not the case, then the above
expression would not have a finite limit as
z\uparrow 1
, and this
would be a contradiction. Putting things together, we finally obtain
that

\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}s\,\mathrm{e}^{-s}s^{\alpha-1/2}\bigg(I_{\nu}(s)-\frac{\mathrm{e}^{s}}{\sqrt{2\pi s}}\Big(1-\frac{c}{s}\Big)\bigg)=\frac{\Gamma(\nu+\alpha+1/2)\Gamma(-\alpha)}{2^{\alpha+1/2}\sqrt{\pi}\,\Gamma(\nu-\alpha+1/2)}=0\,.

Since
\alpha\in(1,2)
and
\nu\geqslant 1/2>0
, this can only happen if the denominator explodes, namely, if

\nu-\alpha+1/2\in\{0,-1,-2,\ldots\}
, and the
only possible value is in fact
0
, giving

\nu=\alpha-\frac{1}{2}\qquad\mbox{ and
}\qquad c=\frac{4\nu^{2}-1}{8}=\frac{\alpha(\alpha-1)}{2}\,,

as
wanted.
∎

5.3
Records of
Z
on loops

Our goal now is to use (
5.4
) to study the local minima of
Z
on loops. More precisely, we aim to establish the properties stated at the beginning of the section. In this direction, we start with the ideal model made of the process
(X,Z)
under the law
\mathbf{Q}
as described in Section
3.3
. Next,
we consider the associated excursions of
(X,Z)
above the running infimum
X
. Namely, we let

(a_{i},b_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
be the connected components of
\{t\geqslant 0:\penalty\ X_{t}>I_{t}\}
, and

\ell_{i}:=-I_{a_{i}}\quad;\quad X^{i}_{t}:=X_{(a_{i}+t)\wedge b_{i}}-X_{a_{i}}\quad\text{ and }\quad Z^{i}_{t}:=Z_{(a_{i}+t)\wedge b_{i}}-Z_{a_{i}},\quad t\geqslant 0,

for
i\in\mathbb{N}
. By excursion theory, the point measure

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}:=\sum\limits_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\delta_{\ell_{i},X^{i},Z^{i}},

(5.21)

is a Poisson measure with intensity
\mathbbm{1}_{\ell\geqslant 0}{\rm d}\ell\otimes\mathbf{N}({\rm d}X{\rm d}Z)
, independent of the Brownian motion
\mathrm{b}_{0}
of the “infinite loop”. To keep a picture in mind, the reader can informally think of the looptree coded by
(X^{i},Z^{i})
as glued at position
\ell_{i}
on the “infinite loop” coded by
\mathbb{R}_{+}
. We shall be interested in times
t
which are left minimal record times of
Z
and which happen on the infinite loop attached to time
0
.
More precisely, we consider the set

\mathscr{B}:=\big\{\mathrm{b}_{0}(t):\penalty\ \text{ such that }\mathrm{b}_{0}(\ell_{i})+\inf Z^{i}>\mathrm{b}_{0}(t)\text{ for every }i\in\mathbb{N}\text{ with }0\leqslant\ell_{i}<t\big\},

(5.22)

and we stress that the times
t\geqslant 0
here only parametrize the infinite loop and not the whole process
(X,Z)
, and we refer to Figure
20
for an illustration.

Figure 20:
A simulation of a Brownian motion (in blue) and its running infimum process (in orange). The trace is decorated with red slits which happen at time
t_{i}
and extend over
[\inf Z^{i}+\mathrm{b_{0}}(t_{i}),\mathrm{b}_{0}(t_{i})+\sup Z^{i}]
. We are interested in the set of values
\mathscr{B}
(in green on the left) which correspond to the coordinates of the points
(t,\mathrm{b}_{0}(t))
which are visible from the left, i.e. not blocked by a red slit: a few examples are shown in light green.

Proposition 5.6
.

Under
\mathbf{Q}
, the set
-\mathscr{B}
is distributed as the range of a stable subordinator of index
2-\alpha
.

Proof.

Since we are looking at values which are in particular running minimal values for
\mathrm{b}_{0}
, we can first gather the contribution by excursions of
\mathrm{b}_{0}
above its running infimum. To this end, we consider
(s_{k},t_{k})_{k\geqslant 1}
an indexation of the connected components of the open set
\{t\geqslant 0:\penalty\ \mathrm{b}_{0}(t)>\inf\limits_{[0,t]}\mathrm{b}_{0}\}
. To fix notation, write
\mathrm{b}_{0}^{k}(t):=\mathrm{b}_{0}((s_{k}+t)\wedge t_{k})-\mathrm{b}_{0}(s_{k})
, for
t\geqslant 0
, and introduce the quantity

M_{k}:=\inf\big\{\mathrm{b}_{0}(\ell_{i})+\inf Z^{i}:\penalty\ \ell_{i}\in[s_{k},t_{k}]\big\}-\mathrm{b}_{0}(s_{k}).

The reason to define these random variables is that the set

\mathscr{B}
can be rewritten in the form:

\mathscr{B}=\mathbb{R}_{-}\setminus\bigcup\limits_{k=1}^{\infty}\big(\mathrm{b}_{0}(s_{k})\penalty\ ,\penalty\ \mathrm{b}_{0}(s_{k})+M_{k}\big).

(5.23)

We will obtain the desired result by examining the point measure:

\sum\limits_{k\geqslant 1}\delta_{\mathrm{b}_{0}(s_{k}),M_{k}}.

(5.24)

In this direction remark that, for
z>0
, by properties of Poisson random measures we have:

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}(M_{k}<-z\>|\>\mathrm{b}_{0})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1-\exp\Big(-\int_{s_{k}}^{t_{k}}{\rm d}y\,\mathbf{N}\Big(\inf Z\leqslant-\big(z+\mathrm{b}_{0}(y)-\mathrm{b}_{0}(s_{k})\big)\Big)\Big)

(5.25)

\displaystyle\underset{\mathrm{Cor.\ }\ref{Z<-1}\ \&\ \mathrm{Prop.\ }\ref{sec:stable-map-3}}{=}

\displaystyle 1-\exp\Big(-\int_{0}^{t_{k}-s_{k}}{\rm d}y\frac{\alpha(\alpha-1)}{2(z+\mathrm{b}^{k}_{0}(y))^{2}}\Big).

Recall now that the excursion measure of
\mathrm{b}_{0}
reflected above its infimum is
2\mathbf{n}({\rm d}e)
, where
\mathbf{n}
is the Itô measure of positive excursions of Brownian motion.
It follows by excursion theory that the measure (
5.24
)
is a Poisson point measure with intensity
\mathbbm{1}_{t\geqslant 0}{\rm d}t\mu({\rm d}z),

where
\mu
is the measure with support on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
defined by:

\mu((z,\infty))=\int 2\mathbf{n}({\rm d}e)\Big(1-\exp\Big(-\int_{0}^{\sigma(e)}{\rm d}y\frac{\alpha(\alpha-1)}{2(e_{y}+z)^{2}}\Big)\Big)\,

(5.26)

here
\sigma(e)
stands for the lifetime of the excursion
e
. Let us compute the latter expression. To simplify notation, we write
c(\alpha):=\alpha(\alpha-1)/2
. First, we note that:

\displaystyle 1-\exp\Big(-\int_{0}^{\sigma(e)}\frac{c(\alpha)\,{\rm d}y}{(e_{y}+z)^{2}}\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\sigma(e)}{\rm d}s\,\frac{c(\alpha)}{(e_{s}+z)^{2}}\exp\Big(-\int_{s}^{\sigma(e)}\frac{c(\alpha){\rm d}y}{(e_{y}+z)^{2}}\Big)\,.

Now we use Bismut’s
decomposition of the Brownian excursion
[
125
, Theorem 4.5, Chap. XII, p502]
to obtain:

\displaystyle\mu((z,\infty))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\int\mathbf{n}({\rm d}e)\int_{0}^{\sigma(e)}{\rm d}s\,\frac{c(\alpha)}{(e_{s}+z)^{2}}\exp\Big(-\int_{s}^{\sigma(e)}\frac{c(\alpha){\rm d}y}{(e_{y}+z)^{2}}\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2c(\alpha)\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{{\rm d}a}{(a+z)^{2}}\mathbb{E}_{a}\Big[\exp\Big(-\int_{0}^{T_{0}}\frac{c(\alpha){\rm d}y}{(B_{y}+z)^{2}}\Big)\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2c(\alpha)\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{{\rm d}a}{(a+z)^{2}}\mathbb{E}_{a+z}\Big[\exp\Big(-\int_{0}^{T_{z}}\frac{c(\alpha){\rm d}y}{B_{y}^{2}}\Big)\Big]\,,

where
T_{z}=\inf\{t\geqslant 0:\penalty\ B_{t}=z\}
. The computation of the expectation inside the above integral is standard. Specifically, an application of Itô’s formula
entails that the process

M_{t}:=\left(\frac{a+z}{B_{t\wedge T_{z}}}\right)^{\alpha-1}\exp\left(-\frac{\alpha(\alpha-1)}{2}\int_{0}^{t\wedge T_{z}}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{B_{s}^{2}}\right)

is a local martingale (for the filtration generated by
B
). Since, it is bounded above by
(\frac{a+z}{z})^{\alpha-1}
, we infer from the optional sampling theorem and letting
t\to\infty
that

\mathbb{E}_{a+z}\Big[\exp\Big(-\int_{0}^{T_{z}}\frac{c(\alpha){\rm d}y}{B_{y}^{2}}\Big)\Big]=\left(\frac{z}{a+z}\right)^{\alpha-1}.

We conclude that:

\mu((z,\infty))=\frac{2c(\alpha)}{\alpha}\cdot\frac{1}{z}=\frac{\alpha-1}{z}\,.

Recalling (
5.23
), our proposition is now a direct consequence of Shepp’s covering theorem, as it appears in
[
61
, Corollary 1]
or more explicitly in
[
18
, Theorem 7.2]
, which states that the set (
5.23
) is the range of a stable subordinator of index
2-\alpha
.
∎

Since the range of a
(2-\alpha)
-stable subordinator contains arbitrarily small values, by invariance by translation and an obvious symmetry, the previous result shows that local left (or right) minimal record of
Z
are actually dense within loop times under
\mathbf{Q}
. We complete this picture by showing that there are no two-sided records on loops:

Lemma 5.7
.

\mathbf{Q}
-a.s., for every
\varepsilon>0
we have
\{t>0:\mathrm{b}_{0}(\ell_{i})+\inf Z^{i}\geqslant\mathrm{b}_{0}(t),\forall\ell_{i}\in[t-\varepsilon,t+\varepsilon]\}=\varnothing.

Proof.

First remark that by Brownian motion’s invariance by translation, combined with the fact that
\mathcal{P}
is a Poisson measure with intensity
\mathbbm{1}_{t\geqslant 0}{\rm d}t\otimes\mathbf{N}({\rm d}X{\rm d}Z)
and the density of rational numbers, it suffices to establish that, for every
\varepsilon>0
, the set

\big\{t>0:\mathrm{b}_{0}(\ell_{i})+\inf Z^{i}\geqslant\mathrm{b}_{0}(t),\forall\ell_{i}\in[0,t+\varepsilon]\big\}

(5.27)

is empty
\mathbf{Q}
-a.s. To this end, fix
\varepsilon>0
and, as in the previous proof, write
(s_{k},t_{k})_{k\geqslant 1}
for an indexation of the connected components of the open set
\{t\geqslant 0:\penalty\ \mathrm{b}_{0}(t)>\inf_{[0,t]}\mathrm{b}_{0}\}
. Since the point
0
is regular recurrent for
\mathrm{b}_{0}(t)-\inf_{[0,t]}\mathrm{b}_{0}
,
t\geqslant 0
, we infer that any time in (
5.27
) must be of the form
s_{k}
, for some
k\geqslant 1
. Now remark that a point
s_{k}
, for
k\geqslant 1
, belongs to (
5.27
) if and only if the quantity

M_{k}(\varepsilon):=\inf\big\{\mathrm{b}_{0}(\ell_{i})+\inf Z^{i}:\penalty\ \ell_{i}\in[s_{k},(s_{k}+\varepsilon)\wedge t_{k}]\big\}-\mathrm{b}_{0}(s_{k})

is non negative. Lastly, with the notation of the previous lemma, an application of excursion theory gives:

\mathbf{Q}\Big[\sum_{k\geqslant 1}\mathbbm{1}_{M_{k}(\varepsilon)>0}\Big]=2\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t\penalty\ \int\mathbf{n}({\rm d}e)\exp\big(-\int_{0}^{\sigma(e)\wedge\varepsilon}{\rm d}y\frac{\alpha(\alpha-1)}{2e_{y}^{2}}\big).

The previous display is null since
\int_{0}^{\cdot}{\rm d}y\penalty\ e_{y}^{-2}=\infty
,
\mathbf{n}(\mathrm{d}e)
-a.e. This implies that the set (
5.27
) is empty and concludes the proof.
∎

We now use the ideal model under
\mathbf{Q}
to deduce the analog result under
\mathbf{P}
:

Proposition 5.8

(Records on loops)
.

\mathbf{P}
-almost surely, the following properties hold:

\mathrm{(i)}

the sets
\mathrm{LeftRec}\cap\Pi_{d}^{-1}(\mathrm{Loops})
and
\mathrm{RightRec}\cap\Pi_{d}^{-1}(\mathrm{Loops})
are dense in
\Pi_{d}^{-1}(\mathrm{Loops})
;

\mathrm{(ii)}

\mathrm{LeftRec}\cap\mathrm{RightRec}\cap\Pi_{d}^{-1}(\mathrm{Loops})
is empty;

\mathrm{(iii)}

if
\alpha\in[3/2,2)
then
Z
is injective on
(\mathrm{LeftRec}\cup\mathrm{RightRec})\cap\Pi_{d}^{-1}(\mathrm{Loops})
, whereas if
\alpha<3/2
, one can find times
0<t<t^{\prime}<1
belonging to
\Pi_{d}^{-1}(\mathrm{Loops})
which are not identified in the looptree, i.e.
d(t,t^{\prime})>0
, but for which

Z_{t}=Z_{t^{\prime}}=\inf_{[t,t^{\prime}]}Z.

Proof.

As usual, we are going to establish the statement of the proposition under the excursion measure
\mathbf{N}
, the desired result under
\mathbf{P}
then follows directly by scaling. Let
\varepsilon>0
and
T
a
(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}
–stopping time taking values in
\{s\geqslant 0:\penalty\ \Delta_{s}\geqslant\varepsilon\}
– if this set is empty we take
T=\infty
by convention. Under
\mathbf{N}(\cdot|T<\infty,\Delta_{T})
, recall the definition of
\mathcal{N}^{[T]}:=\sum\limits_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\delta_{X_{u_{i}},X^{i},Z^{i}}

given in (
3.16
), and set:

\mathcal{P}^{[T]}:=\mathop{\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N},\penalty\ X_{u_{i}}>X_{T}-\Delta_{T}}}\delta_{X_{T}-X_{u_{i}},X^{i},Z^{i}}

and
B_{T}(t):=Z_{\mathrm{f}_{T}(t/\Delta_{T})}-Z_{\rm{f}_{T}(0)},
for
t\in[0,\Delta_{T}]
. We can now apply the Markov property, as stated in Corollary
3.6
, and Proposition
3.3
, to deduce that under
\mathbf{N}(\cdot|T<\infty,\Delta_{T})
, the measure
\mathcal{P}^{[T]}
is a Poisson point measure with intensity
\mathbbm{1}_{[0,\Delta_{T}]}(t){\rm d}t\mathbf{N}({\rm d}X{\rm d}Z)
, and that the process
B_{T}
haw law
\mathbb{P}_{0\to 0}^{(\Delta_{T})}(\mathrm{d}B)
. Moreover,
\mathcal{P}^{[T]}
and
B_{T}
are independent since we have conditioned on
\Delta_{T}
. Now remark that the set

\Big\{Z_{\rm{f}_{T}(r)}:\penalty\ r\in(0,1/2]\text{ such that }Z_{\rm{f}_{T}(r)}=\inf_{s\in[\mathrm{f}_{T}(0),\mathrm{f}_{T}(r)]}Z_{s}\Big\}

is exactly

Z_{\rm{f}_{T}(0)}+\Big\{B_{T}(t):\penalty\ t\in(0,\Delta_{T}/2]\text{ such that }B_{T}(\ell_{i})+\inf Z^{i}>B_{T}(t)\text{ for every }i\in\mathbb{N}\text{ with }\ell_{i}<t\Big\},

where, by analogy with the notation under
\mathbf{Q}
, we write
\ell_{i}:=X_{T}-X_{u_{i}}
. By absolute continuity of the law of the Brownian bridge over
[0,\Delta_{T}/2]
with respect to that of Brownian motion, and the fact that the range of the
2-\alpha
stable subordinator contains arbitrarily small values, we deduce from Proposition
5.6
that there exist left-local minima of
Z
belonging to
\mathrm{f}_{T}([0,1])
that are arbitrarily close to
\mathrm{f}_{T}(0)
. By translation invariance of Brownian motion and Brownian bridge, the argument extends to any point
\mathrm{f}_{T}(r)
, for fixed
r\in[0,1]
and proves that
\mathrm{LeftRec}
is dense within
\mathrm{f}_{T}([0,1])
. The same absolute continuity argument together with Lemma
5.7
shows that there are no two-sided records of
Z
in
\mathrm{f}_{T}([0,1])
. Since
\varepsilon
can be taken as close to
0
as wanted, this gives points (i) for
\mathrm{LeftRec}
and (ii). But point (i) for
\mathrm{RightRec}
follows directly from the analog result for
\mathrm{LeftRec}
and the invariance by time-reversal (
3.13
).

Let us move on to the third point. In this direction, under
\mathbf{N}(\cdot|T<\infty,\Delta_{T})
, remark that

\Big\{z:\penalty\ \exists(r,r^{\prime})\in[0,1/4)\times(1/4,1/2]\text{ such that }z=Z_{\rm{f}_{T}(r)}=Z_{\rm{f}_{T}(r^{\prime})}=\inf\limits_{[\rm{f}_{T}(r),\rm{f}_{T}(r^{\prime})]}Z\Big\}

can be written as the intersection of:

\Big\{B_{T}(t):\penalty\ t\in[0,\Delta_{T}/4)\text{ such that }B_{T}(\ell_{i})+\inf Z^{i}>B_{T}(t)\text{ for every }i\in\mathbb{N}\text{ with }t<\ell_{i}\leqslant\Delta_{T}/4\Big\}

and

\Big\{B_{T}(t):\penalty\ t\in(\Delta_{T}/4,\Delta_{T}/2]\text{ such that }B_{T}(\ell_{i})+\inf Z^{i}>B_{T}(t)\text{ for every }i\in\mathbb{N}\text{ with }\Delta_{T}/4<\ell_{i}<t\Big\},

shifted again by
Z_{\rm{f}_{T}(0)}
. Since, under
\mathbf{Q}
and by Proposition
5.6
combined with classical results from regenerative set theory (see
[
72
, Example 1]
), the intersection of two independent copies of
-\mathscr{B}
is the range of a
(3-2\alpha)
-stable subordinator when
\alpha<3/2
and empty otherwise, we infer from the translation invariance of Brownian motion that the intersection is non-empty if and only if
\alpha<3/2
. Here, we again use the fact that the range of a stable subordinator contains arbitrarily small values. Since this holds for every
\varepsilon>0
, we deduce statement (iii) when
\alpha\in(1,3/2)
. For the remainder of the proof, we assume that
\alpha\in[3/2,2)
. As the argument parallels the previous cases, we omit some details. First, note that by re-rooting invariance (
3.12
), it suffices to show that for every set of rational numbers
p_{1},p_{2},p_{1}^{\prime},p_{2}^{\prime}
in
(0,1)
with
p_{1}<p_{2}
and
p_{1}^{\prime}<p_{2}^{\prime}
, and jumping times
t,t^{\prime}
of
X
such that
\mathrm{f}_{t}(p_{2})<\mathrm{f}_{t^{\prime}}(p_{1}^{\prime})
, the process
Z
does not take the same value on

\mathrm{f}_{t}((p_{1},p_{2}))\cap(\mathrm{LeftRec}\cup\mathrm{RightRec})\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathrm{f}_{t^{\prime}}((p_{1}^{\prime},p_{2}^{\prime}))\cap(\mathrm{LeftRec}\cup\mathrm{RightRec}).

To this end, consider a second
(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}
-stopping time
T^{\prime}
taking values in the set
\{s\geqslant 0:\Delta_{s}\geqslant\varepsilon\}
such that either
T^{\prime}=T
(to handle the case
t=t^{\prime}
) or
T^{\prime}
is measurable with respect to
X_{t+\mathrm{f}_{T}(p_{2})}-X_{\mathrm{f}_{T}(p_{2})}
for
t\geqslant 0
(to apply the Markov property and treat the case
t\neq t^{\prime}
). Using the above arguments, we infer that the set

\big\{Z_{u}:\ u\in\mathrm{f}_{T}((p_{1},p_{2}))\cap\mathcal{R}\big\}\cap\big\{Z_{u}:\ u\in\mathrm{f}_{T^{\prime}}((p_{1}^{\prime},p_{2}^{\prime}))\cap\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\big\},

where
\mathcal{R},\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\in\{\mathrm{LeftRec},\mathrm{RightRec}\}
, has an absolutely continuous law with respect to that of two (pieces of) ranges of independent
(2-\alpha)
-stable subordinators (excluding the origin), each shifted by a random variable independent of the ranges. Hence, since
2\cdot(2-\alpha)\leqslant 1
when
\alpha\in[3/2,2)
, this intersection is empty almost surely by
[
72
, Example 1]
. Point (iii) for
\alpha\in[3/2,2)
now follows letting
\varepsilon\to 0
.
∎

6
Neighborhood of the point with minimal label along the spine

The goal of this section is to derive a technical estimate for the process
(X,Z)
, which will later be translated into a geometric estimate for typical points on geodesics in the scaling limits of random planar maps. The results of this section will be used exclusively in Section
12.4
, and the remainder of this work can be read independently.

In order to state the main result of this section, let us introduce some notation. In this direction, recall the definition of the height process
H
and of
\xi_{t}
given in (
2.14
) and extended by scaling in Section
3.3
, in particular recall from Corollary
4.6
that the process
s\mapsto Z_{\xi_{t}(s)}
encodes the labels along the pinch points of
\mathrm{Branch}(0,t)
(a closure is required to include the labels at the origin of loops).
Introduce the measure
\mathbf{N}^{\bullet}
defined by:

\mathbf{N}^{\bullet}\big(F\big(X,Z,t_{\bullet}\big)\big)=\mathbf{N}\Big(\int_{0}^{\sigma}{\rm d}t\penalty\ F\big(X,Z,t\big)\Big),

(6.1)

which can be thought of as the measure
\mathbf{N}
together with a uniform distinguished time
t_{\bullet}
.
In this section we shall work under
\mathbf{N}^{\bullet}
. We also introduce
Y^{\bullet}_{s}:=Z_{\xi_{t_{\bullet}}(s)}
,
s\in[0,H_{t_{\bullet}}]
, the process of labels along the branch connecting
0
and
t_{\bullet}
. Recall from (
4.16
) that, under
\mathbf{N}^{\bullet}
, we have the following properties:

—

The “distribution” of
H_{t_{\bullet}}
is
\mathbbm{1}_{h\geqslant 0}{\rm d}h
;

—

Conditionally on
H_{t_{\bullet}}=h
, the process
Y^{\bullet}
is a
2(\alpha-1)
- symmetric stable Lévy process stopped at time
h
.

By standard properties of stable processes (see Lemma
4.7
), there exists a.s. a unique time
\varpi\in(0,H_{t_{\bullet}})
such that
Y^{\bullet}_{\varpi}=\min Y^{\bullet}
and
\varpi
is not a jumping time of
Y^{\bullet}
. By the classification of points in the looptree (Section
2.1
), we infer that
{\varpi}_{1}=\xi_{t_{\bullet}}(\varpi)
is a pinch point time and let us consider
{\varpi}_{2}
the unique element strictly larger than
{\varpi}_{1}
such that
d({\varpi}_{1},{\varpi}_{2})=0
. Now, let us introduce the event that we aim to study in this section, see also Figure
21
for an illustration. For
\varepsilon>0
, we say that the point
\mathfrak{X}=\Pi_{d}(\varpi_{1})=\Pi_{d}(\varpi_{2})
is
\varepsilon
-trapped
if there exist
0<s_{1}<{\varpi}_{1}<s_{2}<t_{\bullet}<s_{3}<{\varpi}_{2}<s_{4}<\sigma

such that:

Figure 21:
Illustration of an
\varepsilon
-trapped point
\mathfrak{X}
(in dark red on the figure). The spine between
0
and
t_{\bullet}
is made of the black loops. When following the looptree from the point
\mathfrak{X}
in one of the four directions, one encounters a (one-sided) minimal record time of
Z
corresponding to a one-side minima below
Z_{\varpi_{1}}-\varepsilon
on two loops of the spine.

There exist
0<s_{1}<{\varpi}_{1}<s_{2}<t_{\bullet}<s_{3}<{\varpi}_{2}<s_{4}<\sigma

such that:

—

\max(Z_{s_{1}},Z_{s_{2}},Z_{s_{3}},Z_{s_{4}})<Z_{{\varpi}_{1}}-\varepsilon.

—

Z_{s_{1}}<Z_{t}
for every
t\in(s_{1},{\varpi}_{1})
,

Z_{s_{2}}<Z_{t}
for every
t\in({\varpi}_{1},s_{2})
,

Z_{s_{3}}<Z_{t}
for every
t\in(s_{3},{\varpi}_{2})

Z_{s_{4}}<Z_{t}
for every
t\in({\varpi}_{2},s_{4})
.

—

The images of
s_{1},s_{4}
and
s_{2},s_{3}
belong to a same loop of the spine, i.e. there exists
r_{1,4}\prec{\varpi}_{1}
verifying that

X_{s_{1}}<X_{t}\ \ \mbox{ for every }t\in(r_{1,4},s_{1}),\quad\text{ and }\quad X_{s_{4}}<X_{t}\ \ \mbox{ for every }t\in(r_{1,4},s_{4}),

and there exists
{\varpi}_{1}\prec r_{2,3}\prec t_{\bullet}
verifying that

X_{s_{2}}<X_{t}\ \ \mbox{ for every }t\in(r_{2,3},s_{2}),\quad\text{ and }\quad X_{s_{3}}<X_{t}\ \ \mbox{ for every }t\in(r_{2,3},s_{3}).

This section is devoted to the proof of the following uniform bound:

Theorem 6.1

(Polynomial tail for not being trapped)
.

There exists
C,c>0
, such that:

\mathbf{N}^{\bullet}\Big(\mathfrak{X}\textnormal{ is }\varepsilon-\textnormal{trapped}\penalty\ \Big|\penalty\ H_{t_{\bullet}}=h\Big)\geqslant 1-C\cdot\Big(\frac{\varepsilon}{h^{2(\alpha-1)}}\Big)^{c},\quad\text{ for }h,\varepsilon>0.

We make a couple of remarks. First, the conditional version of
\mathbf{N}^{\bullet}
with respect to
H_{t_{\bullet}}
is well-defined by scaling and disintegration. Second, again by scaling, it suffices to establish the estimate for
h=1
(or
\varepsilon=1
), and third, although our application will require the existence of the four times
s_{1},s_{2},s_{3},s_{4}
, it is sufficient by Lemma
3.4
to prove the analogous estimate for the one-sided event where we only require the existence of the times
s_{2},s_{3}
in the definition above, in which case we say that
\mathfrak{X}
is
\varepsilon
-trapped from the right
. Before embarking on the proof, let us sketch the underlying idea: we already proved in Proposition
5.8
that one-sided minimal record times of the process
Z
may happen on loops so that the reader should be convinced that, at least, the event in the above theorem has a positive probability to happen. Actually for each scale
\varepsilon
, the point
\mathfrak{X}
has a probability bounded away from
0
to be
\varepsilon
-trapped at this scale, i.e., with times
s_{1},s_{2},s_{3},s_{4}
satisfying
Z_{\varpi_{1}}-Z_{s_{i}}\in[\varepsilon,2\varepsilon]
. If scales were independent, we would naturally get that the probability that
\mathfrak{X}
is not trapped at all scales between
\varepsilon
and
1
should decay at least as
\varepsilon^{c}
for some
c>0
, which is the statement of the theorem. The rest of this section will implement this heuristic rigorously since the behaviors at different scales are not independent.

We divide the proof of Theorem
6.1
in three parts. In Section
6.1
, we study the law of the spine towards
t_{\bullet}
, that is informally the labeled-loops containing points of
\Pi_{d}(\mathrm{Branch}(0,t_{\bullet}))
, and of the labeled looptrees that are attached on the left and right sides of it, this will lead to a spinal decomposition – see
[
100
]
for similar results in Brownian geometry and
[
127
, Section 6]
for an analog decomposition in the setting of Markov processes indexed by Lévy trees. In Section
6.2
, we compare the distribution of the spine with an ideal model in which the analog of Theorem
6.1
is easier to establish. Finally in Section
6.3
, we prove the technical lemmas used along the way.

6.1
Spinal decomposition

In this section we work under
\mathbf{N}^{\bullet}
. Our first goal is to evaluate the probability that
\mathfrak{X}
is
\varepsilon
-trapped on the right
after conditioning on the process

(Y^{\bullet}_{s}:=Z_{\xi_{t_{\bullet}}(s)},s\in[0,H_{t_{\bullet}}])
of the labels along the spine. Informally, we first describe the law of the spine (i.e. of the loops and their labels connecting
0
and
t_{\bullet}
in the looptree) conditionally on
Y^{\bullet}
and then argue that the labeled looptrees attached to this spine are, after subtracting the label of their root, independent and distributed according to the standard excursion measure
\mathbf{N}
. To proceed formally, we need some notation which we illustrate in Figure
22
.

By Lemma
2.5
, if
\Delta Y^{\bullet}_{r}\neq 0
then
\xi_{t_{\bullet}}(r-)
is a jumping time for
X
and this defines a one-to-one correspondence between
\{r\geqslant 0:\penalty\ \Delta Y^{\bullet}_{r}\neq 0\}
and

\{r\in[0,\sigma]:\penalty\ r\preccurlyeq t_{\bullet}\text{ and }\Delta X_{r}>0\}
; here we use that
\Delta X_{t_{\bullet}}=0
. Now we write:

\check{\mathfrak{S}}_{r}:=X_{\xi_{t_{\bullet}}(r-)}-X_{\xi_{t_{\bullet}}(r)}\text{ and }\mathfrak{S}_{r}:=X_{\xi_{t_{\bullet}}(r)}-X_{\xi_{t_{\bullet}}(r-)-},

and remark that
\Delta X_{\xi_{t_{\bullet}}(r-)}=\check{\mathfrak{S}}_{r}+\mathfrak{S}_{r}
. We also introduce the associated labels along the corresponding loop:

\check{B}_{s}^{(r)}:=Z_{\mathrm{f}_{\xi_{t_{\bullet}}(r-)}(\frac{s}{\check{\mathfrak{S}}_{r}+\mathfrak{S}_{r}})},\quad s\in[0,\check{\mathfrak{S}}_{r}],\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \text{ and }\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ B_{s}^{(r)}:=Z_{\mathrm{f}_{\xi_{t_{\bullet}}(r-)}(\frac{\check{\mathfrak{S}}_{r}+\mathfrak{S}_{r}-s}{\check{\mathfrak{S}}_{r}+\mathfrak{S}_{r}})},\quad s\in[0,\mathfrak{S}_{r}].

By convention, we also let
\check{B}_{s}^{(r)}
(resp.
B_{s}^{(r)}
) equal to
Z_{\mathrm{f}_{\xi_{t_{\bullet}}(r-)}(\frac{\check{\mathfrak{S}}_{r}}{\check{\mathfrak{S}}_{r}+\mathfrak{S}_{r}})}
, for
s\geqslant\check{\mathfrak{S}}_{r}
(resp.
s\geqslant\mathfrak{S}_{r}
). These describe what is happening on the loops connecting
0
and
t_{\bullet}
. We also need to keep track of the labeled looptrees glued along this spine. In order to encapsulate this information, for every
r
with
\Delta Y^{\bullet}_{r}\neq 0
, consider
(u_{i},v_{i})_{i\geqslant 1}
the connected components of the open set

\Big\{t\in[\mathrm{f}_{\xi_{t_{\bullet}}(r-)}(0),\mathrm{f}_{\xi_{t_{\bullet}}(r-)}(1)]:\>X_{t}>I_{\xi_{t_{\bullet}}(r-),t}\Big\}

and
introduce the excursion processes

X_{s}^{i}:=X_{(u_{i}+s)\wedge v_{i}}-X_{u_{i}}\>\>\>\>\>\text{ and }\>\>\>\>\>Z_{s}^{i}:=Z_{(u_{i}+s)\wedge v_{i}}-Z_{u_{i}},\qquad s\geqslant 0.

Finally, we consider the point measures

\check{\mathcal{P}}_{r}:=\sum\limits_{i\geqslant 1:\penalty\ u_{i}<\xi_{t_{\bullet}}(r)}\delta_{X_{\xi_{t_{\bullet}}(r-)}-X_{u_{i}},X^{i},Z^{i}}\>\>\>\>\text{ and }\>\>\>\>\mathcal{P}_{r}:=\sum\limits_{i\geqslant 1:\penalty\ u_{i}>\xi_{t_{\bullet}}(r)}\delta_{X_{u_{i}}-X_{\xi_{t_{\bullet}}(r-)-},X^{i},Z^{i}}.

(6.2)

We stress that the excursion
(X^{i},Z^{i})
associated with the interval
[u_{i},v_{i}]
containing
t_{\bullet}
is not taken into account in
\check{\mathcal{P}}_{r}
nor in
\mathcal{P}_{r}
. For definiteness, if
r
is not a jumping time we simply take
\check{\mathcal{P}}_{r}=\mathcal{P}_{r}=0
. Recall from Section
5.1
the notation
\mathbb{P}^{(r)}_{a\to b}
for the law of a Brownian bridge going from
a
to
b
with lifetime
r
.

Figure 22:
Illustration of Proposition
6.2
: construction of the spine (and the dangling looptrees) from the process
Y^{\bullet}
.

Proposition 6.2

(Spinal decomposition)
.

Under
\mathbf{N}^{\bullet}
and conditionally on
r\mapsto Y_{r}^{\bullet}
, the collection of random variables:

\Big(\check{\mathfrak{S}}_{r},\mathfrak{S}_{r},\check{B}^{(r)},B^{(r)},\check{\mathcal{P}}_{r},\mathcal{P}_{r}\Big),\quad\text{ for every }r\text{ with }\Delta Y_{r}^{\bullet}\neq 0,

are independent and their conditional distribution can be determined as follows. First, the law of
\big(\check{\mathfrak{S}}_{r},\mathfrak{S}_{r}\big)
is:

\mathbbm{1}_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}>0}C(\alpha)\cdot|\Delta Y_{r}^{\bullet}|^{2\alpha-1}\frac{(\ell_{1}+\ell_{2})^{-\alpha-\frac{1}{2}}}{\sqrt{2\pi\ell_{1}\ell_{2}}}\exp\left(-(\Delta Y_{r}^{\bullet})^{2}\frac{\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}}{2\ell_{1}\ell_{2}}\right){\rm d}\ell_{1}{\rm d}\ell_{2},

where
C(\alpha):=2^{\alpha-1}(2\alpha-1)/\Gamma(\alpha)
is a normalization constant. Then conditionally on the family
\big((Y^{\bullet}_{r},\check{\mathfrak{S}}_{r},\mathfrak{S}_{r}):\penalty\ r\geqslant 0\big)
, all the random variables
\big((\check{B}^{(r)},B^{(r)},\check{\mathcal{P}}_{r},\mathcal{P}_{r}):\penalty\ r\geqslant 0\big)
are independent and for every
r
jumping time of
Y^{\bullet}
we have:

\bullet
The law of
\big(\check{B}^{(r)},B^{(r)}\big)
is
\mathbb{P}_{Y^{\bullet}_{r-}\to Y^{\bullet}_{r}}^{(\check{\mathfrak{S}}_{r})}\otimes\mathbb{P}_{Y^{\bullet}_{r-}\to Y^{\bullet}_{r}}^{(\mathfrak{S}_{r})}\big({\rm d}\check{B},{\rm d}B\big);

\bullet
The measures
\big(\check{\mathcal{P}}_{r},\mathcal{P}_{r}\big)
are two independent Poisson point measures, also independent of
(\check{B}_{r},B_{r})
, with respective intensities:

\mathbbm{1}_{[0,\check{\mathfrak{S}}_{r}]}(t){\rm d}t\mathbf{N}({\rm d}X,{\rm d}Z)\text{ and }\mathbbm{1}_{[0,\mathfrak{S}_{r}]}(t){\rm d}t\mathbf{N}({\rm d}X,{\rm d}Z).

Proof.

We start by introducing the measure:

\sum\limits_{r\geqslant 0,\penalty\ \Delta Y^{\bullet}_{r}\neq 0}\delta_{r;\penalty\ \check{\mathfrak{S}}_{r};\penalty\ \mathfrak{S}_{r};\penalty\ \Delta Y^{\bullet}_{r}}.

(6.3)

In order to describe the law of (
6.3
), we let
\mathfrak{M}
be the distribution of a Poisson point measure, say
\mathscr{M}({\rm d}r,{\rm d}\ell_{1},{\rm d}\ell_{2},{\rm d}y)
, with intensity:

\mathbbm{1}_{r,\ell_{1},\ell_{2}>0}{\rm d}r\,{\rm d}\ell_{1}\,{\rm d}\ell_{2}\,{\rm d}y\frac{1}{\Gamma(-\alpha)}\frac{(\ell_{1}+\ell_{2})^{-\alpha-\frac{1}{2}}}{\sqrt{2\pi\ell_{1}\ell_{2}}}\exp(-y^{2}\frac{\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}}{2\ell_{1}\ell_{2}}),

and for every
a>0
, we write
\mathfrak{M}_{a}
for the distribution of the restriction

\mathscr{M}_{a}({\rm d}r,{\rm d}\ell_{1},{\rm d}\ell_{2},{\rm d}y):=\mathbbm{1}_{[0,a]}(r)\mathscr{M}({\rm d}r,{\rm d}\ell_{1},{\rm d}\ell_{2},{\rm d}y).

Next, we notice that by Proposition
3.3
, conditionally on the point measure:

\sum\limits_{r\geqslant 0,\penalty\ \Delta Y^{\bullet}_{r}\neq 0}\delta_{r;\penalty\ \check{\mathfrak{S}}_{r};\penalty\ \mathfrak{S}_{r}}

the random variables
(\Delta Y^{\bullet}_{r}:\penalty\ s\geqslant 0\text{ with }\penalty\ \Delta Y^{\bullet}_{s}\neq 0)
are independent and the distribution of
\Delta Y^{\bullet}_{r}
is a centered Gaussian random variable with variance
\check{\mathfrak{S}}_{r}\mathfrak{S}_{r}/(\check{\mathfrak{S}}_{r}+\mathfrak{S}_{r})
. Furthermore, the point measure
\sum\limits_{r\geqslant 0,\penalty\ \Delta Y^{\bullet}_{r}\neq 0}\delta_{r;\penalty\ \check{\mathfrak{S}}_{r};\penalty\ \mathfrak{S}_{r}}
has already been studied in the context of Lévy trees and is distributed as

\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}a\int_{y\in\mathbb{R}}\mathscr{M}_{a}({\rm d}r,{\rm d}\ell_{1},{\rm d}\ell_{2},{\rm d}y),

we refer to
[
59
, Proposition 3.1.3]
for a proof. Putting all together, we have obtained that the distribution of (
6.3
) under
\mathbf{N}^{\bullet}
is precisely
\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}a\penalty\ \mathfrak{M}_{a}
. Consequently, if we condition on
(r,\Delta Y^{\bullet}_{r})_{r\geqslant 0}
, the random variables
(\check{\mathfrak{S}}_{r},\mathfrak{S}_{r})_{r\geqslant 0}
become independent with common distribution:

\mathbbm{1}_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}>0}C(\alpha)\cdot|\Delta Y_{r}^{\bullet}|^{2\alpha-1}\frac{(\ell_{1}+\ell_{2})^{-\alpha-\frac{1}{2}}}{\sqrt{2\pi\ell_{1}\ell_{2}}}\exp(-(\Delta Y_{r}^{\bullet})^{2}\frac{\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}}{2\ell_{1}\ell_{2}}){\rm d}\ell_{1}{\rm d}\ell_{2},

where:

C(\alpha)^{-1}:=\int_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}>0}{\rm d}\ell_{1}{\rm d}\ell_{2}\frac{(\ell_{1}+\ell_{2})^{-\alpha-\frac{1}{2}}}{\sqrt{2\pi\ell_{1}\ell_{2}}}\exp(-\frac{\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}}{2\ell_{1}\ell_{2}}).

A direct computation then gives the explicit expression
C(\alpha):=2^{\alpha-1}(2\alpha-1)/\Gamma(\alpha)
.
Moreover, since
Y^{\bullet}
is a Lévy process without Brownian part, we can recover the entire process
Y^{\bullet}
from the structure of its jumps
(r,\Delta Y^{\bullet}_{r})_{r\geqslant 0}
. This gives us the first statement of the proposition. Let us now explain how to obtain the second part. First we remark that the collection
(\mathcal{P}_{r}:\penalty\ r\geqslant 0)
can be recovered directly from the sequence
(\mathfrak{S}_{r}:\penalty\ r\geqslant 0)
and the point measure
\mathcal{N}^{[t_{\bullet}]}
defined in (
3.16
). Now we can apply the Markov property, as stated in Corollary
3.6
, to see that conditionally on
X_{t_{\bullet}}
, the measure
\mathcal{N}^{[t_{\bullet}]}
is a Poisson point measure with intensity
\mathbbm{1}_{[0,X_{t_{\bullet}}]}(p)\mathrm{d}p\>\mathbf{N}(\mathrm{d}X\mathrm{d}Z)
independent of
\big((Y^{\bullet}_{r},\check{\mathfrak{S}}_{r},\mathfrak{S}_{r},\check{B}^{(r)},B^{(r)},\check{\mathcal{P}}_{r}):\penalty\ r\geqslant 0\big)
. This implies the desired result for
(\mathcal{P}_{r}:\penalty\ r\geqslant 0)
, since by property (
A_{3}
) above Proposition
2.1
we have
X_{t_{\bullet}}=\sum_{r\geqslant 0}\mathfrak{S}_{r}
. Furthermore, by the definition of
\mathbf{N}^{\bullet}
given in (
6.1
) and the re-rooting property (
3.12
), we obtain the same property for
(\check{\mathcal{P}}_{r}:\penalty\ r\geqslant 0)
– replacing
\mathfrak{S}_{r}
by
\check{\mathfrak{S}}_{r}
. Therefore to conclude it remains to show that, conditionally on
\big((Y^{\bullet}_{r},\check{\mathfrak{S}}_{r},\mathfrak{S}_{r}):\penalty\ r\geqslant 0\big)
,
the processes
\big((\check{B}^{(r)},B^{{(r)}}):\>r\geqslant 0\big)
are independent and the distribution of
\big(\check{B}^{(r)},B^{(r)}\big)
is

\mathbb{P}_{Y^{\bullet}_{r-}\to Y^{\bullet}_{r}}^{(\check{\mathfrak{S}}_{r})}\otimes\mathbb{P}_{Y^{\bullet}_{r-}\to Y^{\bullet}_{r}}^{(\mathfrak{S}_{r})}\big({\rm d}\check{B},{\rm d}B\big),

but this is a direct consequence of Proposition
3.3
.
∎

The idea to check if
\mathfrak{X}=\Pi_{d}(\varpi_{1})=\Pi_{d}(\varpi_{2})
is
\varepsilon
-trapped from the right is to explore the spine from
\mathfrak{X}
to
\Pi_{d}(t_{\bullet})
and for each loop encountered along the way, to check whether we can find two times
s_{2},s_{3}
on that loop satisfying the desired condition, see also Figure
21
for an illustration. In particular, if those times exist, their labels must be smaller than the labels encountered on all the looptrees attached on the respective sides of the loops discovered so far. As in Section
5.3
this is conveniently encoded for each jump time
r\geqslant\varpi
of
Y^{\bullet}
by recording the minimal label attained by the looptrees attached on that loop. In order to formalize this, let us rewrite the point measures of (
6.2
) in the form
\mathcal{P}_{r}=:\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{r}}\delta_{t_{i},X^{i},Z^{i}}
, and
\check{\mathcal{P}}_{r}=:\sum_{i\in\check{\mathcal{I}}_{r}}\delta_{\check{t}_{i},\check{X}^{i},\check{Z}^{i}}
, then we set

\mathscr{R}_{r}:=\inf\big\{B_{t_{i}}^{(r)}+\inf Z^{i}:\penalty\ i\in\mathcal{I}_{r}\big\}\wedge\inf\big\{\check{B}_{\check{t}_{i}}^{(r)}+\inf\check{Z}^{i}:\penalty\ i\in\check{\mathcal{I}}_{r}\big\}-Y_{r-}^{\bullet},

(6.4)

which is the (shifted) minimal label attained by a looptree attached to that loop, as well as

\displaystyle\mathscr{I}_{r}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\max\Big\{\inf\big\{B_{t}^{(r)}-Y^{\bullet}_{r-}:\penalty\ \text{ s.t. }B_{t_{i}}^{(r)}+\inf Z^{i}>B_{t}^{(r)},\ \forall i\in\mathcal{I}_{r}\text{ with }0\leqslant t_{i}<t\big\},

(6.5)

\displaystyle\qquad\inf\big\{\check{B}_{t}^{(r)}-Y^{\bullet}_{r-}:\penalty\ \text{ s.t. }\check{B}_{\check{t}_{i}}^{(r)}+\inf\check{Z}^{i}>\check{B}_{t}^{(r)},\ \forall i\in\check{\mathcal{I}}_{r}\text{ with }0\leqslant\check{t}_{i}<t\big\}\Big\}

which corresponds to the smallest possible minimal record on each side of the loop, only taking into account the looptrees attached to that loop. By convention, if
\Delta Y^{\bullet}_{r}=0
, we set
(\mathscr{R}_{r},\mathscr{I}_{r}):=(0,0)
.

Figure 23:
Illustration of the definition of the random variables
\mathscr{R}_{r}
and
\mathscr{I}_{r}
. Here, only one side of the loops is displayed for visibility. The label on one side of the spine is obtained by concatenating the shifted process
B^{(r)}
(in different colors above). The red slits correspond to the range of
Z
-values reached by the looptrees attached to the spine as in Figure
20
. The maximal negative displacements of those looptrees over a single loop is recorded in the random variables
\mathscr{R}_{\cdot}
and are displayed by thicker green slits. If (
6.6
) holds, there is a point a the loop corresponding to a new minimal record of
Z
(visible from the left, not blocked by the green slits) with label below
Y^{\bullet}_{\varpi}-\varepsilon
. A corresponding point exists on the otherside of the spine, thus trapping
\mathfrak{X}
at level
\varepsilon
.

The key observation, being that if
r>\varpi
is a jump time of
Y^{\bullet}
satisfying

\displaystyle Y_{r_{-}}^{\bullet}+\mathscr{I}_{r}\leqslant\inf_{\varpi\leqslant s<r}\big(Y_{s_{-}}^{\bullet}+\mathscr{R}_{s}\big)\leqslant Y^{\bullet}_{\varpi}-\varepsilon,

(6.6)

then the loop corresponding to time
r
supports two times
s_{2},s_{3}
trapping the point
\mathfrak{X}
at level
\varepsilon
. We will thus call such time
r
an
\varepsilon
-trapping time
. Notice that this is not an equivalence, since we do not split the contribution of each side in the definition of
\mathscr{R}_{r}
. See Figures
23
and
24
for an illustration of these definitions.

Figure 24:
Companion illustration to Figure
23
where the contributions of each loop have been regrouped: The process
Y^{\bullet}
is in pink, the green sticks attached to each of its jumps correspond to the minimal label attained by looptrees attached to the corresponding loop of the spine, and the orange points correspond to the values
Y_{r-}^{\bullet}+\mathscr{I}_{r}
. If such an orange point is not shadowed by the previous green sticks (as illustrated by the horizontal orange dotted line above), then one can trap the point
\mathfrak{X}
using that loop.

Thanks to Proposition
6.2
, we see that, conditionally on
Y^{\bullet}
the random variables
(\mathscr{R}_{r},\mathscr{I}_{r})_{r\geqslant 0}
are independent. We now formally describe the law of
(\mathscr{R}_{r},\mathscr{I}_{r})
given
\Delta Y^{\bullet}_{r}
. In this direction, fix
a\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
and, under
\mathbb{P}_{0\to a}^{(\ell)}
and independently of
B
, consider a Poisson point measure
\mathcal{P}({\rm d}t,{\rm d}X,{\rm d}Z)
with intensity
\mathbbm{1}_{[0,\ell]}(t){\rm d}t\mathbf{N}({\rm d}X,{\rm d}Z).
We also introduce the measure

\displaystyle\mathbb{Q}_{a}

\displaystyle\big({\rm d}(\check{B},\check{\mathcal{P}}),{\rm d}(B,\mathcal{P})\big)

\displaystyle=C(\alpha)\cdot|b-a|^{2\alpha-1}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}\ell\int_{0}^{1}{\rm d}u\penalty\ \frac{\ell^{-\alpha-\frac{1}{2}}}{\sqrt{2\pi u(1-u)}}\exp(-\frac{(b-a)^{2}}{u(1-u)\ell})\mathbb{P}_{0\to a}^{(u\ell)}\otimes\mathbb{P}_{0\to a}^{((1-u)\ell)}.

(6.7)

By convention, we take
\mathbb{Q}_{0}:=\mathbb{P}^{(0)}_{0\to 0}\otimes\mathbb{P}_{0\to 0}^{(0)}
, i.e.
B
and
\check{B}
are constant processes equal to
0
with
0
lifetime and
\mathcal{P}=\check{\mathcal{P}}=0
. Under
\mathbb{Q}_{a}
, we define
\mathscr{R}
and
\mathscr{I}
replacing in (
6.4
) and (
6.5
) the random variables
\big((\check{B}^{(r)},\check{\mathcal{P}}_{r}),(B^{(r)},\mathcal{P}_{r})\big)
by
\big((\check{B},\check{\mathcal{P}}),(B,\mathcal{P})\big)
and
Y_{r}^{\bullet}
by
0
.
We consider these measures because, by Proposition
6.2
, under
\mathbf{N}^{\bullet}
and conditionally on
\big(Y^{\bullet}_{r}:\penalty\ r\geqslant 0\big)
, for every
r\geqslant 0
, the pair
\big(\mathscr{R}_{r},\mathscr{I}_{r}\big)
is distributed as
\big(\mathscr{R},\mathscr{I}\big)
under
\mathbb{Q}_{\Delta Y^{\bullet}_{r}}
. We also stress that that by scaling, for
a\neq 0
, the pair

\Big((|a|\cdot\mathscr{R},|a|\cdot\mathscr{I}):\penalty\ \text{ under }\mathbb{Q}_{\mathrm{sign}(a)}\Big)\overset{(d)}{=}\Big((\mathscr{R},\mathscr{I}):\penalty\ \text{ under }\mathbb{Q}_{a}\Big)

(6.8)

We conclude this section with some estimates under
\mathbb{Q}_{a}
, for
a\neq 0
.

Lemma 6.3
.

The following properties hold:

\mathrm{(i)}

For every
x>0
, we have
\mathbb{Q}_{1}\big(\mathscr{I}<-x\big)>0
and
\mathbb{Q}_{-1}\big(\mathscr{I}<-x\big)>0
.

\mathrm{(ii)}

There exists
C_{\mathbb{Q}}>0
, such that for every
x>0
we have:

\mathbb{Q}_{\pm 1}\big(\mathscr{R}<-x\big)\leqslant C_{\mathbb{Q}}\cdot q_{\alpha}\Big(\frac{1}{x}\Big),

where
q_{\alpha}:\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
is the function

q_{\alpha}(y)=\begin{cases}y^{2\alpha-1}&\text{if }\alpha\in(1,3/2),\\
y^{2}\cdot\log\big(2+y\big)&\text{if }\alpha=3/2,\\
y^{2}&\text{if }\alpha\in(3/2,2).\end{cases}

Proof.

Point (i) follows straightforwardly from Proposition
5.6
(where we showed that the minimal record values on an infinite ideal loop are distributed as the range of a stable subordinator) and standard absolute continuity between Brownian bridges and Brownian motion as in the proof of Proposition
5.8
.
Let us proceed with the proof of the second item which is more involved. In this direction, remark that, for
z>0
, we have:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{0\to 1}^{(z)}(\mathscr{R}<-x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1-\mathbb{E}_{0\to 1}^{(z)}\Big[\exp\Big(-\int_{0}^{z}\mathbf{N}(\sup Z\geqslant B_{u}+x){\rm d}u\Big)\Big]

\displaystyle\underset{\mathrm{Cor.\ }\ \ref{Z<-1}\ \&\ \mathrm{Prop.\ }\ref{sec:stable-map-3}}{=}

\displaystyle 1-\mathbb{E}_{0\to 1}^{(z)}\Big[\exp\Big(-\int_{0}^{z}\frac{\alpha(\alpha-1)\penalty\ {\rm d}u}{2(B_{u}+x)^{2}}\Big)\Big]

\displaystyle\underset{\mathrm{Lem.\ }\ref{lem:E:a:to:b}}{=}

\displaystyle g\left(\frac{x(x+1)}{z}\right),

where
g(p):=1-\sqrt{2\pi p}\cdot\exp(-p)\cdot I_{\alpha-1/2}(p)
. It then follows from the definition of
\mathbb{Q}_{1}
, given in (
6.7
), that

\displaystyle\mathbb{Q}_{1}(\mathscr{R}<-x)

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle C(\alpha)\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}\ell_{1}\mathrm{d}\ell_{2}\ \frac{(\ell_{1}+\ell_{2})^{-\alpha-\frac{1}{2}}}{\sqrt{2\pi\ell_{1}\ell_{2}}}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}}{2\ell_{1}\ell_{2}}}\Big(g\big(\frac{x(x+1)}{\ell_{1}}\big)+g\big(\frac{x(x+1)}{\ell_{2}}\big)\Big)

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle 2C(\alpha)\cdot\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}\ell\penalty\ \frac{\ell^{-\alpha-\frac{1}{2}}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}g\big(\frac{x^{2}}{\ell}\big){\int_{0}^{1}{\rm d}u\penalty\ u^{-\frac{1}{2}}(1-u)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\exp(-\frac{1}{u(1-u)\ell})},

where to obtain the second line we used that since
g:\mathbb{R}_{+}\to[0,1]
is non-increasing, we have
g(\frac{x(x+1)}{\ell_{i}})\leqslant g(\frac{x^{2}}{\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}})
