. Although this information can in principle be extracted from the encoding presented in Section
7
, it will be more practical to introduce another discrete encoding of maps. This encoding is a variant of the BDG construction, originally introduced in
[
111
]
for quadrangulations, and offers a clearer and more transparent view of the behavior of a typical point along a typical geodesic in the discrete.

10
Boltzmann stable maps with two sources

In this section, we introduce a tool that will be useful to study
the properties of geodesics in discrete stable maps and their scaling
limits. It
consists of a generalization to multi-marked maps of the BDG
bijection presented in Section
7
, which is directly
inspired from the particular case of quadrangulations that is
considered in
[
111
]
.

10.1
The BDG bijection with two sources and delays

Consider the set
\mathcal{M}^{2\bullet}
of
triples
({\bf m},(v_{1},v_{2}),\triangle)
where:

—

{\bf m}
is a planar bipartite map with one distinguished oriented
edge
e_{0}
;

—

v_{1},v_{2}
are two vertices of
{\bf m}
;

—

\triangle
is an integer such that

|\triangle|<\mathrm{d}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{\bf m}(v_{1},v_{2})
, and

\mathrm{d}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{{\bf m}}(v_{1},v_{2})+\triangle
is an even number.

Note that the last condition implies that

\mathrm{d}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{\bf m}(v_{1},v_{2})\geqslant 2
, and, in particular, that

v_{1},v_{2}
are distinct.
The integer
\triangle
is referred to as the
delay
and is said to be
admissible
when it satisfies the above conditions. For a given bi-pointed planar map
(\mathbf{m},(v_{1},v_{2}))
, the set of all admissible delays will be denoted by

\displaystyle\triangle\!\!\!\!\triangle_{\mathbf{m},v_{1},v_{2}}.

(10.1)

In order to obtain more harmonious notation, it will sometimes be useful to fix arbitrarily two integers

\triangle_{1},\triangle_{2}
such that
\triangle=\triangle_{1}-\triangle_{2}
.

We also consider the set
\mathcal{U}
of
unicyclomobiles
,
that are rooted plane maps
{\bf u}
having the property that:

—

{\bf u}
is a bipartite map with exactly two faces labeled as
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
;

—

the vertices of
{\bf u}
are partitioned into two “black
and white” sets of vertices

V_{\bullet}({\bf u}),V_{\circ}({\bf u})
in such a way that a vertex of one
set is only incident to vertices of the other set;

—

{\bf u}
is rooted at a corner incident to a white vertex of

V_{\circ}({\bf u})
;

—

the white vertices carry a function

\ell:V_{\circ}({\bf u})\to\mathbb{Z}
, in such a way that
({\bf u},\ell)

is well-labeled according to the definition given in the opening of Section

7.1
. In particular, the label of the root vertex is
equal to
0
.

The reason for the fancy name is that
unicyclomobiles are planar maps with exactly one
cycle
, of even
length. This comes from the usual core decomposition of planar maps
with two faces: removing inductively all edges incident to a vertex of
degree 1, one obtains a planar map with two faces and with only vertices of
degree at least
2
, which does not depend on the order of the edge
deletions.
By Euler’s formula, this map must have an equal number
of vertices and edges, and the only possibility is that the resulting
map is a cycle, which must have even length by the bipartite nature
of the map we started from. Conversely, it is a consequence of the
Jordan curve theorem that a planar map with exactly one cycle has
exactly two
faces

Figure 38:
A unicyclomobile.

The sets
\mathcal{M}^{2\bullet}
and
\mathcal{U}\times\{-1,1\}
are in natural
correspondence, as we now discuss.

10.1.1
From a unicyclomobile to a map

It is easier to describe the mapping which, to an element of

({\bf u},\epsilon)\in\mathcal{U}\times\{-1,1\}
, associates an element of

\mathcal{M}^{2\bullet}
, as it consists in performing the BDG
construction, described in Section
7.1
,
within each face of
{\bf u}
. We refer the reader to Figure

39
for an illustration of this mapping, which we now
define.

For
i\in\{1,2\}
, we first add a new vertex
v_{i}

inside the face
f_{i}
, with a label
\ell(v_{i})=\min\{\ell(v):v\mbox{
incident to }f_{i}\}-1
. For each white corner
c
incident to the
face
f_{i}
, we define its successor to be the next white corner
c^{\prime}=s(c)
in the
(counterclockwise) contour order around the face
f_{i}
with label
\ell(c)-1
, or

s(c)=v_{i}
if
c
has minimal label in the face
f_{i}
. Then, from
every white corner, we draw an arc connecting this corner to its successor,
in such a way that these arcs do not cross each other, nor an edge of

{\bf u}
. In particular, the arcs always connect two corners incident to
the same face
f_{i}
. Lastly, we remove the edges originally present in

{\bf u}
.

The resulting map, whose vertex set is
V_{\circ}({\bf u})\cup\{v_{1},v_{2}\}
and
whose edge-set is the set of arcs from the white corners to their successors, is denoted by
{\bf m}
.
It is naturally
2
-marked at the vertices

(v_{1},v_{2})
. We also let

\displaystyle\triangle_{i}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\ell(v_{i}),\quad\mbox{ for }\penalty\ i\in\{1,2\}.

(10.2)

Finally, the map
{\bf m}
is rooted at the edge emanating from the
root corner
c_{0}
of

{\bf u}
, which we choose to orient from
c_{0}
to
s(c_{0})
if

\epsilon=+1
, and from
s(c_{0})
to
c_{0}
if
\epsilon=-1
. With this
rooting choice, and letting

\triangle:=\triangle_{1}-\triangle_{2}
, we denote by

\rm{BDG}^{2\bullet}({\bf u})
the tuple
({\bf m},(v_{1},v_{2}),\triangle)
.

Figure 39:
The bi-pointed BDG construction applied on the unicyclomobile of Figure
38
. Notice the two distinguished vertices in each face of the unicyclomobile, and the root edge of the map in bold line. For better visibility two edges have been drawn on the sphere and ”go around” the figure.

Lemma 10.1
.

It holds that
\rm{BDG}^{2\bullet}({\bf u})
is an element of

\mathcal{M}^{2\bullet}
.

This statement will be proved by deriving some important geometric
properties of the map
\mathrm{BDG}^{2\bullet}({\bf u})
.
First, the following can be directly deduced from the construction and
adapting classical arguments in the Bouttier–Di Francesco–Guitter construction
[
32
]
.

—

The map
\mathrm{BDG}^{2\bullet}({\bf u})
is a plane bipartite
map, whose faces of degree
2k
are
in one-to-one correspondence with the black vertices of
{\bf u}
of degree
k
.

—

Fix
i\in\{1,2\}
, as well as a white corner
c
incident to
f_{i}
,
and denote the vertex incident to
c
by
v
. Then the path
\gamma^{(c)}
of arcs from
v
to
v_{i}
following the
consecutive
successors
c,s(c),s(s(c)),\ldots
(until eventually reaching
v_{i}
) is a geodesic path in
\mathrm{BDG}^{2\bullet}({\bf u})
, again called the
simple geodesic
starting from
c
. In particular, we have:

\mathrm{d}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{\bf m}(v,v_{i})=\ell(v)-\min\limits_{v\in f_{i}}\ell(v)+1=\ell(v)-\triangle_{i}.

(10.3)

The so-called “Schaeffer bound”, analog of (
7.2
), is
an improved version of the last display and states that for every

i\in\{1,2\}
and every pair of white vertices
v,v^{\prime}
of
\mathrm{BDG}^{2\bullet}({\bf u})\setminus\{v_{i}\}
incident to
f_{i}
, we have:

|\ell(v)-\ell(v^{\prime})|\leqslant\mathrm{d}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{\bf m}(v,v^{\prime})\leqslant\ell(v)+\ell(v^{\prime})-2\max\left(\min\limits_{[v,v^{\prime}]_{{\bf u},f_{i}}}\ell;\min\limits_{[v^{\prime},v]_{{\bf u},f_{i}}}\ell\right)+2\penalty\ ,

(10.4)

where
[v,v^{\prime}]_{{\bf u},f_{i}}
are the white vertices appearing in a minimal interval in the clockwise
order when going from
v
to
v^{\prime}
inside the face
f_{i}
.
Another important consequence of the construction of
\mathrm{BDG}^{2\bullet}({\bf u})

is that a path
\gamma
in the map
{\bf m}
between the two distinguished vertices
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
necessarily passes through a (white) vertex
v
of the cycle of

{\bf u}
. If
c_{1},c_{2}
denote corners incident to
v
that are also
respectively incident to
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
, then the concatenation of the two simple geodesics
\gamma^{(c_{1})}
and
\gamma^{(c_{2})}
yields a path of length

2\ell(v)-\triangle_{1}-\triangle_{2}
. In particular, if the path

\gamma
as a geodesic, then it is necessary that:

\ell(v)=\min\big\{\ell(v^{\prime}):v^{\prime}\text{ white vertex belonging to the
cycle of }{\bf u}\big\}\,.

And in that case indeed, it is easy to check that the concatenation of
\gamma^{(c_{1})}
and
\gamma^{(c_{2})}
produces a geodesic between
v_{1},v_{2}
in the map
\mathbf{m}
. Recalling (
10.2
) and (
10.3
), we have in particular for
v
minimizing the label on the cycle:

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{{\bf m}}(v,v_{1})=\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathrm{d}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{{\bf m}}(v_{1},v_{2})-\triangle\right)\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\mathrm{d}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{{\bf m}}(v,v_{2})=\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathrm{d}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{{\bf m}}(v_{1},v_{2})+\triangle\right).

(10.5)

Note that in particular, the parity of

\triangle=\triangle_{1}-\triangle_{2}
is the same as that of
\mathrm{d}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{\bf m}(v_{1},v_{2})
, while
the triangle inequality gives

\mathrm{d}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{\bf m}(v_{1},v_{2})>|\triangle_{1}-\triangle_{2}|=|\triangle|
, justifying
that
({\bf m},(v_{1},v_{2}),\triangle)
is indeed an element of

\mathcal{M}^{2\bullet}
, which proves Lemma

10.1
.

The above discussion on geodesic paths will be crucial to our
purposes.
By establishing that the set of “minimal” vertices
v
along the
cycle of
{\bf u}
is typically very
small and localized, this will allow us to prove, in Section
12.2
,
that geodesics are typically unique in the scaling limit. This line of
reasoning is parallel to
[
111
, Section 7]
, but with some
important differences due to the very different scaling limits we are
working with. This property will also allow us, in Section

12.4
, to study the local structure of geodesics in

(\mathcal{S},D)
and prove Proposition
9.3
.

10.1.2
Inverse construction

We now argue that the mapping
\rm{BDG}^{2\bullet}
is indeed a bijection
from
\mathcal{U}\times\{-1,1\}
onto
\mathcal{M}^{2\bullet}
. We will skip some details, as the discussion closely mirrors that in
[
111
, Section 2.4]
, requiring only minor adaptations to fit the current extended context. Additionally, we will not employ this inverse construction in the sequel.
Consider
({\bf m},(v_{1},v_{2}),\triangle)\in\mathcal{M}^{2\bullet}
and let
\triangle_{1},\triangle_{2}
be integers such that

\triangle_{1}-\triangle_{2}=\triangle
. We introduce the labeling function defined
on vertices
v\in V({\bf m})
by

\widetilde{\ell}(v):=\min\big(\mathrm{d}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{{\bf m}}(v,v_{1})+\triangle_{1};\mathrm{d}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{{\bf m}}(v,v_{2})+\triangle_{2}\big)\,.

From the bipartite nature of the map
{\bf m}
, and the fact that
\triangle

has the same parity as
\mathrm{d}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{{\bf m}}(v_{1},v_{2})
, one can check that

|\widetilde{\ell}(v)-\widetilde{\ell}(v^{\prime})|=1
for any two adjacent vertices
v,v^{\prime}
of

{\bf m}
. For this reason, we may canonically orient the edges
e
of

{\bf m}
in such a way that
\widetilde{\ell}(e^{+})=\widetilde{\ell}(e^{-})-1
. Any maximal path of edges
that is oriented in this way will necessarily end in a vertex that is
a local minimum of
\widetilde{\ell}
, in the sense that none of its neighbors has
smaller label. It is easy to see that only
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
are local
minima of
\widetilde{\ell}
.

We now construct an element of
\mathcal{U}
in the following
way. We first add a “black”, dual vertex
v_{f}
inside each face
f

of
{\bf m}
. Then, we let
(v^{f}_{i}:i\in\mathbb{Z})
be the sequence of vertices of

{\bf m}
appearing in clockwise contour order around the face
f
,
starting from an arbitrary vertex, and extended
periodically. Note that
(\widetilde{\ell}(v^{f}_{i}):i\in\mathbb{Z})
is then an infinite,

\deg(f)
-periodic path with
\pm 1
steps. We then draw
\deg(f)/2

arcs
\gamma_{i}^{f}
(that are non-intersecting and disjoint from the edges of
{\bf m}
) from
v_{f}
to each of the vertices
v^{f}_{i}
such
that
\widetilde{\ell}(v^{f}_{i})=\widetilde{\ell}(v^{f}_{i+1})+1
.

Finally, we let
e_{0}
be the canonical orientation of the
root edge of
{\bf m}
, and we let
\epsilon=+1
if this canonical
orientation coincides with the original orientation of the root, and

\epsilon=-1
otherwise. We define the label function

\ell(v)=\widetilde{\ell}(v)-\widetilde{\ell}(e_{0}^{-})
.

Lemma 10.2
.

The graph with “white” vertices
V({\bf m})\setminus\{v_{1},v_{2}\}
, and “black” vertices
\{v^{f}_{i}:(f,i)\in\mathrm{Faces}({\bf m})\times\mathbb{Z}\}
, whose
set of edges is the set of arcs
A=\{\gamma_{i}^{f}\}
as above, and
with label function inherited from
\ell
, is a unicyclomobile
{\bf u}
,
such that the removed vertices
v_{1},v_{2}
belong to distinct faces of

{\bf u}
, thereby labeled
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
. It is rooted at the corner
inherited from the root corner incident to the left of the origin of
e_{0}
.

The proof of this lemma is exactly parallel to Lemma 1 in

[
111
]
. By construction, the graph
{\bf u}
comes with a bipartite
coloration of its vertices and is well-labeled by
\ell
. To prove
that
{\bf u}
is indeed a map with two faces,
we consider the augmented map obtained from
{\bf m}
by
taking all (white) vertices of
{\bf m}
, all (black) dual vertices, and
all edges and arcs. In this new map, we consider the edges
e^{*}
dual to the
original edges
e
of
{\bf m}
, oriented in such a way that the vertex
incident to
e
of
smaller label lies systematically to the right of the edge
e^{*}
. Then
it is easy to check that any oriented dual edge can be uniquely
extended into an infinite oriented dual path, and this path has the
property that the sequence
of vertices
lying to the right of the successive primal edges crossed by the path
have non-increasing label. This implies that the path eventually
cycles around a vertex of locally minimal label, and the only two such
vertices are
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
. Therefore, the oriented dual edges
form a spanning graph which is a cycle-rooted forest with two
components, and whose unique cycles circle around the vertices

v_{1}
and
v_{2}
. The remaining edges, which are the arcs

A=\{\gamma_{i}^{f}\}
, therefore form
a map with two faces.

10.2
Combinatorial decompositions

Let us discuss some decompositions of unicyclomobiles into simpler
combinatorial objects. This will eventually allow
us to study the random unicyclomobiles associated with random Boltzmann
maps via the BDG
2∙
construction.

Let
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}}=(\mathcal{T},\ell)
and
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}}^{\prime}=(\mathcal{T^{\prime}},\ell^{\prime})

be two well-labeled mobiles, and, provided the sets

\widehat{V}_{\circ}(\mathcal{T})
and

\widehat{V}_{\circ}(\mathcal{T}^{\prime})
of
white leaves of
\mathcal{T},\mathcal{T^{\prime}}
are not empty, we let

\hat{v}_{\circ},\hat{v}^{\prime}_{\circ}
be two distinguished elements of these
sets respectively.
We define a natural
concatenation operation by changing the labeling function on

\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
to
\ell(\hat{v}_{\circ})+\ell^{\prime}
, and then identifying the leaf

\hat{v}_{\circ}
of
\mathcal{T}
with the root vertex of

\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
, in such a way that the corner incident to

\hat{v}_{\circ}
is merged with the root corner of
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
. The result
is a new well-labeled mobile with a distinguished white leaf

\hat{v}^{\prime}_{\circ}
. The neutral element of this concatenation operation
is the trivial vertex-mobile, and the non-trivial irreducible
elements are well-labeled mobiles with a marked
white leaf at generation
2
, which will be called
mobile buckle
(the name will become clear after the forthcoming Proposition
10.4
). If
(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}},\hat{v}^{\prime}_{\circ})
is a mobile buckle, we denote by
C_{\circ}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}},\hat{v}^{\prime}_{\circ})
the set of white corners incident to a white vertex different from
\hat{v}^{\prime}_{\circ}
and where the root white corner has been duplicated.

Mobile buckles can be described in the following convenient way, see Figure
40
. Start
with a mobile “star”, that is, a well-labeled mobile buckle

(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}},\hat{v}^{\prime}_{\circ})
with exactly one black vertex

\hat{v}_{\bullet}
, which necessarily has to be the
parent vertex of
\hat{v}^{\prime}_{\circ}
. The latter is the parent of the
white vertices
v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{k}
for some
k\geqslant 1
, arranged in
clockwise order, whose respective labels are denoted by

\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{k}
, and we let
r\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
be the index such
that
v_{r}=\hat{v}^{\prime}_{\circ}
. By convention we also let
\ell_{0}=\ell_{k+1}=0
.

For every
i\in\{0,1,\ldots,k,k+1\}\setminus\{r\}
, we let

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}}^{(i)}
be a well-labeled mobile,
whose label function has been shifted by the addition of
\ell_{i}
. For
1\leqslant i\leqslant k
, we graft the root of

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}}^{(i)}
(at the level of its
root corner) to the vertex
v_{i}
, and we identify
the root vertices of

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}}^{(0)},\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}}^{(k+1)}
with
the root vertex of
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}
, by grafting them to
the two sides of the root of
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}
separated by the
edge from the root to
\hat{v}_{\bullet}
. See Figure

40
for an illustration. The result is clearly a well-labeled
mobile buckle.

Figure 40:
Decomposition of a mobile buckle into a mobile star and
sub-mobiles, with
k=4
and
r=3
in the notation of the text.

Proposition 10.3
.

The previous construction is a bijection onto the set of mobile
buckles
(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}},\hat{v}^{\prime}_{\circ})
, where

\hat{v}^{\prime}_{\circ}
has
r-1
older siblings and
k-r
younger
siblings.

Next, we observe that unicyclomobiles

{\bf u}\in\mathcal{U}
are in correspondence with pairs

((\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}},\hat{v}_{\circ}),(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}},\hat{v}^{\prime}_{\circ},c))
, where:

—

(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}},\hat{v}_{\circ})
is well-labeled mobile with a distinguished white leaf

\hat{v}_{\circ}
;

—

(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}},\hat{v}^{\prime}_{\circ})
is a well-labeled mobile buckle,
and
c\in C_{\circ}(\mathcal{P},\hat{v}^{\prime}_{\circ})
is a
distinguished white corner distinct from the one incident to
\hat{v}_{\circ}^{\prime}
(and where the root corner has been duplicated);

—

we have

\ell_{\mathcal{T}}(\hat{v}_{\circ})=-\ell_{\mathcal{P}}(\hat{v}^{\prime}_{\circ})
.

The correspondence is illustrated in Figure

41
. We concatenate

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}}
with
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}}
as in the
beginning of the section, resulting in a non-trivial
13
13

13

Note
that, contrary to
\mathcal{P}
, the mobile
\mathcal{T}
may well be the
trivial vertex-mobile.
well-labeled mobile with a distinguished
white leaf
\hat{v}^{\prime}_{\circ}
, whose label is

\ell_{\mathcal{T}}(\hat{v}_{\circ})+\ell_{\mathcal{P}}(\hat{v}^{\prime}_{\circ})=0
. This
allows us to merge the corner incident to this leaf with the root corner
of
\mathcal{T}
, resulting in a unicyclomobile

{\bf u}=\Phi((\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}},\hat{v}_{\circ}),(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}},\hat{v}^{\prime}_{\circ},c))\in\mathcal{U}
, rooted at the white
corner
c
. By convention, we let

f_{1}
be the face that is incident to the left side of
\mathcal{P}
,
and
f_{2}
be the other face. Finally, we shift all the labels by
subtracting the label of the root corner
c
in
\mathcal{P}
.
This construction is easily inverted, as indicated on Figure

41
. The only delicate
point is to decide where to cut the cycle of a unicyclomobile
{\bf u}
to
recover the pair

((\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}},\hat{v}_{\circ}),(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}},\hat{v}^{\prime}_{\circ},c))
:
to this end, we start from the distinguished corner
c
. If it is
incident to
f_{1}
(resp.
f_{2}
), we run in counterclockwise (resp. clockwise) contour order until we first
meet a black vertex
\hat{v}^{\prime}_{\bullet}
on the cycle of
{\bf u}
, and then stop and cut the
cycle at the first
encounter of a white vertex
\hat{v}^{\prime}_{\circ}
on the cycle
afterwards. This is materialized by the two blue oriented curves on
the picture. The vertex
\hat{v}_{\circ}
is then found by
backtraking two edges along the cycle. Summarizing, we obtain the
following statement.

Proposition 10.4

(Belt-Buckle decomposition)
.

The mapping
\Phi
is a bijection between

{\mathcal{U}}
and the set of pairs

((\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}},\hat{v}_{\circ}),(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}},\hat{v}^{\prime}_{\circ},c))

described above. We will call them respectively the
belt
and the

buckle
associated with the corresponding unicyclomobile.

Figure 41:
An element
{\bf u}\in\mathcal{U}
is described by a pair

(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}},\hat{v}_{\circ}),(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}},\hat{v}^{\prime}_{\circ},c)
, where the
first element (the belt) is a mobile with a marked leaf, and
the second (the buckle)
is a mobile buckle with a marked white corner
c
. The grey
blobs indicate sub-mobiles, and
circled grey blobs indicate the places where the distinguished
white corner can be located. The oriented curves and dashed
lines indicate how one should cut and split
{\bf u}
to recover
the mobiles.

10.3
Boltzmann measures

Recall (
1.1
). Let us now consider the natural sigma-finite measure on
\mathcal{M}^{2\bullet}
defined by:

w_{\bf q}^{2\bullet}\big({\bf m},(v_{1},v_{2}),\triangle\big)=w_{\bf q}({\bf m})\,,\qquad({\bf m},(v_{1},v_{2}),\triangle)\in\mathcal{M}^{2\bullet}\,,

which we may formally rewrite as

w_{{\bf q}}^{2\bullet}\big({\rm d}({\bf m},(v_{1},v_{2}),\triangle)\big):=\#V({\bf m})^{2}\,w_{{\bf q}}({\rm d}{\bf m})\mathrm{vol}_{{\bf m}}({\rm d}v_{1})\mathrm{vol}_{{\bf m}}({\rm d}v_{2})\penalty\ \#_{\triangle\!\!\!\!\triangle_{{\bf m},v_{1},v_{2}}}({\rm d}\triangle)\,.

(10.6)

Here,
\#_{\triangle\!\!\!\!\triangle_{{\bf m},v_{1},v_{2}}}({\rm d}\triangle)
stands for the counting measure on the set
\triangle\!\!\!\!\triangle_{\mathbf{m},v_{1},v_{2}}
of integers
\triangle
such that
|\triangle|<\mathrm{d}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{{\bf m}}(v_{1},v_{2})
and

\mathrm{d}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{{\bf m}}(v_{1},v_{2})+\triangle
is an even number. Simply, this set
has cardinality

\displaystyle\#\triangle\!\!\!\!\triangle_{\mathbf{m},v_{1},v_{2}}=(\mathrm{d}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{{\bf m}}(v_{1},v_{2})-1)_{+},

(10.7)

and in particular
\#\triangle\!\!\!\!\triangle_{\mathbf{m},v_{1},v_{2}}\leqslant\#V({\bf m})
. Consequently, for every
n\geqslant 1
, the event
\{\#V({\bf m})=n\}
has finite measure under
w_{{\bf q}}^{2\bullet}
.
Notice that the pushforward of
w_{\bf q}^{2\bullet}
by the bijection
\rm{BDG}^{2\bullet}:\mathcal{M}^{2\bullet}\to\mathcal{U}\times\{-1,1\}
described in the preceding section, and
after forgetting the sign variable
\epsilon\in\{-1,1\}
, is given by:

\displaystyle\widetilde{w}_{\bf q}^{2\bullet}({\bf u})=2\prod_{v\in V_{\bullet}({\bf u})}q_{\deg(v)}\,.

(10.8)

In particular, if
(\mathfrak{M}_{n},v_{1}^{n},v_{2}^{n},\triangle_{n})

has distribution
w_{{\bf q}}^{2\bullet}({\rm d}({\bf m},(v_{1},v_{2}),\triangle)|\#V({\bf m})=n)
, then
the associated unicyclomobile
{\bf u}_{n}
has distribution

\widetilde{w}^{2\bullet}_{\bf q}({\rm d}{\bf u}\,|\,\#V_{\circ}({\bf u})=n-2)
.
Our primary aim now is to investigate the behavior of
{\bf u}_{n}
as
n\to\infty
. This analysis will enable us to deduce certain geometric
properties of
(\mathfrak{M}_{n},v_{1}^{n},v_{2}^{n},\triangle_{n})
and deliver the proofs of the results announced in Section
9
.
To implement this program, we will describe the unicyclomobile
{\bf u}
under the
sigma-finite “law”
\widetilde{w}^{2\bullet}_{\bf q}
by using simpler
probabilistic objects, with the help of the decompositions of Section
10.2
.

Recall the notation
\mu_{\circ},\mu_{\bullet}
of (
7.3
), and recall that

\rm{GW}_{\bf q}({\rm d}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}})
denotes the law of a
well-labeled mobile described by an alternating
2-type branching process, starting with a white vertex, with offspring distributions

\mu_{\circ},\mu_{\bullet}
, and where the labels are uniformly distributed
among possible well labelings conditionally given the tree structure.

For
h\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, we define the measure

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathrm{GW}}^{(h)}_{\bf q}({\rm d}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}},\hat{v}))=\frac{\mathrm{GW}_{\bf q}({\rm d}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}})}{\mu_{\circ}(0)}\sum_{v\in\widehat{V}_{\circ}(\mathcal{T})}\mathbbm{1}_{\{|\hat{v}|=2h\}}\delta_{v}({\rm d}\hat{v})\,,

(10.9)

which consist in marking one distinguished white leaf at height
2h
according to
the counting measure.

In particular, note that

\widehat{\mathrm{GW}}_{\bf q}^{(1)}
is supported on the set of mobile buckles. As
an easy exercise, we invite the reader to check that the image measure
of
\widehat{\mathrm{GW}}_{\bf q}^{(h)}\otimes\widehat{\mathrm{GW}}_{\bf q}^{(h^{\prime})}
under the
concatenation operation is

\widehat{\mathrm{GW}}_{\bf q}^{(h+h^{\prime})}
(notice the factor
1/{\mu_{\circ}(0)}
has conveniently be included in the definition).
In order to give a description of random mobile buckles under

\widehat{\mathrm{GW}}_{\bf q}^{(1)}
, we invite the reader to recall the
discussion around Proposition
10.3
, and define the following random
variables associated with a mobile buckle

(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}},\hat{v}^{\prime}_{\circ})
:

—

K
is the number of children of
\hat{v}_{\bullet}
, the parent vertex
of
\hat{v}^{\prime}_{\circ}
;

—

R
is the rank in clockwise order around
\hat{v}_{\bullet}
of
the vertex
\hat{v}^{\prime}_{\circ}
among its siblings.

Lemma 10.5
.

Under
the law
\widehat{\mathrm{GW}}_{\bf q}^{(1)}
,

—

the random variable
K
has distribution

\hat{\mu}_{\bullet}(k)=k\mu_{\bullet}(k)/m_{\bullet}
, the size-biased
distribution associated with
\mu_{\bullet}
;

—

conditionally given
K
, the random variable
R
is uniformly
distributed in
\{1,\ldots,K\}
.

Moreover, conditionally given
(K,R)=(k,r)
,

—

the labels

(\ell_{0},\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{k},\ell_{k+1})
of vertices around

\hat{v}_{\bullet}
form a random walk with i.i.d. steps distributed according to a shifted geometric law
\sum_{n\geqslant-1}2^{-n-2}\delta_{n}
,
and conditioned on
\ell_{0}=\ell_{k+1}=0
;

—

the random mobiles
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}}^{(i)}
for
i\in\{0,\ldots,k+1\}\setminus\{r\}
are i.i.d. with distribution

\mathrm{GW}_{\bf q}
.

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of Proposition

10.3
. For a given mobile buckle

(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}},\hat{v}^{\prime}_{\circ})
obtained by appending the
given well-labeled mobiles
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}}^{(i)}
, for
i\in\{0,\ldots,k+1\}\setminus\{r\}
, to a mobile “star” with
k+1

branches, we have,

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathrm{GW}}_{\bf q}^{(1)}(\{(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}},\hat{v}^{\prime}_{\circ})\})

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{GW}_{\bf q}(\{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}}\})}{\mu_{\circ}(0)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\binom{2k+1}{k}}\mu_{\bullet}(k)\prod_{v\in V_{\circ}(\mathcal{P})\setminus\{\hat{v}^{\prime}_{\circ}\}}\mu_{\circ}(k_{v}(\mathcal{P}))\prod_{v\in V_{\bullet}(\mathcal{P})\setminus\{\hat{v}_{\bullet}\}}\frac{\mu_{\bullet}(k_{v}(\mathcal{P}))}{\binom{2k_{v}(\mathcal{P})+1}{k_{v}(\mathcal{P})}}\,,

where the inverse binomial factors come from the number of possible
labelings of the neighbours of the different black vertices, and the
first term is the contribution of
\hat{v}_{\bullet}
. Note that, from
the fact that
\mu_{\circ}
is a geometric distribution and the
criticality assumption
m_{\circ}m_{\bullet}=1
, we may
rewrite

\mu_{\circ}(k_{\mathrm{root}}(\mathcal{P}))=\frac{1}{m_{\bullet}}\mu_{\circ}(k_{\mathrm{root}}(\mathcal{T}^{(0)}))\mu_{\circ}(k_{\mathrm{root}}(\mathcal{T}^{(k+1)}))\,,

so that we may re-express the above display as

\widehat{\mathrm{GW}}_{\bf q}^{(1)}(\{(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}},\hat{v}^{\prime}_{\circ})\})=\frac{1}{\binom{2k+1}{k}}\frac{\hat{\mu}_{\bullet}(k)}{k}\prod_{i\in\{0,1,\ldots,k+1\}\setminus\{r\}}\mathrm{GW}_{\bf q}(\{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}}^{(i)}\})\,.

We conclude by the elementary observation that a uniformly chosen
possible labeling
(\ell_{0},\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{k},\ell_{k+1})
among the

\binom{2k+1}{k}
possible ones has the same law as the claimed
conditioned random walk.
∎

Next, we define the two measures
\widehat{\mathrm{GW}}_{\bf q}=\sum_{h\geqslant 0}\widehat{\mathrm{GW}}^{(h)}_{\bf q}
, and

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathrm{GW}}_{\bf q}({\rm d}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}},\hat{v}^{\prime},c))=\widehat{\mathrm{GW}}_{\bf q}^{(1)}({\rm d}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}},\hat{v}^{\prime}))\sum_{c\in C_{\circ}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}},\hat{v}^{\prime})}({\rm d}c)\,,

(10.10)

where we recall that
C_{\circ}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}},\hat{v}^{\prime})
is the set of corners incident to
a white vertex different from
\hat{v}^{\prime}
and where the white root corner has been duplicated.

Proposition 10.6
.

The measure
\widetilde{w}^{2\bullet}_{\bf q}/2
is the image measure under

\Phi
(recall Figure
41
) of

\widehat{\mathrm{GW}}_{\bf q}({\rm d}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}},\hat{v}))\widetilde{\mathrm{GW}}_{\bf q}({\rm d}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}},\hat{v}^{\prime},c))\mathbbm{1}_{\{\ell_{\mathcal{T}}(\hat{v})=-\ell_{\mathcal{P}}(\hat{v}^{\prime})\}}\,.

Proof.

We fix a unicyclomobile
{\bf u}
, that we may write as

\Phi((\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}},\hat{v}_{\circ}),(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}},\hat{v}^{\prime}_{\circ},c))
in a unique way. In
particular, notice that
\ell_{\mathcal{T}}(\hat{v}_{\circ})=-\ell_{\mathcal{P}}(\hat{v}_{\circ}^{\prime})
. Then, it
suffices to show that

\mathrm{GW}_{\bf q}(\{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}}\})\mathrm{GW}_{\bf q}(\{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}}\})=\prod_{v\in V_{\bullet}({\bf u})}q_{\deg(v)}\,.

Using the fact that, in a mobile, every black vertex is the child of a
white vertex, while every white vertex but the root is the child of a
black vertex, we observe that

\displaystyle\mathrm{GW}_{\bf q}(\{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}}\})

\displaystyle=\prod_{v\in V_{\circ}(\mathcal{T})\setminus\{\hat{v}_{\circ}\}}\mu_{\circ}(k_{v}(\mathcal{T}))\prod_{v\in V_{\bullet}(\mathcal{T})}\frac{\mu_{\bullet}(k_{v}(\mathcal{T}))}{\binom{2k_{v}(\mathcal{T})+1}{k_{v}(\mathcal{T})}}

\displaystyle=\prod_{v\in V_{\circ}(\mathcal{T})\setminus\{\hat{v}_{\circ}\}}z_{\mathbf{q}}^{-1}\left(1-z_{\mathbf{q}}^{-1}\right)^{k_{v}(\mathcal{T})}\prod_{v\in V_{\bullet}(\mathcal{T})}\frac{z_{\mathbf{q}}^{k_{v}(\mathcal{T})}q_{k_{v}(\mathcal{T})+1}}{1-z_{\mathbf{q}}^{-1}}

\displaystyle=\prod_{v\in V_{\bullet}(\mathcal{T})}q_{k_{v}(\mathcal{T})+1}\,.

In the first line, the product of inverses of binomial coefficients comes from the choice of the labeling function of
\mathcal{T}
.
Similarly,

\displaystyle\mathrm{GW}_{\bf q}(\{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}}\})=\prod_{v\in V_{\circ}(\mathcal{P})\setminus\{\hat{v}^{\prime}_{\circ}\}}z_{\mathbf{q}}^{-1}\left(1-z_{\mathbf{q}}^{-1}\right)^{k_{v}(\mathcal{P})}\prod_{v\in V_{\bullet}(\mathcal{P})\setminus\{\hat{v}^{\prime}_{\circ}\}}\frac{z_{\mathbf{q}}^{k_{v}(\mathcal{P})}q_{k_{v}(\mathcal{P})+1}}{1-z_{\mathbf{q}}^{-1}}=\prod_{v\in V_{\bullet}(\mathcal{P})}q_{k_{v}(\mathcal{P})+1}\,,

which gives the result.
∎

11
Scaling limit of Boltzmann unicyclomobiles

The purpose of this section is to obtain scaling limit results for a
random unicyclomobile under the measure
\tilde{w}^{2\bullet}_{\bf q}({\rm d}{\bf u})

conditioned on the event that
{\bf u}
has
n
white vertices, as

n\to\infty
. In this section we will use the
assumption that
{\bf q}
is strictly non-generic
i.e. satisfies
furthermore (
7.7
) which we supposed from
Section
7.4.2
on.

11.1
Scaling limit for the belt of a random unicyclomobile

Let us state the main result of this section. Let
\mathbf{u}_{n}
be a random
unicyclomobile of law
\tilde{w}^{2\bullet}_{\bf q}({\rm d}{\bf u}\mid\#{V}_{\circ}(\mathbf{u})=n-2)
and denote the belt part in the
belt-buckle decomposition of Proposition
10.4
by
(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{B}}_{n},\hat{v}_{\circ}^{n})
. In
particular,
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{B}}_{n}
is a pointed labeled tree
of random size
\theta^{n}\leqslant n-1
, and we write

(S^{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{B}}_{n}}_{k},L^{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{B}}_{n}}_{k})_{k\geqslant 0}

for its Lukasiewicz encoding as in Section
7.3
.
We also
denote the time of visit of
\hat{v}_{\circ}
by
a^{n}
.
The main result of this section identifies the scaling limit of

(S^{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{B}}_{n}},L^{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{B}}_{n}},a^{n},\theta^{n})

in terms of the law of
(X,Z,t_{\bullet},\sigma)
under the
measure
\mathbf{N}^{\bullet}
defined in Section
6
.
In order to state it, we introduce two bits of notation. We let

\displaystyle\bar{p}_{t}(z):=\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}{\rm d}s\,\sqrt{\frac{t}{2\pi s(t-s)}}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{t\,z^{2}}{2s(t-s)}}\underset{\eqref{mellin:erfc}}{=}\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2t}}\,\mathrm{erfc}\left(|z|\sqrt{\frac{2}{t}}\right)\,,\qquad z\in\mathbb{R}\,,

(11.1)

be the
density of a standard Brownian bridge of duration
t>0
sampled at an independent, uniformly
random time in
[0,t]
. Finally, for
x>0
and
z\in\mathbb{R}
, we let

\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(x,z):=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{{\rm d}t}{\Gamma(-\alpha)\,t^{\alpha-1}}\,q^{[\alpha]}_{x}(-t)\,\bar{p}_{t}(z)\,,

(11.2)

where, for every
\beta\in(0,1)\cup(1,2)
, and
c>0
,
q^{[\beta]}_{c}
is the density of a stable spectrally
positive Lévy process with exponent
\beta
taken at time
c
,
defined by its Laplace transform

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda x}q^{[\beta]}_{c}(x)\,{\rm d}x=\begin{cases}\exp(-c\lambda^{\beta})&\mbox{ if }\beta\in(0,1)\\
\exp(c\lambda^{\beta})&\mbox{ if }\beta\in(1,2)\end{cases},\qquad\lambda\geqslant 0\,,

(11.3)

and we let

\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(0,z):=0
for every
z\in\mathbb{R}
.
Note that
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}
is a
continuous function on
(0,\infty)\times(\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\})
, because of the Gaussian tails of the error function for

t\to 0
, and because of the stretched-exponential tails of

q_{x}^{[\alpha]}(-t)
as
t\to\infty
, see (
11.5
) below. For
\alpha\in(1,3/2)
, it holds that
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(x,0)<\infty

and
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}
is in fact continuous on
(0,1)\times\mathbb{R}
. On the other
hand, since
q_{x}^{[\alpha]}(0)>0
, one should note that
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(x,0)=\infty
when

\alpha\in[3/2,2)
and
x>0
.

Proposition 11.1

(Scaling limits for the belt of a Boltzmann-distributed
unicyclomobile)
.

Assume that
{\bf q}
is strictly non-generic.
For any bounded continuous function
F:\mathbb{D}([0,1],\mathbb{R})^{2}\times[0,1]^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[F\left(2(\mathfrak{s}_{\mathbf{q}}n)^{-\frac{1}{\alpha}}S_{\lfloor(n-1)\cdot\rfloor}^{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{B}}_{n}},(\mathfrak{s}_{\mathbf{q}}n)^{-\frac{1}{2\alpha}}L_{\lfloor(n-1)\cdot\rfloor}^{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{B}}_{n}},\frac{a^{n}}{n},\frac{\theta^{n}}{n}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}\quad

\displaystyle\mathrm{Cst}\cdot\mathbf{N}^{\bullet}\left(F(X,Z,t_{\bullet},\sigma)\cdot\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}\left(1-\sigma,-Z_{t_{\bullet}}\right)\right),

where the constant is such that the
right-hand side defines a probability
distribution.

As a consequence of our study, we will also obtain the following
statement that will be useful
in the next section. Recall that
\mathfrak{M}_{n}
denotes a random

{\bf q}
-Boltzmann map conditioned to have
n
vertices.

Proposition 11.2
.

Conditionally on
\mathfrak{M}_{n}
, let
v_{1}^{n}
and
v_{2}^{{n}}

be two independent uniform random vertices. Then
the sequence of random variables

(n^{-\frac{1}{2\alpha}}\mathrm{d}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{\mathfrak{M}_{n}}(v_{1}^{n},v_{2}^{n}))_{n\geqslant 1}
is uniformly
integrable. Consequently, along the subsequence
(n_{k})_{k\geqslant 1}
defined before
(
7.10
) it holds that

\mathbf{E}[(\mathfrak{s}_{\mathbf{q}}n)^{-\frac{1}{2\alpha}}\cdot\mathrm{d}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{\mathfrak{M}_{n}}(v_{1}^{n},v_{2}^{n})]\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}\mathbf{E}[D(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})]\,,

where
\rho_{1},\rho_{2}
are two independent random points in
(\mathcal{S},D)
of law
\mathrm{Vol}
.

The rest of this technical section is devoted to the proofs of Propositions

11.1
and
11.2
, which will be based on Proposition

10.6
, and first requires to address a similar
question for mobiles with
\sigma
-finite “distributions”

\widetilde{\mathrm{GW}}_{\bf q}
and
\widehat{\mathrm{GW}}_{\bf q}
using
some slightly delicate local limit theorems.
At first, we gather some needed estimates for heavy-tailed random variables.

11.2
Some classical estimates on heavy-tailed random variables

In this section, we are going to make an extensive use of classical
local limit theorems for random variables in stable domains of
attraction, so let us recall some basic facts about stable densities
and domains of attractions.

On the stable densities
q_{c}^{[\beta]}
defined at (
11.3
).

For
\beta\in(0,1)\cup(1,2)
and
c>0
, we note that, by

[
85
, Theorem 1.18]
, it
holds that:

c^{1/\beta}q^{[\beta]}_{c}(0)=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{|\Gamma(-1/\beta)|}&\mbox{ if }\beta>1\\
\frac{1}{|\Gamma(-\beta)|}&\mbox{ if }\beta<1\,.\end{cases}

(11.4)

We will also use stretched-exponential decay of the left tail of

q^{[\beta]}_{1}
: there exist
c_{1},c_{2}>0
(depending on
\beta
) such that

\displaystyle q_{c}^{[\beta]}(-t)\leqslant\frac{c_{1}}{c^{1/\beta}}\exp\left(-c_{2}\left(\frac{t}{c^{1/\beta}}\right)^{\frac{\beta}{\beta-1}}\right),

(11.5)

for
c,t>0
, see
[
136
, Theorem 2.5.3]
.
Moreover, assuming that
\beta\in(0,1)
, the following
identity is known as Zolotarev’s duality
[
85
, Theorem 1.16 and (1.31)]
, which can also be seen as a continuous version of the cyclic lemma:

q^{[\beta]}_{c}(x)=\frac{c}{x}q^{[1/\beta]}_{x}(-c)\,,\qquad c,x>0\,.

(11.6)

On heavy-tailed random variables.

Now let
\xi_{1}^{\circ},\xi_{2}^{\circ},\ldots
be integer-valued, i.i.d. nonnegative random
variables with a non-lattice distribution, meaning that

\mathrm{gcd}(\{k:\mathbf{P}(\xi^{\circ}_{1}=k)>0\})=1
. We assume that

\mathbf{P}(\xi^{\circ}_{1}>k)\sim\frac{c}{|\Gamma(1-\beta)|k^{\beta}}\,,\qquad k\to\infty,

(11.7)

for some
c\in(0,\infty)
. We let
\xi_{i}:=\xi^{\circ}_{i}
if
\beta\in(0,1)
, and

\xi_{i}:=\xi^{\circ}_{i}-\mathbf{E}[\xi^{\circ}_{i}]
if
\beta\in(1,2)
.
These assumptions imply, by an Abelian theorem,
that

\mathbf{E}[\exp(-\lambda\xi_{1})]=\begin{cases}1-c\lambda^{\beta}(1+o(1))&\mbox{ if }\beta\in(0,1)\\
1+c\lambda^{\beta}(1+o(1))&\mbox{ if }\beta\in(1,2)\end{cases}\,,\quad\lambda\to 0\,,

(11.8)

which in turn implies that the rescaled random sum

(\xi_{1}+\dots+\xi_{n})/n^{1/\beta}
converges in distribution as
n\to\infty
to a
random variable with density
q^{[\beta]}_{c}
.
We are going to make extensive use of the Gnedenko
local limit theorem (see
[
73
, Theorem 4.2.1]
), according to which

\sup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\left|n^{1/\beta}\mathbf{P}(\xi_{1}+\cdots+\xi_{n}=k)-q^{[\beta]}_{c}\left(\frac{k}{n^{1/\beta}}\right)\right|\underset{n\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}0\,.

(11.9)

This result will be complemented
by exponential bounds for the left-tail of
\xi_{1}+\dots+\xi_{n}
. If

\beta\in(1,2)
, the Chernov bound, together with (
11.8
) applied at
\lambda=n^{-1/\beta}
, implies that

\mathbf{P}(\xi_{1}+\dots+\xi_{n}\leqslant-k)\leqslant C\exp\left(-\frac{k}{n^{1/\beta}}\right)\,,\quad k,n\geqslant 1\,,

(11.10)

for some constant
C\in(0,\infty)
.

Finally, in the
case where
\beta\in(0,1)
, it holds that

\mathbf{P}(\xi_{1}+\dots+\xi_{n}\leqslant k)\leqslant C\exp\left(-\frac{n}{k^{\beta}}\right)\,,\quad k,n\geqslant 1\,,

(11.11)

for some constant
C\in(0,\infty)
. This is obtained by
combining the bound

\mathbf{P}(\xi_{1}+\cdots+\xi_{n}\leqslant k)\leqslant\mathbf{E}\left[\exp\left(\lambda\left(1-\frac{\xi_{1}+\cdots+\xi_{n}}{k}\right)\right)\right]=\mathrm{e}^{\lambda}\,\mathbf{E}\left[\exp\left(-\frac{\lambda\xi_{1}}{k}\right)\right]^{n}\,,

where
\lambda
is chosen arbitrarily in
(0,1/c^{1/\beta})
,
with the estimate (
11.8
).

11.3
Scaling limit of the buckle

We first deal with the scaling limit of a marked mobile piece with
sigma-finite “distribution”
\widetilde{\mathrm{GW}}_{\bf q}
defined in (
10.10
), starting with
the easier case of an unmarked mobile piece with law

\widehat{\mathrm{GW}}_{\bf q}^{(1)}
see (
10.9
). Recall the definition of the random
variables
K,R

associated with mobile pieces
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}}
discussed around Lemma

10.5
, and introduce two extra random
variables, this time associated with any pointed mobile

(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}},\hat{v}_{\circ})
:

—

M
is the number of white vertices of
\mathcal{T}
different from

\hat{v}_{\circ}
;

—

L=\ell_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}}}(\hat{v}_{\circ})
is the label of
\hat{v}_{\circ}
.

Let
\Xi
denote the law of
(M,L)
under

\widehat{\mathrm{GW}}_{\bf q}^{(1)}
. We write

\Xi(m,l)=\Xi(\{(m,l)\})=\widehat{\mathrm{GW}}_{\bf q}^{(1)}(M=m,L=l)
for
simplicity. Out first goal is
to describe the asymptotic behavior of this law.

Proposition 11.3
.

Fix
x>0
and
z\in\mathbb{R}
. Then, for any two sequences

(m_{N}),(l_{N})
such that
m_{N}\sim N^{\frac{\alpha}{\alpha-1}}\,x
and

l_{N}\sim N^{\frac{1}{2(\alpha-1)}}\,\mathfrak{s}_{\mathbf{q}}^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}}z
, it holds that

2\mathfrak{s}_{\mathbf{q}}^{1-\frac{1}{2\alpha}}\,N\cdot N^{\frac{\alpha}{\alpha-1}}\cdot N^{\frac{1}{2(\alpha-1)}}\cdot\Xi(m_{N},l_{N})\underset{N\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\frac{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(x,z)}{x}\,.

(11.12)

Similarly, it holds that, for every
\eta\in(0,\infty]
,

2\mathfrak{s}_{\mathbf{q}}\,N\cdot N^{\frac{\alpha}{\alpha-1}}\cdot\Xi\big(\{m_{N}\}\times(-\eta N^{1/2(\alpha-1)},\eta N^{1/2(\alpha-1)})\big)\underset{N\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\int_{-\eta}^{\eta}\frac{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(x,z)}{x}\,\mathrm{d}z\,.

(11.13)

Here, we recall that the function
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}
is defined at (
11.2
), and note that the integral
in (
11.13
) is always finite despite the fact
that
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(x,z)
may explode at
z=0
, because

\bar{p}_{t}
is an approximation of
\delta_{0}
as
t\downarrow 0
.
From this, it will be easy to deduce the following scaling limit result for the
buckle measure
\widetilde{\mathrm{GW}}_{\bf q}
, which, we recall, is
the measure
\widehat{\mathrm{GW}}_{\bf q}^{(1)}
biased by the total
number of white corners
\#C_{\circ}
(not incident to the pointed white leaf, and with root corner duplicated). Let us introduce
the notation

\widetilde{G}(m,l)=\widetilde{\mathrm{GW}}_{\bf q}(M=m,L=l)
, which we
view as a measure on
\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{Z}
.
The measure
\widetilde{G}
is closely connected to

\Xi
, as the following result shows.

Lemma 11.4
.

For every
\epsilon>0
, there exists
c(\varepsilon)\in(0,\infty)
such that,
for every
A\subset\mathbb{Z}
and
m\geqslant 1
,

(z_{\mathbf{q}}-\varepsilon)m\,\Xi(\{m\}\times A)-\mathrm{e}^{-c(\varepsilon)m}\leqslant\widetilde{G}(\{m\}\times A)\leqslant(z_{\mathbf{q}}+\varepsilon)m\,\Xi(\{m\}\times A)+\mathrm{e}^{-c(\varepsilon)m}\,.

Together with Proposition
11.3
, this lemma
will allow to show the following estimates on
\widetilde{G}
.

Proposition 11.5
.

Fix
x>0
and
z\in\mathbb{R}
, and let
(m_{N}),(l_{N})
be
sequences such that
m_{N}\sim N^{\frac{\alpha}{\alpha-1}}\,x
and
l_{N}\sim N^{\frac{1}{2(\alpha-1)}}\,\mathfrak{s}_{\mathbf{q}}^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}}z
. Then it holds that

N\cdot N^{\frac{1}{2(\alpha-1)}}\cdot\widetilde{G}(m_{N},l_{N})\underset{N\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(x,z)\,.

(11.14)

Moreover, it holds that

\lim_{\eta\downarrow 0}\limsup_{m\to\infty}m^{1-1/\alpha}\widetilde{G}(\{m\}\times[-\eta m^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}},\eta m^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}}])=0\,.

(11.15)

Proving these statements requires some preliminary notation.
For
k\geqslant 1,m\geqslant 1
, we let
Q^{*}_{k}(m)
be the
probability that the total number of white vertices in a sequence of

k
independent random mobiles with law
\mathrm{GW}_{\bf q}
is equal to

m
. By the cyclic lemma, we have

Q^{*}_{k}(m)=\frac{k}{m}Q_{m}(-k)\,,

(11.16)

where, recalling the notation
\mu_{\circ\bullet}
of Section

7.2
for the law of the number of grandchildren in a

\mathrm{GW}_{\bf q}
-distributed mobile, we let

Q_{m}=\mu_{\circ\bullet}(\cdot+1)^{*m}
. The latter can also be
interpreted as the law at time
m
of the white Lukaciewicz path in a
forest of independent
\mathrm{GW}_{\bf q}
-distributed mobiles. Under our hypotheses (
7.5
), the local
limit theorem (
11.9
) and formula (
11.4
) imply that, as
m\to\infty
,

Q^{*}_{1}(m)\sim\frac{q^{[\alpha]}_{\mathfrak{s}_{\mathbf{q}}/2^{\alpha}}(0)}{m^{1+1/\alpha}}=\frac{2}{\mathfrak{s}_{\mathbf{q}}^{1/\alpha}|\Gamma(-1/\alpha)|m^{1+1/\alpha}}\,.

(11.17)

For
1\leqslant r\leqslant k
and
l\in\mathbb{Z}
, we also let
P_{k}(l)
be the probability that a sum of
k

independent random variables with shifted geometric law
\sum_{n\geqslant-1}2^{-n-2}\delta_{\{n\}}
equals
l
, and

P_{r}^{(k)}(l):=\frac{P_{r}(l)P_{k+1-r}(-l)}{P_{k+1}(0)}\,,

(11.18)

That is,
P_{r}^{(k)}
is the law of the value at time
r
of a bridge of a random walk with step
distribution
P_{1}(\cdot)
with duration
k+1
. We define the
distribution
\bar{P}_{k}
by the formula

\bar{P}_{k}:=\frac{1}{k}\sum_{r=1}^{k}P_{r}^{(k)}\,,

(11.19)

which corresponds to the law at a uniformly random time in

\{1,2,\ldots,k\}
of that same random walk bridge.
Note that
\bar{P}_{k}
is a centered distribution, and moreover, it has
variance
(k+1)/3
, as shown for instance in
[
105
, Section
3.2]
.

Finally, recall that

\widehat{\mu}_{\bullet}(k)=k\mu_{\bullet}(k)/m_{\bullet}
, where

m_{\bullet}=(z_{\mathbf{q}}-1)^{-1}
,
is the size-biased law associated with
\mu_{\bullet}
defined in (
7.3
).
The key formula we will need is the following.

Lemma 11.6
.

For
k\geqslant 1
,
m\geqslant 1
and
l\in\mathbb{Z}
, one has

\Xi(m,l)=\sum_{k\geqslant 1}\widehat{\mu}_{\bullet}(k)Q^{*}_{k+1}(m+1)\bar{P}_{k}(l)\,.

(11.20)

This is an immediate application of Lemma

10.5
, which implies in fact the more detailed formula

\widehat{\mathrm{GW}}_{\bf q}^{(1)}(K=k,R=r,M=m,L=l)=\frac{\widehat{\mu}_{\bullet}(k)}{k}Q^{*}_{k+1}(m+1)P_{r}^{(k)}(l)\,,

of which (
11.20
) is obtained by summing over
r
and
k
.

Manipulating this formula will require the following estimates.
First, it is an
easy consequence of the hypotheses on
{\bf q}
that

\widehat{\mu}_{\bullet}(k)\sim\frac{\mathfrak{s}_{\mathbf{q}}}{4z_{\mathbf{q}}\Gamma(-\alpha)(2k)^{\alpha}}\,,

(11.21)

as
k\to\infty
.

Next, we state some estimates on
\bar{P}_{k}
.

Lemma 11.7
.

(i — Local limit theorem) Recalling (
11.1
), it holds that:

\lim_{k\to\infty}\sup_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}\left|\sqrt{k}\bar{P}_{k}(l)-\bar{p}_{2}\left(\frac{l}{\sqrt{k}}\right)\right|=0\,.

(11.22)

(ii — Local bound) For every
\beta>0
, there exists a constant
C=C_{\beta}\in(0,\infty)
such that, for every
k\geqslant 0
and
l\in\mathbb{Z}
,

\sqrt{k}\bar{P}_{k}(l)\leqslant\frac{C}{1+\left(\frac{|l|}{\sqrt{k}}\right)^{\beta}}\,.

(11.23)

(iii — Concentration function) There exists a constant
c>0
such
that for every integers
j\geqslant 1
and
k_{1},k_{2},\ldots,k_{j}\geqslant 1
,

\sup_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}\bar{P}_{k_{1}}*\bar{P}_{k_{2}}*\cdots*\bar{P}_{k_{j}}(l)\leqslant\frac{c}{\sqrt{k_{1}+k_{2}+\cdots+k_{j}}}\,.

(11.24)

Proof.

Recall that we use the notation
\lesssim
for upper bounds that
hold up to some universal multiplicative constant which we do not want to
keep track of.

(i) For a given
l\in\mathbb{Z}
, the difference in absolute values is bounded by

\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}s\,\left|\frac{\sqrt{k}P_{\lceil ks\rceil}(l)\,\sqrt{k}P_{k+1-\lceil ks\rceil}(-l)}{\sqrt{k}P_{k+1}(0)}-\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{l^{2}}{4sk}}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{l^{2}}{4(1-s)k}}}{\sqrt{4\pi s(1-s)}}\right|\,,

and the integral on
[\varepsilon,1-\varepsilon]
is immediately controlled by the
local limit theorem, since the variance of a random variable with law

P_{1}
is
2
. It remains to estimate the boundary terms, that
it, to show that the integral outside
[\varepsilon,1-\varepsilon]
is uniformly
small in
l
as
n\to\infty
, provided
\varepsilon
has been chosen small
enough. Let us
look at the integral on
[0,\varepsilon]
, the other one is dealt with
by symmetry. First, we observe that the sequence

\sup_{k/2\leqslant r\leqslant k+1}\sup_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}P_{r}(l)/P_{k+1}(0),k\geqslant 1
is
bounded, as a consequence of the local limit theorem for
P_{r}
. Therefore,
Assuming that
\varepsilon\in(0,1/2)
, we can bound
ratio
P_{k+1-\lceil ks\rceil}(-l)/P_{k+1}(0)
uniformly in
s\in[0,\varepsilon]
and

l\in\mathbb{Z}
, and it remains to check that:

\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\limsup_{k\to\infty}\sup_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{k}}\sum_{r=1}^{\epsilon k}P_{r}(l)=0\,.

However, using the local limit theorem for
P_{r}
again, we have

\sup_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}P_{r}(l)=O(1/\sqrt{r})
, so that the last sum above is

O(\sqrt{\varepsilon k})
uniformly in
l
, which implies
(
11.22
).

(ii) We
rely on the refined bounds on the local limit theorem stated in

[
124
, Theorem VII.3.16]
, which entail that, for every

\beta>0
,

C(\beta)=\sup_{l\in\mathbb{Z},r\geqslant 1}\left(1+\left(\frac{|l|}{\sqrt{r}}\right)^{\beta}\right)\sqrt{r}P_{r}(l)<\infty\,.

Therefore, using (
11.18
) and (
11.19
), it holds that for every
k\geqslant 0,l\in\mathbb{Z}
,

\displaystyle\sqrt{k}\bar{P}_{k}(l)

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{r=1}^{k}\frac{C(\beta)^{2}}{\sqrt{r(k+1-r)}}\cdot\frac{1}{\left(1+\left(\frac{|l|}{\sqrt{r}}\right)^{\beta}\right)\left(1+\left(\frac{|l|}{\sqrt{k+1-r}}\right)^{\beta}\right)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\Big(\sum_{r=1}^{k}\frac{C(\beta)^{2}}{\sqrt{r(k+1-r)}}\Big)\cdot\frac{1}{\left(1+\left(\frac{|l|}{\sqrt{k}}\right)^{\beta}\right)}\,,

which gives the result since the sum is a converging Riemann sum.
(iii) Observe that (i)
entails that

\sup_{k\geqslant 1}\sup_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}\sqrt{k}\bar{P}_{k}(l)<\infty\,,

so that the wanted result is a direct application of

[
34
, Theorem 33.1.1]
.
∎

We can now proceed with the proofs of Propositions

11.3
and
11.5
.

Proof of Proposition
11.3
.

We apply (
11.20
) to
m=m_{N}
and
l=l_{N}
, to obtain that

\Xi(m_{N},l_{N})=\sum_{k\geqslant 1}\widehat{\mu}_{\bullet}(k)Q^{*}_{k+1}(m_{N}+1)\bar{P}_{k}(l_{N})

Next, fix
\varepsilon>0
.
The local limit theorem and dominated
convergence, justified by the fact that

N^{\frac{\alpha}{\alpha-1}}Q^{*}_{k+1}(\cdot)
and

N^{\frac{1}{2(\alpha-1)}}\bar{P}_{k}(\cdot)
are uniformly bounded
functions for
k\geqslant\varepsilon N^{\frac{1}{\alpha-1}}
, entail that

\displaystyle 2\mathfrak{s}_{\mathbf{q}}^{1-\frac{1}{2\alpha}}N\cdot N^{\frac{\alpha}{(\alpha-1)}}\cdot N^{\frac{1}{2(\alpha-1)}}\,\sum_{k>\varepsilon N^{1/(\alpha-1)}}\widehat{\mu}_{\bullet}(k)Q^{*}_{k+1}(m_{N}+1)\bar{P}_{k}(l_{N})

\displaystyle\xrightarrow[\eqref{eq:ballot},\eqref{eq:cycliclemmaQ},\eqref{eq:whmubulletasymp},\eqref{eq:loclimpbar}]{}

\displaystyle\int_{\varepsilon}^{\infty}\frac{{\rm d}t}{\Gamma(-\alpha)t^{\alpha}}q^{[1/\alpha]}_{t}(x)\bar{p}_{t}(z)\,,

which converges to
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(x,z)/x
as
\varepsilon\to 0
.
It remains to show the smallness of the remainder term

N\cdot N^{\frac{\alpha}{\alpha-1}}\cdot N^{\frac{1}{2(\alpha-1)}}\,\sum_{k\leqslant\varepsilon N^{1/(\alpha-1)}}\widehat{\mu}_{\bullet}(k)Q^{*}_{k+1}(m_{N}+1)\bar{P}_{k}(l_{N})\\
=N^{\frac{4\alpha-1}{2(\alpha-1)}}\sum_{k\leqslant\varepsilon N^{1/(\alpha-1)}}\widehat{\mu}_{\bullet}(k)\frac{k+1}{m_{N}+1}Q_{m_{N}+1}(-k-1)\bar{P}_{k}(l_{N}).

By the local limit theorem, it holds that

m_{N}^{1/\alpha}Q_{m_{N}+1}(-k-1)
is uniformly bounded in
k
, so we
can bound the latter term by a constant times

N^{\frac{2\alpha-3}{2(\alpha-1)}}\sum_{k\leqslant\varepsilon N^{1/(\alpha-1)}}\frac{\bar{P}_{k}(l_{N})}{k^{\alpha-1}}\,.

(11.25)

If
\alpha\in(1,3/2)
, we simply bound
\sqrt{k}\bar{P}_{k}(l)
by
a constant using (i) or (ii) in Lemma
11.7
, yielding
an upper bound of the form of a constant times

N^{\frac{2\alpha-3}{2(\alpha-1)}}\sum_{k\leqslant\varepsilon N^{\frac{1}{\alpha-1}}}k^{1/2-\alpha}\lesssim\varepsilon^{3/2-\alpha}\,,

which goes to
0
as
\varepsilon\to 0
, as wanted.
Now suppose that
\alpha\in[3/2,2)
. If
z=0
, then
the result follows directly from Fatou’s lemma and the fact that

\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(x,0)=\infty
. So we assume that
z\neq 0
and use the
bound (ii) in Lemma

11.7
for
\beta=1
, yielding

\bar{P}_{k}(l_{N})\leqslant C_{1}(1+|l_{N}|)^{-1}=O(N^{-1/2(\alpha-1)})
(this is where we use that

z\neq 0
), and this gives
an upper bound for (
11.25
) of the form of a constant
multiple of

N^{\frac{\alpha-2}{\alpha-1}}\sum_{k\leqslant\varepsilon N^{\frac{1}{\alpha-1}}}k^{1-\alpha}\lesssim\varepsilon^{2-\alpha}\,.

Again, this goes to
0
as
\varepsilon\to 0
.
The proof of (
11.13
) goes along similar lines,
but is simpler: in the error term, it suffices to bound

\bar{P}_{k}((-\eta N^{1/2(\alpha-1)},\eta N^{1/2(\alpha-1)}))
by
1
.
∎

Next, we turn to the comparison between
\Xi
and
\widetilde{G}
.

Proof of Lemma
11.4
.

For
m\geqslant 1
and
A\subset\mathbb{Z}
, write

\widetilde{G}(\{m\}\times A)=\widehat{\mathrm{GW}}^{(1)}_{\bf q}(\#C_{\circ}\mathbbm{1}_{\{M=m,L\in A\}})
,
so that

(z_{\mathbf{q}}-\varepsilon)m\,\Xi(\{m\}\times A)+R_{m}(\varepsilon)\leqslant\widetilde{G}(\{m\}\times A)\leqslant(z_{\mathbf{q}}+\varepsilon)m\,\Xi(\{m\}\times A)+R_{m}(\varepsilon)\,,

where
R_{m}(\varepsilon)=\widehat{\mathrm{GW}}^{(1)}_{\bf q}(\#C_{\circ}\mathbbm{1}_{\{M=m,|\#C_{\circ}-z_{\mathbf{q}}m|>\varepsilon m\}})
.
It remains to show that
R_{m}(\varepsilon)
is exponentially small.
To this end, let us work under the law

\mathrm{GW}_{\bf q}^{\otimes\infty}
of an infinite forest of i.i.d. trees with law
\mathrm{GW}_{\bf q}
, and let
v_{1},v_{2},\ldots
be the list of white
vertices of this infinite forest listed in lexicographical (depth-first) order. If

\chi_{i}
is the number of children of
v_{i}
plus
1
, then

(\chi_{i}:i\geqslant 1)
is an i.i.d. sequence with geometric law

\sum_{k\geqslant 1}\mu_{\circ}(k-1)\delta_{k}
, which has expectation

z_{\mathbf{q}}
, and exponential
moments. Hence, for a fixed
\varepsilon>0
, Cramér’s theorem
implies that, for some
C(\varepsilon)\in(0,\infty)
,

\mathrm{GW}_{\bf q}^{\otimes\infty}\left(\left|\sum_{i=1}^{m}\chi_{i}-z_{\mathbf{q}}m\right|>\varepsilon m\right)\leqslant\mathrm{e}^{-C(\varepsilon)m}

(11.26)

for every
m\geqslant 1
. On the other
hand, letting
\#V_{\circ}[k]
be the number of white vertices of the first
k
trees
in the infinite forest, Lemma

10.5
implies that
R_{m}(\varepsilon)
writes

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathrm{GW}}^{(1)}_{\bf q}(\#C_{\circ}\mathbbm{1}_{\{M=m,|\#C_{\circ}-z_{\mathbf{q}}m|>\varepsilon m\}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\geqslant 1}\widehat{\mu}_{\bullet}(k)\mathrm{GW}_{\bf q}^{\otimes\infty}\left(\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m+1}\chi_{i}\right)\mathbbm{1}_{\{\#V_{\circ}[k+1]=m+1,\left|\sum_{i=1}^{m+1}\chi_{i}-z_{\mathbf{q}}m\right|>\varepsilon m\}}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathrm{GW}_{\bf q}^{\otimes\infty}\left(\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m+1}\chi_{i}\right)\mathbbm{1}_{\{\left|\sum_{i=1}^{m+1}\chi_{i}-z_{\mathbf{q}}m\right|>\varepsilon m\}}\right)\,,

where the second inequality is obtained by
bounding the indicator that
\#V_{\circ}[k+1]=m+1
by
1
, and using
the fact that
\widehat{\mu}_{\bullet}
is a probability distribution.
By the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, the latter
quantity is bounded by

\mathrm{GW}_{\bf q}^{\otimes\infty}\left(\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m+1}\chi_{i}\right)^{2}\right)^{1/2}\mathrm{GW}_{\bf q}^{\otimes\infty}\left(\left|\sum_{i=1}^{m+1}\chi_{i}-z_{\mathbf{q}}m\right|>\varepsilon m\right)^{1/2}\,,

which, by (
11.26
), is bounded by
\mathrm{e}^{-c(\varepsilon)m}
for some

c(\varepsilon)\in(0,C(\varepsilon))
, as wanted.
∎

Finally, the proof of Proposition
11.5

consists in using Lemma
11.4
to transfer
the estimates of Proposition
11.3
to
\widetilde{G}
.

Proof of Proposition
11.5
.

The limit (
11.14
) is a direct consequence of (
11.12
),
with Lemma
11.4
.
To obtain (
11.15
), we fix
\eta\in(0,\infty]
and write:

\widetilde{G}(\{m\}\times[-\eta m^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}},\eta m^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}}])\leqslant(z_{\mathbf{q}}+1)m\,\Xi(\{m\}\times[-\eta m^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}},\eta m^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}}])+\mathrm{e}^{-c(1)m}.

Applying (
11.13
) for
x=1

and

m=N^{\alpha/(\alpha-1)}
, we obtain (
11.15
) by first
letting
N\to\infty
and then
\eta\downarrow 0
.
∎

11.4
Joint convergence of the belt and the buckle

We can now give the proof of Proposition
11.1
, which
will consist in studying a joint convergence of the pair

((\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}},\hat{v}_{\circ}),(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}},\hat{v}^{\prime}_{\circ},c))

under the sigma-finite measure

\mathrm{U}_{\bf q}:=\widehat{\mathrm{GW}}_{\bf q}({\rm d}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}},\hat{v}_{\circ}))\widetilde{\mathrm{GW}}_{\bf q}({\rm d}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}},\hat{v}^{\prime}_{\circ},c))\mathbbm{1}_{\{\ell_{\mathcal{T}}(\hat{v}_{\circ})=-\ell_{\mathcal{P}}(\hat{v}^{\prime}_{\circ})\}}

appearing in Proposition
10.6
, and which, we
recall, is pushed to the measure
\tilde{w}_{{\bf q}}^{{2\bullet}}/2
by the mapping

\Phi
. As explained at
the beginning of this
section, we will be interested in conditioning this measure on the total number

\#V_{\circ}=M(\mathcal{T})+M(\mathcal{P})
of white vertices of the
unicyclomobile
\Phi((\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}},\hat{v}_{\circ}),(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}},\hat{v}^{\prime}_{\circ},c))
being some large integer
n
. If
F
is some
non-negative measurable function, we have

\mathrm{U}_{\bf q}(F(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}},\hat{v}_{\circ})\mathbbm{1}_{\{\#V_{\circ}=n\}})=\widehat{\mathrm{GW}}_{\bf q}\left(\widetilde{G}(n-M,-L)F(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}},\hat{v}_{\circ})\right)

(11.27)

where, as before,

\widetilde{G}(m,l)=\widetilde{\mathrm{GW}}_{\bf q}(M=m,L=l)
.
Now recall the notation of Section
7.3
, and consider the rescaled
Lukasiewicz and label process
of
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}}
:

S^{(n)}=\frac{2S^{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}}}_{\lfloor n\cdot\rfloor}}{(\mathfrak{s}_{\mathbf{q}}n)^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}}\,,\qquad L^{(n)}=\frac{L^{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}}}_{\lfloor n\cdot\rfloor}}{(\mathfrak{s}_{\mathbf{q}}n)^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}}}\,.

We also let
a
be the rank of
\hat{v}_{\circ}
in
the depth-first order of white vertices of
\mathcal{T}
, that is the
integer such that
v^{\circ}_{a}=\hat{v}_{\circ}
, and let

\theta=\#V_{\circ}(\mathcal{T})
.

Proposition 11.8
.

It holds that, for every bounded uniformly continuous function
F
,

n^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}}\mathrm{U}_{\bf q}\left(F\left(S^{(n)},L^{(n)},\frac{a}{n},\frac{\theta}{n}\right)\mathbbm{1}_{\{\#V_{\circ}=n-2\}}\right)\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}\mathrm{Cst}\cdot\mathbf{N}^{\bullet}\left(F(X,Z,t_{\bullet},\sigma)\cdot\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}\left(1-\sigma,-Z_{t_{\bullet}}\right)\right)\,.

By
applying this result to
F=1
, we obtain

n^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}}\mathrm{U}_{\bf q}\left(\#V_{\circ}=n-2\right)\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}\mathrm{Cst}\cdot\mathbf{N}^{\bullet}\left(\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}\left(1-\sigma,-Z_{t_{\bullet}}\right)\right)\,,

(11.28)

which allows to obtain the conditioned result stated in Proposition

11.1
by dividing.
To prove Proposition
11.8
, we rewrite the
left-hand side of the displayed expression of the statement by using
(
11.27
), as

\displaystyle n^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}}\widehat{\mathrm{GW}}_{\bf q}\left(\widetilde{G}(n-M,-L)F\left(S^{(n)},L^{(n)},\frac{a}{n},\frac{M}{n}\right)\right)

(11.29)

\displaystyle=n^{1+\frac{1}{2\alpha}}\int_{0}^{1}{\rm d}x\,\widehat{\mathrm{GW}}_{\bf q}(M=\lceil nx\rceil)\,\widehat{\mathrm{GW}}_{\bf q}\left(\widetilde{G}(n-\lceil nx\rceil,-L^{(n)}_{a_{n}/n})F\left(S^{(n)},L^{(n)},\frac{a_{n}}{n},\frac{\lceil nx\rceil}{n}\right)\,\Big|\,M=\lceil nx\rceil\right)\,.

Note that, by the definition of
\widehat{\mathrm{GW}}_{\bf q}
just
before (
10.10
), it holds that
\widehat{\mathrm{GW}}_{\bf q}(M=n)=\sum_{y\geqslant 0}\Xi^{*y}(\{n\}\times\mathbb{Z})
. Since
n^{2-1/\alpha}\,\Xi(\{n\}\times\mathbb{Z})
converges as
n\to\infty
by (
11.13
), a standard
renewal theorem implies the existence of a
constant
c\in(0,\infty)
such that
\widehat{\mathrm{GW}}_{\bf q}(M=\lceil nx\rceil)\sim c\lceil nx\rceil^{-1/\alpha}
, with a uniformly bounded error over values of

x
in a compact subset of
(0,1]
.
The next lemma says that, in a sense, it is almost impossible to
distinguish a large white leaf-pointed random mobile with law

\widehat{\mathrm{GW}}_{\bf q}
from a large random mobile with law

\mathrm{GW}_{\bf q}
of same size, marked by a uniformly chosen white
leaf.

Lemma 11.9
.

The total variation distance between the laws

\widehat{\mathrm{GW}}_{\bf q}({\rm d}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}},\hat{v}_{\circ})\,|\,M=n)\qquad\mbox{ and }\qquad\mathrm{GW}_{\bf q}({\rm d}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}}\,|\,\#V_{\circ}=n+1)\frac{\sum_{v\in\widehat{V}_{\circ}(\mathcal{T})}\delta_{v}({\rm d}\hat{v}_{\circ})}{\#\widehat{V}_{\circ}(\mathcal{T})}

converges to
0
as
n\to\infty
.
Moreover, if we let
N_{\circ}(k)
be such that
v^{\circ}_{N_{\circ}(k)}
is
the
k
-th white leaf appearing in the list

v_{1}^{\circ},v_{2}^{\circ},\ldots,v_{\#V_{\circ}}^{\circ}
of white vertices of
\mathcal{T}
, then, under
\mathrm{GW}_{\bf q}(\cdot\,|\,\#V_{\circ}=n)
, we have

\Big(\frac{N_{\circ}(\lfloor\#\widehat{V}_{\circ}t\rfloor)}{n}:0\leqslant t\leqslant 1\Big)\underset{n\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\big(t:0\leqslant t\leqslant 1\big)\,,

in probability for the uniform norm.

Proof.

Both statements are a consequence of exponential concentration of the
number of white leaves in random mobiles, analog to that discussed in
the proof of Proposition
11.5
. Recall that, in
a
\mathrm{GW}_{\bf q}^{\otimes\infty}
-distributed random forest, we let

\chi_{i}-1
be the number of children of the
i
-th white vertex

v_{i}^{\circ}
in depth-first order of exploration. This expresses the
fact that
v_{i}^{\circ}
is a white leaf if and only if

\zeta_{i}:=\mathbbm{1}_{\{\chi_{i}-1=0\}}=1
, the latter being i.i.d. Bernoulli random
variables with expectation
1/z_{\mathbf{q}}
by
(
7.3
). Then, recalling that
\#V_{\circ}[1]
is the number
of white vertices of the first tree in the above infinite forest, we have

\mathrm{GW}_{\bf q}\big(|\#\widehat{V}_{\circ}-n/z_{\mathbf{q}}\big|>\varepsilon n\,|\,\#V_{\circ}=n\big)=\frac{\mathrm{GW}_{\bf q}^{\otimes\infty}\Big(\#V_{\circ}[1]=n,\Big|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\zeta_{i}-n/z_{\mathbf{q}}\Big|>\varepsilon n\Big)}{\mathrm{GW}_{\bf q}(\#V_{\circ}=n)}\,,

which decays exponentially fast in
n
for every fixed
\varepsilon>0
by Cramér’s theorem and the fact
that
\mathrm{GW}_{\bf q}(\#V_{\circ}=n)=Q^{*}_{1}(n)
has a power-law decay, by
(
11.17
).
Using a simple union bound, we even obtain that the
process
(n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor nt\rfloor}\zeta_{i}:0\leqslant t\leqslant 1)

under
\mathrm{GW}_{\bf q}(\cdot\,|\,\#V_{\circ}=n)
is exponentially
concentrated around
(t\#\widehat{V}_{\circ}/n:0\leqslant t\leqslant 1)
, which easily implies
the second statement since
N_{\circ}(\lfloor\cdot\rfloor)
can be seen
as a right-continuous inverse of this process. We leave details to the reader.

To prove the first statement, we express the total variation distance
between the two measures as

\sum_{(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}},\hat{v}_{\circ}):M(\mathcal{T})=n}\left|\widehat{\mathrm{GW}}_{\bf q}(\{(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}},\hat{v}_{\circ})\}\,|\,M=n)-\frac{\mathrm{GW}_{\bf q}(\{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}}\}\,|\,\#V_{\circ}=n+1)}{\#\widehat{V}_{\circ}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}})}\right|\\
=\sum_{(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}},\hat{v}_{\circ}):M(\mathcal{T})=n}\left|\frac{\mathrm{GW}_{\bf q}(\{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}}\})}{\widehat{\mathrm{GW}}_{\bf q}(M=n)}-\frac{\mathrm{GW}_{\bf q}(\{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}}\}\,|\,\#V_{\circ}=n+1)}{\#\widehat{V}_{\circ}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}})\mathrm{GW}_{\bf q}(\#V_{\circ}=n+1)}\right|\\
=\mathrm{GW}_{\bf q}\left(\frac{\mathbbm{1}_{\{M=n\}}}{\mathrm{GW}_{\bf q}(\#V_{\circ}=n+1)}\left|\frac{\#\widehat{V}_{\circ}\mathrm{GW}_{\bf q}(\#V_{\circ}=n+1)}{\widehat{\mathrm{GW}}_{\bf q}(\#V_{\circ}=n+1)}-1\right|\right)\,.

(11.30)

Now, since

\widehat{\mathrm{GW}}_{\bf q}(M=n)=\mathrm{GW}_{\bf q}(\#\widehat{V}_{\circ}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\#V_{\circ}=n+1\}})
,
we have, for every
\varepsilon>0
,

\left|\widehat{\mathrm{GW}}_{\bf q}(M=n)-\frac{n}{z_{\mathbf{q}}}\mathrm{GW}_{\bf q}(\#V_{\circ}=n+1)\right|\leqslant\varepsilon n\,\mathrm{GW}_{\bf q}(\#V_{\circ}=n+1)+\mathrm{GW}_{\bf q}\left(\#\widehat{V}_{\circ}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\#V_{\circ}=n+1,|\#\widehat{V}_{\circ}-n/z_{\mathbf{q}}|>\varepsilon n\}}\right)\,,

where

\mathrm{GW}_{\bf q}\left(\#\widehat{V}_{\circ}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\#V_{\circ}=n+1,|\#\widehat{V}_{\circ}-n/z_{\mathbf{q}}|>\varepsilon n\}}\right)\leqslant\mathrm{GW}_{\bf q}^{\otimes\infty}\Big(\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}\zeta_{i}\Big)^{2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\cdot\mathrm{GW}_{\bf q}^{\otimes\infty}\Big(\Big|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\zeta_{i}-n/z_{\mathbf{q}}\Big|>\varepsilon n\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}

has exponential decay in
n
. Therefore, using again that

\mathrm{GW}_{\bf q}(\#V_{\circ}=n+1)
has a power-law decay, it holds that

\mathrm{GW}_{\bf q}(\#V_{\circ}=n+1)/\widehat{\mathrm{GW}}_{\bf q}(M=n)\sim z_{\mathbf{q}}/n
as

n\to\infty
. Plugging this back into (
11.30
), and
applying a similar reasoning as above, distinguishing whether
z_{\mathbf{q}}\#\widehat{V}_{\circ}/n
is at a distance greater than
\varepsilon
from
1
or not, and using the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we obtain the first wanted result.
∎

Let us now fix
\varepsilon,\eta\in(0,1/2)
and consider the integral expression (
11.29
)
restricted to values of
x
in
[\varepsilon,1-\varepsilon]
, and to values of

L^{(n)}_{a_{n}/n}
in
[-\eta,\eta]^{\mathrm{c}}
.
Then, by the first statement of the previous lemma, this integral
expression is equivalent to

cn^{1+\frac{1}{2\alpha}}\int_{\varepsilon}^{1-\varepsilon}\frac{\mathrm{d}x}{(nx)^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}}\mathrm{GW}_{\bf q}\left(\frac{1}{\#\widehat{V}_{\circ}}\sum_{k=1}^{\#\widehat{V}_{\circ}}\widetilde{G}(n-\lceil nx\rceil,-L^{(n)}_{N_{\circ}(k)/n})F\Big(S^{(n)},L^{(n)},\frac{N_{\circ}(k)}{n},\frac{\lceil nx\rceil}{n}\Big)\,\Big|\,M=\lceil nx\rceil\right)\,.

By the second statement of Lemma
11.9
, together with
(
7.10
) and Proposition
11.5
for
1-x
instead
of
x
and
N=n^{(\alpha-1)/\alpha}
, this expression has an equivalent of the form

c\int_{\varepsilon}^{1-\varepsilon}\frac{{\rm d}x}{x^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}}\int_{\varepsilon}^{x}\frac{{\rm d}a}{x}\mathbf{N}^{(x)}\left(\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}\left(1-x,-Z_{a}\right)F\left(X,Z,a\right)\mathbbm{1}_{\{|Z_{a}|>\eta\}}\right)\,,

where
c\in(0,\infty)
is some constant, and
\mathbf{N}^{(x)}
is the probability measure introduced above (
1.12
) under which
(X,Z)
is the process with total
duration
x
. By (
1.12
) and (
6.1
), we may rewrite the previous expression as

c\mathbf{N}^{\bullet}\Big(\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(1-\sigma,-Z_{t_{\bullet}})F\big(X,Z,t_{\bullet}\big)\mathbbm{1}_{\{t_{\bullet}\in[\varepsilon,1-\varepsilon],|Z_{t_{\bullet}}|>\eta\}}\Big)\,,

and as we let
\eta\downarrow 0
and then
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
, the above integral converges to the limiting expression appearing in Proposition

11.1
. In order to conclude, we need to show that the
remainder terms are asymptotically negligible, which is the object of
the next lemma.

Lemma 11.10
.

For every
\varepsilon\in(0,1/2)
, it holds that

\lim_{\eta\downarrow 0}\limsup_{n\to\infty}n^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}}\mathrm{U}_{\bf q}\left(M(\mathcal{T})\wedge M(\mathcal{P})>\varepsilon n,\#V_{\circ}=n,|L|\leqslant\eta n^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}}\right)=0\,.

(11.31)

Moreover, one has

\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\limsup_{n\to\infty}n^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}}\,\mathrm{U}_{\bf q}(M(\mathcal{T})\wedge M(\mathcal{P})\leqslant\varepsilon n\,,\,\#V_{\circ}=n)=0\,.

(11.32)

Proof.

In this proof, we introduce the quantity

G(m,l)=\widehat{\mathrm{GW}}_{\bf q}(M=m,L=l)\,,

(11.33)

which we view as a measure on
\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{Z}
. Note that it
is the Green function
of the random walk whose step distribution is
\Xi
, that is,

G=\sum_{y\geqslant 0}\Xi^{*y}
. Our proofs will all rely on expansions of
expressions of the form

n^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}}\sum_{m,l}G(m,l)\widetilde{G}(n-m,-l)\,,

(11.34)

where the sum over
m
and
l
are over certain subsets of
\mathbb{N}
and

\mathbb{Z}
.
On the one hand, we can express
\widetilde{G}(n-m,-l)
by first
expressing it in terms of
\Xi
using Lemma

11.4
, and then expanding it by using
(
11.20
). On the other hand,
G(m,l)

can also be expanded using (
11.20
), yielding

\displaystyle G(m,l)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\geqslant 0\\
k\geqslant 1\\
r\geqslant 1\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1}+\cdots+k_{y}=k\\
m_{1}+\cdots+m_{y}=m\\
l_{1}+\cdots+l_{y}=l\end{subarray}}\widehat{\mu}_{\bullet}(k_{1})\cdots\widehat{\mu}_{\bullet}(k_{y})\bar{P}_{k_{1}}(l_{1})\cdots\bar{P}_{k_{y}}(l_{y})Q^{*}_{k_{1}+1}(m_{1}+1)\cdots Q^{*}_{k_{y}+1}(m_{y}+1)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\geqslant 0\\
k\geqslant 1\\
r\geqslant 1\end{subarray}}Q^{*}_{k+y}(m+y)\sum_{k_{1}+\cdots+k_{y}=k}\widehat{\mu}_{\bullet}(k_{1})\cdots\widehat{\mu}_{\bullet}(k_{y})\left(\bar{P}_{k_{1}}*\cdots*\bar{P}_{k_{y}}\right)(l)\,.

(11.35)

Such expansions will be especially useful in conjunction with the
concentration bound (
11.24
), which allows one to
get rid of the sum over
k_{1},\ldots,k_{y}
, and of the dependence on
l
. Let us demonstrate this by proving (
11.31
).
For a given
\varepsilon\in(0,1/2)
and
\eta>0
, we write the quantity of
interest in the form (
11.34
):

n^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}}\mathrm{U}_{\bf q}\left(M(\mathcal{T})\wedge M(\mathcal{P})>\varepsilon n,\#V_{\circ}=n,|L|\leqslant\eta n^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}}\right)=n^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}}\sum_{\varepsilon n\leqslant m\leqslant(1-\varepsilon)n}\sum_{|l|\leqslant\eta n^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}}}G(n-m,l)\widetilde{G}(m,-l)\,.

(11.36)

Next, we expand
G
as in (
11.35
), and use the bound
(
11.24
), showing

G(m,l)\lesssim\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\geqslant 0\\
k\geqslant 1\end{subarray}}\frac{Q^{*}_{k+y}(m+y)\widehat{\mu}_{\bullet}^{*y}(k)}{\sqrt{k}}\,.

(11.37)

This yields that the quantity of
(
11.36
) is

\displaystyle\lesssim n^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}}\sum_{\varepsilon n\leqslant m\leqslant(1-\varepsilon)n}\widetilde{G}(\{n-m\}\times[-\eta n^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}},\eta n^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}}])\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\geqslant 0\\
k\geqslant 1\end{subarray}}\frac{Q^{*}_{k+y}(m+y)\widehat{\mu}_{\bullet}^{*y}(k)}{\sqrt{k}}

(11.38)

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(n^{1-\frac{1}{\alpha}}\sup_{\varepsilon n\leqslant m\leqslant(1-\varepsilon)n}\widetilde{G}(\{m\}\times[-\eta n^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}},\eta n^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}}])\right)\cdot n^{\frac{3}{2\alpha}-1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\geqslant 0\\
k\geqslant 1\end{subarray}}\frac{Q^{*}_{k+y}([y+\varepsilon n,y+(1-\varepsilon)n])\widehat{\mu}_{\bullet}^{*y}(k)}{\sqrt{k}}\,,

and we observe that the term in brackets involving
\widetilde{G}
is
uniformly bounded in
n
by a function of
\varepsilon
and
\eta
that
vanishes as
\eta\to 0
for
\varepsilon
fixed, as a consequence
of (
11.15
). Hence, to conclude, it suffices to show that the rest of
the expression is uniformly bounded.
To this end, we aim at getting rid of the manifestly superfluous presence of
y
in
the term
Q^{*}
. To achieve this, we use the left-tail bound
(
11.11
), yielding (note that the possible values of

y
are trivially less than or equal to
n
)

Q^{*}_{k+y}([y+\varepsilon n,y+(1-\varepsilon)n])\lesssim\exp\left(-C\frac{k+y}{(n+y)^{1/\alpha}}\right)\lesssim\exp\left(-C\left(\frac{k}{n^{1/\alpha}}\wedge n^{1-1/\alpha}\right)\right),

(11.39)

as well as

\widehat{\mu}_{\bullet}^{*y}(k)\lesssim\exp\left(-C\frac{y}{k^{\alpha-1}}\right)\,,

for some universal
C\in(0,\infty)
. These two facts together easily
entail that the contribution of the values of
y\geqslant n^{1-\frac{1}{\alpha}+\lambda}
to the above sum are
stretched-exponentially decaying for every
\lambda\in(0,(2/\alpha-1)\wedge(1/2\alpha))
. Since the rest
of the terms behave as powers of
n
, we can restrict without loss of
generality our attention to values
y\leqslant n^{1-\frac{1}{\alpha}+\lambda}
.
If we further assume that
k\geqslant\delta n^{1/\alpha}
, then the local
limit theorem (
11.9
) entails that

n^{\frac{3}{2\alpha}-1}\cdot\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\leqslant n^{1-\frac{1}{\alpha}+\lambda}\\
k\geqslant\delta n^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}\end{subarray}}\frac{Q^{*}_{k+y}([y+\varepsilon n,y+(1-\varepsilon)n])\widehat{\mu}_{\bullet}^{*y}(k)}{\sqrt{k}}\lesssim\int_{\delta}^{\infty}t^{\alpha-\frac{5}{2}}\,{\rm d}t\,\int_{\varepsilon}^{1-\varepsilon}q_{2t/\mathfrak{s}_{\mathbf{q}}^{1/\alpha}}^{[1/\alpha]}(x){\rm d}x

where the dominated convergence is justified by a use of the
stretched-exponential bound (
11.39
), and where we
have used the fact that
\sum_{y\geqslant 0}\widehat{\mu}_{\bullet}(k)\lesssim k^{\alpha-2}
by virtue of the
renewal theorem.
In turn, this integral converges to a finite value as
\delta\to 0
, due to the fact that
q^{[1/\alpha]}_{ct}(x)=(ct/x)q^{[\alpha]}_{x}(-t)
.
Hence, to conclude, it remains to show that the remaining terms
k\leqslant\delta n^{1/\alpha}
have a negligible role.
Resuming from the expression (
11.38
) and re-expressing

Q^{*}_{k+y}(m+y)
by the cyclic lemma (
11.16
), we
obtain that this term, restricted to the said values of
y
(so that
in particular, one has
m+y\leqslant n
for large enough
n
), is

\lesssim R(\eta)n^{\frac{3}{2\alpha}-1}\max_{\varepsilon n\leqslant m\leqslant n}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\leqslant n^{1-\frac{1}{\alpha}+\lambda}\\
k\leqslant\delta n^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}\end{subarray}}\frac{(k+y)\widehat{\mu}_{\bullet}^{*y}(k)Q_{m}(-k-y)}{\sqrt{k}}\,.

(11.40)

Finally, an application of the local limit theorem shows that

Q_{m}(-k-y)\lesssim m^{-1/\alpha}
, uniformly in
k,y
. Putting things
together, we obtain a bound of the form

C(\varepsilon)R(\eta)n^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}-1}\sum_{k\leqslant\delta n^{1/\alpha}}\left(k^{\alpha-\frac{3}{2}}+n^{1-\frac{1}{\alpha}+\lambda}k^{\alpha-\frac{5}{2}}\right)\lesssim C(\varepsilon)R(\eta)\left(\delta^{\alpha-1/2}+n^{-\frac{1}{2\alpha}+\lambda}\sum_{k\leqslant\delta n^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}}k^{\alpha-\frac{5}{2}}\right)\,,

for some finite constant
C(\varepsilon)
depending only on
\varepsilon
. The
second term is
\lesssim n^{\lambda-1/2\alpha}
if
\alpha<3/2
, and
is
\lesssim\delta^{\alpha-3/2}n^{1-2/\alpha+\lambda}
if
\alpha\geqslant 3/2
(with an extra logarithmic factor if
\alpha=3/2
), so that it
vanishes as
n\to\infty
. Since the first term
vanishes as
\delta\to 0
, this concludes the proof of
(
11.31
).

The proof of (
11.32
) is separated in two parts,
depending on which of

M(\mathcal{T})
or
M(\mathcal{P})
is smaller than
\varepsilon n
, and
follows the same general lines as above.

We start with the case where
M(\mathcal{T})\leqslant\varepsilon n
. The relevant form is now

n^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}}\mathrm{U}_{\bf q}(M(\mathcal{T})\leqslant\varepsilon n\,,\,\#V_{\circ}=n)=n^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}}\sum_{m=0}^{\varepsilon n}\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}G(m,l)\widetilde{G}(n-m,-l)\,.

To estimate the latter, we use Lemma

11.4
, which yields, for
0\leqslant m\leqslant n/2
,

\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}G(m,l)\widetilde{G}(n-m,-l)\lesssim n\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}\Xi(n-m,-l)G(m,l)+n\mathrm{e}^{-c(1)n/2}G(\{m\}\times\mathbb{Z})\,,

(11.41)

and the remainder term has exponential decay as
n\to\infty

uniformly in
0\leqslant m\leqslant n/2
since
G(\{m\}\times\mathbb{Z})=\widehat{\mathrm{GW}}_{\bf q}(M=m)
is of order
m^{-1/\alpha}
, as
we already observed.
Next, we express
\Xi(n-m,-l)
by (
11.20
) to get

\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}\Xi(n-m,-l)G(m,l)=n\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{r\geqslant 1}\widehat{\mu}_{\bullet}(r)Q^{*}_{r+1}(n-m+1)G(m,l)\,,

and then we expand the Green function
G(m,l)
as in
(
11.35
),
showing
that the last displayed quantity equals

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\geqslant 0\\
k\geqslant 1\\
r\geqslant 1\end{subarray}}\widehat{\mu}_{\bullet}(r)Q^{*}_{r+1}(n-m+1)Q^{*}_{k+y}(m+y)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1}+\cdots+k_{y}=k\\
k_{1},\ldots,k_{y}\geqslant 1\end{subarray}}\widehat{\mu}_{\bullet}(k_{1})\cdots\widehat{\mu}_{\bullet}(k_{y})\left(\bar{P}_{k_{1}}*\cdots*\bar{P}_{k_{y}}*\bar{P}_{r}\right)(0)

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{n}\sum_{y\geqslant 0}\sum_{k\geqslant 0,r\geqslant 1}\frac{(r+1)\widehat{\mu}_{\bullet}(r)\widehat{\mu}_{\bullet}^{*y}(k)}{\sqrt{r+k}}Q^{*}_{k+y}(m+y)Q_{n-m+1}(-r-1).

Here we have used (iii) in Lemma

11.7
to control the convolution term, and (
11.16
) to re-express
Q^{*}_{r+1}(n-m+1)
.
Summing this over
0\leqslant m\leqslant\varepsilon n
and using (
11.41
), this yields

n^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}}\mathrm{U}_{\bf q}(M(\mathcal{T})\leqslant\varepsilon n\,,\,\#V_{\circ}=n)\\
\lesssim n^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\geqslant 0\\
k\geqslant 0\\
r\geqslant 1\end{subarray}}\frac{(r+1)\widehat{\mu}_{\bullet}(r)\widehat{\mu}_{\bullet}^{*y}(k)}{\sqrt{r+k}}\sum_{0\leqslant m\leqslant\varepsilon n}Q^{*}_{k+y}(y+m)Q_{n-m+1}(-r-1)+R_{n}\,,

(11.42)

where
R_{n}
has exponential decay, and can be removed from further discussions.
Moreover, similar argument as around (
11.39
) show that we can restrict our
attention to values of
y
at most
n^{1-1/\alpha+\lambda}
, which we
now assume. Here, one has to pay attention to values of
k,r
that
significantly deviate from the typical magnitude
n^{1/\alpha}
. To
fix the ideas, we consider
\delta>0
and consider the situation where

k\leqslant\delta n^{1/\alpha}
and
r\geqslant n^{1/\alpha}
.
To estimate this last term, we write

\displaystyle n^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\leqslant n^{1-\frac{1}{\alpha}+\lambda}\\
k\leqslant\delta n^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}\\
r\geqslant n^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}\end{subarray}}\widehat{\mu}_{\bullet}^{*y}(k)\sum_{0\leqslant m\leqslant\varepsilon n}Q^{*}_{k+y}(y+m)\frac{(r+1)\widehat{\mu}_{\bullet}(r)}{\sqrt{r+k}}Q_{n-m+1}(-r-1)

\displaystyle\lesssim n^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\leqslant n^{1-\frac{1}{\alpha}+\lambda}\\
k\leqslant\delta n^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}\end{subarray}}\widehat{\mu}_{\bullet}^{*y}(k)\sum_{0\leqslant m\leqslant\varepsilon n}Q^{*}_{k+y}(y+m)\sum_{r\geqslant n^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}}\frac{r^{1-\alpha}}{\sqrt{r+k}}Q_{n-m+1}(-r-1)

(11.43)

and we smoothen the terms of this sum over
r
by splitting it in
scales, rewriting it as

\sum_{w=1}^{\infty}\sum_{r=2^{w-1}n^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}}^{2^{w}n^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}-1}\frac{r^{1-\alpha}}{\sqrt{r+k}}Q_{n-m+1}(-r-1)\lesssim\sum_{w=1}^{\infty}\frac{\left(2^{w}n^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}\right)^{1-\alpha}}{\sqrt{2^{w}n^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}+k}}Q_{n-m+1}(-1-[2^{w-1}n^{\frac{1}{\alpha}},2^{w}n^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}-1])\,.

This allows to use the left-tail bound
(
11.10
), uniformly over the value of
n-m\geqslant(1-\varepsilon)n
, to
bound the last term by
\exp(-c2^{w-1})
, for some finite constant

c
. Reverting the steps, we obtain a bound of the form

\lesssim n^{-\frac{1}{\alpha}}\sum_{r\geqslant n^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}}\frac{r^{1-\alpha}}{\sqrt{r+k}}\exp\left(-c\frac{r}{n^{1/\alpha}}\right)\,,

possibly for some other universal constant
c
.
This quantity does not depend on
m
anymore, and so we may estimate
(
11.43
) by

\lesssim n^{-\frac{1}{2\alpha}}\sum_{k\leqslant\delta n^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}}\sum_{y\geqslant 0}\widehat{\mu}_{\bullet}^{*y}(k)\sum_{r\geqslant n^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}}\frac{\exp(-cr/n^{1/\alpha})}{r^{\alpha-1}\sqrt{r+k}}\lesssim\int_{0}^{\delta}{\rm d}t\int_{1}^{\infty}{\rm d}s\frac{\exp(-cs)}{s^{\alpha-1}t^{2-\alpha}\sqrt{s+t}}\,.

The latter integral is finite for every
\delta>0
, as shown by
a polar change of coordinates.
Dealing with the situation where
k\leqslant\delta n^{1/\alpha}
and

r\leqslant n^{1/\alpha}
is easier, as one can simply use the bound

Q_{n-m+1}(-r-1)\lesssim n^{-1/\alpha}
provided by the local limit theorem,
which is uniform in
m\leqslant\varepsilon n
and
r
.
The contribution of the
remaining terms,
when
k\geqslant\delta n^{1/\alpha}
, can then be bounded
similarly, using, as a final extra input, the fact that

Q^{*}_{k+y}([y,y+\varepsilon n])\lesssim Q^{*}_{k}([0,\varepsilon n])
for
k\geqslant\delta n^{1/\alpha}
and
y\leqslant n^{1-1/\alpha+\lambda}
(choosing

0<\lambda<2/\alpha-1
), yielding the estimate

\lesssim n^{-\frac{1}{2\alpha}}\sum_{k\leqslant\delta n^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}}\sum_{r\geqslant 1}Q^{*}_{k}([0,\varepsilon n])\frac{\exp(-cr/n^{1/\alpha})}{k^{2-\alpha}r^{\alpha-1}\sqrt{r+k}}\lesssim\int_{\delta}^{\infty}{\rm d}t\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}s\frac{\exp(-cs)\int_{0}^{\varepsilon}\,q^{[1/\alpha]}_{2t/\mathfrak{s}_{\mathbf{q}}^{1/\alpha}}(x)\,{\rm d}x}{s^{\alpha-1}t^{2-\alpha}\sqrt{s+t}}\,.

This is a converging integral, as can again be checked by
