changing to polar coordinates, and this goes to
0
as

\varepsilon\downarrow 0
, as wanted.

The case where the buckle part
M(\mathcal{P})\leqslant\varepsilon n
is small in
(
11.32
) is dealt with by similar
methods. We start from

\displaystyle n^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}}\mathrm{U}_{\bf q}(M(\mathcal{P})\leqslant\varepsilon n\,,\,\#V_{\circ}=n)

\displaystyle\lesssim n^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}}\sum_{m=0}^{\varepsilon n}\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}G(n-m,l)\widetilde{G}(m,-l)

\displaystyle\lesssim n^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}}\sum_{m=0}^{\varepsilon n}\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}m\Xi(m,-l)G(n-m,l)+n^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}}\sum_{m=0}^{\varepsilon n}e^{-c(1)m}\sup_{0\leqslant m\leqslant\varepsilon n}G(\{n\}\times\mathbb{Z})\,.

Here, we have used Lemma
11.4
. We
already observed several times that
G(\{n\}\times\mathbb{Z})\lesssim n^{-1/\alpha}
, and therefore the second term is negligible and can
be omitted from the discussion. We then expand the main term as we
are now used to, similarly to the previous discussion. This yields
an estimate

n^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}}\sum_{m=0}^{\varepsilon n}m\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\geqslant 0\\
k\geqslant 1\\
r\geqslant 1\end{subarray}}\frac{\widehat{\mu}_{\bullet}(r)\widehat{\mu}_{\bullet}^{*y}(k)}{\sqrt{k+r}}Q^{*}_{r+1}(m+1)Q^{*}_{k+y}(n-m+y)\,.

Now, more than before, we will make use of cyclic lemmas to adapt to
the values of
k,r
under study. As a preliminary remark, the same
argument as around (
11.39
) show that we can restrict our attention to
y\leqslant n^{1-\frac{1}{\alpha}+\lambda}
. We will now understand the behavior
of the above estimate depending on whether
k
and
r
are small (say

\leqslant n^{1/\alpha}
) or large (
>n^{1/\alpha}
). The contribution of
both
k,r
small is obtained by applying the cyclic lemma to the terms

Q^{*}_{r+1}(m+1)
and
Q^{*}_{k+y}(n-m+y)
, giving the
estimate

n^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}-1}\sum_{m=0}^{\varepsilon n}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\leqslant n^{1-1/\alpha+\lambda}\\
k\leqslant n^{1/\alpha}\\
r\leqslant n^{1/\alpha}\end{subarray}}\frac{r\widehat{\mu}_{\bullet}(r)(k+y)\widehat{\mu}_{\bullet}^{*y}(k)}{\sqrt{k+r}}Q_{m+1}(-r-1)Q_{n-m+y}(-k-y)\,.

In this expression, we claim that we can get rid of the additive
factor
y
in the quantity
(k+y)
numerator: this is proved by
a similar argument to the one following (
11.40
), and is left to the reader. Then, we simply use
the local limit theorem in the form
Q_{m+1}(-r-1)\lesssim m^{-1/\alpha}
and
Q_{n-m+y}(-k-y)\lesssim n^{-1/\alpha}
, allowing
to sum
\widehat{\mu}_{\bullet}^{*y}(k)
over values of
y
, yielding a bound

n^{-\frac{1}{2\alpha}-1}\sum_{r,k\leqslant n^{1/\alpha}}\frac{k^{\alpha-1}}{r^{\alpha-1}\sqrt{k+r}}\sum_{m=0}^{\varepsilon n}m^{-1/\alpha}\lesssim\varepsilon^{1-\frac{1}{\alpha}}\int_{0}^{1}{\rm d}t\int_{0}^{1}\frac{t^{\alpha-1}{\rm d}s}{s^{\alpha-1}\sqrt{s+t}}\,,

a converging integral. We see that this vanishes as
\varepsilon\to 0
.
Let us now consider the contribution of small
k
and large
r
. This time, we
only apply the cyclic lemma on the term
Q^{*}_{k+y}(n-m+y)
, yielding
the estimate

n^{-\frac{1}{2\alpha}-1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\leqslant n^{1/\alpha}\\
r\geqslant n^{1/\alpha}\end{subarray}}\frac{k^{\alpha-1}}{r^{\alpha}\sqrt{k+r}}\sum_{m=0}^{\varepsilon n}mQ^{*}_{r+1}(m+1)\,.

The contribution of the last sum is at most
\varepsilon nQ^{*}_{r+1}([0,\varepsilon n])
by using an Abel transform, and this is
\lesssim\varepsilon n\exp(-cr/(\varepsilon n)^{1/\alpha})
by the left-tail bound
(
11.11
). This justifies the dominated
convergence in the resulting estimate

\varepsilon n^{-\frac{1}{2\alpha}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\leqslant n^{1/\alpha}\\
r\geqslant n^{1/\alpha}\end{subarray}}\frac{k^{\alpha-1}\exp(-cr/(\varepsilon n)^{1/\alpha})}{r^{\alpha}\sqrt{k+r}}\lesssim\varepsilon\int_{0}^{1}{\rm d}t\int_{1}^{\infty}\frac{t^{\alpha-1}\exp(-cs/\varepsilon^{1/\alpha}){\rm d}s}{s^{\alpha}\sqrt{t+s}}\,,

again a converging integral, that vanishes when
\varepsilon\to 0
.
Finally, let us consider
the case where
k
is large. This time, we start
from the estimate based on applying the local limit theorem to

Q_{m+1}
:

\displaystyle n^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}}\sum_{m=0}^{\varepsilon n}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\leqslant n^{1-1/\alpha+\lambda}\\
k\geqslant n^{1/\alpha}\end{subarray}}\frac{r\widehat{\mu}_{\bullet}(r)\widehat{\mu}_{\bullet}^{*y}(k)}{\sqrt{k+r}}Q_{m+1}(-r-1)Q^{*}_{k+y}(n-m+y)

(11.44)

\displaystyle\lesssim n^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\leqslant n^{1-1/\alpha+\lambda}\\
k\geqslant n^{1/\alpha}\end{subarray}}\frac{\widehat{\mu}_{\bullet}^{*y}(k)}{r^{\alpha-1}\sqrt{k+r}}\sum_{m=0}^{\varepsilon n}\frac{Q_{k+y}^{*}(n-m+y)}{m^{1/\alpha}}\,.

(11.45)

Now, we first perform an Abel transform on the last sum,
to turn it into a sum of the form

\sum_{m=0}^{\varepsilon n}m^{-1-\frac{1}{\alpha}}Q^{*}_{k+y}([n-m+y,n+y])\,.

The terms of this sum are (at least for
m

greater than some large enough constant) respectively
\lesssim Q^{*}_{k}([n-m,n])
, by the convergence in distribution entailed by the
local limit theorem. This allows to revert the Abel transformation, and to
obtain as estimate of the form

n^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}-1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\leqslant n^{1-1/\alpha+\lambda}\\
k\geqslant n^{1/\alpha}\end{subarray}}\frac{\widehat{\mu}_{\bullet}^{*y}(k)}{r^{\alpha-1}\sqrt{k+r}}\sum_{m=0}^{\varepsilon n}Q_{n-m}(-k)\,,

where
Q_{n-m}(-k)\lesssim\exp(-ck/n^{1/\alpha})
. This allows to
bound the superior limit of this expression by a constant multiple of the converging integral

\varepsilon^{1-\frac{1}{\alpha}}\int_{1}^{\infty}{\rm d}t\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\exp(-cs){\rm d}s}{s^{\alpha-1}t^{2-\alpha}\sqrt{s+t}}\,,

which vanishes as
\varepsilon\to 0
.

This concludes the proof of Lemma
11.10
, and
hence of Proposition
11.8
and of Proposition

11.1
.
∎

11.5
Proof of Proposition
11.2

Fix
A>0
, and let
D_{(n)}=n^{-\frac{1}{2\alpha}}(\mathrm{d}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{\mathfrak{M}_{n}}(v_{1}^{n},v_{2}^{n})-1)_{+}
for simplicity.

Recall from (
10.5
) that if

({\bf m},(v_{1},v_{2}),\triangle)=\mathrm{BDG}^{2\bullet}({\bf u})
, then it
holds that
\mathrm{d}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{\mathfrak{M}_{n}}(v_{1},v_{2})=2\ell-\triangle_{1}-\triangle_{2}
, where
\ell
is the
minimal label along the cycle of
{\bf u}
, and
\triangle_{i}
is the
minimal label along the face
f_{i}
, minus
1
. We denote this quantity
by
\mathrm{D}_{1,2}({\bf u})
.

Then, recalling (
10.6
), we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[D_{(n)}\mathbbm{1}_{\{D_{(n)}>A\}}\right]

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{M}}\frac{w_{\bf q}({\rm d}{\bf m})}{w_{\bf q}(\#V=n)}\mathrm{vol}_{\bf m}({\rm d}v_{1})\mathrm{vol}_{{\bf m}}({\rm d}v_{2})\frac{\#\triangle\!\!\!\!\triangle_{{\bf m},v_{1},v_{2}}}{n^{2+\frac{1}{2\alpha}}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\#\triangle\!\!\!\!\triangle_{{\bf m},v_{1},v_{2}}>An^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}},\#V=n\}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{M}^{2\bullet}}\frac{w^{2\bullet}_{\bf q}({\rm d}({\bf m},(v_{1},v_{2}),\triangle))}{n^{2+\frac{1}{2\alpha}}w_{\bf q}(\#V=n)}\mathbbm{1}_{\{(\mathrm{d}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{{\bf m}}(v_{1},v_{2})-1)_{+}>An^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}},\#V=n\}}

\displaystyle=2\int_{\mathcal{U}}\frac{\tilde{w}^{2\bullet}_{\bf q}({\rm d}{\bf u})}{n^{2+\frac{1}{2\alpha}}w_{\bf q}(\#V=n)}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\mathrm{D}_{1,2}({\bf u})>An^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}},\#V_{\circ}({\bf u})=n-2\}}

\displaystyle=2\frac{\tilde{w}^{2\bullet}_{\bf q}(\#V_{\circ}=n-2)}{n^{2+\frac{1}{2\alpha}}w_{\bf q}(\#V=n)}\mathbb{P}(\mathrm{D}_{1,2}({\bf u}_{n})>An^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}})\,.

Next, we observe that the prefactor

2\frac{\tilde{w}^{2\bullet}(\#V_{\circ}=n-2)}{n^{2+\frac{1}{2\alpha}}w_{\bf q}(\#V=n)}

is a bounded function of
n
, by virtue of (
11.28
) and of
the fact that
w_{\bf q}(\#V=n)=2n^{-1}\mathrm{GW}_{\bf q}(M=n)\sim cn^{-2-1/\alpha}
.
In particular, the previously
displayed quantity
is bounded by a constant multiple of

\mathbb{P}(4\omega({\bf u}_{n})>An^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}})
,
where
\omega({\bf u}_{n})
is equal to one plus the maximal
label of
{\bf u}_{n}
in absolute value. In turn, the latter quantity can be written
as the maximum of
\omega^{\mathrm{belt}}({\bf u}_{n})
and

\omega^{\mathrm{buckle}}({\bf u}_{n})
, these quantities being equal to
one plus the maximal label in absolute value along the belt (resp. buckle) of
{\bf u}_{n}
. Proposition
11.1
entails that

(\omega^{\mathrm{belt}}({\bf u}_{n})/n^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}}:n\geqslant 1)
forms a tight family of
random variables, so that it
suffices to show that the same is true of

(\omega^{\mathrm{buckle}}({\bf u}_{n}):n\geqslant 1)
. This can be achieved by a re-rooting
argument. Indeed, consider a white corner
c_{2}
chosen uniformly at
random in
the unicyclomobile
{\bf u}_{n}
. Clearly, the unicyclomobile
{\bf u}_{n}^{*}

obtained by re-rooting
{\bf u}_{n}
at
c_{2}
(and forgetting the first root) has same
distribution as
{\bf u}_{n}
. Therefore, provided that
c_{2}
belongs to
the belt of
{\bf u}_{n}
(with the initial root), we have

\omega^{\mathrm{buckle}}({\bf u}_{n}^{*})\leqslant\omega^{\mathrm{belt}}({\bf u}_{n})
.
Fix
\varepsilon>0
. Let
E_{\varepsilon}(n)
be the event

\#C_{\circ}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{B}}_{n})>\varepsilon\#C_{\circ}({\bf u}_{n})
. We have by the
above observation,

\displaystyle\varepsilon\,\mathbf{P}(\omega^{\mathrm{buckle}}({\bf u}_{n}^{*})>An^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}},E_{\varepsilon}(n))

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathbf{P}(\omega^{\mathrm{buckle}}({\bf u}_{n}^{*})>An^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}},E_{\varepsilon}(n),c_{2}\in C_{\circ}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{B}}_{n}))

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathbf{P}(\omega^{\mathrm{belt}}({\bf u}_{n})>An^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}})\,.

Therefore, from the tightness of labels in the belt entailed by
Proposition
11.1
, we obtain that
for every
\varepsilon>0
,

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathbf{P}(\omega^{\mathrm{buckle}}({\bf u}_{n}^{*})>An^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}},E_{\varepsilon}(n))=0
.
We conclude by writing

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}(\omega^{\mathrm{buckle}}({\bf u}_{n})>An^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{P}(\omega^{\mathrm{buckle}}({\bf u}^{*}_{n})>An^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}})

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathbf{P}(\omega^{\mathrm{buckle}}({\bf u}_{n}^{*})>An^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}},E_{\varepsilon}(n))+\mathbf{P}(E_{\varepsilon}(n)^{c})\,,

and by observing that the superior limit of the term
\mathbf{P}(E_{\varepsilon}(n)^{c})

goes to
0
as
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
. Indeed, since

\#C_{\circ}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{B}_{n}})\geqslant\#V_{\circ}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{B}_{n}})
, we deduce that the family

(\#C_{\circ}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{B}_{n}})/n:n\geqslant 1)
is tight in
(0,\infty]
by
Proposition
11.1
. Therefore, our result will follow
from the fact that
(\#C_{\circ}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{n}})/n:n\geqslant 1)
is tight in

[0,\infty)
, where
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{n}}
is the buckle part
of
{\bf u}_{n}
. To prove this, observe that the following variant of
equation (
11.27
) holds:

\mathrm{U}_{\bf q}(\#C_{\circ}(\mathcal{P})>An,\#V_{\circ}=n)=\widehat{\mathrm{GW}}_{\bf q}\left(\widetilde{G}(n-M,-L;An)\mathbbm{1}_{M\leqslant n}\right)\,,

where

\widetilde{G}(m,l;m^{\prime})=\widetilde{\mathrm{GW}}_{\bf q}(M=m,L=l,\#C_{\circ}>m^{\prime})
.
We conclude from the fact that, for
A>z_{\bf q}
, it holds that

\widetilde{G}(m,l;An)
is exponentially small in
n
, for
reasons similar to the ones appearing in the proof of Lemma

11.4
, while, on the other hand,

\widehat{\mathrm{GW}}_{\bf q}(M\leqslant n)
diverges as a power of
n
. This concludes
the proof of Proposition
11.2
.

12
Geodesics between typical points

In this final section, we establish the key properties of geodesics needed to complete the proof of
D=D^{*}
presented in Section
9
. Specifically, we study geodesics in
(\mathcal{S},D,\mathrm{Vol},\rho_{*})
using the bi-marked construction introduced in Section
10
and the scaling limit results of Section
11
. More precisely, we first prove that geodesics between typical points in
(\mathcal{S},D,\mathrm{Vol},\rho_{*})
are almost surely unique (Theorem
9.1
). This result enables us to show (Proposition
9.2
) that all geodesics to the root
\rho_{*}
are simple. Finally, we prove the last remaining estimate on good points along typical geodesics (Proposition
9.3
), thereby completing the proof of our main result.

12.1
Coupling the bi-marked construction with
(\mathcal{S},D,\mathrm{Vol},\rho_{*})

The purpose of this section is to connect the scaling limits of the well-labeled unicyclomobiles from the previous section with the construction of
(\mathcal{S},D,\mathrm{Vol},\rho_{*})
. As in Section
7.4
, passing from the discrete constructions to the continuum provides a new perspective on
(\mathcal{S},D,\mathrm{Vol},\rho_{*})
, enabling computations that were previously highly intricate when using the single-pointed
\text{BDG}^{\bullet}
construction and its scaling limit. In this section, we work under the probability measure
\mathbf{P}
. We will also introduce new random variables, and when doing so, we implicitly enlarge the underlying probability space.

Recall from Section
7.3
that
\mathfrak{M}_{n}
is a rooted
\mathbf{q}
-Boltzmann map with
n
vertices, that is, with law
w_{\bf q}(\cdot\mid\#V=n)
. Conditionally on
\mathfrak{M}_{n}
, let
v_{1}^{n},v_{2}^{n}
be two independent uniform vertices of
\mathfrak{M}_{n}
, and finally let
\triangle_{n}\in\triangle\!\!\!\!\triangle_{\mathfrak{M}_{n},v_{1}^{n},v_{2}^{n}}
be a uniform integer satisfying
|\triangle_{n}|<\mathrm{d}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{\mathfrak{M}_{n}}(v_{1}^{n},v_{2}^{n})
and such that
\triangle_{n}+\mathrm{d}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{\mathfrak{M}_{n}}(v_{1}^{n},v_{2}^{n})
is even. In particular,
\big(\mathfrak{M}_{n},(v_{1}^{n},v_{2}^{n}),\triangle_{n}\big)
is a random element of
\mathcal{M}^{2\bullet}
, but its law is not
w_{\bf q}^{2\bullet}(\cdot\mid\#V=n)
, as defined in (
10.6
). Indeed, there is a bias between these two distributions given by
\#\triangle\!\!\!\!\triangle_{\mathfrak{M}_{n},v_{1}^{n},v_{2}^{n}}
, which is equal to
(\mathrm{d}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{\mathfrak{M}_{n}}(v_{1},v_{2})-1)_{+}
by (
10.7
). More precisely, we define a random variable
\big(\widehat{\mathfrak{M}}_{n},(\widehat{v}_{1}^{n},\widehat{v}_{2}^{n}),\widehat{\triangle}_{n}\big)\in\mathcal{M}^{2\bullet}
of law
w_{\bf q}^{2\bullet}(\cdot\mid\#V=n)
by biasing the law of
\big(\mathfrak{M}_{n},(v_{1}^{n},v_{2}^{n}),\triangle_{n}\big)
by
(\mathrm{d}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{\mathfrak{M}_{n}}(v_{1},v_{2})-1)_{+}
. Specifically, for every non-negative measurable function
f:\mathcal{M}^{2\bullet}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\Big[f\big(\widehat{\mathfrak{M}}_{n},(\widehat{v}_{1}^{n},\widehat{v}_{2}^{n}),\widehat{\triangle}_{n}\big)\Big]

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbf{E}\Big[\displaystyle\left(\mathrm{d}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{\mathfrak{M}_{n}}(v_{1}^{n},v_{2}^{n})-1\right)_{+}\,f\left(\mathfrak{M}_{n},(v_{1}^{n},v_{2}^{n}),\triangle_{n}\right)\Big]}{\mathbf{E}\Big[\left(\mathrm{d}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{\mathfrak{M}_{n}}(v_{1}^{n},v_{2}^{n})-1\right)_{+}\Big]},

(12.1)

and consequently it holds that:

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[f\big(\widehat{\mathfrak{M}}_{n},(\widehat{v}_{1}^{n},\widehat{v}_{2}^{n}),\widehat{\triangle}_{n}\big)\right]

\displaystyle\underset{\eqref{eq:w2bullet}}{=}

\displaystyle w_{{\bf q}}^{2\bullet}\Big(f\big({\bf m},(v_{1},v_{2}),\triangle\big)\penalty\ \Big|\penalty\ \#V({\bf m})=n\Big)

\displaystyle\underset{\eqref{eq:defwtilde2pt}}{=}

\displaystyle\tilde{w}_{{\bf q}}^{2\bullet}\Big(f\big(\mathrm{BDG}^{2\bullet}(\mathbf{u})\big)\penalty\ \Big|\penalty\ \#V_{\circ}(\mathbf{u})=n-2\Big)

\displaystyle\underset{\mathrm{Sec.\ }\ref{sec:result}}{=}

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[f\big(\mathrm{BDG}^{2\bullet}(\mathbf{u}_{n},\epsilon)\big)\right].

In particular, we may and will think of
\big(\widehat{\mathfrak{M}}_{n}^{2\bullet},\widehat{\triangle}_{n}\big):=\big(\widehat{\mathfrak{M}}_{n},(\widehat{v}_{1}^{n},\widehat{v}_{2}^{n}),\widehat{\triangle}_{n}\big)
as being coded by the well-labeled unicyclomobile
\mathbf{u}_{n}
together with a random sign
\epsilon
. According to the previous section, let us write
(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{B}}_{n},\hat{v}_{\circ})
for the belt part in the belt-buckle decomposition (Proposition
10.4
) of
\mathbf{u}_{n}
and recall that
(S^{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{B}}_{n}}_{k},L^{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{B}}_{n}}_{k})_{k\geqslant 0}
is its Lukasiewicz encoding as in Section
7.3
. We also recall the notation
\theta^{n}
for the number of white vertices in
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{B}}_{n}
,
a^{n}
for the first time of visit of
\hat{v}_{\circ}
and the constant
\mathfrak{s}_{\mathbf{q}}
appearing in the asymptotic (
7.5
). We encapsulate these random variables in the quadruplet:

\mathrm{Cod}_{n}:=\left(2(\mathfrak{s}_{\mathbf{q}}n)^{-\frac{1}{\alpha}}S_{\lfloor(n-1)\cdot\rfloor}^{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{B}}_{n}},(\mathfrak{s}_{\mathbf{q}}n)^{-\frac{1}{2\alpha}}L_{\lfloor(n-1)\cdot\rfloor}^{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{B}}_{n}},\frac{a^{n}}{n},\frac{\theta^{n}}{n}\right),

(12.2)

that we interpret as a random variable in
\mathbb{D}([0,1],\mathbb{R})^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
We now introduce the continuous analog of (
12.2
). Following Proposition
11.1
, we consider a random variable
(\widehat{X},\widehat{Z},\widehat{t}_{\bullet},\widehat{\sigma})
characterized by

\mathbf{E}\big[f(\widehat{X},\widehat{Z},\widehat{t}_{\bullet},\widehat{\sigma})\big]=\mathrm{Cst}\cdot\mathbf{N}^{\bullet}\Big(f\big(X,Z,t_{\bullet},\sigma\big)\cdot\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}\big(1-\sigma,-Z_{t_{\bullet}}\big)\Big),

(12.3)

for every non-negative measurable function
f
on
\mathbb{D}([0,1],\mathbb{R})^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
. We interpret
(\widehat{X},\widehat{Z},\widehat{t}_{\bullet},\widehat{\sigma})
as the scaling limit of (
12.2
) since, by the previous discussion and Proposition
11.1
, we have

\mathrm{Cod}_{n}\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{(d)}(\widehat{X},\widehat{Z},\widehat{t}_{\bullet},\widehat{\sigma}),

(12.4)

where, of course, the convergence takes place in
\mathbb{D}([0,1],\mathbb{R})^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Let us now also mimic (
12.1
) on the continuous side. To this end, we introduce a Polish space suitable for defining random rooted bi-marked weighted compact metric spaces, i.e. tuples
(M,d_{M},\mu,x,(x_{1},x_{2}))
where
(M,d_{M},\mu,x)
is a rooted weighted compact metric space and
x_{1},x_{2}
are two points of
M
called the marks. We stress that it is also possible to interpret the root
x
as a mark; however, it will be useful for us to break the symmetry between the three distinguished points. Following Section
7.3
, we consider the set of all isometry classes
14
14

14

Here we say that
(M,d_{M},\mu,x,(x_{1},x_{2}))
and
(M^{\prime},d^{\prime},\mu^{\prime},x^{\prime},(x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2}^{\prime}))
are isometric if there exists an isometric bijection
\varphi:M\to M^{\prime}
such that
\varphi_{*}\mu=\mu^{\prime}
and
(\varphi(x),\varphi(x_{1}),\varphi(x_{2}))=(x^{\prime},x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2}^{\prime})
.
of rooted bi-marked weighted compact metric spaces, denoted by

\mathbb{M}^{2\bullet}_{\mathrm{root}}:=\{(M,d_{M},\mu,x,(x_{1},x_{2})):x,x_{1},x_{2}\in M\}/\mathrm{iso},

and we endow it with the rooted bi-marked Gromov–Hausdorff–Prokhorov metric, i.e.

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{GHP}}^{\mathrm{root},2\bullet}\big(\big(M,d_{M},\mu,x,(x_{1},x_{2})\big),\big(M^{\prime},d_{M^{\prime}},\mu^{\prime},x^{\prime},(x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2}^{\prime})\big)\big)

\displaystyle:=\inf\limits_{\phi,\phi^{\prime}}\Big(\delta_{\text{H}}\big(\phi(M),\phi^{\prime}(M^{\prime})\big)\vee\delta_{\text{P}}\big(\phi_{*}\mu,\phi^{\prime}_{*}\mu^{\prime}\big)\vee\delta\big(\phi(x),\phi^{\prime}(x^{\prime})\big)\vee\delta\big(\phi(x_{1}),\phi^{\prime}(x_{1}^{\prime})\big)\vee\delta\big(\phi(x_{2}),\phi^{\prime}(x_{2}^{\prime})\big)\Big)\penalty\ ,

where again the infimum is taken over all isometries
\phi
,
\phi^{\prime}
from
M
,
M^{\prime}
into a metric space
(Z,\delta)
. As usual, here
\delta_{\text{H}}
(resp.
\delta_{\text{P}}
) stands for the classical Hausdorff distance (resp. the Prokhorov distance).
The space
(\mathbb{M}^{2\bullet}_{\mathrm{root}},d_{\mathrm{GHP}}^{\mathrm{root},2\bullet})
is a Polish space (see e.g.
[
1
,
80
]
) and we equip it with the corresponding Borel sigma-field. When no ambiguity is possible, we identify a rooted bi-marked weighted compact metric space with its equivalence class. In particular, we can see the random variables

\big(V(\widehat{\mathfrak{M}}_{n}),(\mathfrak{s}_{\mathbf{q}}n)^{-\frac{1}{2\alpha}}\mathrm{d}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{\widehat{\mathfrak{M}}_{n}},\mathrm{vol}_{\widehat{\mathfrak{M}}_{n}},\widehat{\rho}_{*}^{n},(\widehat{v}_{1}^{n},\widehat{v}_{2}^{n})\big)\quad\text{ and }\quad\big(V(\mathfrak{M}_{n}),(\mathfrak{s}_{\mathbf{q}}n)^{-\frac{1}{2\alpha}}\mathrm{d}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{\mathfrak{M}_{n}},\mathrm{vol}_{\mathfrak{M}_{n}},\rho_{*}^{n},(v_{1}^{n},v_{2}^{n})\big),

for
n\geqslant 1
, as random variables on
\mathbb{M}^{2\bullet}_{\mathrm{root}}
, where
\widehat{\rho}_{*}^{n}
and
\rho_{*}^{n}
stand for the starting vertex of the distinguished oriented edge of
\widehat{\mathfrak{M}}_{n}
and
\mathfrak{M}_{n}
respectively. When further equipped with the delays, these become random variables taking values in the space
\mathbb{M}^{2\bullet}_{\mathrm{root}}\times\mathbb{R}
, which is endowed with the product topology and Borel sigma-field. We stress that the projection mapping a rooted bi-marked weighted compact metric space
\big(M,d_{M},\mu,x,(x_{1},x_{2})\big)
to
\big(M,d_{M},\mu,x\big)
plainly defines a continuous projection from
\mathbb{M}^{2\bullet}_{\mathrm{root}}
onto
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{root}}
.

This framework provides a natural way to translate (
12.1
) into the continuum and to obtain scaling limit results. Specifically, recall the definition of
(\mathcal{S},D,\mathrm{Vol},\rho_{*})
from Proposition
7.1
along with the subsequence
(n_{k})_{k\geqslant 1}
on which it may depend. Conditionally on
(\mathcal{S},D,\mathrm{Vol},\rho_{*})
, let
\rho_{1},\rho_{2}\in\mathcal{S}
be two independent points sampled according to
\mathrm{Vol}
(that is,
\Pi_{D}(U_{1}),\Pi_{D}(U_{2})
for two i.i.d. uniform random variables
U_{1},U_{2}
on
[0,1]
independent of
(X,Z,D)
). The random variable
(\mathcal{S},D,\mathrm{Vol},\rho_{*},(\rho_{1},\rho_{2}))
takes values in
\mathbb{M}^{2\bullet}_{\mathrm{root}}
, and conditionally to it, we consider
\triangle
a random variable uniform on
[-D(\rho_{1},\rho_{2}),D(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})]
. We then introduce
(\widehat{\mathcal{S}},\widehat{D},\widehat{\mathrm{Vol}},\widehat{\rho}_{*},(\widehat{\rho}_{1},\widehat{\rho}_{2}),\widehat{\triangle})
, a biased version of
(\mathcal{S},D,\mathrm{Vol},\rho_{*},(\rho_{1},\rho_{2}),\triangle)
, whose law is defined by

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\Big[f\big(\widehat{\mathcal{S}},\widehat{D},\widehat{\mathrm{Vol}},\widehat{\rho}_{*},(\widehat{\rho}_{1},\widehat{\rho}_{2}),\widehat{\triangle}\big)\Big]

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbf{E}\Big[D(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})f\left(\mathcal{S},D,\mathrm{Vol},\rho_{*},(\rho_{1},\rho_{2}),\triangle\right)\Big]}{\mathbf{E}[D(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})]}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbf{E}\left[\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{-D(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})}^{D(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})}\mathrm{d}uf\left(\mathcal{S},D,\mathrm{Vol},\rho_{*},(\rho_{1},\rho_{2}),u\right)\right]}{\mathbf{E}[D(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})]},

(12.5)

for every measurable function
f:\mathbb{M}^{2\bullet}_{\mathrm{root}}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
. We stress that (
12.5
) defines a probability measure on
\mathbb{M}^{2\bullet}_{\mathrm{root}}\times\mathbb{R}
since
\mathbf{E}[D(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})]<\infty
by Lemma
7.3
. In particular, the projection
(\widehat{\mathcal{S}},\widehat{D},\widehat{\mathrm{Vol}},\widehat{\rho}_{*})
has the law of
(\mathcal{S},D,\mathrm{Vol},\rho_{*})
biased by
\int\mathrm{Vol}(\mathrm{d}x_{1})\mathrm{Vol}(\mathrm{d}x_{2})\ D(x_{1},x_{2})
.

Lemma 12.1
.

With the notation above, we have:

\Big(V(\widehat{\mathfrak{M}}_{n_{k}}),(\mathfrak{s}_{\mathbf{q}}n_{k})^{-\frac{1}{2\alpha}}\cdot\mathrm{d}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{\widehat{\mathfrak{M}}_{n_{k}}},\mathrm{vol}_{\widehat{\mathfrak{M}_{n}}},\widehat{\rho}_{*}^{n_{k}},\big(\widehat{v}_{1}^{n_{k}},\widehat{v}_{2}^{n_{k}}\big),(\mathfrak{s}_{\mathbf{q}}n_{k})^{-\frac{1}{2\alpha}}\cdot{\widehat{\triangle}}_{n_{k}}\Big)\xrightarrow[k\to\infty]{(d)}\Big(\widehat{\mathcal{S}},\widehat{D},\widehat{\mathrm{Vol}},\widehat{\rho}_{*},\big(\widehat{\rho}_{1},\widehat{\rho}_{2}\big),\widehat{\triangle}\Big),

(12.6)

where the convergence takes place on the space
\mathbb{M}^{2\bullet}_{\mathrm{root}}\times\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

First, recall that

\widehat{Y}_{n}:=\Big(V(\widehat{\mathfrak{M}}_{n}),(\mathfrak{s}_{\mathbf{q}}n)^{-\frac{1}{2\alpha}}\cdot\mathrm{d}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{\widehat{\mathfrak{M}}_{n}},\mathrm{vol}_{\widehat{\mathfrak{M}}_{n}},\widehat{\rho}_{*}^{n},(\widehat{v}_{1}^{n},\widehat{v}_{2}^{n})\Big)

has the law of
Y_{n}:=(V(\mathfrak{M}_{n}),(\mathfrak{s}_{\mathbf{q}}n)^{-\frac{1}{2\alpha}}\mathrm{d}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{{\mathfrak{M}}_{n}},\mathrm{vol}_{\mathfrak{M}_{n}},\rho_{*}^{n},({v}_{1}^{n},{v}_{2}^{n}))
biased by the Radon–Nikodym derivative:

A_{n}:=\Big(\mathrm{d}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{{\mathfrak{M}}_{n}}({v}_{1}^{n},{v}_{2}^{n})-1\Big)_{+}\Big/\mathbf{E}\Big[\Big(\mathrm{d}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{{\mathfrak{M}}_{n}}({v}_{1}^{n},{v}_{2}^{n})-1\Big)_{+}\Big].

By Proposition
7.1
, the family
(Y_{n_{k}})_{k\geqslant 1}
is tight in
\mathbb{M}^{2\bullet}_{\mathrm{root}}
and converges in law towards:

\big(\mathcal{S},D,\mathrm{Vol},\rho_{*},(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})\big).

Moreover, Corollary
11.2
entails that the family
(A_{n})_{n\geqslant 1}
is uniformly integrable. It then follows that
(\widehat{Y}_{n_{k}})_{k\geqslant 1}
converges in law towards:

\big(\widehat{\mathcal{S}},\widehat{D},\widehat{\mathrm{Vol}},\widehat{\rho}_{*},(\widehat{\rho}_{1},\widehat{\rho}_{2})\big).

To extend the result to the delays, notice that conditionally on
\widehat{Y}_{n}
, the delay
\widehat{\triangle}_{n}
is uniform on the set
\{m\in\mathbb{Z}:\penalty\ |m|<\mathrm{d}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{\widehat{\mathfrak{M}}_{n}}(\widehat{v}_{1}^{n},\widehat{v}_{2}^{n})\text{ and }m+\mathrm{d}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{\widehat{\mathfrak{M}}_{n}}(\widehat{v}_{1}^{n},\widehat{v}_{2}^{n})\text{ is even}\}
.
It is then straightforward to deduce the desired convergence (
12.6
) along the subsequence
(n_{k})_{k\geqslant 1}
.
∎

Combining (
12.4
) and (
12.6
), we derive that the family of random vectors

\Big(\Big(V(\widehat{\mathfrak{M}}_{n_{k}}),(\mathfrak{s}_{\mathbf{q}}n_{k})^{-\frac{1}{2\alpha}}\cdot\mathrm{d}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{\widehat{\mathfrak{M}}_{n_{k}}},\mathrm{vol}_{\widehat{\mathfrak{M}}_{n_{k}}},\widehat{\rho}_{*}^{n_{k}},\big(\widehat{v}_{1}^{n_{k}},\widehat{v}_{2}^{n_{k}}\big),(\mathfrak{s}_{\mathbf{q}}n_{k})^{-\frac{1}{2\alpha}}\cdot{\widehat{\triangle}}_{n_{k}}\Big),\mathrm{Cod}_{n_{k}}\Big),\quad k\geqslant 1,

(12.7)

living in the Polish space
(\mathbb{M}^{2\bullet}_{\mathrm{root}}\times\mathbb{R})\times(\mathbb{D}([0,1],\mathbb{R})^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2})
, is tight, since each of its coordinates is tight. By the Skorokhod representation theorem, we can further extract a subsequence
(m_{k})_{k\geqslant 1}
from
(n_{k})_{k\geqslant 1}
along which we can couple all realizations to have almost sure convergence towards some vector of
(\mathbb{M}^{2\bullet}_{\mathrm{root}}\times\mathbb{R})\times(\mathbb{D}([0,1],\mathbb{R})^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2})
with marginals distributed as
((\widehat{\mathcal{S}},\widehat{D},\widehat{\mathrm{Vol}},\widehat{\rho}_{*},(\widehat{\rho}_{1},\widehat{\rho}_{2})),\widehat{\triangle})
and
(\widehat{X},\widehat{Z},\widehat{t}_{\bullet},\widehat{\sigma})
respectively. With a slight abuse of notation, we still denote this vector by:

\Big(\big((\widehat{\mathcal{S}},\widehat{D},\widehat{\mathrm{Vol}},\widehat{\rho}_{*},(\widehat{\rho}_{1},\widehat{\rho}_{2})),\widehat{\triangle}\big),(\widehat{X},\widehat{Z},\widehat{t}_{\bullet},\widehat{\sigma})\Big).

(12.8)

We stress that the coupling between the two parts of this vector is a priori unknown. The point is that we can use this coupling to pass the discrete
\mathrm{BDG}^{2\bullet}
to the scaling limit. In this way, we can import some new geometric information on
\widehat{D}
(equivalently on
D
after de-biasing) from
(\widehat{X},\widehat{Z},\widehat{t}_{\bullet},\widehat{\sigma})
similarly as we imported information on
D
from
(X,Z)
in Section
7.4
. We also stress that for convenience, all random variables
((\widehat{\mathcal{S}},\widehat{D},\widehat{\mathrm{Vol}},\widehat{\rho}_{*},(\widehat{\rho}_{1},\widehat{\rho}_{2})),\widehat{\triangle},\widehat{X},\widehat{Z},\widehat{t}_{\bullet},\widehat{\sigma})
as well as
((\mathcal{S},D,\mathrm{Vol},\rho_{*},(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})),\triangle,X,Z,t_{\bullet},\sigma)
are defined under the probability measure
\mathbf{P}
, but there is no coupling between those two vectors; they could very well be independent.

In the remainder of Section
12
, we shall always suppose that we have made the above couplings and implicitly restrict to the subsequence
(m_{k})_{k\geqslant 1}
.

Let us conclude this section by addressing a technicality. Under
\mathbf{P}
, the space
\big(\mathcal{S},D,\mathrm{Vol},\rho_{*},(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})\big)
is constructed from the pseudo-distance
D:[0,1]^{2}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
and two uniform independent random variables on
[0,1]
.
Therefore,
\big(\mathcal{S},D,\mathrm{Vol},\rho_{*},(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})\big)
is a
concrete
rooted bi-marked weighted compact metric space, which we then view as a random variable when considering its equivalence class in
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{root}}^{2\bullet}
.
This allowed us to discuss metric properties directly using the representative
\big(\mathcal{S},D,\mathrm{Vol},\rho_{*},(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})\big)
.
However, this is not longer the case for
(\widehat{\mathcal{S}},\widehat{D},\widehat{\mathrm{Vol}},\widehat{\rho}_{*},(\widehat{\rho}_{1},\widehat{\rho}_{2}))
which is directly defined as an element of
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{root}}^{2\bullet}
. To circumvent this difficulty and treat it as a
rooted bi-marked weighted compact metric space, we rely on general representation theorems for Gromov-Hausdorff-type topologies
[
80
]
. Namely, we fix
\omega
(in the underlying probability space) such that the convergence of (
12.7
) to (
12.8
) holds. Then, by
[
80
, Lemma 2.5]
, we can
embed all the rooted bi-marked weighted compact metric spaces in (
12.7
) and (
12.8
) isometrically in the same compact
metric space
(E,d_{E})
, in such a way that

V(\widehat{\mathfrak{M}}_{m_{k}})\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\longrightarrow}\limits_{k\to\infty}}\widehat{\mathcal{S}}\quad\text{ and }\quad\big(\widehat{\rho}_{*}^{m_{k}},\widehat{v}_{1}^{m_{k}},\widehat{v}_{2}^{m_{k}}\big)\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\longrightarrow}\limits_{k\to\infty}}\big(\widehat{\rho}_{*},\widehat{\rho}_{1},\widehat{\rho}_{2}\big),

(12.9)

where the first convergence is with respect to the Hausdorff distance in
(E,d_{E})
. We also have the weak convergence
\mathrm{vol}_{\widehat{\mathfrak{M}}_{m_{k}}}\rightarrow\widehat{\mathrm{Vol}}
, but this last convergence will not be needed in this work. We stress that the compact metric space
(E,d_{E})
might depends on
\omega
.
Then, if we establish that some property holds, for
\mathbf{P}
almost every such
\omega
, then we can use (
12.5
) to import it to
\big(\mathcal{S},D,\mathrm{Vol},\rho_{*},(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})\big)
,
\mathbf{P}
-a.s., provided that the related events are measurable. We will implement this method to deduce the uniqueness of typical geodesics in Section
12.2
and to study good points in Section
12.4
.
Let us list here the almost sure properties that we will use: We write
\Omega^{\prime}
for the subset of all such
\omega
such that (
12.9
) hold, such that the process
\widehat{X}
does not have negative jumps and verifies properties
(A_{1})
–
(A_{4})
of Section
2.1
with
X
replaced by
\widehat{X}
, and such that
\widehat{Z}
is continuous and its restriction to
\widehat{\mathrm{Branch}}(0,\widehat{t}_{\bullet}):=\{h\leqslant\widehat{t}_{\bullet}:\penalty\ \widehat{X}_{h-}\leqslant\min_{[h,\widehat{t}_{\bullet}]}\widehat{X}\}
realizes its infimum at a unique point. We stress that the set
\widehat{\mathrm{Branch}}(0,\widehat{t}_{\bullet})
corresponds, in terms of the looptree encoded by
\widehat{X}
, to the pinch point times in the branch from
0
to
\widehat{t}_{\bullet}
, in the sense of Section
2.1
. By the above discussion, (
12.3
) and Lemmas
2.5
and
4.7
, we have
\mathbf{P}(\Omega^{\prime})=1
.

12.2
Uniqueness of geodesics between two typical points

Let us present the first application of the above methodology to prove uniqueness of typical geodesics. Specifically, we are going to show that,
\mathbf{P}
-a.s., there is a unique geodesic on
\mathcal{S}
going from
\rho_{1}
to
\rho_{2}
, thereby establishing Theorem
9.1
. Since to achieve this, we rely on the biased version

(\widehat{\mathcal{S}},\widehat{D},\widehat{\mathrm{Vol}},\widehat{\rho}_{*},(\widehat{\rho}_{1},\widehat{\rho}_{2})),

we need to check measurability issues. In this direction, we introduce
\mathbb{PM}_{\mathrm{root}}^{2\bullet,1}
, the subset of
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{root}}^{2\bullet}
consisting of all equivalence classes of weighted geodesic compact metric spaces with exactly one geodesic between the two marked points. We emphasize that
\mathbb{PM}_{\mathrm{root}}^{2\bullet,1}
is well-defined, as the property of having exactly one geodesic between the two marked points is invariant under isometry.

Lemma 12.2
.

\mathbb{PM}_{\mathrm{root}}^{2\bullet,1}
is a measurable subset of
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{root}}^{2\bullet}
.

Proof.

First note that by (a marked extension of)
[
39
, Theorem 7.5.1]
, the space
\mathbb{PM}_{\mathrm{root}}^{2\bullet}

of equivalence classes of rooted bi-marked weighted
geodesic
compact metric spaces is a closed subset of
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{root}}^{2\bullet}
, and furthermore that the number of geodesics connecting
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
does not depend on the choice of representative
(M,d,\mu,x,(x_{1},x_{2}))
.
For every
\delta>0
, consider
\mathbb{PM}_{\mathrm{root}}^{2\bullet}[\delta]
, the space of equivalence classes of spaces
(M,d,\mu,x,(x_{1},x_{2}))
possessing at least two geodesics,
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
, going from
x_{1}
to
x_{2}
, such that

\sup\big\{d(\gamma(t),\gamma^{\prime}(t)):\penalty\ t\in[0,d(x_{1},x_{2})]\big\}\geqslant\delta.

Then it is straightforward to verify that
\mathbb{PM}_{\mathrm{root}}^{2\bullet}[\delta]
is a closed subset of
\mathbb{PM}_{\mathrm{root}}^{2\bullet}
, and
\mathbb{PM}_{\mathrm{root}}^{2\bullet,1}
is a measurable set since:

\mathbb{PM}_{\mathrm{root}}^{2\bullet,1}=\mathbb{PM}_{\mathrm{root}}^{2\bullet}\setminus\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbb{PM}_{\mathrm{root}}^{2\bullet}[2^{-n}].

∎

Therefore, our formal goal is to establish that
(\mathcal{S},D,\mathrm{Vol},\rho_{*},(\rho_{1},\rho_{2}))\in\mathbb{PM}_{\mathrm{root}}^{2\bullet,1}
,
\mathbf{P}
-a.s., which by (
12.5
) is equivalent to:

\big(\widehat{\mathcal{S}},\widehat{D},\widehat{\mathrm{Vol}},\widehat{\rho}_{*},(\widehat{\rho}_{1},\widehat{\rho}_{2})\big)\in\mathbb{PM}_{\mathrm{root}}^{2\bullet,1},\quad\mathbf{P}\text{-a.s.}

(12.10)

To demonstrate (
12.10
), we exploit the connection with well-labeled unicyclomobiles, following a method similar to that in
[
111
, Section 7.2.3]
. Specifically, we fix an element
\omega\in\Omega^{\prime}
in the underlying probability space, as described at the end of Section
12.8
. In particular,
(\widehat{\mathcal{S}},\widehat{D},\widehat{\mathrm{Vol}},\widehat{\rho}_{*},(\widehat{\rho}_{1},\widehat{\rho}_{2}))
is embedded into a compact space
(E,d_{E})
. Next, for every
h\in[-\widehat{D}(\widehat{\rho}_{1},\widehat{\rho}_{2}),D(\widehat{\rho}_{1},\widehat{\rho}_{2})]
, we write

\widehat{\mathscr{G}}(h):=\Big\{x\in\widehat{\mathcal{S}}:\widehat{D}(x,\widehat{\rho}_{1})=\frac{\widehat{D}(\widehat{\rho}_{1},\widehat{\rho}_{2})-h}{2}\mbox{ and }\widehat{D}(x,\widehat{\rho}_{2})=\frac{\widehat{D}(\widehat{\rho}_{1},\widehat{\rho}_{2})+h}{2}\Big\},

which is the set of all
h
-median points in a
\widehat{D}
-geodesic between
\widehat{\rho}_{1}
and
\widehat{\rho}_{2}
. The assertion that there is a unique geodesic connecting
\widehat{\rho}_{1}
to
\widehat{\rho}_{2}
is equivalent to stating that
\widehat{\mathscr{G}}(h)
is a singleton for every
h\in[-\widehat{D}(\rho_{1},\rho_{2}),\widehat{D}(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})]
. Moreover by continuity, if
\#\widehat{\mathscr{G}}(h)\geqslant 2
for some
h
, then
\#\widehat{\mathscr{G}}(h^{\prime})\geqslant 2
for all
h^{\prime}
in a small neighborhood of
h
.
Thus, to obtain the uniqueness of geodesics going from
\widehat{\rho}_{1}
to
\widehat{\rho}_{2}
, it suffices to show that:

Lemma 12.3
.

For every
\omega\in\Omega^{\prime}
and with the notation above, the set
\widehat{\mathscr{G}}(\widehat{\triangle})
is a singleton.

Proof.

Let
\omega
be fixed as explained above. We begin by reformulating the statement of the lemma in terms of
(\widehat{\mathfrak{M}}_{m_{k}},\widehat{v}_{1}^{m_{k}},\widehat{v}_{2}^{m_{k}},\widehat{\triangle}_{m_{k}})
. In this direction, we introduce the set
\mathcal{A}
of all sequences
(x_{m_{k}})_{k\geqslant 1}
, with
x_{m_{k}}
a vertex of
\widehat{\mathfrak{M}}_{m_{k}}
, such that

(\mathfrak{s}_{\mathbf{q}}m_{k})^{-\frac{1}{2\alpha}}\cdot\mathrm{d}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{\widehat{\mathfrak{M}}_{m_{k}}}(\widehat{v}_{1}^{m_{k}},x_{m_{k}})\to\frac{\widehat{D}(\widehat{\rho}_{1},\widehat{\rho}_{2})-\widehat{\triangle}}{2}\mbox{ \ \ and \ \ }(\mathfrak{s}_{\mathbf{q}}m_{k})^{-\frac{1}{2\alpha}}\cdot\mathrm{d}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{\widehat{\mathfrak{M}}_{m_{k}}}(\widehat{v}_{2}^{m_{k}},x_{m_{k}})\to\frac{\widehat{D}(\widehat{\rho}_{1},\widehat{\rho}_{2})+\widehat{\triangle}}{2}.

(12.11)

Next, remark that if there exist
x,x^{\prime}\in\widehat{\mathscr{G}}(\widehat{\triangle})
with
x\neq x^{\prime}
, then by the definition of
\widehat{\mathscr{G}}(\widehat{\triangle})
and (
12.9
) we can find two sequences
(x_{m_{k}})_{k\geqslant 1},(x_{m_{k}}^{\prime})_{k\geqslant 1}
in
\mathcal{A}
such that

\limsup_{k\to\infty}(\mathfrak{s}_{\mathbf{q}}m_{k})^{-\frac{1}{2\alpha}}\cdot\mathrm{d}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{\widehat{\mathfrak{M}}_{m_{k}}}(x_{m_{k}},x_{m_{k}}^{\prime})>0.

(12.12)

We shall prove that this is impossible. The interest of this reformulation lies in the fact that
(
12.11
) and (
12.12
) involve quantities that can be directly controlled with the construction
(\widehat{\mathfrak{M}}_{m_{k}},\widehat{v}_{1}^{m_{k}},\widehat{v}_{2}^{m_{k}},\widehat{\triangle}_{m_{k}})=\mathrm{BDG}^{2\bullet}(\mathbf{u}_{m_{k}},\epsilon)
of Section
10
. In this direction, for
k\geqslant 1
, we recall the notation
f_{1}^{m_{k}}
and
f_{2}^{m_{k}}
for the faces containing
\widehat{v}_{1}^{m_{k}}
and
\widehat{v}_{2}^{m_{k}}
respectively, and let us start with a couple of easy remarks. First, for each
k\geqslant 1
, consider
J_{m_{k}}
a white vertex attaining the minimal label along the cycle of
\mathbf{u}_{m_{k}}
, and remark that by (
10.3
), we must have:

(\mathfrak{s}_{\mathbf{q}}m_{k})^{-\frac{1}{2\alpha}}\cdot\Big(\mathrm{d}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{\widehat{\mathfrak{M}}_{m_{k}}}(\widehat{v}_{1}^{m_{k}},J_{m_{k}})-\mathrm{d}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{\widehat{\mathfrak{M}}_{m_{k}}}(\widehat{v}_{2}^{m_{k}},J_{m_{k}})\Big)\to\widehat{\triangle}.

Next, recall from the discussion preceding (
10.5
) that if we concatenate two simple geodesics in the map starting from
J_{m_{k}}
and obtained by iterating the successor function in the faces
f_{1}^{m_{k}}
and
f_{2}^{m_{k}}
respectively, until reaching
\widehat{v}_{1}^{m_{k}}
and
\widehat{v}_{2}^{m_{k}}
, then this concatenation produces a geodesic
\widehat{\gamma}_{1,2}^{m_{k}}
connecting
\widehat{v}_{1}^{m_{k}}
to
\widehat{v}_{2}^{m_{k}}
. Hence, we must also have

(\mathfrak{s}_{\mathbf{q}}m_{k})^{-\frac{1}{2\alpha}}\cdot\Big(\mathrm{d}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{\widehat{\mathfrak{M}}_{m_{k}}}(\widehat{v}_{1}^{m_{k}},J_{m_{k}})+\mathrm{d}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{\widehat{\mathfrak{M}}_{m_{k}}}(\widehat{v}_{2}^{m_{k}},J_{m_{k}})\Big)\to\widehat{D}(\widehat{\rho_{1}},\widehat{\rho}_{2}).

We infer that
(J_{m_{k}})_{k\geqslant 1}
is in
\mathcal{A}
. Now remark that in order to have (
12.12
) for two sequences in
\mathcal{A}
, there must exist
(x_{m_{k}})_{k\geqslant 1}\in\mathcal{A}
such that
\limsup_{k\to\infty}(\mathfrak{s}_{\mathbf{q}}m_{k})^{-\frac{1}{2\alpha}}\cdot\mathrm{d}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{\widehat{\mathfrak{M}}_{m_{k}}}(J_{m_{k}},x_{m_{k}})>0
. We argue by contradiction and assume that we can find such a sequence
(x_{m_{k}})_{k\geqslant 1}
. For
k\geqslant 1
, consider a path
\gamma_{m_{k}}
going from
\widehat{v}_{1}^{m_{k}}
to
\widehat{v}_{2}^{m_{k}}
and passing through
x_{m_{k}}
obtained by concatenating two geodesics from
x_{m_{k}}
to
\widehat{v}_{1}^{m_{k}}
and from
x_{m_{k}}
to
\widehat{v}_{2}^{m_{k}}
. By the Jordan theorem, this path must go through a white vertex
J_{m_{k}}^{\prime}
of the cycle of
\mathbf{u}_{m_{k}}
, and since
(x_{m_{k}})_{k\geqslant 1}
is in
\mathcal{A}
, the length of
\gamma_{m_{k}}
is equal to
\mathrm{d}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{\widehat{\mathfrak{M}}_{m_{k}}}(\widehat{v}_{1}^{m_{k}},\widehat{v}_{2}^{m_{k}})+o(m_{k}^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}})
,
and as a consequence

\mathrm{d}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{\widehat{\mathfrak{M}}_{m_{k}}}(\widehat{v}_{1}^{m_{k}},\widehat{v}_{2}^{m_{k}})+o(m_{k}^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}})\geqslant\mathrm{d}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{\widehat{\mathfrak{M}}_{m_{k}}}(\widehat{v}_{1}^{m_{k}},J_{m_{k}}^{\prime})+\mathrm{d}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{\widehat{\mathfrak{M}}_{m_{k}}}(J_{m_{k}}^{\prime},\widehat{v}_{2}^{m_{k}})\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathrm{d}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{\widehat{\mathfrak{M}}_{m_{k}}}(x_{m_{k}},J_{m_{k}}^{\prime})=o(m_{k}^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}}).

(12.13)

We refer to Figure
42
for an illustration.

Figure 42:
Illustration of the notation. The (essentially unique) vertex minimizing the label along the cycle of
\mathbf{u}_{m_{k}}
is denoted by
J_{m_{k}}
. The vertex
x_{m_{k}}
must be close to a vertex
J_{m_{k}}^{\prime}
on the cycle which asymptotically minimizes the label.

In particular, to obtain a contradiction it suffices to show that
\mathrm{d}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{\widehat{\mathfrak{M}}_{m_{k}}}(J_{m_{k}},J_{m_{k}}^{\prime})=o(m_{k}^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}})
.
In this direction, remark that by (
10.3
) and (
12.13
), combined with the fact that
J_{m_{k}}
minimizes the label along the cycle of
\mathbf{u}_{m_{k}}
, we have

\displaystyle\ell_{m_{k}}(J_{m_{k}}^{\prime})=\ell_{m_{k}}(J_{m_{k}})+o(m_{k}^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}}),

(12.14)

where
\ell_{m_{k}}
stands for the label function of
\mathbf{u}_{m_{k}}
.
We conclude using now the convergence of
\mathrm{Cod}_{m_{k}}
towards
(\widehat{X},\widehat{Z},\widehat{t}_{\bullet},\widehat{\sigma})
by an argument parallel to the one of the proof of the Cactus bound of Lemma
7.5
. In this direction, let us write
j_{m_{k}}\leqslant a^{m_{k}}
(resp.
j_{m_{k}}^{\prime}\leqslant a^{m_{k}}
) for the visit time of the vertex
J_{m_{k}}
(resp.
J_{m_{k}}^{\prime}
) in the belt-part of
\mathbf{u}_{m_{k}}
(by definition all white vertices of the cycle are present in the belt part). Up to further extracting, we can suppose that almost surely
j_{m_{k}}/n\to\mathfrak{j}\in[0,\widehat{t}_{\bullet}]
(resp.
j_{m_{k}}^{\prime}/n\to\mathfrak{j}^{\prime}\in[0,\widehat{t}_{\bullet}]
).
It is straightforward to deduce from the facts that
J_{m_{k}}
and
J_{m_{k}}^{\prime}
lie in the cycle of
\mathbf{u}_{m_{k}}
, the assumed properties of
(\widehat{X},\widehat{Z})
on
\omega
, and (
12.14
),
that

\mathfrak{j},\mathfrak{j}^{\prime}\in\widehat{\mathrm{Branch}}(0,\widehat{t}_{\bullet})
and
\widehat{Z}_{\mathfrak{j}}=\widehat{Z}_{\mathfrak{j}^{\prime}}=\min\{\widehat{Z}_{t}:\penalty\ t\in\widehat{\mathrm{Branch}}(0,\widehat{t}_{\bullet})\}
. Since the restriction of
\widehat{Z}
at
\widehat{\mathrm{Branch}}(0,\widehat{t}_{\bullet})
realizes its infimum at a unique point we derive that
\mathfrak{j}^{\prime}=\mathfrak{j}
. Lastly, by the Schaeffer bound (
10.4
) and the continuity of
\widehat{Z}
, we deduce that

\limsup\limits_{k\to\infty}(\mathfrak{s}_{\mathbf{q}}m_{k})^{-\frac{1}{2\alpha}}\cdot\mathrm{d}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{\widehat{\mathfrak{M}}_{m_{k}}}(J_{m_{k}},J_{m_{k}}^{\prime})\leqslant\widehat{Z}_{\mathfrak{j}}+\widehat{Z}_{\mathfrak{j}^{\prime}}-2\min\limits_{[\mathfrak{j}\wedge\mathfrak{j}^{\prime},\mathfrak{j}\vee\mathfrak{j}^{\prime}]}\widehat{Z}=0.

This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

For simplicity, we let
\Omega^{\prime\prime}
be the subset of all
\omega\in\Omega^{\prime}
such that
\big(\mathcal{S},D,\mathrm{Vol},\rho_{*},(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})\big)
and
\big(\widehat{\mathcal{S}},\widehat{D},\widehat{\mathrm{Vol}},\widehat{\rho}_{*},(\widehat{\rho}_{1},\widehat{\rho}_{2})\big)
belong to
\mathbb{PM}_{\mathrm{root}}^{2\bullet,1}
. By Lemma
12.3
, we have
\mathbf{P}(\Omega^{\prime\prime})=1
.

12.3
Classification of the geodesics towards
\rho_{*}

The aim of this section is to use Theorem
9.1
to derive the structure of the
D
-geodesics towards
\rho_{*}
in the metric space
(\mathcal{S},D,\mathrm{Vol},\rho_{*})
. Specifically, recall from Section
7.4.1
the notion of simple geodesics
\gamma^{(s)}
and the definition of the path
\gamma^{(s\to t)}
going from
\Pi_{D}(s)
to
\Pi_{D}(t)
. Informally, this path is obtained by following
\gamma^{(s)}
down to the merging point with
\gamma^{(t)}
and then ascending to
\Pi_{D}(t)
. The goal of this section is to prove Proposition
9.2
, which we restate here for clarity:

\mathbf{P}
- a.s., all the geodesics towards
\rho_{*}
in
(\mathcal{S},D,\mathrm{Vol},\rho_{*})
are simple geodesics.

In particular, the latter entails that the geodesics to
\rho_{*}
coincide for both metrics
D^{*}
and
D
. The idea of the proof, adapted from
[
23
, Section 6]
, is to cage general geodesics towards the root by typical geodesics towards the root.

Proof.

We begin by introducing the set
\mathfrak{S}
of all points
x\in\mathcal{S}
such that there exists only one
D
-geodesic from
x
to
\rho_{*}
. If
x\in\mathfrak{S}
, then the unique geodesic between
x
and
\rho_{*}
is a simple geodesic of the form
\gamma^{(s)}
, with
s\in\Pi^{-1}_{D}(x)
. By Theorem
9.1
and the re-rooting property (
7.12
), if
(U_{k}:k\geqslant 1)
are i.i.d. uniform random variables independent of
(X,Z,D)
, the event
\{\Pi_{D}(U_{k}):\penalty\ k\geqslant 1\}\subset\mathfrak{S}
has full
\mathbf{P}
-probability. Moreover, since
(U_{k}:k\geqslant 1)
is dense in
[0,1]
and
\mathfrak{z}
is continuous, (
7.16
) guarantees that for any
t\in[0,1]
and
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
k\geqslant 0
such that the simple geodesics
\gamma^{(t)}
and
\gamma^{(U_{k})}
coincide outside
B_{D}(\Pi_{D}(t),\varepsilon)
. We thus deduce that the event:

\big\{\gamma^{(t)}\big((0,Z_{t}-Z_{t_{*}}]\big):\penalty\ t\in[0,1]\big\}\subset\mathfrak{S},

has full probability. For the remainder of the proof, we work on this event and we argue by contradiction, supposing that there exists a geodesic
\gamma:[0,r_{0}]\to\mathcal{S}
, with
\gamma(r_{0})=\rho_{*}
, that does not coincide with a simple geodesic. By a compactness argument, there must exist
\varepsilon\in(0,r_{0})
such that:

\gamma([0,\varepsilon])\cap\Big\{\gamma^{(t)}\big((0,Z_{t}-Z_{t_{*}}]\big):\penalty\ t\in[0,1]\Big\}=\varnothing.

(12.15)

For the sake of clarity, let us fix
t\in\Pi^{-1}_{D}(\gamma(0))
and note that
t\notin\{t_{*}\}\cup\{U_{k}:\penalty\ k\geqslant 1\}
. We argue based on whether
\Pi_{d}(t)
is a leaf or a pinch point of
\mathcal{L}
, and we refer to Figure
43
for an illustration of the argument.

Case 1: The point
\Pi_{d}(t)
is a leaf.
First assume that
t\notin\{0,1\}
. Using the characterization of leaves given in Section
2.1
, we know that
\Pi_{d}(t)
belongs to a loop or that there exists a sequence
(t_{n})_{n\geqslant 0}
of jumping times converging towards
t
and with
t_{n}\preccurlyeq t_{n+1}
for every
n\geqslant 0
– in particular the loop associated with
t_{n}
disconnects the points
\Pi_{d}(0)
and
\Pi_{d}(t)
. Now recall that, conditionally on
X
, the process
Z
along a loop of
\mathcal{L}
is a Brownian bridge. We deduce that we can always find
u_{m}<u_{m}^{\prime}<t<v^{\prime}_{m}<v_{m}
verifying the following properties (see Figure
43
for an illustration):

—

There exists
t^{\prime}\prec t
, with
\Delta_{t^{\prime}}>0
, such that
\Pi_{d}(u_{m}),\Pi_{d}(u_{m}^{\prime}),\Pi_{d}(v_{m}),\Pi_{d}(v^{\prime}_{m})
belong to the loop associated with
t^{\prime}
;

—

All the sequences
(u_{m})_{m>0},(u_{m}^{\prime})_{m>0},(v_{m})_{m>0},(v^{\prime}_{m})_{m>0}
converge to
t
;

—

We have
Z_{u_{m}}\wedge Z_{v_{m}}>Z_{u_{m^{\prime}}}\vee Z_{v_{m^{\prime}}}.

Here, we only used the fact that
\Pi_{d}(t)
is a leaf in the second item. We can even suppose that for all
m\geqslant 1
we have
t_{*}\notin[u_{m},v_{m}]
. Furthermore it is straightforward to verify that, for every
m\geqslant 1
, the path
\gamma^{(u_{m}^{\prime}\to v_{m}^{\prime})}
disconnects
\Pi_{D}\big((u_{m}^{\prime},v_{m}^{\prime})\big)
from its complement. To see why, note that the discrete analogue of the previous statement follows directly from the BDG bijection and planarity. The same method used to prove the Cactus Bound (Lemma
7.5
) can then be applied to extend this result to the continuous setting.

Figure 43:
Illustration of the paths
\gamma^{(u_{m}\to v_{m})}
and
\gamma^{(u_{m}^{\prime}\to v_{m}^{\prime})}
disconnecting respectively
\Pi_{D}((u_{m},v_{m}))
and
\Pi_{D}((u_{m}^{\prime},v_{m}^{\prime}))
from its complement. A geodesic starting from
\Pi_{D}(t)
and targeting
\rho_{*}
must either cross those paths or pass through
\Pi_{D}(u_{m}^{\prime})
and
\Pi_{D}(u_{m})
, or
\Pi_{D}(v_{m}^{\prime})
and
\Pi_{D}(v_{m})
, in this order, which is excluded by (
7.13
).

Since
\rho_{*}
is in the complement of
\Pi_{D}\big((u_{m}^{\prime},v_{m}^{\prime})\big)
, it follows that
\gamma
must intersect the range of
\gamma^{(u_{m}^{\prime}\to v_{m}^{\prime})}
. Recalling that
(u_{m}^{\prime})_{m>0}
and
(v_{m}^{\prime})_{m>0}
both converge to
t
, we obtain from (
12.15
) that
\gamma
must hit
\gamma^{(u_{m}^{\prime}\to v_{m}^{\prime})}
at
\Pi_{D}(u_{m}^{\prime})
or
\Pi_{D}(v_{m}^{\prime})
for some time
s_{m}^{\prime}
, for every
m
large enough. We can then apply the same argument with
u_{m}^{\prime}
and
v_{m}^{\prime}
replaced by
u_{m}
and
v_{m}
to deduce that, for
m
large enough,
\gamma
has also to intersect the set
\{\Pi_{D}(u_{m}),\Pi_{D}(v_{m})\}
at some time
s_{m}>s^{\prime}_{m}
. By (
7.13
), we get:

\displaystyle D(\gamma(s_{m}),\rho_{*})

\displaystyle\geqslant D\big(\rho_{*},\Pi_{D}(u_{m})\big)\wedge D\big(\rho_{*},\Pi_{D}(v_{m})\big)

\displaystyle=\big(Z_{u_{m}}-\inf Z\big)\wedge\big(Z_{v_{m}}-\inf Z\big)

\displaystyle\geqslant\big(Z_{u_{m}^{\prime}}-\inf Z\big)\vee\big(Z_{v_{m}^{\prime}}-\inf Z\big)

\displaystyle=D(\rho_{*},\Pi_{D}(u_{m}^{\prime}))\vee D(\rho_{*},\Pi_{D}(v_{m}^{\prime}))\geqslant D(\gamma(s_{m}^{\prime}),\rho_{*}),

where in the second and last line we used (
7.13
). The previous display is in contradiction with the fact that
\gamma
is a geodesic towards
\rho_{*}
since
s_{m}>s_{m}^{\prime}
. This completes the proof in Case
1
for
\Pi_{D}(t)
a leaf different
of
\Pi_{D}(0)=\Pi_{D}(1)
. The same argument as above applies to the case
\Pi_{D}(0)=\Pi_{D}(1)
after re-rooting the looptree at time
U_{1}
. As before, we can disconnect
\Pi_{D}(0)=\Pi_{D}(1)
from
\rho_{*}
using simple geodesics starting from faces near
\Pi_{D}(0)=\Pi_{D}(1)
. The details are left to the reader.

Case 2: The point
\Pi_{d}(t)
is a pinch point
. We can use a similar argument to the previous case, but it requires considering 8 points to trap the geodesic
\gamma
. To avoid this technicality, we can deduce Case 2 directly from Case 1 using topological considerations. Recall that the skeleton of the looptree is the set
\mathrm{Skel}:=\{\Pi_{d}(r):r\in[0,1]\>\>\text{is a pinch point time}\}
. Using the same arguments as in the proof of Proposition
2.2
, it is straightforward to verify that
\mathrm{Skel}
is totally disconnected – meaning that the connected subsets are singletons. Furthermore, by a compactness argument combined with Theorem
8.1
and Proposition
4.2
, it follows that the canonical projection
\texttt{p}:\mathcal{L}\to\mathcal{S}
realizes an homeomorphism between
\mathrm{Skel}
and its image
\texttt{p}(\mathrm{Skel})=\Pi_{D}(r\in[0,1]\>\>\text{is a pinch point time})
. This entails that the set
\texttt{p}(\mathrm{Skel})
is also totally disconnected, implying that the geodesic
\gamma
must take values outside of
\texttt{p}(\mathrm{Skel})
at any non-empty open interval. Therefore, there exists some
\varepsilon^{\prime}<\varepsilon
such that
\gamma(\varepsilon^{\prime})=\Pi_{D}(s)
for some leaf time
s\in[0,1]
. We can then apply Case 1 to derive that
\gamma(\varepsilon^{\prime}+\cdot)
is a simple geodesic, which contradicts (
12.15
).
∎

We conclude this section with a discussion of some consequence of Proposition
9.2
. For simplicity, as in the previous proof, let
\texttt{p}:\mathcal{L}\to\mathcal{S}
denote the canonical projection. Proposition
9.2
, combined with Proposition
4.2
, shows that
\mathbf{P}
-almost surely, for any point
u\in\mathcal{L}
in the looptree, the number of distinct geodesics from
\texttt{p}(u)
towards
\rho_{*}
is equal to the degree of
u
in
\mathcal{L}
(see Section
2.1
) which belongs to
\{1,2\}
. In particular, the
cut locus
15
15

15

There are different notions of cut locus depending on the regularity of the underlying space, here we follow
[
8
]
.
of
\mathcal{S}
relative to the root
\rho_{*}
, i.e. the set of all points that are connected to
\rho_{*}
by at least two distinct geodesics, is exactly
\texttt{p}(\mathrm{Skel})
, where we recall that
\mathrm{Skel}
is the skeleton of
\mathcal{L}
. Furthermore, as seen in the proof of Proposition
9.2
, the set
\texttt{p}(\mathrm{Skel})
is in fact totally disconnected, i.e. the connected subsets are singletons. This situation contrasts with the Brownian sphere case where the cut locus is a tree, with uncountably many regular points of order
2
, from which we can start two distinct geodesics towards
\rho_{*}
, and a countable dense set of branch points of order
3
, from which we can start three such geodesics, see
[
90
]
.

More generally, this raises the question of studying
geodesic networks
in the
\alpha
-stable carpet/gasket, i.e. the possible topology of the set of all geodesics linking two points. This was first addressed by Angel, Kolesnik & Miermont
[
8
]
, and then considerably developed by Miller & Qian
[
114
]
and Le Gall
[
92
]
in the Brownian sphere case. Some universality is expected for planar random metrics and a basis of
27,28
or
29
networks are expected to show up in Brownian geometry, in Liouville Quantum metric
[
65
]
, in Aldous-Kendall planar metrics
[
28
]
and in the directed landscape
[
54
]
. However, our situation differs, as the
\alpha
-stable carpets and gaskets are not homeomorphic to the sphere.

12.4
Estimates for good points along typical geodesics

We finally come to the proof of Proposition
9.3
on the density of good points along typical geodesics, which we recall here for the reader’s convenience:
There exist constants
c,C\in(0,\infty)
such that

\mathbf{E}\left[\int_{0}^{D(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})}\mathrm{d}u\ \mathbbm{1}_{\{\gamma_{1,2}(u)\mbox{ is $\varepsilon$-bad}\,\}}\right]\leqslant C\cdot\varepsilon^{c},\quad\text{for every }\varepsilon>0.

As in the previous section, since the derivation of the last display will use the biased version of
(\mathcal{S},D)
, one first needs to check measurability issues and begin with some remarks on the concept of good points in a fixed geodesic metric space.

In this direction, consider
\mathbf{M}:=(M,d_{M},\mu,x,(x_{1},x_{2}))
a rooted bi-marked weighted geodesic compact space with a unique geodesic
\gamma_{1,2}
going from
x_{1}
to
x_{2}
. Generalizing the notion introduced in Section
9
, we say that
u\in[0,d_{M}(x_{1},x_{2})]
is an
\varepsilon
-good time (for
\mathbf{M}
) if
\gamma_{1,2}(t)
for
(u-\varepsilon)\vee 0\leqslant t\leqslant(u+\varepsilon)\wedge d_{M}(x_{1},x_{2})
, coincides with the concatenation of at most two portions of geodesics towards the root
x
. Otherwise, we say that
u
is an
\varepsilon
-bad time (for
\mathbf{M}
), and we use the same terminology for the point
\gamma_{1,2}(u)
.
We now relate this notion with that of
aligned points
. Specifically, three points
{y_{1},y_{2},y_{3}}
are said to be aligned if they all lie on the range of the same geodesic. This condition can be expressed equivalently in terms of the metric:
d_{M}(y_{\tau(1)},y_{\tau(3)})=d_{M}(y_{\tau(1)},y_{\tau(2)})+d_{M}(y_{\tau(2)},y_{\tau(3)})
for some permutation
\tau
of the indices
{1,2,3}
. The connection with good points is formalized in the following lemma for which we introduce some notation, and refer to Figure
44
for an illustration. Fix
u\in(0,d(x_{1},x_{2}))
as well as
\varepsilon>0
and decompose
{\gamma_{1,2}}
into the three parts

\displaystyle L_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\big\{{\gamma_{1,2}}(t):t\in[0,(u-\varepsilon)\vee 0]\big\},

\displaystyle C_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\big\{{\gamma_{1,2}}(t):t\in((u-\varepsilon)\vee 0,(u+\varepsilon)\wedge{d}({x}_{1},{x}_{2}))\big\},

\displaystyle R_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\big\{{\gamma_{1,2}}(t):t\in[(u+\varepsilon)\wedge{d}({x}_{1},{x}_{2}),{d}(x_{1},{x}_{2})]\big\}

thus isolating the
\varepsilon
-neighborhood
C_{\varepsilon}
of
{\gamma_{1,2}}(u)
inside
{\gamma_{1,2}}
. We write
y=\gamma_{1,2}(u)
and

\qquad y_{1}^{(\varepsilon)}=\gamma_{1,2}\left((u-\varepsilon)\vee 0\right),\qquad y_{2}^{(\varepsilon)}=\gamma_{1,2}\left((u+\varepsilon)\wedge d(x_{1},x_{2})\right)

for the extremities of
C_{\varepsilon}
inside
\gamma_{1,2}
. Then we have:

Lemma 12.4
.

With the notation above, the point
y
is
\varepsilon
-good if and only if for every
z\in C_{\varepsilon}
, the points
\{z,y_{1}^{(\varepsilon)},x\}
or the points
\{z,y_{2}^{(\varepsilon)},x\}
are aligned. Moreover, if
y
is
\varepsilon
-bad, then there exists
z^{\prime}\in C_{\varepsilon}
such that the geodesic from
z^{\prime}
to
x
is non-trivial
16
16

16

A trivial geodesic is a constant geodesic with length
0
.
and only intersects the range of
\gamma_{1,2}
at its starting point
z^{\prime}
.

Figure 44:
Illustration of the notation for Lemma
12.4
. On this picture,
y
is an
\varepsilon
-bad point, because there exists a geodesic starting from the point
z^{\prime}\in C_{\varepsilon}
that immediately leaves
\gamma_{1,2}
.

Remark 12.5

(Geodesic stars)
.

In particular, with the notation of the lemma, the point
z^{\prime}
is a
3
-star point along
\gamma_{1,2}
, where we recall that a

k
-geodesic star
is a point from which which we can start
k
disjoint geodesics (appart from their starting point). These points have been studied in depth in the case of the Brownian geometry
[
8
,
92
,
112
,
114
]
and in related contexts
[
28
,
54
]
.

Proof.

Notice first that since there is a unique geodesic between
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
, if a geodesic
\gamma
intersects
{\gamma_{1,2}}
at two points, then it must actually coincide with it in-between. A geodesic
\gamma
to
x
starting from
C_{\varepsilon}
must then stick to a part of
{\gamma_{1,2}}
before leaving
{\gamma_{1,2}}
at some point
x_{\gamma}
, and then go to
x
without crossing
{\gamma_{1,2}}
again. Using this remark, if there exists
z\in C_{\varepsilon}
such that neither
\{z,y_{1}^{(\varepsilon)},x\}
nor
\{z,y_{2}^{(\varepsilon)},x\}
are aligned, then a geodesic
\gamma
going from
z
to
x
necessary leaves
\gamma_{1,2}
at
x_{\gamma}\in C_{\varepsilon}
. As a consequence, the part of
\gamma
from
z^{\prime}=x_{\gamma}
to
x
gives a non-trivial geodesic only intersecting the range of
\gamma_{1,2}
at its starting point
x_{\gamma}\in C_{\varepsilon}
, as claimed in the statement of the lemma. Similarly if
y
is
\varepsilon
-good, then
C_{\varepsilon}
, and its closure, is covered by the range of two geodesics towards the root
x
. Those must form two closed intervals by the above remark, and we infer that for any
z\in C_{\varepsilon}
either
\{z,y_{1}^{(\varepsilon)},x\}
or
\{z,y_{2}^{(\varepsilon)},x\}
belong to one of the above two geodesics and are thus aligned.

Figure 45:
Illustration of the proof of Lemma
12.4
. If any point of
C_{\varepsilon}
is aligned with either
\{y_{1}^{(\varepsilon)},x\}
or
\{y_{2}^{(\varepsilon)},x\}
, then we can cover
C_{\varepsilon}
with the range of two geodesics
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}
towards the root
x
.

To conclude, it remains to show that if for every
z\in C_{\varepsilon}
the points
\{z,y_{1}^{(\varepsilon)},x\}
or the points
\{z,y_{2}^{(\varepsilon)},x\}
are aligned, then
y
is necessarily
\varepsilon
-good.
In this direction, consider the set
\widetilde{L}_{\varepsilon}
(resp.
\widetilde{R}_{\varepsilon}
) of all points in the closure of
C_{\varepsilon}
which are aligned with
\{y_{1}^{(\varepsilon)},x\}
(resp.
\{y_{2}^{(\varepsilon)},x\}
), that is from which there is a geodesic
\gamma
to
x
that leaves
{\gamma_{1,2}}
on the left part
L_{\varepsilon}
, i.e.
x_{\gamma}\in L_{\varepsilon}
(resp.
x_{\gamma}\in R_{\varepsilon}
). By our assumption these two sets cover the closure of
C_{\varepsilon}
. If the right extremity
y_{2}^{(\varepsilon)}
of
C_{\varepsilon}
belongs to
\widetilde{L}_{\varepsilon}
then there exists a geodesic
\gamma
going to
x
which coincides with
{\gamma_{1,2}}
on
C_{\varepsilon}
, this implies that
y
is
\varepsilon
-good. Otherwise, by the Hopf-Rinow theorem we can find a point
z\in C_{\varepsilon}
from which we can start two geodesics to
x
, one leaving
{\gamma_{1,2}}
on
L_{\varepsilon}
and one on
R_{\varepsilon}
. This implies in particular that
\mathrm{Cl}(C_{\varepsilon})
is covered by the range of these two geodesics targeting
x
, this again gives that
y
is
\varepsilon
-good.
∎

Let us now use the above lemma to prove that the random variables considered in Proposition
9.3
are indeed measurable. To this end, for every
\mathbf{M}:=(M,d_{M},\mu,x,(x_{1},x_{2}))
rooted bi-marked weighted geodesic compact space with exactly one geodesic going from
x_{1}
to
x_{2}
and
u\geqslant 0
, we set
\mathrm{Bad}_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M},u):=\mathbbm{1}_{u\mbox{\small\penalty\ is a $\varepsilon$-bad time for $\mathbf{M}$}}
, with the convention
\mathrm{Bad}(\mathbf{M},u):=0
if
u>d_{M}(x_{1},x_{2})
. This function is invariant by isometry and therefore we can view it as a maps from
\mathbb{PM}^{2\bullet,1}_{\mathrm{root}}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
to
\mathbb{R}_{+}
, that we still denote by
\mathrm{Bad}_{\varepsilon}
with a slight abuse of notation.

Lemma 12.6
.

The function
\mathrm{Bad}_{\varepsilon}:\mathbb{PM}_{\mathrm{root}}^{2\bullet,1}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\{0,1\}
is measurable.

Proof.

It suffices to establish that the function
\mathrm{Bad}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}:\mathbb{PM}_{\mathrm{root}}^{2\bullet,1}\times[0,1]\to\{0,1\}
defined by
\mathrm{Bad}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(\mathbf{M},v)=\mathrm{Bad}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(\mathbf{M},v\cdot d_{M}(x_{1},x_{2}))
is measurable; here
d_{M}(x_{1},x_{2})
stands for the distance between the two marks which is a quantity invariant by isometry and it is thus well defined. We are going to prove that
\mathrm{Bad}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}
is lower semicontinuous, which directly implies the desired result. Let
(\mathbf{M},v)\in\mathbb{PM}_{\mathrm{root}}^{2\bullet,1}\times[0,1]
and
(\mathbf{M}_{n},v_{n})
,
n\geqslant 1
, a sequence in
\mathbb{PM}_{\mathrm{root}}^{2\bullet,1}\times[0,1]
converging to
(\mathbf{M},v)
. We need to show that:

\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mathrm{Bad}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(\mathbf{M}_{n},v_{n})\geqslant\mathrm{Bad}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(\mathbf{M},v).

Moreover, since
\mathrm{Bad}_{\varepsilon}
takes values on
\{0,1\}
, it suffices to consider the case when
\mathrm{Bad}_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M}_{n},v_{n})=0
, for every
n\geqslant 1
. In this direction, we recall that by
[
80
, Lemma 2.5]
, we may assume that all the spaces
(M_{n},d_{M_{n}},\mu_{n},x^{n},(x_{1}^{n},x_{2}^{n}))
,
n\geqslant 1
, and
(M,d_{M},\mu,x,(x_{1},x_{2}))
are isometrically embedded into the same compact metric space
(Z,\delta)
, and

M_{n}\to M,\quad\mu_{n}\to\mu,\quad(x^{n},x_{1}^{n},x_{2}^{n})\to(x,x_{1},x_{2}),

(12.16)

as
n\to\infty
.
Next, in this embedding, consider
\gamma
(resp.
\gamma_{n}
,
n\geqslant 1
) the unique geodesic staying in
M
(resp.
M_{n}
,
n\geqslant 1
) going from
x_{1}
to
x_{2}
(resp.
x_{1}^{n}
to
x_{2}^{n}
,
n\geqslant 1
). We claim that for every
t_{n}\in[0,d_{M_{n}}(x_{1}^{n},x_{2}^{n})]
,
n\geqslant 1
, converging to some
t\in[0,d_{M}(x_{1},x_{2})]
we must have
\gamma_{n}(t_{n})\to\gamma(t)
. To see why the claim holds, remark that by compactness, it is equivalent to establish that
\gamma(t)
is the unique limit point of
(\gamma_{n}(t_{n}))_{n\geqslant 1}
. In this direction, fix
z
a limit point and note that necessarily
z\in M
, by the convergence
M_{n}\to M
. Next, observe that since
\gamma_{n}
,
n\geqslant 1
, are geodesics we must have
\delta(x_{1}^{n},\gamma_{n}(t_{n}))=t_{n}
and
\delta(x_{2}^{n},\gamma_{n}(t_{n}))=\delta(x_{1}^{n},x_{2}^{n})-t_{n}
. Hence, passing to the limit, we obtain
\delta(x_{1},z)=t
and
\delta(x_{2},z)=\delta(x_{1},x_{2})-t
. This implies that
z
is in a geodesic taking values on
M
and going from
x_{1}
to
x_{2}
. Since
(M,d,\mu,x,(x_{1},x_{2}))
only has one such geodesic, it follows that
z=\gamma(t)
. Next set
t_{n}^{-}:=\big(v_{n}\cdot d_{M_{n}}(x_{1}^{n},x_{2}^{n})-\varepsilon\big)\vee 0
and
t_{n}^{+}:=\big(v_{n}\cdot d_{n}(x_{1}^{n},x_{2}^{n})+\varepsilon\big)\wedge d_{M_{n}}(x_{1}^{n},x_{2}^{n})
. Similarly, let
t^{-}:=(v\cdot d_{M}(x_{1},x_{2})-\varepsilon)\vee 0
and
t^{+}:=(v\cdot d_{M}(x_{1},x_{2})+\varepsilon)\wedge d_{M}(x_{1},x_{2})
. Consider now
t^{-}\leqslant t\leqslant t^{+}
and remark that, by (
12.16
) and since
v_{n}\to v
, we can always find
t^{-}_{n}\leqslant t_{n}\leqslant t^{+}_{n}
, for every
n\geqslant 1
, such that
t_{n}\to t
, as
n\to\infty
. Then, by the previous discussion, we must have:

\big(\gamma_{n}(t_{n}^{-}),\gamma_{n}(t_{n}),\gamma_{n}(t_{n}^{+})\big)\to\big(\gamma(t^{-}),\gamma(t),\gamma(t^{+})\big),\quad n\to\infty.

(12.17)

Since
\mathrm{Bad}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(\mathbf{M}_{n},v_{n})=0
, for
n\geqslant 1
, then by definition it holds that, for every
n\geqslant 1
, the points
\{\gamma_{n}(t_{n}^{-}),\gamma_{n}(t_{n}),x^{n}\}
or
\{\gamma_{n}(t_{n}^{+}),\gamma_{n}(t_{n}),x^{n}\}
are aligned in
M_{n}
. Hence, by the convergences (
12.16
) and (
12.17
), we derive that the points
\{\gamma(t^{-}),\gamma(t),x\}
or
\{\gamma(t^{+}),\gamma(t),x\}
are aligned in
M
. Since this holds for any arbitrary
t\in[t^{-},t^{+}]
, an application of Lemma
12.4
gives
\mathrm{Bad}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(\mathbf{M},v)=0
, completing the proof.
∎

Coming back to Proposition
9.3
, recall that, under
\mathbf{P}
, the random variables:

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}:=\big(\mathcal{S},D,\mathrm{Vol},\rho_{*},(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})\big)\quad\text{and }\quad\boldsymbol{\widehat{\mathcal{S}}}:=\big(\widehat{\mathcal{S}},\widehat{D},\widehat{\mathrm{Vol}},\widehat{\rho}_{*},(\widehat{\rho}_{1},\widehat{\rho}_{2})\big),

are a.s. in
\mathbb{PM}^{2\bullet,1}_{\mathrm{root}}
. In particular, Lemma
12.6
entails that

\int_{0}^{D(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})}\mathrm{d}u\ \mathbbm{1}_{\{\gamma_{1,2}(u)\mbox{ is $\varepsilon$-bad}\}}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}u\penalty\ \mathrm{Bad}_{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}},u)

is a well defined random variable. Furthermore, since conditionally on
\big(\mathcal{S},D,\mathrm{Vol},\rho_{*},(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})\big)
, the random variable
\triangle
is uniformly distributed in
[-D(\rho_{1},\rho_{2}),D(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})]
, Proposition
9.3
is equivalent to the existence of two constants
c,C\in(0,\infty)
such that:

\mathbf{E}\Big[D(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\{(D(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})-\triangle)/2\mbox{ is $\varepsilon$-bad time for }\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}\}}\Big]\leqslant C\cdot\varepsilon^{c},\quad\text{for every }\varepsilon>0.

(12.18)

By (
12.5
), the latter can be finally be rewritten in the form:

\mathbf{P}\Big(\,\frac{\widehat{D}(\widehat{\rho}_{1},\widehat{\rho}_{2})-\widehat{\triangle}}{2}\mbox{ is an $\varepsilon$-bad time for }\boldsymbol{\widehat{\mathcal{S}}}\,\Big)\leqslant C\cdot\varepsilon^{c},\quad\text{for every }\varepsilon>0,

(12.19)

for possibly two other constants. Here we use again the fact that the random variable and event appearing in (
12.18
) and (
12.19
) are well defined and measurable by Lemma
12.6
.

We aim to prove (
12.19
) by connecting it to the framework established in Section
6
and the results presented therein. In this direction, recall that
(\widehat{X},\widehat{Z},\widehat{t}_{\bullet},\widehat{\sigma})
under
\mathbf{P}
is distributed as
(X,Z,t_{\bullet},\sigma)
under
\mathrm{cst}\cdot\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}\big(1-\sigma,-Z_{t_{\bullet}}\big)\cdot\mathbf{N}^{\bullet}
.
As a key consequence, we adopt the notation from Part
I
for the process
(\widehat{X},\widehat{Z},\widehat{t}_{\bullet},\widehat{\sigma})
by adding a “
\widehat{}
” in the notation. For instance, we write
\widehat{\varpi}\leqslant\widehat{t}_{\bullet}
for the unique pinch point time that minimizes the label along the spine (which is unique by Lemma
4.7
), and we denote by
\widehat{\mathfrak{X}}
its image in the looptree coded by
\widehat{X}
. To obtain (
12.19
) it suffices to establish that:

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}\Big(\Big\{\widehat{\mathfrak{X}}\text{ is }2\varepsilon\text{-trapped}\Big\}\cap\Big\{\frac{(\widehat{D}(\widehat{\rho}_{1},\widehat{\rho}_{2})-\widehat{\triangle})}{2}\text{ is an $\varepsilon$-bad time for }\boldsymbol{\widehat{\mathcal{S}}}\,\Big\}\Big)=0,

(12.20)

and

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}(\widehat{\mathfrak{X}}\text{ is not }\varepsilon\text{-trapped})\leqslant C\cdot\varepsilon^{c},

(12.21)

for yet possibly other constants
c,C\in(0,\infty)
.

The proof of (
12.21
) is based on the estimates of Section
6
, more precisely Theorem
6.1
, and can be found at the end of the section (Lemma
12.9
). We shall thus start with (
12.20
). As in Section
12.2
, this is addressed by translating the problem in terms of planar maps, for which we use the construction from well-labeled unicyclomobiles. In this direction, recall the notation
\Omega^{\prime\prime}
introduced at the end of Section
12.2
, which has full probability, and fix
\omega\in\Omega^{\prime\prime}
. In particular,
(\widehat{\mathcal{S}},\widehat{D},\widehat{\mathrm{Vol}},\widehat{\rho}_{*},(\widehat{\rho}_{1},\widehat{\rho}_{2}))
is embedded on a compact space
(E,d_{E})
, and has a unique geodesic
\widehat{\gamma}_{1,2}
going from
\widehat{\rho}_{1}
to
\widehat{\rho}_{2}
(staying in
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}
). Let us now give the underlying geometric idea which is best illustrated by putting Figures
4
and
21
side by side, see also Figure
46
and its caption. For an interval
I\subset\mathbb{R}
, we write

\Gamma_{I}=\widehat{\gamma}_{1,2}\Big(\Big(I+\frac{1}{2}\big(\widehat{D}(\widehat{\rho}_{1},\widehat{\rho}_{2})-\widehat{\Delta}\big)\Big)\cap[0,\widehat{D}(\widehat{\rho}_{1},\widehat{\rho}_{2})]\Big),

which corresponds to the
I
-neighborhood of
\mathbb{J}
in
\widehat{\gamma}_{1,2}
where
\mathbb{J}
correspond to

\mathbb{J}=\Gamma_{[0]}=\widehat{\gamma}_{1,2}\Big(\frac{1}{2}\big(\widehat{D}(\widehat{\rho}_{1},\widehat{\rho}_{2})-\widehat{\Delta}\big)\Big).

For simplicity, we write
\Gamma
for
\Gamma_{\mathbb{R}}
. Heuristically, when
\widehat{\mathfrak{X}}
is
\varepsilon
-trapped in the sense of Section
6
, then there exist two faces
\mathfrak{F}_{1},\mathfrak{F}_{2}
of
(\widehat{\mathcal{S}},\widehat{D})
, corresponding respectively to the times
\widehat{r}_{1,4}
and
\widehat{r}_{2,3}
therein, such that the piece
\Gamma_{(-\infty,\varepsilon]}
of
\widehat{\gamma}_{1,2}
going to
\widehat{\rho}_{1}
touches both
\mathfrak{F}_{1},\mathfrak{F}_{2}
, and similarly the piece
\Gamma_{[\varepsilon,\infty)}
of
\widehat{\gamma}_{1,2}
going to
\widehat{\rho}_{2}
of
\widehat{\gamma}_{1,2}
going to
\widehat{\rho}_{2}
touches both
\mathfrak{F}_{1},\mathfrak{F}_{2}
(in any order), in such a way that the
parts of these sub-geodesics in-between
\mathfrak{F}_{1},\mathfrak{F}_{2}
separate the root
\widehat{\rho}_{*}
and
\mathbb{J}
. We call these sub-geodesics the left and right “doors”, see Figure
46
for an illustration. In presence of such doors, it is impossible for a geodesic as in Lemma
12.4
to exist, and a fortiori the point
\mathbb{J}
is
\varepsilon
-good.

Figure 46:
On the left, the
\varepsilon
-trapped condition for the point
\widehat{\mathfrak{X}}
imported from Section
6
and its geometric consequence on the right figure in
(\widehat{\mathcal{S}},\widehat{D})
. The color code is the same in the left and right pictures, in particular, the geodesic
\widehat{\gamma}_{1,2}
is constructed by concatenating the simple geodesics starting from
\widehat{\mathfrak{X}}
in each face and the
\varepsilon
-trap condition shows that those geodesics hit two faces separating the
\varepsilon
-neighborhood of
\mathbb{J}
from
\widehat{\rho}_{*}
. In such a situation, it is impossible for a geodesic
\gamma
to start from the
\varepsilon
-neighborhood of
\mathbb{J}
and targeting
\widehat{\rho}_{*}
without crossing
\widehat{\gamma}_{1,2}
. This contradicts Lemma
12.4
and thus implies that
\mathbb{J}
is
\varepsilon
-good.

Let us now proceed with the formal proof and recall that we have fixed
\omega\in\Omega^{\prime\prime}
. We start discussing the implication of
(\widehat{D}(\widehat{\rho}_{1},\widehat{\rho}_{2})-\widehat{\triangle})/2
being
\varepsilon
-bad for
\boldsymbol{\widehat{\mathcal{S}}}
in terms of the planar maps
(\widehat{\mathfrak{M}}_{m_{k}},\widehat{v}_{1}^{m_{k}},\widehat{v}_{2}^{m_{k}},\widehat{\triangle}_{m_{k}})
. In this direction, recall the construction from well-labeled unicyclomobiles,
(\widehat{\mathfrak{M}}_{m_{k}},\widehat{v}_{1}^{m_{k}},\widehat{v}_{2}^{m_{k}},\widehat{\triangle}_{m_{k}})=\mathrm{BDG}^{2\bullet}(\mathbf{u}_{m_{k}},\epsilon)
of Section
10
. As in the proof of Lemma
12.3
, for every
k\geqslant 1
, we fix a white vertex
J_{m_{k}}
attaining the minimal label along the cycle of
\mathbf{u}_{m_{k}}
, and write
\widehat{\gamma}_{1,2}^{m_{k}}
for the geodesic in the map between
\widehat{v}_{1}^{m_{k}}
and
\widehat{v}_{2}^{m_{k}}
obtained by iterating the successor function in each face. Specifically, we see the belt part
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{B}}_{m_{k}}
(with its labels) as a submap of
\mathbf{u}_{m_{k}}
, and for every white corner
c
, we write
\gamma^{(c)}
for the geodesic obtained by iterating the successor function. Then, we let
c^{m_{k}}_{f}
and
c^{m_{k}}_{\ell}
be respectively the first and last corner of
J_{m_{k}}
in the contour order of
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{B}}_{m_{k}}
. In particular,
\gamma^{(c^{m_{k}}_{f})}
(resp.
\gamma^{(c^{m_{k}}_{\ell})}
) defines a geodesic staying in the face
f_{1}^{m_{k}}
(resp.
f_{2}^{m_{k}}
), starting in
J_{m_{k}}
and ending in
\widehat{v}_{1}^{m_{k}}
(resp.
\widehat{v}_{2}^{m_{k}}
). The geodesic
\widehat{\gamma}_{1,2}^{m_{k}}
is obtained by reversing
\gamma^{(c^{m_{k}}_{f})}
and concatenating it with
\gamma^{(c^{m_{k}}_{\ell})}
.

We begin with a preliminary lemma, translating the fact that
(\widehat{D}(\widehat{\rho}_{1},\widehat{\rho}_{2})-\widehat{\triangle})/2
is
\varepsilon
-bad into a geometric condition in the discrete maps:

Lemma 12.7
.

With the notation above, let
\varepsilon>0
and assume that
(\widehat{D}(\widehat{\rho}_{1},\widehat{\rho}_{2})-\widehat{\triangle})/2
is an
\varepsilon
-bad time for
\boldsymbol{\widehat{\mathcal{S}}}
. Then for every
\omega\in\Omega^{\prime\prime}
, we can find
\eta\equiv\eta(\omega)>0
and a sequence, of paths
\gamma_{m_{k}},k\geqslant 1
, respectively in
\widehat{\mathfrak{M}}_{m_{k}}
, from a point of
B_{\mathrm{d}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{\widehat{\mathfrak{M}}_{m_{k}}}}(J_{m_{k}},\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon(\mathfrak{s}_{\mathbf{q}}m_{k})^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}})
to
\widehat{\rho}_{*}^{m_{k}}
, such that, for
k
large enough, the path
\gamma_{m_{k}}
stays at graph distance at least
\eta(\mathfrak{s}_{\mathbf{q}}m_{k})^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}}
from
\widehat{\gamma}_{1,2}^{m_{k}}
in
\widehat{\mathfrak{M}}_{m_{k}}
.

Proof.

Fix
\varepsilon>0
and
\omega\in\Omega^{\prime\prime}
. Recall that the spaces in (
12.7
) and (
12.8
) are embedded in
(E,d_{E})
,
\widehat{\gamma}_{1,2}
is the unique geodesic going from
\widehat{\rho}_{1}
to
\widehat{\rho}_{2}
, the notation
\Gamma=\widehat{\gamma}_{1,2}([0,\widehat{D}(\widehat{\rho}_{1},\widehat{\rho}_{2})])
, and:

V(\widehat{\mathfrak{M}}_{m_{k}})\to\widehat{\mathcal{S}}\quad\text{ and }\quad\big(\widehat{\rho}_{m_{k}},\widehat{v}_{1}^{m_{k}},\widehat{v}_{2}^{m_{k}}\big)\to\big(\widehat{\rho}_{*},\widehat{\rho}_{1},\widehat{\rho}_{2}\big),\quad\text{as}\quad k\to\infty.

(12.22)

Now we claim that the trace of
\gamma^{m_{k}}_{1,2}
in
V(\widehat{\mathfrak{M}}_{m_{k}})
is arbitrarily close to
\Gamma
, asymptotically. Namely, let
\Gamma_{k}
be the set of all points of
V(\widehat{\mathfrak{M}}_{m_{k}})
corresponding to the vertices appearing in the range of
\gamma^{m_{k}}_{1,2}
. Then we claim that:

\lim\limits_{k\to\infty}\sup\big\{d_{E}\big(x,\Gamma\big):\penalty\ x\in\Gamma_{k}\big\}=0.

(12.23)

To see this, remark that by compactness it suffices to show that any adherent point
z
of a sequence
x_{k}\in\Gamma_{k},

k\geqslant 1
, must verify
d_{E}\big(z,\Gamma\big)=0
. But by (
12.22
), any such adherent point belongs to
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}
and satisfies
\widehat{D}(\widehat{\rho}_{1},\widehat{\rho}_{2})=\widehat{D}(\widehat{\rho}_{1},z)+\widehat{D}(z,\widehat{\rho}_{2})
. This implies that
z
is on a geodesic of
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}
going from
\widehat{\rho}_{1}
to
\widehat{\rho}_{2}
, and thus
z\in\Gamma
, since
\widehat{\gamma}_{1,2}
is the unique such geodesic. For the remainder of the proof we assume that
d_{*}:=(\widehat{D}(\widehat{\rho}_{1},\widehat{\rho}_{2})-\widehat{\triangle})/2
is
\varepsilon
-bad, and notice that by the above argument we have

J_{m_{k}}\to\widehat{\gamma}_{1,2}(d_{*})=\mathbb{J},\quad\text{as}\quad k\to\infty,

(12.24)

in the embeddings. Then by Lemma
12.4
, we know that we can find a point
t\in[(d_{*}-\varepsilon)\vee 0,(d_{*}+\varepsilon)\wedge\widehat{D}(\widehat{\rho}_{1},\widehat{\rho}_{2})]
such that there exists a geodesic
\widehat{\gamma}_{\perp}:[0,\widehat{D}(\widehat{\gamma}_{1,2}(t),\widehat{\rho}_{*})]\to\widehat{\mathcal{S}}
connecting
\widehat{\gamma}_{1,2}(t)
and
\widehat{\rho}_{*}
, which only intersects
\Gamma
at its starting point. In particular,
\widehat{\rho}_{*}
is not in
\Gamma
. Next set

\widehat{\gamma}(r):=\widehat{\gamma}_{\perp}\big(r+(\varepsilon/4)\wedge\widehat{D}(\widehat{\gamma}_{1,2}(t),\widehat{\rho}_{*})\big),\quad 0\leqslant r\leqslant R:=(\widehat{D}(\widehat{\gamma}_{1,2}(t),\widehat{\rho}_{*})-\varepsilon/4)\vee 0.

By construction and (
12.23
), we can find
\eta\in(0,\varepsilon/4)
, such that
\widehat{\gamma}
stays at distance at least
5\eta
from
\Gamma_{k}
, for
k
large. The desired result now follows straightforwardly by approximating
\widehat{\gamma}
using paths in
\widehat{\mathfrak{M}}_{m_{k}}
,
k\geqslant 1
. Specifically, pick an integer
N\geqslant 1
such that
R/N<\eta
. By (
12.22
), for
k
even larger, we can find,
0\leqslant i\leqslant N
,
a point
x_{i}^{k}
in
V(\widehat{\mathfrak{M}}_{m_{k}})
such that
d_{E}(\widehat{\gamma}(iR/N),x_{i}^{k})<\eta
, and without loss of generality we may assume that
x_{N}^{k}=\widehat{\rho}_{m_{k}}
since
\widehat{\gamma}(R)=\widehat{\rho}_{*}
. By the triangle inequality and using the fact that
\widehat{\gamma}
is a geodesic, we infer that

d_{E}\big(x_{i}^{k},\Gamma_{m_{k}}\big)>4\eta\quad\text{ and }\quad d_{E}\big(x_{i}^{k},x_{(i+1)\wedge N}^{k}\big)<3\eta,

(12.25)

for every
1\leqslant i\leqslant N
. Now, concatenate in
\widehat{\mathfrak{M}}_{m_{k}}
geodesics from
x_{i}^{k}
to
x_{i+1}^{k}
, for
1\leqslant i\leqslant N-1
, and denote the obtained path on the map

\widehat{\mathfrak{M}}_{m_{k}}
by
\gamma_{m_{k}}
. Then (
12.25
) entails that the trace of
\gamma_{m_{k}}
in
V(\widehat{\mathfrak{M}}_{m_{k}})
is at Hausdorff distance in
(E,d_{E})
from
\Gamma_{k}
bigger than
\eta
